Phần Lý Luận / Theory

Trang sách 43 · scan p076

…emphasize emptiness and fullness (虚实 xuēshí), hardness and softness issuing forth together (刚柔齐发 gāngróu qífā) — only then does one recognize that Taiji (太极 tàijí) truly has real form, and is not merely entrusted to empty words.

Hetu Diagram of the Interaction of Heaven and Earth (河图天地交图 Hétú tiāndì jiāo tú)

Residing in the center (居中), fifty (五十)

Yang within (阳内), yin without (阴外)

Born of yang, completed in yin (生于阳成于阴)

In the east, the child grows to extreme yin (东子长极阴)

Yang wanes in the west; yin grows in the west (阳消于西，阴长于西)

Luoshu Diagram of the Interaction of Sun and Moon (洛书日月交图 Luòshū rìyuè jiāo tú)

Residing in the center (居中), fifty (五十)

Yang wanes at due west (阳消正西)

Yin wanes at due east (阴长正东)

Yin at its extreme in the northeast (阴极东北)

Yang at its extreme in the southwest (阳极西南)

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, First Fascicle (陈氏太极拳图说卷首)

Discourse on the Hetu's Interaction of Heaven and Earth and the Luoshu's Interaction of Sun and Moon (河图天地交、洛书日月交论)

When heaven and earth interact, there is Peace (泰 tài, hexagram 11); the Book of Changes accordingly speaks of hardship and steadfastness at the third line (九三 jiǔsān). When sun and moon interact, there is After Completion (既济 jìjì, hexagram 63); the Changes accordingly counsels vigilance against the leaky garment — the tattered rags — at the fourth line (六四 liùsì). How deep is the gentleman's caution, drawn from the Diagram and the Writing, concerning interaction!

[Diagram: Two cosmological diagrams — the Hetu (River Diagram) showing heaven-and-earth interaction with a central cross 居中/五十, yang within and yin without, ten circles above and ten dots below; and the Luoshu showing sun-and-moon interaction with the same central cross and directional annotations of waning and waxing yang and yin.]
Trang sách 44 · scan p077

As for surveying the Hetu (河图 Hétú, River Diagram) as a whole: setting aside the central five and ten, the outer numbers total thirty; one diameter encircles three, hence round. To say the Diagram is the image of Heaven is acceptable. Surveying the Luoshu (洛书 Luòshū, Luo Writing) as a whole: setting aside the central five, the outer numbers total forty; one diameter encircles four, hence square. To say the Writing is the image of Earth is also acceptable.

The Diagram's numbers total fifty-five — odd and full: is that not the image of the sun? The Writing's numbers total forty-five — even and deficient: is that not the image of the moon? Can one who dwells in spirit upon the Diagram and the Writing be without the work of turning back upon oneself?

For the interaction of heaven and earth, sun and moon, is precisely the principle of our own nature and life, the pivot of Gou (姤 gòu, hexagram 44) and Fu (复 fù, hexagram 24). If one can truly with this wash the heart and retire to store oneself in secrecy: heaven and earth interact and the single yang is held within the six yins; sun and moon interact and one steadfastness completes the body of pure yang. Then heaven and earth unite in virtue, sun and moon unite in light, and the wondrous workings of generation, restriction, control and transformation reside no longer in the Diagram and the Writing, but in me. Otherwise, the Diagram and the Writing should not be dragged in at all — the Diagram stays merely a Diagram, the Writing merely a Writing, just figures and numbers of square and round, odd and even: what have they to do with the learning of exhausting principle, fulfilling nature, and settling the mandate of fate (Lai's annotation, 来注 Lái zhù)?

Taijiquan Internal Essence Round Diagram (太极拳内精圆图 tàijíquán nèi jīng yuántú)

The Hetu is in truth the ancestor of the essence of silk-reeling (缠丝 chánsī). Opened singly it is like the character 一. Joining the two ends and propping open the circumference yields the Taiji round diagram; crossed, it becomes the silk-reeling diagram; doubled, the narrow (褊 biǎn) diagram; doubled again, with the four corners propped open, the square diagram — down to three-, five-, six-, seven-, eight-, and nine-cornered figures, all square diagrams, all derived from one generating two. Even the round diagram of the sun and moon's courses and their interleaved reeling sequences recorded in the Canon of Yao (《尧典》 Yáodiǎn) — is it not all born from the single stroke 一? This is not discussed in full here. This diagram is devoted exclusively to the theory of silk-reeling energy (缠丝劲 chánsī jìng). Because the arms and legs (股肱 gǔgōng) of the quan (拳 quán) ought to employ it, it is extended to them here.

[Diagram: A complex circular diagram of concentric rings titled 太极拳内精圆图; at the center a hexagram-like figure within a circle surrounded by small dotted circles; the outer rings carry Hetu numbers (7 top, 3 left, 4 right, 9 lower right, 1/5/6/10 along the bottom); vertical caption to the right: 太极拳微河图 / 作图缘起 / 劲图. It represents silk-reeling energy derived from the River Map's numerology.]
Trang sách 45 · scan p078

*From the preliminary volume (juanshou 卷首) of Chen shi Taijiquan Tushuo. No diagrams; pure theoretical prose.*

English translation

Yet what is drawn upon — whether the names of the hexagrams, the line statements (yaoci 爻辞), or passages on the generation and conquest of water, fire, wood, metal, and earth — is cited only because of superficial resemblance, used as supporting evidence. Such loose, shallow borrowings are no more than the dregs of the ancients' writing. Scattered and unordered, picked at will, they bear no likeness to the ordered sequence of the great numbers of generation in the [River] Chart and [Luo] Writing — how much less to the essence within them!

That said, one need not be rigid about this. The ancients' writings were not originally composed for the martial art (quan 拳); yet they are so all-embracing that it seems as if they had been written for it — whatever one picks up at random fits. This shows how subtle and lively the principle (li 理) of Taiji 太极 is: it makes the ten thousand things and affairs each fit their place, and whoever applies it finds it answers his intent. However minute the matter, the principle is never left out. However tangled and numerous the affairs of the world, the ten thousand differences all return to one — what wonder could be greater!

If later people can take even the dregs of the ancients' writing and, tracing the branches, seek the source, it cannot fail to benefit the learning of body and mind, of life and destiny. Though the martial art is called a small Way, the great Way of Taiji resides within it. And its uses are the broadest; those who practice movement should attend to it. Ponder it deeply, think it through finely, and in time you will gain your own insight. How the art benefits a person is by no means slight!

Taijiquan tuiyuan jie — An Explanation of the Origins of Taijiquan

A person has Heaven for father and Earth for mother; he is nothing but conceived and born of the qi 气 of Taiji's Yin and Yang (speak of qi and the principle is already within it). Heaven and Earth are governed by this very principle (speak of principle and the qi is within it); the unity of the three teachings is also this principle; and of the myriad things and affairs of the cosmos (the Four Directions above and below are called yu 宇; past and present, zhou 宙) — how could any not be this principle? How could the single art of the martial way stand outside this principle and possess another?

This is why the art is named Taijiquan. Quan 拳 here means "weight/scale" (quan 权): that which weighs things and thereby knows their light and heavy. Its principle is truly rooted in Taiji, and its application does not omit the two fists. Moreover, a person's whole body, from crown to heel, is entirely Taiji — that is, the whole body is entirely quan. One must not take a single fist to be all there is of the fist. Its pivot lies in the one heart-mind (xin 心). The heart governs reverence (jing 敬) and also stillness (jing 静). Able to be reverent and still, one naturally preserves emptiness and spirit-quickening. When the sovereign mind holds command, the hundred bones all obey orders. Movement generates Yang; stillness generates Yin; one movement and one stillness are each other's root. The clear qi rises, the turbid qi descends. Baihui 百会 above and Zhongji 中极 below are the bolt and bar of the one body.

The beginner's work: first seek the lurking response (fuying 伏应). Where the incoming line turns its pass, one qi generates all. Hands and eyes must be lively; do not move rashly. As for this qi: utmost great, utmost firm; nourish it straightly and no harm comes; it fills Heaven and Earth. It is paired with righteousness and with the Way — its source lies in accumulating righteousness — vast and flooding it circulates, naturally one qi. Light as willow catkins, hard as metal and stone; in awe compare it to a tiger's ferocity, in speed to a hawk in flight. In motion it is like flowing water; at rest it equals a mountain standing. In advancing it is beyond what others can perceive; in retreating none can name its swiftness. The principle is refined, the methods dense, their threads analyzed strand by strand. Released, it fills the six directions; gathered in, it retires and hides in the secret. In greatness it has nothing outside it; in smallness nothing inside it. The harmonized original qi goes wherever intention (yi 意) sends it; where intention points, the whole spirit is concentrated. Its changes are like a dragon — no one can fathom them.

Summary (3 lines)

Page 78 closes the discussion of borrowing from the Yijing for the art of quan and opens the "Taijiquan tuiyuan jie" (Explaining the origins of Taijiquan). Core claim: quan is rooted in Taiji — quan means scale/weight, the whole body is Taiji, and movement and stillness are each other's root. It ends on the beginner's method — first seek the lurking response, one qi generating all — and the consummate state of "changes like a dragon."

Trang sách 46 · scan p079

*Juanshou 卷首. Ends with a modern editorial footnote ①. No diagrams.*

English translation

The application lies in the heart-mind (xin 心) — this is the true secret. Neither leaning one way nor the other, neither exceeding nor falling short. Within, use it to cultivate the self; without, use it to control the enemy. Adapt as the moment requires, only because your resources overflow. Neither adhering nor parting, neither sticking nor leaving. Fit bone to bone and joint to tenon, examining with fine care. Truly accumulated strength over a long time, and one ascends the hall and enters the inner chamber.

Taijiquan zhu jie — An Explanation of the Postures of Taijiquan

In a person's whole body, the heart-mind is master and governs the flesh. The heart is called the Way-heart (daoxin 道心), that is, the heart of principle (li 心). Yet that which within principle can move and turn is called qi 气, and this qi is precisely Yin-Yang and the Five Phases (wuxing 五行). But without principle, qi has nothing to govern it; and without qi, principle has nothing to move it. Hence principle and qi do not separate from each other but adhere to each other — this is Taiji 太极 rooting in Wuji 无极.

Heaven generates man precisely by planting this principle and this qi in the heart. When one has gained a little knowledge, the principle and qi in the human heart are undifferentiated, without trace or sign. Yet within the heart, arising either from within or stirred from without, thought and meaning (yisi 意思) are born. Before it is born it is a vast undifferentiated mix, nothing at all there. As it is about to be born, the intention is almost imperceptibly slight, yet the principle of Yin and Yang lies stored within it. Following its natural springs (the heart's forming of a shape), it still remains within the human heart (this is what the Zhongyong 中庸 calls "not yet emerged" — wei fa 未发). When it is about to emerge, the shape formed in the heart presents itself outward — up or down, left or right, forward or back, oblique or upright — and the body-methods (shenfa 身法) of the whole frame are all there in readiness.

At the moment before it emerges and takes shape, see what shape the intention resembles and name it accordingly — picked up at will, with no pre-set mind. Naming from the shape at that moment is called a "posture" (zhuo 着, i.e. zhao 招). And within each posture, the five senses and hundred bones follow their natural momentum, while the qi of Yin-Yang and the Five Phases moves within them. This is what is meant by: movement generates Yang, stillness generates Yin, and one movement and one stillness are each other's root — Yang contains Yin, Yin contains Yang. This is precisely the natural constitution of Taijiquan.

If, within each posture, one insisted on pointing out what exactly is Yang, what is Yin, what is Yin-within-Yang, what is Yang-within-Yin, such talk would be far too rigid — more words than could be spoken. Even if one could say it, omissions would remain. It rests on the student examining with fine care and awakening of himself over time. The sages of old said: "One can give a person the compass and square, but cannot make them skillful." Drawing one inference from three rests with the learner: neither cling rigidly, nor cling one-sidedly.

Seven-character folk verse, the first

Peng 拥 (ward-off), zhi 摭 (pluck/seize), ji 挤 (press), na 捺 (push down) must be taken seriously;

Draw in the opponent and land on emptiness, let him intrude at will.

When the whole body follows itself in concert, the enemy can hardly come near;

Four ounces (四两) transform and move eight thousand catties.

① [Editorial footnote] In his Taijiquan Tuhua Jiangyi 陈鑫 (Chen Xin) called body, heart, principle, qi, intention, will, feeling, scene, and spirit the "rules of Taijiquan," noting specially that "the nine items from body to spirit were all composed by Pinsan" (Chen Pinsan 陈品三, Chen Xin's courtesy name). The book's "editor" Chen Chunyuan 陈椿元, besides revising and supplementing those nine items, added discussions of constancy (heng 恒), postures (zhuo 着), an appended discrimination of the central qi (zhongqi 中气), and transformation (hua 化). Compared side by side with the "nine items from body to spirit," the meaning is broadly the same with minor differences, but the language style and angle of exposition differ.

Summary (3 lines)

Page 79 closes the "true secret" — application lies in the heart, neither leaning nor exceeding — and opens the "Taijiquan zhu jie." It argues the heart governs the body, principle and qi adhere inseparably (Taiji rooting in Wuji 无极), and intention begets a shape from which postures are named. It ends with a seven-character folk verse on peng/zhi/ji/na and "four ounces moving eight thousand catties," plus an editorial footnote on the text's transmission.

Trang sách 47 · scan p080

Heaven, earth, and the ten thousand things are all born from nothingness into being. Once there is being, things cannot be enumerated. Take, for instance, the art of practicing quan (拳): at first this technique did not exist; once it exists, there is no harm in using this being to arrive at the point where transformation is achieved without deliberate mind and leaves no trace of form — then what exists returns to nothingness. As it is said: form is emptiness, emptiness is form; emptiness and form, form and emptiness. The reason I again draw a circle is precisely this.

Front-view diagram of silk reeling (chansi, 缠丝) on the human body

The whole body is entirely reeling jin (劲). Roughly speaking, inward reeling and outward reeling are both issued in accordance with movement. There is the case of the left hand forward and right hand behind, or right hand forward and left hand behind, united in one smooth coordination. There is also the case of the left inward-coiling while the right coils behind, and the case of using reverse-backward jin that coils toward the back. Each follows the circumstances of the posture, executed in the natural way.

Wait until the qi (气) issued by the hands has traveled to the feet, then the big toe joins together with the hand, and only at this point may the foot press down firmly. This jin is all issued from the heart, entering inward into the bone seams and reaching outward to the skin and flesh. It is one stream, not several streams. Jin is precisely the qi issued from the heart; when it attains central correctness it becomes central qi (中气), and when cultivated it becomes the vast, flooding qi (浩然之气).

Back-view diagram of silk reeling on the human body

On the back: the crown of the head is ding jin (顶劲, top jin); the Dazhui point (大椎) is the fork of the routes; below the fork are the arms; the central bone is the spine; the two kidneys are the waist. The emptiness and fullness of the feet follow the hands: when the hand is empty the foot is also empty; when the hand is full the foot is also full.

Essential discussion of silk reeling in Taijiquan (太极拳)

Taijiquan is the method of silk reeling: reeling while advancing, reeling while retreating, reeling left and right, reeling up and down, reeling inward and outward, reeling large and small, smooth reeling and reverse reeling. In essence, none of it is anything other than reeling while drawing in, reeling while advancing — the techniques cannot each stand as separate moves. If each stood as a separate move, yin and yang would not serve as each other's root. People in the world do not understand this and all dismiss it as "soft hands" — judging only from the outside, they

[Diagram: Front view of a human figure with silk-reeling pathways; the label links the haidi (海底, "sea bottom," perineum area) with the origins of the Ren (任) and Du (督) meridians.]
[Diagram: Back view of a human figure with silk-reeling pathways; the label notes the Du meridian connecting forward through the haidi.]
Trang sách 48 · scan p081

…outward signs (迹象, jìxiàng). If judged by spirit and rhythm (神韵), at the moment of crossing hands, hardness and softness are used together, hitting exactly the middle measure; only one long practiced in this way can fathom its depths. Both shoulders snap down, both elbows sink; refined as a maiden meeting people, unbridled as a fierce tiger descending the mountain. The hand is itself a balance (权衡, quánhéng): weighing a thing, one knows its light or heavy. In the way of practicing quan (拳), the balance resides within my own mind. According to the opponent's advancing, retreating, slowness, or haste, I meet it with the spirit I have long trained — this is the formless balance. Using the formless balance to weigh the tangible outward signs, whether light or heavy is fitting, and weighing it carefully with the two hands so that it is exactly right — this is what makes a marvelous hand (妙手).

Four poems on the silk-reeling method of Taijiquan (太极拳缠丝法诗四首)

Seven-character ancient verse, No. 1

Movement generates yang, stillness generates yin; one movement and one stillness are each other's root.

Truly recognizing the delight within the ring (环中), one turns and turns, and at will sees natural genuineness.

No. 2

Yin and yang have no beginning and no end; coming and going, bending and stretching embody transforming power.

Truly penetrating the secret within this, one circles at will and moves the primal vastness (鸿蒙).

No. 3

A moment of clarity, a moment of confusion; the linked rings of closing and opening depend on tearing and lifting.

When principle passes through samadhi (三昧) it shines forth; the spiritual realm is a sheet of glass.

Five-character ancient verse

The realm of principle is originally without end; it stems entirely from sincerity that binds like ants.

Three years without looking at the garden, single-minded purpose with spirit concentrated.

One must follow a good teacher, and also visit lofty friends.

Everywhere follow the rules and measures; one thread lights up the spirit.

One layer deepens another; layer upon layer, the meaning is inexhaustible.

One opening links with one closing; opening and closing follow one another in turn.

Sometimes it leads one into wonder; the work reaches a point one cannot stop.

Constant practice with diligence, day by day it rises like steam.

Once there are no obstructions, suddenly one awakens to empty space (太空).

Record of Meridian-Point Songs, with Notes from the Neijing (經穴歌錄內經注)

The eleven points of the Hand Taiyin Lung Meridian (手太陰肺經)

Points: Zhongfu (中府), Yunmen (雲門), Tianfu (天府), Xiabai (俠白), Chize (尺澤), Kongzui (孔最), Jingqu (經渠), Lieque (列缺), Taiyuan (太淵), Yuji (魚際), Shaoshang (少商).

The song says:

The Taiyin Lung emerges at Zhongfu; one cun or so below Yunmen.

Yunmen lies six cun beside the Xuanji; two bones' breadth below the Jugu.

Tianfu rests three cun below the ribs; Xiabai, five cun above the elbow.

Chize, at the elbow crease's middle; Kongzui, taken seven cun above the wrist.

Lieque, an inch and a half on the wrist's side; Jingqu, taken in the hollow at the wrist pulse (寸口).

Taiyuan, at the transverse crease behind the palm; Yuji, where the scattered vessel rises behind the joint.

Shaoshang, on the inner side of the tip of the thumb — if these points are needled, the illness at once heals.

Hand Yangming Large Intestine Meridian (shou yangming da chang jing 手阳明大肠经) — Twenty Points

Song of the points (shangyang 商阳 ... yingxiang 迎香):

- Shangyang 商阳 — at the inner side of the index finger.

- Erjian 二间 — seek it in front of the proximal joint.

- Sanjian 三间 — take it in the hollow behind the joint.

- Hegu 合谷 (written 合骨) — between the bones at the tiger's mouth (web of the thumb).

- Yangxi 阳溪 — on the upper side of the wrist.

- Pianli 偏历 — three cun behind the wrist.

- Wenliu 温溜 — five cun behind the wrist.

- Xialian 下廉 — five cun below (in front of) Quchi.

- Shanglian 上廉 — three cun below Quchi.

- Shousanli 手三里 — meet it two cun below Quchi.

- Quchi 曲池 — where the bend ends at the crease of the elbow.

- Zhouliao 肘髎 — near the outer border of the great bone.

- Wuli 五里 — seek it in the middle, five li of the large intestine channel.

- Binao 臂臑 — measure seven cun above the elbow.

- Three cun above the elbow, going inward.

- Jianliao 肩髎 — at the shoulder end, take with the arm raised.

- Jugu 巨骨 — ascending from the shoulder end.

- Tianding 天鼎 — truly four cun beside the throat.

- Futu 扶突 — three cun beside Tianding.

- Heliao 禾髎 — five fen beside the philtrum (shuigou 水沟).

- Yingxiang 迎香 — one cun above Heliao.

- The points of the Large Intestine meridian are thus perfectly clear.

The Stomach meridian — the Foot Yangming: Chengqi is sought seven fen below the eye.

Quepen, inside the horizontal bone below Qishe; each lies an inch and a half from the midline, clearly.

Verse catalogue of acupuncture points on the Foot Yangming Stomach meridian (足陽明胃經, Zú Yángmíng Wèi Jīng), continuing the rhymed locational mnemonic begun on the previous page.

Transcription (scan order, right block then left block):

乳中正在乳頭心，各一寸六不相侵，以前穴道与君陳，却近幽门寸五新，关门太乙滑肉门，共去中行三寸中，枢下一寸外陵安，枢下四寸水道全，共去中行二寸边，又去中行四寸专，伏菟膝上六寸是，梁邱膝上二寸记，膝下二寸三里至，膝下七寸条口味，膝下九寸丰隆系，比那下廉外边缀，搏阳庭后五寸换，内庭次指五间陷，去爪如韭胃井判。

库房屋翳膺窗迎，次有乳根出乳下，却去中行须四寸，不容巨阙旁三寸，其下承满与梁门，上下一寸无多少，天枢脐下二寸间，枢下二寸大巨穴，枢上六寸归来是，气冲鼠鼷上一寸，髀关膝上有尺二，阴市膝上方三寸，膝髌陷中犊鼻存，膝下六寸上廉穴，膝下八寸下廉看，却是踝上八寸量，解溪去庭六寸半，陷谷庭后二寸间，厉兑大指次指端。

English translation (readable stanza sequence)

Ruzhong (乳中, ST17) lies at the very center of the nipple; each is one cun six apart and does not encroach on the other.

The points described before are now set out for you anew: they lie close to Youmen (幽門, KI21), one cun five away.

Kufang (庫房, ST16), Wuyi (屋翳, ST15), Yingchuang (膺窗, ST14) follow in line; next, Rugen (乳根, ST18) emerges below the breast, four cun from the midline.

Burong (不容, ST19) sits three cun beside Juque (巨闕, CV14); below it come Chengman (承滿, ST20) and Liangmen (梁門, ST21), then Guanmen (關門, ST22), Taiyi (太乙, ST23), and Huaroumen (滑肉門, ST24) — a cun apart, no more and no less — all three cun from the midline.

Tianshu (天樞, ST25) lies level with the navel, two cun from it; one cun below the hub is Wailing (外陵, ST26), two cun below is Daju (大巨, ST27), four cun below is Shuidao (水道, ST28), and six cun above the hub is Guilai (歸來, ST29) — each two cun from the midline, and then four cun from the midline again.

Qichong (氣衝, ST30) lies one cun above the "rat cleft" (the groin, 鼠鼷). Biguan (髀關, ST31) stands twelve cun (a chi two) above the knee; Futu (伏兔, ST32) is six cun above the knee; Yinshi (陰市, ST33) three cun above the knee; Liangqiu (梁丘, ST34) two cun above the knee — remember it well; and within the hollow of the kneecap rests Dubi (犢鼻, ST35).

Two cun below the knee, Zusanli (足三里, ST36) arrives; six cun below the knee is Shanglian (上廉, ST37); seven cun below is Tiaokou (條口, ST38); eight cun below is Xiaolian (下廉, ST39); nine cun below the knee, Fenglong (豐隆, ST40) connects — it is measured eight cun above the ankle, joined to the outer side of the lower belly-line. Fenglong (豐隆, ST40) is eight cun above the ankle. Jiexi (解谿, ST41) is six and a half cun from the "court" (庭, i.e. Neiting); Xian'gu (陷谷, ST43) two cun behind the court; Neiting (內庭, ST44) in the hollow between the second and third toes; Lidui (厲兌, ST45) at the tip of the second toe, a shallow well a fen from the corner of the nail — the Well point (井) of the Stomach channel, thus determined.

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Foot Shaoyin Kidney Meridian: 27 acupoints (足少陰腎經二十七穴)

[Diagram: a line drawing of a seated male figure facing left with a traditional topknot; 27 small circles mark the points of the Kidney meridian, from the sole of the foot (Yongquan 涌泉) up the inner leg, through the lower abdomen and chest; leader lines connect the name labels to the points on the body.]

Diagram labels:

Youguan 右關, Shimen 石門, Tonggu 通谷, Youdu 幽都, Bulang 步廊, Shenfeng 神封, Lingxu 靈墟, Shencang 神藏, Yuzhong 彧中, Fuzhong 府中, Shicang 食倉, Shangqu 商曲, Huangshu 盲俞, Zhongzhu 中注, Siman 四滿, Qixue 氣穴, Dahe 大赫, Henggu 横骨, Yingu 陰谷, Zhubin 築賓, Jiaoxin 交信, Fuliu 復溜, Zhaohai 照海, Shuiquan 水泉, Taixi 太谿, Dazhong 大鐘, Rangu 然谷

Mnemonic verse (歌云)

然骨踝下一寸前，(Rangu is one cun forward below the ankle,)

大钟跟后踵中边，(Dazhong is behind the heel at the edge of the mid-heel,)

照海踝下四分安，(Zhaohai sits four fen below the ankle,)

交信踝上二寸联，(Jiaoxin joins two cun above the ankle,)

太阳之后少阴前，(Behind the Taiyang channel, before the Shaoyin,)

阴谷膝下屈膝间，(Yingu is below the knee, in the bend of the knee,)

四满中注亦相连，(Siman and Zhongzhu are also joined in sequence,)

上下相去一寸便，(Each is one cun from the next,)

肓俞脐旁半寸边，(Huangshu is half a cun beside the navel,)

阴都通谷幽门开，(Yindu, Tonggu and Youmen open in line,)

足掌心中是涌泉，(In the center of the sole of the foot is Yongquan,)

太谿踝后跟骨上，(Taixi is behind the ankle above the heel bone,)

水泉谿上一寸觅，(Shuiquan is sought one cun above Taixi,)

复溜踝上前二寸，(Fuliu is two cun in front of and above the ankle,)

二穴止止隔筋前后，(The two points are separated only by a tendon, front and back,)

筑宾内踝上腨分，(Zhubin is on the calf above the inner ankle,)

横骨大赫并气穴，(Henggu, Dahe and Qixue together,)

各开中行止半寸，(Each lies half a cun from the midline,)

上隔肓俞亦一寸，(Above, one cun apart from Huangshu likewise,)

肓俞商曲石关来，(From Huangshu come Shangqu and Shiguan,)

*Chen shi Taiji Quan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Juan Shou (卷首, preliminary scroll)

The Sixty-Three Points of the Foot Taiyang Bladder Meridian (足太阳膀胱经六十三穴)

Verse (歌, gē):

"The Foot Taiyang is the Bladder meridian (足太阳兮膀胱经);

It begins at the inner canthus of the eye at Jingming (睛明).

At the hollow of the brow, Zanzhu (攒竹) is taken;

Quchai (曲差) is five fen above the hairline.

Wuchu (五处) is one cun above the hair;

Chengguang (承光) is two and a half cun above the hair."

"Six points, each one cun apart, measured one above the other;

Shencang (神藏), Yuzhong (彧中), and Shufu (俞府) stand in honour.

Upper and lower, six cun for the same six points;

Taken with the right method, success follows of itself."

"Each opens five fen from the centre line;

Bulang (步廊), Shenfeng (神封), Lingxu (灵墟) are kept there.

Each opens two cun from the centre line by count;

Shufu (俞府) lies two cun beside Xuanji (璇玑)."

Diagram

[Diagram: Line drawing of human figures (front and back views) showing the 63 points of the Foot Taiyang Bladder meridian from the inner eye canthus, over the head, down both sides of the spine, and along the back of the leg to the little toe.]

Point labels: head/face — Jingming, Zanzhu, Quchai, Wuchu, Chengguang, Tongtian, Luoque, Yuzhen, Tianzhu, Dazhu; back (lateral line) — Fufen, Pohu, Gaohuang, Shentang, Yixi, Geguan, Hunmen, Yanggang, Yishe, Weicang, Huangmen, Zhishi, Baohuang, Zhibian; back (medial line) — Fengmen, Feishu, Jueyinshu, Xinshu, Dushu, Geshu, Ganshu, Danshu, Pishu, Weishu, Sanjiaoshu, Shenshu, Qihaishu, Dachangshu, Guanyuanshu, Xiaochangshu, Pangguangshu, Zhonglüshu, Baihuanshu, Shangliao, Ciliao, Zhongliao, Xialiao, Huiyang; leg — Chengfu, Yinmen, Fuxi, Weiyang, Weizhong, Heyang, Chengjin, Chengshan, Feiyang, Fuyang, Kunlun, Pucan, Shenmai, Jinmen, Jinggu, Shugu, Tonggu, Zhiyin.

Trang sách 58 · scan p091

*Chen shi Taiji Quan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Verse on the points of the Foot Taiyang Bladder meridian (continued)

"Keep them evenly spaced, five fen apart, and observe;

Two cun within the hair, the occipital bone appears;

In the hollow outside the great tendon they present.

First of all, the two Fengmen (风门);

Xinshu (心俞) is discussed below the fifth vertebra;

Eleventh is Pishu (脾俞), twelfth Weishu (胃俞);

Infer below the sixteenth — Dachangshu (大肠俞);

Zhonglüshu (中膂俞) at the twentieth vertebra;

All the points above may thus be arrayed.

One, two, three, four — the lumbar gaps are good,

And with them the second line of back points is complete.

Below the second vertebra is Fufen (附分);

Below the fifth vertebra, Shentang (神堂) stands honoured;

Ninth is Hunmen (魂门), tenth Yanggang (阳纲);

Twelfth is Weicang (胃仓), the point already distinguished;

Fourteenth is Zhishi (志室), needing no further words —

Thus the third line of back points is even.

Chengfu (承扶) presides in the hollow;

Weiyang (委阳) is counted one cun six below Chengfu;

In the popliteal hollow, inside, where the two tendons meet;

Three cun below that, look for Heyang (合阳);

The point lies in the middle of the calf muscle.

Seven cun above the outer malleolus it rises high;

Kunlun (昆仑) is in the central hollow behind the heel;

Shenmai (申脉) spreads five fen below the malleolus;

Jinggu (京骨) is measured at the bone's edge on the outside;

Tonggu (通谷) is strong in the hollow before the joint —

And so the points of the Taiyang are set out in full detail."

Tongtian (通天), Luoque (络却), Yuzhen (玉枕) as points;

Yuzhen flanks the brain one cun three;

Tianzhu (天柱) is at the hairline behind the nape.

From here, flanking the spine, open one cun five:

Feishu (肺俞) at the third vertebra, Jueyin at the fourth;

Geshu (膈俞) at seven, Ganshu (肝俞) at nine, Danshu (胆俞) at ten;

Sanjiaoshu (三焦俞) at thirteen, Shenshu (肾俞) at fourteen;

Xiaochangshu (小肠俞) at eighteen, Pangguangshu (膀胱俞) at nineteen;

Baihuanshu (白环俞) at twenty-one, below a vertebra;

There are further the Shang, Ci, Zhong and Xia liaos (上髎、次髎、中髎、下髎);

Huiyang (会阳) is beside the coccyx, in front of the tailbone.

Again, from the spine open three cun:

Pohu (魄户) at the third vertebra, Gaohuang (膏肓) at the fourth;

Yixi (噫嘻) at the sixth, Geguan (膈关) at the seventh;

Yishe (意舍) as a point is kept at eleven;

Huangmen (肓门) stands squarely at thirteen;

Baohuang (胞肓) at nineteen and Zhibian (秩边) at twenty.

Again, take them from below the buttock at the gluteal fold;

Fuxi (浮郄) is six fen below Chengfu;

Yinmen (殷门) is six cun long below Chengfu;

Weizhong (委中) is inside the crease of the knee's popliteal hollow;

Chengjin (承筋) is seven cun up from the heel;

Chengshan (承山) is below the calf, between the muscle bellies;

Fuyang (跗阳) is three cun above the outer malleolus;

Pucan (仆参) too lies below the malleolus bone;

Jinmen (金门) is one cun below Shenmai;

Shugu (束骨) is in the hollow behind the joint of the great toe-side;

And Zhiyin (至阴) is at the outer side of the little toe.

Trang sách 59 · scan p092

*Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chén shì tàijíquán túshuō), Preliminary Volume (卷首, juànshǒu)*

[Diagram: Anatomical chart of the back showing the acupuncture points of the Foot Taiyang Bladder Meridian (足太陽膀胱經, zú tàiyáng pángguāng jīng), arranged in two vertical columns flanking the spine.]

Right column (points of the first bladder line, from top to bottom): Dazhu (大杼), Fengmen (風門, Wind Gate), Feishu (肺俞, Lung Shu), Jueyinshu (厥陰俞, Pericardium Shu), Xinshu (心俞, Heart Shu), Geshu (膈俞, Diaphragm Shu), Ganshu (肝俞, Liver Shu), Danshu (膽俞, Gallbladder Shu), Pishu (脾俞, Spleen Shu), Weishu (胃俞, Stomach Shu), Sanjiaoshu (三焦俞, Triple Burner Shu), Shenshu (腎俞, Kidney Shu), Dachangshu (大腸俞, Large Intestine Shu), Xiaochangshu (小腸俞, Small Intestine Shu), Pangguangshu (膀胱俞, Bladder Shu), Zhonglüshu (中膂俞), Baihuanshu (白環俞), Shangliao (上髎), Ciliao (次髎), Zhongliao (中髎), Xialiao (下髎), Huiyang (會陽).

Center column: the five zang organs — lung (肺), heart (心), liver (肝), spleen (脾), kidney (腎).

Left column (points of the second bladder line): Fufen (附分), Pohu (魄戶, Po Door), Gaohuang (膏肓), Shentang (神堂, Spirit Hall), Yixi (噫嘻), Geguan (膈關), Hunmen (魂門, Hun Gate), Yanggang (陽綱), Yishe (意舍), Weicang (胃倉), Zhishi (志室, Will Chamber), Baohuang (胞肓), Zhibian (秩邊), Fucheng (扶承), Fuxi (浮郄), Weiyang (委陽), Yinmen (殷門).

Commentary on the correspondence with the five zang organs:

For example: Pohu corresponds to Feishu, Shentang corresponds to Xinshu, Hunmen corresponds to Ganshu, Yishe corresponds to Pishu, and Zhishi corresponds to Shenshu — these are the Five Spirits (五神, wǔshén). The Five Spirits are stored within the five zang organs, and therefore they correspond to the five zang. Considering what each of the Five Spirits stores reveals the reality: the lung stores the po (魄, po, the corporeal soul), the heart stores the shen (神, shén, spirit), the liver stores the hun (魂, hún, ethereal soul), the spleen stores the yi (意, yì, intention), and the kidney stores the zhi (志, zhì, will) — these are the five zang (五藏, wǔzàng).

Note at bottom of the chart: Baihuanshu is the same point as Yaoshu (腰俞).

*Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation (陳氏太極拳圖說)*

The Nine Points of the Hand Jueyin Pericardium Meridian (手厥陰包絡經, shǒu juéyīn bāoluò jīng)

[Diagram: Classical woodblock-style line drawing of a man in profile, right arm extended, tracing the Hand Jueyin Pericardium Meridian from the chest down the inner arm to the tip of the middle finger, with nine labeled points.]

Points labeled on the figure: Xinbao / chest (心包), Tianquan (天泉, Heavenly Spring), Quze (曲澤, Marsh at the Bend), Ximen (郄門, Xi Gate), Jianshi (間使, Envoy), Neiguan (內關, Inner Pass), Daling (大陵, Great Mound), Laogong (勞宮, Palace of Toil), Zhongchong (中沖, Middle Rush). The meridian is identified as Hand Jueyin Pericardium (手厥陰包絡).

Verse (歌云, gē yún):

One cun and three [fen] below the armpit, behind the nipple;

Quze lies in the central hollow when the elbow is bent;

Jianshi is measured five cun behind the wrist;

Daling lies behind the palm between the two tendons;

At the tip of the middle finger, Zhongchong is best.

The pericardium channel begins between heaven and earth [chest and abdomen];

Tianquan is two cun below the bent armpit;

Ximen is just five cun from the wrist;

Neiguan is only two cun from the wrist;

Laogong is found by bending the middle and ring fingers [into the palm].

*Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation (陳氏太極拳圖說), Preliminary Volume (卷首)*

The Twenty-Four Points of the Hand Shaoyang Triple Burner Meridian (手少陽三焦經, shǒu shàoyáng sānjiāo jīng)

[Diagram: Front-facing standing figure in Han robes; the meridian line runs from the tip of the ring finger up the outer arm, across the shoulder, up the neck and side of the head to the temple. Twenty-four open circles mark the points, each connected by a leader line to its name.]

Points labeled on the figure (from the hand upward): Chongchong/Guanchong (關沖, Gate Rush), Yemen (液門, Fluid Gate), Zhongzhu (中渚, Middle Islet), Sanyangluo (三陽絡, Triple Yang Connection), Sidu (四瀆, Four Rivers), Waiguan (外關, Outer Pass), Yangchi (陽池, Yang Pool), Zhigou (支溝, Branch Ditch), Huizong (會宗, Meeting of Ancestors), Qinglengyuan (清冷淵, Cold Clear Deep), Tianjing (天井, Heavenly Well), Naohui (臑會, Upper Arm Meeting), Jianliao (肩髎, Shoulder Crevice), Xiaoluo (消爍, Dispersing Larder), Yifeng (翳風, Wind Screen), Chimai (瘈脈, Spasmodic Vessel), Luxi (顱息, Skull Rest), Jiaosun (角孫, Vertex Grandson), Heliao (和髎, Harmony Crevice), Sizhukong (絲竹空, Silk Bamboo Hollow), Ermen (耳門, Ear Gate), Tianrong (天容, Heavenly Countenance), Tianyou (天牖, Heavenly Window), Tianliao (天髎, Heavenly Crevice). The meridian is identified as Hand Shaoyang Triple Burner (手少陽三焦).

Verse (歌云):

Guanchong lies at the outer end of the ring finger;

Yemen lies in the hollow between the little and ring fingers;

Zhongzhu is one cun above Yemen;

Yangchi lies in the hollow above the wrist;

Waiguan is two cun behind the wrist;

Three cun behind the wrist opens Zhigou;

Three cun within behind the wrist lies Huizong —

Outside the hollow there is a point; seek it with care;

Four cun behind the wrist is Sanyangluo;

Sidu is seen five cun before the elbow;

Tianjing lies behind the great bone outside the elbow;

Feel one cun within the bone's interstice;

Two cun behind the elbow is Qinglengyuan;

Xiaoluo, opposite Yemen, on the outer arm;

Naohui lies three cun before the shoulder;

Jianliao lies in the central hollow above the upper arm.

*Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chén shì tàijíquán túshuō), Preliminary Volume (卷首, juànshǒu)

Verse (continuation — point locations of the Foot Shaoyang Gallbladder meridian, 足少阳胆经, Zú Shàoyáng dǎn jīng):

> 耳前起骨开口空 — Before the ear, on the rising bone, at the hollow that opens when the mouth opens;

> 脑空上廉曲角下 — Above Naokong's upper edge, below the bent angle;

> 脑空下廉曲角上 — Below Naokong's lower edge, above the bent angle;

> 率骨耳上半安安 — Shuaigu rests midway up the ear;

> 浮白入发一寸间 — Fubai one cun within the hairline;

> 完骨之上有空连 — Above Wangu a hollow connects;

> 量得四分须用记 — Measured at four fen, this must be remembered;

> 入发一寸耳上系 — One cun within the hairline, above the ear;

> 发上五分临泣用 — Five fen above the hairline serves for Linqi;

> 发上半寸目窗贡 — Half a cun above, Muchuang presents itself;

> 承灵发上二寸摊 — Chengling spreads two cun above the hairline;

> 风池耳后发陷中 — Fengchi in the hairline hollow behind the ear;

> 大骨之前一寸半 — One and a half cun before the great bone;

> 辄筋期下五分判 — Zhejin five fen below Qimen, so judged;

> 相去巨阙四寸半 — Four and a half cun from Juque;

> 京门监骨下腰绊 — Jingmen below the watchbone, at the waist's tether;

> 五枢章下寸八贯 — Wushu one cun eight below Zhangmen;

> 居髎章下八寸三 — Juliao eight cun three below Zhangmen;

> 下脘之旁九寸含 — Nine cun beside Xiawan;

> 屈上伸下取穴同 — Bend up, stretch down — the point is found the same either way;

> 膝上五寸渎论 — Five cun above the knee, as discussed;

> 阳陵膝下一寸从 — Yanglingquan one cun below the knee;

> 踝上六寸外邱用 — Six cun above the ankle serves Waiqiu;

> 踝上四寸阳辅分 — Four cun above the ankle marks Yangfu;

> 邱墟踝前之陷中 — Qiuxu in the hollow before the ankle;

> 却是胆经原穴功 — It is in fact the Yuan (source) point of the Gallbladder meridian;

> 五会窍阴二穴同 — (Xia)xi and Qiaoyin, the two points alike.

> 客主人名上关同 — Keruzhuren is another name for Shangguan;

> 颔厌悬颅之二穴 — Hanyan and Xuanlu, the two points;

> 悬厘之穴异于兹 — The point Xuanli differs from these;

> 曲鬓耳上发际隅 — Qubin at the corner of the hairline above the ear;

> 天冲耳后入发二 — Tianchong two cun into the hair behind the ear;

> 窍阴即是枕骨穴 — Qiaoyin is the point on the occipital bone;

> 完骨耳后入发际 — Wangu behind the ear within the hairline;

> 本神神庭旁三寸 — Benshen three cun beside Shenting;

> 阳白眉上方一寸 — Yangbai one cun above the brow;

> 发上一寸当阳穴 — One cun above the hairline, the Dangyang point.

> 正营发上二寸半 — Zhengying two and a half cun above the hairline;

> 脑空发上五寸半 — Naokong five and a half cun above the hairline;

> 肩井肩上陷中求 — Jianjing sought in the depression atop the shoulder;

> 渊液腋下方三寸 — Yuanye three cun below the armpit;

> 期门却是肝之穴 — Qimen is in fact a point of the Liver (meridian);

> 日月期门下五分 — Riyue five fen below Qimen;

> 带脉章门下寸八 — Daimai one cun eight below Zhangmen;

> 维道章下五寸三 — Weidao five cun three below Zhangmen;

> 章门亦是肝经穴 — Zhangmen too is a point of the Liver meridian;

> 环跳髀枢宛宛中 — Huantiao deep in the hollow of the hip joint;

> 风市垂手中指尽 — Fengshi where the middle fingertip falls when the arm hangs;

> 阳关阳陵上三寸 — Yangguan three cun above Yanglingquan;

> 阳交外踝上七寸 — Yangjiao seven cun above the outer ankle;

> 踝上五寸光明穴 — Guangming five cun above the ankle;

> 踝上三寸悬钟在 — Xuanzhong lies three cun above the ankle;

> 此去侠溪四寸五 — From here to Xiaxi, four cun five;

> 临泣侠溪四寸半 — Linqi to Xiaxi, four and a half cun.

Note (按, àn): The twenty points on the head are, in order, divided altogether into three sections (三折, sān zhé).

First Section (第一折, Dì yī zhé)

> 一瞳子髎二听会 — First Tongziliao, second Tinghui;

> 五悬颅兮六悬厘 — Fifth Xuanlu, sixth Xuanli;

> 八率谷兮九天冲 — Eighth Shuaigu, ninth Tianchong;

> 十一窍阴亦相继 — Eleventh Qiaoyin follows in turn;

>

> 三主人兮颔厌四 — Third Keruzhuren, fourth Hanyan;

> 第七数分曲鬓随 — Seventh in count, Qubin follows;

> 十浮白兮之穴从 — Tenth the point Fubai;

> 十二完骨一折终 — Twelfth Wangu ends the first section.

Chen's Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation

Second fold:

Trang sách 68 · scan p101

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume Preface

On the Wei Qi (卫气论, Defensive Qi)

The "Wei Qi Xing" chapter of the *Lingshu* (灵枢) says: The movement of defensive qi (wei qi, 卫气) completes fifty circuits around the body in one day and night. By day it circulates twenty-five times through the yang, by night twenty-five times through the yin. At dawn, when the yin is exhausted, the yang qi issues from the eyes. When the eyes open, the qi ascends to the head (along Jingming, 睛明), then descends through the Foot Taiyang Bladder meridian, the Hand Taiyang Small Intestine meridian, the Foot Shaoyang Gallbladder meridian, the Hand Shaoyang Triple Burner meridian, the Foot Yangming...

Twelve Meridian Drawings · Front View of the Eight Confluences (八会正面图)

Qi meets at the Triple Burner (气会三焦);

The vessels meet at Taiyuan (脉会太渊);

The bowels meet at Zhongwan (腑会中脘);

The organs meet at Zhangmen (脏会章门).

Back View of the Eight Confluences (八会背面图)

Bone meets at Dazhu (骨会大杼);

Blood meets at Geshu (血会膈俞);

Marrow meets at Juegu (髓会绝骨);

Sinew meets at Yanglingquan (筋会阳陵泉).

Points of the Chong Meridian (冲脉)

Youmen, Tonggu, Yinxi, Shiguan, Shangqu, Yushu (Youmen flanks Jueque half a cun to the side);

Zhongzhu, Suifu, Baomen, Yinguan, Xiaji (Zhongzhu lies below Yushu).

The Dai (belt) meridian binds the waist and has no points.

Diagram of the Seven Qi Gates (七冲门图)

Humen (door of the household — lips), Feimen (flying door — teeth), Ximen (door of inhalation — palate), Benmen (cardia), Youmen (pylorus), Lanmen (ileocecal gate), Pomen (anus).

Stomach, small intestine, large intestine.

[Diagram: Three drawings — a front-view figure marking the eight confluences (八会), a back-view figure showing the bone, blood, marrow and sinew confluences, and a diagram of the digestive tract with the seven qi gates from lips to anus.]
Trang sách 69 · scan p102

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說， Chen Family Taiji Quan Illustrated Explanation) — Juan Shou (卷首， front matter).

…stomach channel and the hand Yangming Large Intestine channel — such is how, within one day, [the qi] governs the exterior. At night it travels through the foot Shaoyin Kidney channel, pouring into the hand Shaoyin Heart channel, the hand Taiyin Lung channel, the foot Jueyin Liver channel, and the foot Taiyin Spleen channel, likewise completing twenty-five circuits as the yang did, and rejoining at the eyes. What is meant by "at daybreak the human qi arises" is precisely this ascending to the head and rejoining. In practicing quan (拳， boxing), each posture (勢 shi): when the yang qi moves once, it circuits the whole body; when it comes to stillness, one stillness is one circuit of the body. The moment a single thought of the mind moves, the yang qi immediately makes one circuit of the body; when a thought is still, the yin qi makes one circuit of the body. Throughout the twelve double-hours (十二時)， day after day without interruption, wherever it resides at any moment — this must be known. Needling the point where the "human spirit" (人神) resides causes death; striking it, if it does not kill, will injure.

Song:

At zi the ankle, at chou the waist, at yin the eyes;

At wu the chest, at wei the abdomen, at shen the heart;

At mao the face, at chen…, at si held in the hand;

At you the back, at xu…, at hai the thigh continues.

Another song:

At zi the ankle, at chou the crown, at yin beside the ear;

At wu the ribs, at wei the abdomen, at shen the heart's place;

At mao the face, at chen the neck, at si between the breasts;

At you the knee, at xu the waist, at hai the end of the thigh.

[Diagram: Zang-fu paired with the earthly branches (臟腑配地支圖) — a circular chart; around its rim the twelve earthly branches, each linked to a meridian, organ, and five-phase quality: wu–hand Shaoyin Heart; wei–hand Taiyin Lung; shen–foot Taiyin Spleen; you–hand Taiyang Small Intestine; xu–foot Taiyang Bladder; hai–foot Shaoyin Kidney; zi–foot Shaoyin Kidney; chou–foot Jueyin Liver; yin–hand Jueyin Pericardium; mao–hand Shaoyang Triple Burner (minister fire); chen–foot Yangming Stomach; si–hand Yangming Large Intestine (dry metal). Qualities noted on the wheel: dry metal (金燥)， wood/wind (木風)， water/cold (水寒)， minister fire (相火)， damp earth (土濕).]

Song of the zang-fu paired with the earthly branches:

Zi kidney, wu heart — the Shaoyin sovereign;

Yin gallbladder, shen burner — the Shaoyin pivot;

Chen small intestine, xu bladder — the Taiyang root;

Chou spleen, wei lung — the Taiyin basis;

Mao large intestine, you stomach — the Yangming division;

Si pericardium, hai liver — ending in the Jueyin.

The five movements and six qi govern transformation;

The martial arts that gain this reach the divine of themselves.

The second qi is Shaoyang fire as sovereign;

The fourth qi is minister fire, the fifth qi metal;

The sixth qi, in the spring (zai quan), ends by storing the true.

Jueyin wind-wood governs the first, spring;

The third qi governs heaven as Taiyin earth;

Within the overcoming, life emerges as dry-metal body.

Kidney with bladder, heart with pericardium;
