The twelve meridians together with the Chong, Ren and Du meridians make 351 points in all. This does not exactly match the 360 degrees of the circle; the matter awaits further examination.

Discussion of the Ren and Du Meridians (任脉、督脉论, rènmài dūmài lùn)

The Ren Mai (任脉, rènmài) begins at Huiyin (会阴), ascends along the interior of the abdomen, and ends at Tiantu (天突) and Lianquan (廉泉). The Du Mai (督脈, dūmài) likewise arises at Huiyin, passes through Changqiang (长强), and travels against the current up along the spine to Baihui (百会), then descends to end at Renzhong (人中).

That a human body possesses the Ren and Du is like Heaven and Earth possessing the Zi and Wu (子午, the north–south axis of midnight and noon). The Ren and Du of the human body…

(continues on the next page)

Trang sách 72 · scan p105

Speaking of it in terms of belly and back, and of Heaven and Earth; the Ren (任脉, Ren vessel, Conception vessel) and Du (督脉, Du vessel, Governing vessel) in terms of south and north — both occupy the middle position, and may be separated and may be joined. Separate them and you see that Yin and Yang do not part; join them and you see the undivided wholeness without seam. One becomes two, two becomes one. For if a person understands the Ren and Du in order to circulate the qi (气) and protect the body, it is like understanding how to love the people in order to secure the state. If the people perish the state falls; if the Ren vessel weakens the body declines. Hence the ancients practiced the arts of daoyin (导引, guided stretching and breathing) taking it as the very root of cultivating immortality.

Practicing the quan (拳, the boxing form) regulates and nourishes the blood and qi. Let the breathing follow its natural course, sweep away idle thoughts, expel the turbid qi. First establish the foundation: gather in the gaze and turn the hearing inward, hold the light in silence, regulate the breath continuously and unbroken, hold firm the inner guard, fix attention on the mysterious pass (玄关). With long practice, in an instant water and fire issue forth, flowers bloom in the snow, both kidneys grow hot as boiling soup, the bladder seems scorched by fire, and the true qi (真气) becomes naturally full. The Ren and Du are like a wheel turning; the four limbs are like mountain rock. When idle thoughts vanish, Heaven's mechanism moves of itself. Each time you perform a posture, move it gently, stop it silently, and let the intention alone do the moving — then water and fire will naturally blend. Over time their rising and falling becomes like the well-sweep drawing water, like dew condensing on rice flowers: suddenly one grain as large as a millet seed drops into the Yellow Court (黄庭). This is the true secret of the lead-gatherers casting in the mercury (采铅投汞, the alchemical act).

When the quan practice reaches this point, the attention must not scatter and the work must not stop. One scattering, one stopping, and the elixir (丹) is not formed. As the Purple Yang True Man (紫阳真人, Zhang Boduan) of old said: "True mercury is born in Li (离, the Fire trigram), yet its use lies in Kan (坎, the Water trigram); the fair maid passes the southern garden, holding in her hand the jade olive." This is precisely what is meant.

Practice it day by day without error or interruption. Refine it for one quarter-hour and you have one quarter-hour's celestial cycle (周天); one hour, one hour's cycle; one day, one day's cycle; one year, one year's cycle; a whole lifetime, a lifetime's cycle. After ten years of practice the whole body becomes one undivided whole, utterly replete with empty spirituality: you no longer know the body as "me" or "me" as the body; nor do you know that the spirit is born of qi or that qi naturally has spirit. Full, it conforms to the compass; bending, to the square; attained without thought, achieved without strain. Water arises though unsought; fire issues though unsought. The empty room grows bright; one threads a needle in the dark — it happens without knowing why, and one no longer knows the Ren as the Du or the Du as the Ren, or why the central qi (中气) is the central qi. Applied at every moment it is always apt, in rhythm naturally. This speaks of the rising and falling, the following and reversing of Ren and Du, assisting the central qi to complete the work. The qi, in motion, is generated from the kidneys; at rest it returns to lodge in the kidneys. In each breath and exhale, the exit and entry of true qi take place there.

The point Zhongji (中极穴), also called Qiyuan (气原), is one cun below Guanyuan (关元), four cun below the navel; it is the alarm point of the bladder and the meeting of the three foot-yin channels with the Ren vessel. Qihai (气海, "Sea of Qi"), also called Boyang (脖胦) or Xiahuang (下肓), lies in the small hollow one cun below the navel — the sea where a man's qi is generated. When people say the qi returns to the dantian (丹田), it is not without basis. In short, however many thousands of words one says, nothing surpasses clearing the heart and scantening desire, cultivating the original root to nourish the original qi (元气). If the body is fundamentally strong, then in practicing the quan you daily outdo others by a notch.

Important point list (重要穴目)

Houding (后顶, GV19): one cun behind Baihui (百会).

Fengfu (风府, GV16): one cun above the rear hairline at the crown.

[Diagram: none on this page]

Summary (3 lines):

- The Ren and Du vessels sit at the body's center like the axis between Heaven and Earth; separable and joinable, they show Yin and Yang never part.

- Quan practice nourishes blood and qi: natural breathing, no idle thoughts; with time water and fire blend, true qi fills, and the celestial cycle completes itself over instants, days, years, a lifetime.

- Nothing beats a clear mind and few desires to nurture the origin; the page closes listing the points Houding and Fengfu.

Trang sách 73 · scan p106

A list of acupuncture points (穴位, xuéwèi) of the human body with their locations and the consequences of striking them. No diagram on this page.

Points belonging to the Du Mai (督脈, Dū mài)

- Touwei (头维): at the corner of the forehead, where the hairline begins, 1 cun 5 fen beside Benshen (本神).

- Tinggong (听宫): in the middle of the ear, a bead the size of a red bean; striking it makes a person deaf.

- Naokong (脑空): one cun behind Naohu (脑户).

- Shuigou (水沟): in the center of the groove below the nasal column.

- Xinshu (心俞): two cun to either side below the fifth vertebra.

- Ganshu (肝俞): two cun to either side below the ninth vertebra.

- Danshu (胆俞): two cun to either side below the tenth vertebra.

- Pishu (脾俞): two cun to either side below the eleventh vertebra.

- Weishu (胃俞): two cun to either side below the twelfth vertebra.

- Sanjiao (三焦): two cun to either side below the thirteenth vertebra.

- Shenshu (肾俞): two cun to either side below the fourteenth vertebra.

- Pangguang (膀胱): two cun to either side below the nineteenth vertebra.

- Yaoshu (腰俞): in the hollow below the twenty-first vertebra; from Dazhui (大椎) to here measures three chi.

- Changqiang (长强): three fen below the sacrum.

The above belong to the Du Mai (督脉).

Points belonging to the Ren Mai (任脈, Rèn mài)

- Rugen (乳根): 1 cun 6 fen below the nipple.

- Qimen (期门): 1 cun 5 fen beside the nipple.

- Zhangmen (章门): two cun above the navel, six cun to either side; the measure is taken on the chest, where the span between the two nipples is divided into eight cun, of which these are the inner six.

- Danzhong (膻中): taken at the midpoint between the two nipples.

- Qihai (气海): 1 cun 5 fen below the navel.

- Shimen (石门): two cun below the navel.

- Guanyuan (关元): three cun below the navel.

- Zhongji (中极): one cun below Guanyuan.

- Huiyin (会阴): between the two private parts.

The above belong to the Ren Mai (任脉).

Vital points (要害穴, yàohài xué)

- Taiyang (太阳): beside the Sun and Moon corners of the temple; if struck so the skull breaks and the brain issues, death follows.

- Fenshui (分水): below the Kunmen (困门), where food and drink divide their paths. If struck heavily, food can no longer pass, and after a long time death follows.

- Ganmen (肝门): these are two points.

- Ermen (耳门): this is the ear itself. Struck lightly it confuses; struck heavily it kills.

- Doumen (斗门): on the breast-plate. If struck, inhaling causes pain beyond words, but it is not fatal.

- Feimen (肺门): struck lightly one lives; heavily, one dies.

- Yuguan (玉关): at the back of the head; if it is broken open, death follows within three days.

(continues on the next page)

Trang sách 74 · scan p107

End of list: vital points (xuedao) and consequences of striking them

Feidi (肺底): located at the center of the back, opposite the front of the chest. If struck, the victim laughs, coughs and vomits blood, and dies within two years.

Shenjing (肾茎): if struck on either side, the victim laughs and dies.

Xianmen (闲门): if the throat/wrist is broken open, death is instantaneous.

Mingmen (命门): in the middle of the spine, between the two kidneys.

Front and back heart points, correct positions (前后心穴正位穴): within the breastbone. Injury there causes death.

Shanghai (上海): below the elbow where hair grows. A heavy strike causes death.

Xiahai (下海): the large flesh of the shin. If struck, over time it yellows and the victim dies.

Front qi eye (前气眼): below the Doumen. Striking it is not fatal but brings grave misfortune.

Back qi eye (后气眼): below the Feishu point, aligned with the front qi eye.

One who strikes must know the vital points; not knowing them, one fears injuring people. For example, if Danzhong (膻中) or Shangwan (上腕) — any of these — is struck, the heart-qi rises and the heart-blood gathers, and one can at any moment cause a person to faint, even to the point of death. Therefore the acupuncture charts of the back and the charts of the Ren (任脉) and Du (督脉) meridians have been drawn earlier, so that students may observe the critical points and memorize them thoroughly.

The verse says: The body is like a bow and the jin (劲, power) like the bowstring; the vital point is the target and the hand is like an arrow. Striking on time (按时癸) must be judged precisely; above all never miss the vital point.

Taijiquan Classic in Rhyme (太极拳经谱)

Taiji (太极) and the two polarities (两仪) — the yin and yang of heaven and earth. Closing and opening, movement and stillness — the soft and the hard. Bending and extending, coming and going; advancing and retreating, survival and perishing. One opening, one closing — there is change and there is constancy. Empty and full both attended to — now appearing, now hidden. Strength and compliance in equal measure — drawing in the opponent with precision. Sometimes gathering, sometimes releasing; now slack, now taut. Intricate and interwoven changes — to suppress, first lift up. There must first be action — neither forcing (勿助) nor forgetting (勿忘).

When sincere accumulation lasts long, the substance shines ever brighter. Fullness and emptiness have their images; exiting and entering without fixed method. The spirit knows what is coming; wisdom stores what has passed. Host and guest clearly distinguished — the Middle Way majestic.

The constant (经) and the expedient (权) used together — mend the short, cut off the long. Changes like a divine dragon — who can fathom the vast ocean? Along the path, silky continuity (缠绵, cf. 缠丝 chans); moving in stillness without panic. Skin, flesh, bones and joints — everywhere opened and expanded. Neither before nor behind — meeting and sending in due measure. Front, back, left, right; above, below, the four sides — turning and connecting with agility, slow and fast supporting each other. Raise high, take low — fulfilled as one wishes.

Not stuck in traces, not straying into emptiness. Utmost sincerity (至诚 — that is, the li and qi of Taiji, per the editorial note) in motion — seizing and releasing at one's leisure. The mechanism of heaven is lively; the vast qi (浩气) flows freely. Feigning defeat, faking loss — weighing how to win. Compliant when they come, contrary when they go — making the opponent unable to predict. Adapt to the times — within lies the marvelous secret. Strike above by moving below — never be one-sided. Make noise in the east, strike in the west — awing left and right.

Cold departs, heat arrives — who discerns its beginning? A thousand ages like one day — the ultimate principle cycles on. Upper and lower follow each other — this must not be empty talk. Advance step by step in order (循序渐进) — study carefully. If one can endure hardship, one finally reaches the state of natural wholeness (浑然). Utmost speed and swiftness — coiling, entwining and revolving. Leaving the form, attaining the likeness — how is it not the full moon (perfection)?

[No diagram on this page.]
Trang sách 75 · scan p108

Refined to the utmost, even the tiniest move is a circle (圈).

When the sun reaches mid-heaven it begins to decline; when the moon is full it wanes. If the opponent feints and lures (诈诱), do not press closely after him; if you cross the boundary, it will be hard to turn back. Moreover, once the advantage (势) is lost, what good is regret? I hold my own territory (我守我疆), neither humble nor overbearing. The nine-bend goat path (九折羊肠) must not be yielded one inch. If I yield to another, he stands and I fall. When vying for the advantage urgently, take the upper position, never the lower. Occupy one extra measure and I hold the superior position of terrain (形胜). One man holding the pass, ten thousand men lose their courage. Sticking, connecting, adhering, following (沾连粘随), gather spirit and concentrate essence. Move my emptiness and alertness (虚灵), yet all the more maintain integrity and weight. Delicate and fitting — the center holds the authority and the force comes at the rear (中权后劲). Empty-encircling and feigned luring (虚笼诈诱), only for the sake of one turn. Once the incoming momentum (来脉) is gained advantageously, what difficulty is there in turning the juncture (转关)? Within the real there is the empty — the opponent and I interpenetrate; within the empty there is the real — who can fathom the mechanism? Do not ward off, do not brace; do not prop up, do not delay (延 — that is, be late); neither soft nor hard; neither separating nor sticking — coming as if sudden, no one knows how it happened; one only feels like the wind, toppling and throwing one head over heels. A marvelous numinous state, hard to convey in words.

Let me try to describe it: in the hand there is authority (权); when lightness is fitting, be light, weighing without bias; when heaviness is fitting, be heavy, like a tiger descending the mountain. Draw so the opponent comes; advance proceeds from me. When he comes, listen to him truly; when going, value divine speed. At one glance, see his momentum (势); at one look, spot his gap. If there is a gap to exploit, dare not but enter. Miss this opportunity and it may never be gained again. This one point of the numinous state, I point out for you.

As for the body method (身法), originally there is no fixed rule. No fixation (though one says "no fixation") yet fixation (there is naturally fixation) — it lies in each person's own use. Horizontal, vertical, inverted, upright, standing, sitting, lying, straightening; bending forward, leaning back — the unorthodox (奇) and orthodox (正) generate each other. Revolving, inclining to the side, leaping and gathering — all hit the center (all involve the zhongqi (中气, central qi) releasing and gathering, presiding within). A thousand changes, ten thousand transformations — hard to depict its form.

The qi (气) never departs from principle (理) — one word exhausts it: opening and closing, empty and full — that is the classic of the quan (拳经). Apply effort daily and long, and suddenly it penetrates completely. Renewing day by day without cease, one gradually reaches divinity and sacredness (神圣). Undifferentiated and traceless — the marvelous hands empty of anything (妙手空空). As if ghosts and spirits aided my emptiness and alertness. But who knows my heart keeps only one thing: reverence (敬).

Taijiquan Quan (Expedient) Classic (太极拳权谱)

When the zhongqi (中气 — that is, the primal qi (元气) of supreme harmony (太和), neither leaning nor tilting, neither exceeding nor falling short) is threaded through fully, the spirit is a hundredfold (ten years of practice, ten years of nourishing the qi). Facing battle-array and crossing swords, above all avoid advancing first. If there is no alternative, merely lightly touch and lead. In stillness await movement; strengthen my ramparts.

An imposing battle-array, an orderly banner. With preparation there is no calamity — let him raid the camp stealthily. ... Lead once, then advance — the unorthodox and orthodox generate each other. Feigning loss and faking defeat, turn defeat into achievement.

Lead once and immediately advance; turn (turning means, from the lead, suddenly turning) and advance like the wind. Advance to seven-tenths, then stop swiftly. Warfare employs ruses — strictly guard against invasion from behind (front and back are both enemies). Front, back, left, right — all require attention. Advance without delay; neither straight nor predictable. The feet move following the hands,

Footnote

① The original edition reads 虚笼诈透 ("透" — "penetrate"); this is clearly a typesetting slip, correcting 透 to 诱 ("lure").

[No diagram on this page.]
Trang sách 76 · scan p109

Circling and turning like the divine (圆转如神). Now up (hands and feet upward), now down (hands and feet downward); sometimes compliant (using the compliant coiling method, 顺缠 — the essence complies), sometimes contrary (using the reversed coiling method — the essence goes contrary). Like sunlight shining everywhere, not falling on any edge (the preceding lines concern the opponent invading me).

When I advance to strike a person, I make him fail to guard. If he can guard it, it must be no marvelous method (these four lines concern me invading the opponent).

A great general facing the enemy is careful in every place. Let him be surrounded on all sides — advance all together. Cut down the general and seize the banner — the true way of the Hegemon-King (霸王).

The ultimate principle of Taiji (太极) can hardly be exhausted in one word. The transformations of yin and yang reside in the person. Touch but a little falsehood (use both study and thought — one must put in real effort), and the marvelous principle is hard to find.

Discourse on the Taijiquan Classic (太极拳经论)

Since antiquity, after the primal chaos (混沌), when the first stroke opened up, there was only yin and yang. Heaven and earth have this yin-yang; the ten thousand things likewise have this yin-yang. Only the sage (圣人) can model himself on this yin-yang, governing the qi (气) by principle (理), and moving principle by means of qi, applying it within human relations and daily use — so that looking up he is not ashamed before heaven, looking down he is not ashamed before man, and becomes the utmost person (至人) of heaven and earth. The playful hands (耍手, i.e., the hand practice of taiji) likewise take principle as the master and move it with qi. Its effort is the same as that of the sages and worthies.

But what the sages and worthies practice is the whole body (of learning); this is merely one aspect within the whole — how could it suffice to be prized? Nevertheless, starting from one aspect to make constant one's effort, one may also thereby, through that one aspect, glimpse the whole. Therefore, in ordinary times one must take reverence (敬) as the master; when entering the arena, one must be still more reverent. In ordinary times one must first nourish the qi (养气); when entering the arena, one must all the more move along with the qi. Let no lazy qi (惰气) intrude; let no contrary qi (逆气) run rampant. As for exerting effort long, until one day interest springs up everywhere, the marvelous principles all appear — the ten thousand differences have one root — and suddenly it penetrates completely. Is this not a joy!

Students of today, before doing the work, already expect results; with a little effort they immediately expect completion. How does this compare with what Confucius called "first the hardship, later the harvest"? Ask how the effort is to be applied: it must be as Mencius said — "There must be something toward which one works, but do not expect it; let the heart not forget it, and do not help it grow" — only then is it acceptable. If the principle is unclear, engage a clear teacher; if the path is unclear, visit good friends. When the principle is clear and the path clear and one still cannot do it, add the effort of being vigilant all day long (终日乾乾), advancing without stopping — with time it comes of itself.

Ask how long it takes: small completion (小成) takes three years, great completion (大成) takes nine years. At the stage of nine years, it can be beheld. Or after nine years, one naturally cannot stop even if one wishes — steaming upward day by day, for the rest of life there is no place to stand still. Were a divine hand (神手) to arise again, he would not alter my words. Let the impatient-hearted take note and strive.

Discourse on the Taijiquan Quan (Expedient) (太极拳权论)

Heaven and earth are one great movement (运动). The stars, sun and moon hang down their images in heaven; thunder, rain, wind and clouds bestow their bounty on the earth; and spring, summer, autumn and winter take turns moving without cease. One day and one night, circulating without ...

[No diagram on this page.]
Trang sách 77 · scan p110

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說) — Juan Shou (卷首, volume front matter)*

...this is the great movement (运动, yundong) of Heaven and Earth, and the great movement of the sages. They divided the well-field system (井田) to nourish the people's livelihood, established schools to perfect the people's nature, and managed floods, drought, and banditry with proper methods, aiding widows and orphans, the childless and the solitary with proper measures — this is the sages' great movement. As for the human body, does it alone lack movement? Born endowed with the primal qi (元气, yuanqi) of Heaven and Earth, all things are complete within me. Established through the sages' teaching and transformation, each person preserves their heaven-endowed nature; thus yin-yang and the five phases (阴阳五行), received at the very beginning of life, form the basis of the body's movement. Thereupon one hardens the will and toils the sinews and bones, making stillness and motion (动静) generate each other, closing and opening (阖辟) alternately appear, extending to advance and retreat, survival and peril, exhausting every transformation — this is the movement inherent in my own body.

If only all compatriots within the seas would each practice and ponder diligently, cultivating themselves... the myriad phenomena arrayed in order, manifesting their patterns; and could also calm the mind and steady the qi, nourishing the gongfu, letting the original substance of taiji (太极, 太极本體) be grasped by mind and spirit and penetrating through all at once — then the principles would be clear and the methods complete, with boundless benefit. For the individual, spirit and vitality would be strong and healthy, prolonging one's natural years; for the nation, bandits would be swept away and the borders defended. Inner and outer practical application, neither falling into emptiness; in harmony and joy, forever celebrating peace — how delightful!

Great indeed is the utility of movement! And yet there is still further: movement with form is not as good as movement without form; and movement without form is still not as marvelous as the one who does not move yet moves of itself. When movement reaches this point, it is wondrous movement indeed. Then the achievement of such movement shares the same substance as the sages and unites its virtue with Heaven and Earth; vast and undifferentiated, all traces vanish — one merely returns one's body to the taiji of one's own mind; and with the taiji of one's mind, returns it to the taiji of Taiji itself. How could there be any further separate function?

Marvelous indeed, taiji as taiji! Wondrous indeed, taiji as taiji! In my foolishness I have dared, by my own shallow opinion, to jot a few words to place at its head — surely inviting a smile from the experts.

Taijiquan Mingshuo (太極拳名義說) — On the Meaning of the Name "Taijiquan"

The quan (拳, fist/boxing) is named after taiji. The ancients must have deeply understood the principle of taiji before, in the whole body's up-down, left-right, front-back, using the hands' and feet's rotational movement (旋转运动), they revealed the hidden meaning of taiji and established this name as a fixed standard. The meaning is most refined; the method is most strict. Later people do not take the ancients as their model: if they do not lapse into wild arrogance, they fall into one-sidedness; to seek ... neither hard nor soft, of perfect appropriateness, so as to accord with the principle of taiji — this is indeed extremely difficult.

*(Note: the original print is smudged between "而求" and "不刚不", likely missing characters.)*

Yet I reflect that of the ancient sages who could reveal the principle of taiji, none surpassed Baoxi Shi (包羲氏, Fuxi) and the Xia Hou (夏后氏, the Xia house, i.e., Yu): the Hetu (《河圖》, River Chart) and Luoshu (《洛書》, Luo Writing) are clear proof. Alas, my learning is shallow and I cannot fathom their hidden depths; moreover those books are most profound and not easy to expound. Concerning the Hetu and Luoshu I have not been able to pierce through with a single word; but from the yin-yang interlacing and the changes of the six lines set out in the Yijing of Fuxi, and from the mutual generation and mutual conquest of the five phases transmitted by the great Yu, I have gleaned one part in ten thousand.

[No diagram: the page is entirely text; printed page number 77 at bottom right.]
Trang sách 78 · scan p111

…What they took were sometimes hexagram names, sometimes line statements, or passages on the generation and conquest of water, fire, wood, metal and earth; citing whatever seemed approximate as supporting evidence. Such scattered superficiality is no more than the dregs of the ancients. Disordered and unstructured, picked at random, they bear no resemblance to the ordered sequence of the great numbers of generation in the *Tu* and the *Shu* [River Map and Luo Writing] — how much less to the essence within them?

Even so, one need not be rigid about it. The ancients' writings were not originally composed for the boxing art. Yet their scope is so vast that it is as if they had been written for it: pick up anything at random and it fits. This too shows how subtle, lively and precise the principle of taiji (太極) is, making all things each find their proper place, so that whoever uses it always gets what he intends. Though the affairs it repays may be minute, the principle never omits anything. Let the world be as tangled and complex as it may, the ten thousand differences all return to one — how wonderful is that!

If later people can, even from the ancients' dregs, trace the branch back to its source, it is not without benefit to the study of body and mind and of nature and destiny. Though boxing is called a minor art, the great Way of taiji is present in it. Moreover, its applications are the broadest; those who practice movement should attend to it. Ponder it deeply, think on it carefully, and in time you will gain something of your own. How slight is the benefit boxing brings to man — anything but!

Explanation of the Origin of Taiji Quan (太極拳推原解)

Man, whose father is Heaven and mother is Earth, is born of the fermenting of the qi (氣) of taiji's yin and yang (in speaking of qi, the principle [li 理] is contained within it). Heaven and Earth certainly possess this principle (in speaking of principle, qi is contained within it); the unity of the three teachings is also this principle; even the myriad things and affairs of the cosmos (taiji is the substance; yin and yang are the qi within that substance. The four directions plus up and down are called *yu* 宇 — space; past and present, coming and going, are called *zhou* 宙 — time) — how could any of them not be this principle? How much less could the single art of boxing lie outside this principle and possess another.

This is why this boxing is named "taiji." Quan (拳, boxing) also means *quan* (權, a weighing-scale weight): that by which one weighs things and knows their lightness or heaviness. Its principle is truly rooted in taiji, and its use never abandons the two fists [arms]. Indeed the whole body, from top to bottom, is all taiji — that is, the whole body, top to bottom, is all boxing. One must not regard a single fist as the whole of boxing. Its pivot lies in the one heart-mind (xin 心). The heart governs respect (jing 敬) and also governs stillness (jing 靜). Able to be respectful and still, one naturally preserves the empty and numinous. When the heaven-lord holds sway, the hundred bones all obey commands. Movement generates yang; stillness generates yin. One movement and one stillness are each other's root. The clear qi rises, the turbid qi descends. Baihui (百會, at the crown) and Zhongji (中極, at the lower abdomen) are the bolt and key of the one body.

For the beginner's practice, first seek compliance and response. Coming force and turning transitions generate one another in a single stream of qi. Hands and eyes must be lively; do not move rashly. As for this qi: supremely great and supremely firm; nourish it directly and it does no harm; it fills Heaven and Earth. It is paired with righteousness and the Way, arising from the accumulation of righteousness; it flows vast and unimpeded, naturally a single qi. Light as poplar catkins, hard as metal and stone; in awe-inspiring power compare it to a tiger, in swift soaring to an eagle. In moving it is like flowing water; in stopping it matches a mountain standing. Advancing, no one can tell how you do it; retreating, none can name your speed. Its principle is refined, its methods dense, its threads finely analyzed. Release it and it fills the six directions; gather it in and it retreats and hides in secrecy. In its greatness nothing contains it; in its smallness nothing is within it. The harmonized primal qi goes wherever the intention directs; where the intention points, the whole spirit concentrates. In change it is like a dragon: no one can fathom it.

[Diagram: none — page is entirely text]
Trang sách 79 · scan p112

The application lies in the heart-mind (xin 心): this is the true key. Neither leaning nor tilted, neither excess nor deficiency. Within, cultivate the self; without, control the enemy. Adapt as circumstances require, only because one's resources are abundant. Neither attaching nor separating, neither sticking nor letting go. Joining bone and fitting the joints (jiegu doussun 接骨斗筍) — ponder them carefully. Truly accumulated and long practiced, one ascends the hall and enters the inner chamber.

Explanation of the Writings on Taiji Quan (太極拳著解)

Of the whole body, the heart-mind is the master and governs the flesh. This heart is called the Way-heart (daoxin 道心), that is, the heart of principle (lixin 理心). Now that within principle which can move and act is called qi (氣); this qi is none other than yin-yang and the five phases (wuxing 五行). Yet without principle qi has nothing to govern it, and without qi principle has nothing to carry it. Thus principle and qi are never separate but adhere to each other — this is taiji (太極) rooting in wuji (無極).

When Heaven begets man, it is precisely with this principle and this qi that the heart is born. By the time one has a little awareness, the principle-and-qi in the human heart is whole yet without visible trace. But within the heart, whether stirred from within or moved from without, thoughts (yisi 意思) arise. Before they arise, all is undifferentiated混沌 — nothing at all. As they are about to arise, the intent is fainter than faint, yet the principle of yin-yang dwells within it. Following the natural trigger (the heart forming a shape), it remains within the human heart (this is what the *Doctrine of the Mean* calls "not yet emerged"). And when it is about to emerge, the shape formed in the heart displays itself outwardly — up or down, left or right, front or back, oblique or straight — and the body methods (shenfa 身法) of the whole frame are all present within it.

At the moment before it emerges and takes shape, look at what shape the intent resembles and name it accordingly — take whatever name comes freely, for there is no fixed predisposition. Naming according to that shape at that moment is called a "move" (zhuo 著/着). And within each move, the five sense-organs and hundred bones follow their natural configuration, while the qi of yin-yang and the five phases circulates within them. As is said: movement generates yang, stillness generates yin; one movement and one stillness are each other's root. This is what is meant by "within yang there is yin, within yin there is yang" — and this is the very nature of taiji quan.

If within each move one must specify exactly what is yang, what is yin, what is yin within yang, what is yang within yin — such talk is too rigid; words could not exhaust it. Even if one could say it, nothing would be left out only by omission; it rests with the student to ponder carefully and, with time, come to understand by himself. A former worthy said: "One can give a man the compass and square, but cannot make him skillful." Inferring three things from one example rests with the learner: neither cling rigidly, nor lean habitually to one side.

Vernacular verse in seven-character lines, No. 1

Peng (掤), che (掣), ji (擠), na (捺) must be taken seriously; lure him in to land on emptiness, let him invade at will; the whole body follows in concert so the enemy cannot get close; four taels neutralize and set in motion eight thousand jin.

[Note] ① In his *Taijiquan Tuhua Jiangyi*, Chen Xin listed body, heart, principle, qi, intent, will, feeling, scene, and spirit as the "rules of taijiquan," noting specially that "the nine sections from body to spirit were all composed by Pinsan." The book's editor Chen Chunyuan, besides revising and supplementing these nine, added discussions of constancy, moves, an appended discrimination of the central qi, and transformation. Compared side by side with the "nine sections from body to spirit," the meaning is broadly the same with minor differences, but the language style and angle of exposition differ.

[Diagram: none — only the vertical title block on the right margin: 陳氏太極拳圖說, 卷首]
Trang sách 80 · scan p113

Verse No. 2 (其二 èr qí):

"Strike the throat above, the groin below; in the middle, the two ribs and the heart; below, the two shins together with the two knees; a palm to the back of the head takes the true soul."

The Body (身 shēn)

Quan is a minor art, yet one may see the great through the small. Therefore when taking the floor, do not treat it as child's play: the body must take uprightness as its foundation. Once the body is upright, everything done is correct. Broadly, do not lean, tilt, or collapse. Moreover, this art works entirely by the heart moving the hand, the hand leading the elbow, the elbow leading the body. Though the hand leads the body, the body still has its own fundamental position. In terms of structure, the body governs the hand; in terms of what matters in the hand, the hand leads the body. Though the body may at times incline, within that inclination is inherent centrality and uprightness. Do not cling rigidly. Keep to the rules, do not recklessly invent complications, and it will naturally fall in rhythm; falling in rhythm, one is close to it.

The Heart-Mind (心 xīn)

Between heaven and earth, man is the most spiritual of the ten thousand things, and the heart is the spirit of the sense organs and the whole skeleton; thus the heart is the master of the body — once the heart moves, all five organs obey. When the organs and limbs do not follow the rules, the fault is not theirs but the heart's.

Mencius said, "It comes and goes without a fixed time, none knows its direction" — this speaks of the heart. And, "Though one man listens, the other's mind is on a swan about to arrive." From this we see that having a heart depends only on whether it is held or not. If held, the heart-spirit gathers inward: the feet are weighty, the hands respectful, the head straight, the eyes grave — every action lies within it. If not held, the heart races outward: one looks without seeing, hears without hearing, eats without tasting — every action lies outside it. Moreover, the way of quan has mostly been taught orally; written works are few. As for meanings and principles, they are fully recorded in the classics and histories and transmitted in the philosophers and collections — no need to repeat them here.

Yet in the postures of quan, no one realizes they all issue from Taiji (太极). The outer traces and the inner refined intent are often regarded as: the postures are the postures, the principle-pathway is the principle-pathway — unable to join into one. This is all because they do not know that the posture issues from the principle. That which moves the posture is qi (气); that by which the posture is moved is li (理). Its opening and closing, seizing and releasing admit no addition or subtraction, no shifting — movement accords with what is natural. This is all the natural necessity of heavenly principle. If one contemplates it closely — as the far journey begins from the near, the high climb from the low — one goes from shallow to deep, advancing without skipping steps, stopping not halfway. With my own intelligence and strength, exhaust the Way's ultimate aim. Concentrate the will, focus the spirit, refine without cease; through layered turnings all reach the summit. Even realms high and far beyond reach are nothing but the realm of the Mean before one's eyes. It lies in holding the heart.

Intent (意 yì)

Yi is the meaning conceived by our heart. What the heart sends forth is called yi. When a single thought issues — as in composing an essay or writing characters, where the descending brush carries intent — where is yi seen? It is seen in the hand.

[Diagram: none on this page]
Trang sách 81 · scan p114

This speaks of yi issuing outward. Yi issues from the heart and is transmitted to the hand — utmost in meaningful charm, utmost in expressive spirit.

If what the heart sends forth is upright, what the hand forms is also upright; if what the heart sends forth is skewed, what the hand forms is also skewed. If a person is calm and even-tempered, the hand method and body method are naturally correct; if one is rushed and flustered, or careless and slack, then nothing the hand forms fails to lean aslant. Only when agitation dissolves and arrogance settles does what the organs and limbs form naturally accord with compass and square. True principle pervades the whole, so there is no cluttered framework. Even when the body method sometimes inclines, it is the inclination of centrality — within the slant there is uprightness, containing true intent. With true intent, its continuous, lingering meaningful quality is nothing like the stiff, rigid, overbearing hard school.

This intent on the one hand issues from li (理), on the other is trained through qi (气). A hard hand is purely trained qi; qi trained to completion can indeed kill a man — but compared with li, it is lower after all. Thus my intent can be known, and his intent surmised. The student should attend carefully and discern where intent issues from.

Will (志 zhì)

Where the heart goes is called zhi — will. People must value establishing a will; without it not one thing is accomplished, and one stays beneath others all one's life. If one can establish a will, then all the orderly threads, from beginning to end, layer by layer, are thoroughly fathomed. Daring not to slacken, from effortful striving one reaches the unconditioned whole — the saying, "Where there is a will, the matter succeeds." Otherwise, failure. Can a person indeed do without establishing a will!

Constancy (恒 héng)

The Way of heaven and earth is simply one thing: constancy. Because of constancy, sun and moon attain heaven and shine forever; the four seasons transform and complete things forever; the sage abides long in his Way and the world is transformed — how much more a single art! If one is only earnest and diligent, without slack from start to finish, how could one sprout without flowering, flower without fruiting! The Book (of Documents) says: "In learning, constancy is prized." Confucius said: "A man without constancy cannot even be a shaman or physician." Clearly in applying effort, constancy is the most prized of all.

Will is the foundation's beginning; constancy is the work's completion. Constant, one succeeds; inconstant, one fails. These two characters, zhi and heng, are the key formula for doing anything. The student must know them and above all wake up sharply. One often sees people applying effort by fits and starts; what they did not plant will fall — yet they blame the teacher for not teaching. Why not turn and ask themselves: what was their effort like?

Technique (着 zháo)

Since antiquity, sages who had civil affairs necessarily had martial preparedness. But civil matters all have completed books — classics, histories, philosophers, collections, everything is there. As for martial preparedness, it is passed over without discussion. From the Yellow Emperor, Yao, and Shun down to the Tang, Song, Yuan, and Ming, those who commanded military affairs each produced military books, yet these covered no more than orderly foot formations; as for quan, none touched it.

[Diagram: none on this page]
Trang sách 82 · scan p115

The art of quan — when it began, none knows; no completed books are seen. Through Tang, Song, Yuan, Ming, and the Great Qing, even where books existed, they were no more than drawings, none detailing the principle to show the steps by which one might ascend. Moreover, those who practiced this art often erred toward hardness, because they valued blood-qi and not meaning and principle. When principle is unclear, matters inevitably slide into recklessness and deviance without end.

Our Chen family moved from Shanxi to Wen County carrying this art. Though a manual was transmitted, it too was only pictures; the meanings and principles were also not addressed. I am unlearned, my skill very shallow, and dare not presumptuously annotate the manual. But to guide beginners, I cannot help sketching the main points, to show students how to set to work.

For every technique (着 zháo), one must consider: where does the hand start, where does it pass, where does it stop; what form is outside, what jin-qi inside — passing finely through the center of the heart; and between the underside of this technique and the top of the next, how the seam carries the previous and initiates the next. You must make the blood-vessels connect through, so the two techniques above and below are not seen as two severed stumps. At first each technique stands complete in itself; then one qi threads through, and a hundred, a thousand techniques become as one.

Take Lan-ca-yi (懒擦衣, "Lazy about Tying the Coat"): the right hand starts in front of the left armpit, back of the hand upward, fingers rising obliquely from below, first circling a small loop; midway the hand passes in front of Shenting (神庭) and slowly descends. The arm may extend only nine-tenths; the hand stops level with the shoulder, its back seeming to face up, slightly closing forward. From start to finish the hand travels with the general intent of a drawn bow. Above moves thus; below, the foot likewise moves in unison with the hand on this intent. When the hand reaches the end of its ground, the toes then settle firmly. The inner jin in the hand issues from the heart, passes the right breast, crosses Zhongfu (中府), passes Qingling (青灵), surges through Shaohai (少海), runs Lingdao (灵道), ferries across Lieque (列缺), and stops at Zhongchong (中冲), Shaochong (少冲), Shaoshang (少商) and the other points. The foot first lands at Pucan (仆参), passes Yongquan (涌泉), up to Dadun (大敦), Yinbai (隐白) and the other points. Moreover the inner jin must go through the midst of the bone, fill upward into skin and flesh, travel to the five fingers, and only then stop. Top jin lifts; waist jin presses down; below Changqiang (长强) it turns upward; crotch (裆 dāng) jin drops; the right hand closes with the left, and knee, crotch, chest, and lower abdomen all close together. Closing (合 hé) means the spirit-qi gathers and is not allowed to scatter — not merely going through the motions with an empty frame. Only when one handles the matter reverently does spirit-qi arrive everywhere, and one does not tread on emptiness. The next technique, Danbian (单鞭, "Single Whip"), is roughly the same as this one.

Generally: hand moving is yang (阳), hand still is yin (阴); the back is yang, the chest is yin. There is also yang within yin and yin within yang. Whichever hand holds the moment is yang; whichever does not is yin. Also there is one technique first yang then yin; one hand yin outside yet yang inside. One yin, one yang — and both must be moved with the central-peak jin (中峰劲).

The central peak is neither leaning nor askew: it is the qi within my heart — what is called the flood-like qi (浩然之气) — with li (理) governing within and acting outward. Turbid qi descends; close up the crotch jin. The lower frame stable, the upper frame is likewise nimble. A thousand words cannot picture its subtlety; demonstrated on the spot, everyone can see and understand. Fallen onto paper and brush, it all becomes dregs; formed in hands and feet —

[Diagram: none on this page]
Trang sách 83 · scan p116

...it also becomes a trace, and yet is not the trace itself; without the trace nothing manifests the spirit — just as without dregs nothing writes out meaning and principle. This lies with the good learner. Mencius said: "One can give a man compass and square, but cannot make him skillful." That is surely what is meant.

Principle (理 lǐ)

Li is the ordered pattern of heaven and earth, the standard of human affairs. Follow the nature of what is natural to it, act according to what is proper in the matter, accord with what human hearts agree upon, and exhaust why heavenly principle is so. One opening, one closing with no strain at all; one movement, one stillness, fitting heaven's nature exactly. This is the purity of our Way.

Qi (气)

What is qi? It is precisely the character "moves" in "Heaven moves vigorously" (天行健). Heaven's body is supremely vigorous, and that by which it moves is qi. Unstagnating, unceasing; not diverging, not separating; neither leaning nor askew — this is central qi (中气). Add the effort of "nourishing it with uprightness and doing no harm" and it is the upright qi of qian and kun — the flood-like qi (浩然之气) Mencius spoke of. Once one goes against qi's naturalness and mixes in crosswise qi (横气), one is stiff and hard in the middle; the posture can hardly turn roundly and freely, and on meeting a nimble opponent one finds oneself helpless — unable to advance, not daring to retreat, simply awaiting whatever the other dispenses. How dull! Therefore do not rely on blood-qi alone, and do not mix in crosswise qi.

Appendix: Distinguishing Central Qi

Central qi: zhong is zhong, qi is qi. Zhong means neither leaning nor askew — the name for neither exceeding nor falling short. Speaking of qi in terms of li: heaven uses the qi of yin-yang and the five phases to transform and generate the ten thousand things. Where the form exists, the qi exists — it is the original qi (元气) man receives from heaven. Qi does not leave li; li does not leave qi. Without li, qi cannot be established; without qi, li cannot act. Qi and li are mutually necessary. Li has its deviations, and qi has its deviations. Where li deviates, selfishness intrudes; where qi deviates, crosswise force acts. Only when both attain the center, spoken of together, is it called central qi.

I privately hold that what cannot be described in words is central qi. Central qi is the flood-like qi Mencius spoke of — the original qi the Yijing calls "preserving the union of Great Harmony" (保合太和). Qi does not leave li: speak of qi and li is within it. In quan, moving the qi is primary, yet within it li naturally governs. The li that attains the center is harder still to put into words, so I speak only roughly of qi as it adheres to form.

Qi fills every part of the body, but its command is in the heart. When the heart's qi issues forth, the first to obey is the will in the kidneys (肾中之志). Once the heart's mechanism stirs, the will follows where the heart points, and the five organs and hundred bones all go along with it. Each part has its own essence and stops at the position its part travels to — this is all arriving together. Sometimes earlier and later are distinguished, sometimes not. It is what is meant by "small virtue flows like streams, great virtue transforms massively; the ways run in parallel without conflict."

[Diagram: none on this page]
Trang sách 84 · scan p117

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)*

Take the Single Whip (单鞭, danbian) posture: at the start, when the mind intends first to close (合, he), the two hands immediately use reversed winding (倒转缠丝, daozhuan chansi) to bind together, and the left foot draws in beside the right foot, closing with it. When the mind intends to open out (开), the left hand uses forward winding (顺转缠丝, shunzhuan chansi), the right hand reversed winding; the two thighs apply winding, left forward and right reversed; the crown threads upward (顶劲, ding jin), the chest holds in (含), the waist jin sinks, the crotch opens fully. After this, between intending and not intending, when closure is spoken of, above and below close together as one.

Moreover, the jin of the various organs and body parts each runs along its own channel (经络, jingluo) without the slightest discrepancy. The mind is the "greater part" (大体, datí), the organs and frame the "lesser part" (小体); virtue (德) is the proper principle within both. Once the mind's mechanism stirs, the hundred bones take its commands — is this not what is meant by "the small virtue flows like streams, the great virtue transforms mightily, and the ways run in parallel without conflict"? This is what is called the flow of the central qi (中气, zhongqi), a single qi threading through the whole — just so.

Distinguishing the Central Qi (中气) from the Flood-like Qi (浩然之气) and the Blood Qi (血气)

The central qi differs slightly from the flood-like qi (haoran zhi qi), and greatly differs from the blood qi. The central qi is the primal qi (元气, yuanqi) of supreme harmony (太和, taihe) — what the Zhongyong (《中庸》, Doctrine of the Mean) calls "neither leaning to one side nor the other," the ordinary constant principle, ruling over an upright qi that is neither hard nor soft, perfectly apt. If you can use this qi to move into the hands (speaking of the hands, yet the whole body is included), no one in the world can defeat it. If someone is defeated, either their gongfu has not reached full heat, or they have lapsed into one-sidedness and lost the center. Lapsing into one-sidedness, it is not that others defeat me — I defeat myself. The value of this qi lies in attaining the center; that is why it is named central qi — not in the sense of a qi moving in the middle of the organs and frame (the middle of the body is the center in the spatial sense; the "center" of central qi is the principle of neither leaning nor tilting, neither excess nor shortfall, ruling over the upright primal qi in which hard and soft attain the mean).

The flood-like qi, by and large, leans toward the hard side. Observe the bearing of Confucius and Mencius and you will know: Confucius's words were utterly mild and harmonious, while Mencius's bearing carried edges and corners. Even in his own words he said: "Supremely great, supremely hard." So I say it mostly leans to the hard side; yet it is still inherited primal qi, only tinged with severity. To call it yuanqi is permissible; to call it the qi of supreme harmony is a slight falling short — this is where it differs a little from the central qi. If a practitioner of boxing can move with the flood-like qi, his skill already surpasses most; adding further cultivation and nourishment, he comes close to the central qi.

As for blood qi (血气, xueqi), it is the qi circulating in the blood vessels — what boxers call "crosswise qi" (横气, hengqi). It relies wholly on youth and fierce strength, oppressing the opponent without feeling and without principle. Most such people fail; even winning by chance through excessive strength, once they meet a skilled hand, great as their qi is, they too are defeated. If one can at least follow the rules (the established methods of boxing), one can indeed strike people — but only bend the opponent's body, not convince the opponent's heart. The central qi, however, can make the opponent dare not advance and dare not retreat, drained of strength everywhere, in extreme peril — as though standing on a round stone, not daring to move carelessly (moving, in level tone), nearly falling the instant the feet move. At that moment, even without striking, the opponent is convinced in his own heart.

The distinctions above — whether they are right, I do not know. I await correction from the discerning.

[No diagram: the page is entirely text; printed page number 84.]
Trang sách 85 · scan p118

Chen's Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation · Juan shou (卷首, opening volume)

Qing 情 (Feeling)

When principle (li 理) and vital energy (qi 气) issue outward, that is feeling (qing 情). The comings and goings of people in their dealings with one another are called human feeling (renqing 人情); the rises and falls, the cadences of literature, are called literary feeling (wenqing 文情). In practicing the quan (拳) — when you wish to suppress, first raise; when you wish to raise, first suppress — throughout it there is the lively play of natural impulse (tianji 天机), full of feeling and charm. If the quan has no feeling or charm, it is like a wooden puppet, a dead snake sprawled on the ground — what scenic beauty is there in that? How could anyone see in it the vital dragon and living tiger, so that onlookers' eyes burn to see it, their mouths delight in speaking of it, their hearts wish to learn it? For this reason the quan cannot be without feeling and charm.

As for crossing hands with an opponent, one absolutely must not regard human feeling (renqing). The moment you look to human feeling, he will visit mercilessness upon you. How could that be acceptable?

Jing 景 (Scene)

A single expanse of spirit in movement — that is called scene (jing 景). Its opening and closing, gathering and releasing, its supple windings and turnings, each kind of it like a painting — that is called scene. Scene does not leave feeling, just as feeling does not leave principle (li 理); they are joined together. If the heart holds no subtle delight, then even in practicing the quan one cannot produce fine scenery. Ask how one produces scenery: at first one follows the rules (guiju 规矩); next one transforms within the rules; in the end one is numinous (shen 神) within the rules. On my side I play it so that flowers fall from heaven in wild profusion; in others it naturally brings a slap of astonishment on the table. Within there is feeling, without there is scene — just like a bright sky and clear air, a mild breeze blowing gently, the misted scenery of spring, the great creation's own composition (dakuai wenzhang): everywhere willows dance and blossoms grow proud; every move (zhao 着) is bright mountains and shining waters. Travelers' eyes stir their hearts; poets' minds are soothed and spirits expanded — what fine scenery indeed! When the scene of the quan reaches this point, it is worth beholding.

Shen 神 (Spirit)

Spirit is that numinous qi (lingqi 灵气) which issues outward from essential energy (jingqi 精气) without any flaw of difficulty or stagnation. Among all things between heaven and earth, wherever the vitality (jingshen 精神) is full, the spirited expression is naturally full. In a person, although it dwells within the organs and skeleton, it in truth overflows beyond the organs and skeleton. Generally speaking, when heart, hand, and eye all arrive together, there is spirit; without spirit it is dead and stiff, not alive, and cannot move people.

Spirit in a person is not confined to the eyes, yet in the eyes it is easiest to see. Therefore, when practicing the quan, the eyes must not glance aslant; they must follow the hands in their going and returning. For example, in playing Lan zha yi (懒擦衣 Lazy About the Coat), the eyes follow the middle finger of the right hand; when the hand of Lan zha yi reaches its end, the eyes also reach their end, fixed on the tip of the middle finger, and must not look elsewhere. When the eyes are fixed here, the spirit of the whole body is all fixed here. In this way the entire posture of Lan zha yi has spirit, because the spirit is gathered. In playing Dan bian (单鞭 Single Whip), the eyes fix on the place where the left hand begins, and follow the left hand slowly along; when Dan bian is finished, the eyes fix on the tip of the middle finger and must not move recklessly. In playing Pi shen chui (披身捶 Draping-Body Fist), the eyes fix on the tip of the rear foot. In playing Zhou di zhe quan (肘底着拳 Fist under the Elbow) and Xiao qin zhang (小擒掌 Small Seizing Palm), the eyes fix on the fist under the elbow. In playing Xie xing ao bu (斜行拗步 Slanting Oblique Step), when the right hand is in front, the eyes rest on the right hand. In playing Bao tou tui shan (抱头推山 Cover the Head and Push the Mountain), although both hands are in front, the right hand is primary; although the eyes fix on both, most of their attention is on the right hand. In playing Zhi dang chui (指裆捶 Fist to the Crotch), the eyes fix downward. In playing the next step, Kua hu (跨虎 Straddling the Tiger), the eyes fix upward. In playing Yan shou chui (演手捶 Displaying-Hand Fist), the eyes fix forward. In playing Hui shou chui (回首捶 Turn-Head Fist), the eyes fix backward. In general, of all directions — above, below, the four sides — wherever the command lies at a given moment, there the eyes…

[Diagram: none — text page only]

(continued on next page)

Trang sách 86 · scan p119

…'[The spirit] fixes its attention on a certain spot — this is the great rule. There are also cases where the spirit is fixed here while the intent (yi) is actually directed elsewhere. This is precisely what is meant by "a great general whose awe radiates in eight directions": only when the gaze shoots forth in all four directions does the awe extend to all eight sides, with spirit present everywhere.'

In the way of quan (拳, boxing), originally there was no such posture, and yet this posture was created out of it — this is precisely "being from non-being." How divine that is! And how much more so the divine of the divine — is it not all that Taiji (太极) and yin-yang generate and set in motion? When quan reaches this point, one has already "entered the chamber." Movement and stillness, slow and fast, all turn at the heart's command — what fear is there of sluggishness and lack of spirit and expression!

Hua (化, transformation)

Hua means to transform the rules themselves (guiju). There are two realms of hua: zaohua (造化, creative transformation) and shenhua (神化, divine transformation). Zao speaks of the beginning; hua speaks of the end. Divine transformation is what the Master meant by "At seventy, I could follow my heart's desires without overstepping the bounds." When one's quan has become practiced again and again, so that there is no visible trace to model, like a divine dragon's changes that cannot be grasped — moving at will, spontaneously forming its own standards, beyond all measuring — the art has reached this: it is truly of the "divine grade" (shenpin).

The principle of Taiji issues from without origin and completes itself without trace, without beginning or end, presented all at once like a living plate laid out. Ah, this is perfection — "herein one may behold the ultimate"! Though quan is a minor art, this is what is meant by "seeing the great in the small." How could quan be easily spoken of!

On the Application of Taiji Quan (太极拳用说)

The five phases' generation and conquest (wuxing shengke) is present everywhere and constant at all times. For example, when two people cross hands: if the opponent comes with softness (rou), that belongs to yin, and yin should be conquered with yang; it belongs to water, and water should be conquered with fire — this is the natural principle, the ordinary configuration that everyone easily understands. But quan alone is not so: its application is entirely "the expedient (quan, 权) residing within the constant (jing, 经)," and the expedient never departs from the constant. Why is this so? When the opponent comes with softness, it is first to use softness in listening (ting, 听, i.e. gauging) how I will respond, and then to seize the opportunity to strike me. If I respond with hardness, I fall right into his scheme — nothing could be more foolish. How then should one respond? If he uses a soft method to listen to me (listening with the arm, not with the ear), I use a soft method to listen to him; each quan has its boundary. If he draws me in to advance, I may go only to the edge of my own boundary and no further; to go further is to lose position (shi, 势).

If one says, "If you do not enter the tiger's den, how can you obtain the tiger's cub," that is speaking of those born with great courage, not teaching ordinary people. Even for the greatly courageous, it would mean braving danger. How then should it be handled? If he draws me forward and, before I pass my boundary, he turns to hardness — that is he fears me and changes from soft to hard, showing he is not my equal; I should conquer him with softness. If this change arises midway, I still use the soft way of "drawing in and landing on emptiness" (yinjin luokong) to strike him. If he has drawn me to my boundary, then it is just the time to spy out his timing and disposition, observe his appearance and expression, and gauge his spirit and force. If there is an opening to seize, I suddenly change from soft to hard and strike. This is…

Trang sách 87 · scan p120

陳氏太極拳圖說 卷首

This speaks of conquering softness with hardness, water with fire. If, midway, the opponent has not yet changed his softness, and at the boundary forcibly props and braces, he too should be struck.

If he has drawn me to my boundary and there is no opening to seize, and his soft, finely attuned skill remains as before — that is a strong opponent, a true match, and one must not contend with him directly. I should withdraw and guard my own gate. Earlier, when I advanced in softness to listen (ting) to him, now I still listen by the soft way; gradually turning, I retreat, still leading him forward by the soft way. If he does not advance, he is a wise man. If, because of my leading, he rashly advances, mistaking me for timid — headlong, either coming in softness or suddenly changing soft to hard midway — I simply lower my hand slightly and slowly draw him on, making him advance and leaving him no choice but to advance. When he can no longer gain position (shi), his strength is exhausted, his wisdom is spent, and his very life-breath grows desperate. At that moment, my softness suddenly turns to hardness, and with little effort — one turn and he is conquered.

At that point, does he not know that an army advancing deep alone can hardly win? Yet by then regret comes too late: to advance, he dares not — advancing, he is defeated; to retreat, he dares not — retreating, he is defeated; even neither advancing nor retreating, he comes to defeat. It is like soldiers worn out, baggage all gone, with nothing left but to be bound hand and foot and surrender — how could they still fight!

The subtlety of striking a man lies wholly in this. This is called conquering hardness with softness, water with fire — still the way of the five phases' generation and conquest (wuxing shengke).

Heaven-One generates water (tian yi sheng shui). Water is yin without and yang within, soft without and hard within, and belongs to the kidneys. Its advancing in softness is like water's waves flowing and winding — not first relying on force, but using wisdom. Earth-Two generates fire. Fire is yang without and yin within, hard without and soft within, and in man belongs to the heart. Water and fire have form but no substance (质). Heaven-Three generates wood, Earth-Four generates metal — these have both form and substance. Heaven-Five generates earth; when water and fire are evenly matched in force, neither prevails.

That one speaks of fire conquering water is speaking of fire being more numerous than water. When the opponent advances softly and suddenly changes to hardness, that is the wood generated by water. Wood is yang-substance — the yang aspect within water, nourished until it becomes substance. Water and wood are fundamentally one continuous line, so the change from soft to hard is easiest — because both its form and substance belong to yang.

The foregoing on fire conquering water is because fire can generate earth and earth can generate metal. Fire is bright without and dark within — a yin nature. Metal is the substance formed of yin. Wood in man belongs to the liver; metal to the lungs. In all the world only metal can conquer wood; metal and fire are both of the yin class. The statement "conquer softness with hardness" means fire conquering water and metal conquering wood — spoken of their outer aspects. Fire's nature is violent, metal's substance hard. When heart-fire rises, the spleen's qi (气) is stirred; the angry qi vented outward, audible with sound, is the work of metal. When the spleen's qi stirs, my liver and kidneys are all set in motion with it. Though it is called conquering softness with hardness, its root is really conquering hardness with softness: the opponent is soft first and hard after, while I am hardness lodged within softness — inwardly civilized and bright, outwardly soft and compliant — and therefore I conquer him.

Trang sách 88 · scan p121

If the opponent first attacks with hardness (刚 gang), then subduing him feels even easier. How so? Suppose someone strikes at me with a very fierce momentum. I do not clash hardness against hardness; I align my forearm (肱 gong), body, and step in one smooth direction, sink my body down with the step, let my hand fall to his flank, and yield past the crest of his force. His sharp energy (锐气) rushes straight forward, heedless of left and right — and to suddenly divert his forward-driving strength sideways is very difficult for him. I then strike from the side. Using my smooth, compliant force to strike his sideways-bound and powerless posture — is that easy or not? So I say: "Conquering hardness is easy; conquering softness is hard."

Boundaries (界限 jie xian)

What is a boundary? Whenever land is enfeoffed, offices established and duties assigned — indeed in all movement and stillness, speech and silence — there are boundaries. One step over one's allotment, one careless word, is over the line; over the line, you are interfering with another. All affairs are so — how much more boxing? When a person walks, stretching fully the feet can open two chi five cun, but that is forced, not natural. Naturally, walking at ease covers no more than about one chi one or two cun. The hands of the upper body level with the toes of the lower body — that is the boundary. Roughly: extend the arms only four or five fen, use only half the internal essence (内精), and open the stance only a bit over one chi. Then the whole body, above and below, left and right, circles and turns without the least constraint. For movement never oversteps the boundary: like a general within his own territory, knowing mountains, rivers, terrain, local customs thoroughly in his heart, he attacks and retreats with ample room. Once inside another's territory, one must everywhere be extra careful in defense; the slightest carelessness sprouts the seed of defeat. This is why the gentleman "thinks not beyond his position" (思不出其位).

Practicing the form is fundamentally a means of self-preservation, so when practicing, treat it as facing an enemy and you will progress fastest. Yet fearing this might stir people's combative spirit, the first half of the routine speaks mostly of rules and not of application; only in the latter half are things said frankly, to show the method of use. Even so, it may only be known, not lightly tried. Only if unavoidable, for the sake of preserving one's life, may it be used.

By and large this boxing is a solo performance: moving empty-handed, with no enemy before, behind, or to the sides. Apply your own effort — the more repetitions the better. Though the root is not yet firm, the branches and leaves flourish; and for the way of nurturing life and preserving one's destiny, nothing is better. The learner need only accept the difficulty first. As for later harvest, set it out of mind — do not let even a hair's hope of quick results divide the single-minded devotion of your effort. The golden needle (金针) has already been passed across; may the learner strive on.

Key Essentials of Contending and Yielding (争走要诀 zheng zou yao jue)

When two people's hands engage, each harbors the will to win. They press each other until nine-tenths-and-nine-li of the ground is occupied, leaving only one li. Victory and defeat turn entirely on that one li of ground. If he occupies it first, I lose; if I occupy it first, he loses. Whether one gains the advantage (得势) or not hinges wholly on this. Here both have reached the point where mountains end and waters run out.

[Diagram: none — text-only page]

(Page: 88)

Trang sách 89 · scan p122

At this moment, what should one do? The answer: one must first take the upstream position (据上游). How to take the upstream position? The crown essence (顶精) lifts and holds the central qi (中气); the hand rises slightly, positioned above the opponent's hand; the body leans slightly forward, pressing him so he cannot gain the advantage. Strength values swift release; the moment values divine speed — one instant late is failure, one burst of speed gains the advantage. With the advantage gained, one forward push of the hand splits the bamboo without difficulty — as in splitting bamboo, the blade runs on of itself. Like two people playing chess: when the game reaches its endgame, victory and defeat turn on that single move. Like the pursuit of the deer (逐鹿): only the talented and swift-footed claim it first. Like two kingdoms raising armies: first seize the opponent's baggage and provisions. All these are methods of taking the upstream, seizing the head.

Therefore in everyday practice of the form, everything lies in one rise and one turn. As the saying goes: "To gain the advantage, contest the incoming pulse (来脉 lai mai); to produce the unexpected, lie in the turning point (转关 zhuan guan)." When the hand of a posture is about to rise, you must first make the hand receive and hold the preceding posture, not allowing the flow of spirit, qi, and blood-vessels to be cut off. Once the connection is made, you must consider how the hand may seize the moment and gain the advantage. When the incoming pulse is true, the moment and advantage are won, and the turning point moves nimbly of itself. Able to do this, then one day when crossing hands with another, you will naturally first place yourself on undefeatable ground and command at will. The incoming pulse and the turning point — can these be neglected?

[Diagram: none — text-only page]
