Trang sách 92 · scan p125

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chén Shì Tàijíquán Túshuō) — *Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation*

(This page is a title page only: a black vertical title block reading 陳氏太極拳圖說. The rest of the page is blank; no diagrams, verse, or commentary.)

Trang sách 93 · scan p126

陳氏太極拳圖說 卷一 (*Chén Shì Tàijíquán Túshuō*, juàn 1)

太极拳卷一 · 学拳须知 (Tàijíquán juān yī · Xuéquán xūzhī) — Things to Know When Learning Quan (拳)

1. In learning taijiquan (太极拳), one must not fail to be reverent. Without reverence one is outwardly disrespectful to teachers and friends and inwardly disrespectful to one's own body; if the heart is not gathered and restrained, how can one learn the art?

2. In learning taijiquan one must not be wild (狂, kuáng); wildness breeds trouble. Not only must the hands not be wild — speech too must not be wild. One's outward bearing must carry a scholarly, refined air; otherwise, wildness without must mean loss within.

3. In learning taijiquan one must not be complacent (滿, mǎn); complacency invites loss. As the saying goes, "Beyond heaven there is still heaven." If one can be humble, one receives teaching with an open mind — and who would not gladly tell such a person what is good? Gathering many goods into goodness, goodness becomes great.

4. In learning taijiquan, every single move (着, zhāo) must be studied with care. If one move is not pondered, the mechanism, intent, feeling, and principle of that posture remain forever obscure. The points that link what precedes and what follows especially demand attention; without it, the incoming line (來脈, láimài) is not genuine and the turning points (轉關, zhuǎnguān) lack agility. Each move then stands alone, and the whole cannot be threaded from beginning to end by one qi (一氣貫通); without that unbroken flow one can hardly approach the primordial qi of Great Harmony (太和元氣).

5. In learning taijiquan, first learn to read books. Once the principles of books are clear, learning the quan (拳) comes naturally.

6. In learning taijiquan one learns only the opening and closing of yin (陰) and yang (陽). The body inherently possesses its own yin-yang opening and closing; the teacher cannot add to or subtract from it. Restoring what is inherent is where teaching stops (the teacher teaches the standards and rules — the principle of the Great Mean and Perfect Uprightness).

7. Though taijiquan may seem to have no great use, in today's age of contending powers, without martial skill how shall we preserve ourselves? Take this book and practice it; for the army's methods of stepping and alignment it is not without small benefit. If everyone in our country practiced it, then even in hand-to-hand combat, however strong the enemy, what could they do to us! This too is a way of preserving the nation's integrity. Let those with heart not discard these words.

[Note ①: "Things to Know When Learning Boxing" was compiled and revised by the book's editors, Chen Chunyuan and others, from drafts left by the original author Chen Xin; its content is essentially the same as the "General Principles" (凡例).]

Trang sách 94 · scan p127

Eight: In learning Taijiquan (太極拳, Tàijíquán), one must not use it as a means for theft and robbery. If it is used for plunder, Heaven robs one of one's spirit (魄, pò), ghosts and spirits give no protection — how much less will people! Who in all the world could tolerate such a person!

Nine: In learning Taijiquan, one must not oppress or bully others with ferocity. The moment one bullies with ferocity one provokes the people's wrath — such a one is the chief of criminals.

The Thirteen Postures Divided into Sections (十三势分节)

First section: Jingang Dao Dui (金剛捣碓, "Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounds the Mortar") — just one posture; it says that the principles of Taiji's yin and yang are all contained within it.

Second section: Lan Zha Yi (揽擦衣, "Lazily Tying the Coat"), Dan Bian (单鞭, "Single Whip"), Jingang Dao Dui — three postures in all; this is Taiji producing the Two Forms (两仪). Concluding again with Jingang Dao Dui means the intent never departs from the origin.

Third section: Bai E Liang Chi (白鵝亮翅, "White Goose Spreads Its Wings"), Lou Xi Ao Bu (搂膝拗步, "Brush Knee and Twisting Step") — two postures in all; the Two Forms produce the Four Images (四像). Bai E raises the posture, and Lou Xi Ao Bu corresponds to the positions of the four trigrams Qian, Kun, Kan and Li.

Fourth section: Chu Shou (初收, "First Closing"), Xie Xing Ao Bu (斜行拗步, "Slanting Twisting Step") — two postures in all. The Four Images produce the Eight Trigrams; the slanting walk corresponds to the four corner trigrams Dui, Zhen, Xun and Gen.

Fifth section: Zai Shou (再收, "Second Closing"), Qian Tang Ao Bu (前堂拗步, "Forward Hall Twisting Step"), Yan Shou Chui (演手捶, "Charging Fist"), Jingang Dao Dui — four postures in all. Rooting in stored essence (精, jīng), it proceeds to the issuing of essence, ending in a return to the original image of Taiji.

Sixth section: Pi Shen Chui (披身捶, "Draping-Body Fist"), Bei Zhe Kao (背折靠, "Backward Bent Lean"), Zhou Di Kan Quan (肘底看拳, "Fist Guarded Under the Elbow"), Dao Juan Gong (倒卷肱, "Reverse Roll Forearm"), Bai E Liang Chi, Lou Xi Ao Bu — six postures in all. Within them are the inverted-body method, the backward-body method, the bent-body method and the retreating method — these are all variant modes within the postures, and none is lacking.

Seventh section: Shan Tong Bei (闪通背, "Flash Through the Back"), Yan Shou Chui, Lan Zha Yi, Dan Bian — four postures in all. This is the inverted-turning body method returning to the level, even body method.

Eighth section: Zuo-You Yun Shou (左右云手, "Cloud Hands Left and Right"), Gao Tan Ma (高探马, "High Pat on the Horse"), Zuo-You Ca Jiao① (左右擦脚, "Rubbing Kicks Left and Right"), Zhong Dan Bian (中单鞭, "Middle Single Whip"), Xia Yan Shou (下演手, "Lower Charging Fist"), Er Qi Jiao (二起脚, "Double Kick"), Shou Tou Shi (兽头势, "Beast-Head Posture"), Ti Yi Jiao (踢一脚, "One Kicking Foot"), Deng Yi Gen (蹬一跟, "One Heel Press"), Yan Shou Chui, Xiao Qin Na (小擒拿, "Small Grappling Seizure"), Bao Tou Tui Shan (抱头推山, "Cover the Head and Push the Mountain"), Dan Bian — thirteen postures in all. Yun Shou is the sideways-moving method; in the rubbing kicks, the kick and heel-press both speak of foot methods; Xia Yan Shou is the crouching-body method; Er Qi Jiao is the flying-body method; Bao Tou Tui Shan is the whole-body method; Zhong Dan Bian uses hands and feet together; the kick and heel-press are two great turning body methods; Xiao Qin Na is the hidden-step method; Shou Tou Shi is the method of guarding the head, the heart and the knees. The thirteen minor postures above are for engaging a crowd of enemies — one great battle in a single breath, linked without interruption, with no gap in which to pause; therefore they are combined together as one section.

① In this book, for "Zuo Ca Jiao" and "You Ca Jiao" (left and right rubbing kicks), the discussions of boxing theory use both the character 插 (chā, "insert") and 擦 (cā, "rub"). Based on the discussions in the "Song of Long and Short Lines on the Left and Right Rubbing Kicks Combined" and the "Four-Character Verse on the Left and Right Rubbing Kicks," this edition has uniformly changed the character 插 to 擦 throughout.

Trang sách 95 · scan p128

陳氏太極拳圖說 卷一 (*Chén Shì Tàijíquán Túshuō*, juàn 1)

The Posture List (Postures 9 through 13)

Posture 9: Front Zhaō (前昭)①, Rear Zhaō (后昭), Parting the Wild Horse's Mane (野马分鬃), Single Whip (单鞭), Fair Lady Works the Shuttles (玉女穿梭), Llanca Yi / Lazy-about Tying the Coat (揽擦衣), Single Whip — seven postures in all. Front and Rear Zhaō are the hand and eye methods of watching front and back; Parting the Wild Horse's Mane is the spreading body method with rapid advancing; Fair Lady Works the Shuttles is the right-turning body method and the level leaping body method; Single Whip is the method of letting the qi (气) return to the dantian (丹田), calming heart and qi. These seven postures are also methods of mutual combat.

Posture 10: Second Cloud Hands left and right (左右云手), Swing Kick (摆脚), One Hall Snake (一堂蛇), Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg (金鸡独立), Kick to the Heavens (朝天蹬), Reeling Backward (倒卷帘), White Goose Shows Its Wings (白鹅亮翅), Brush Knee and Twist Step (搂膝拗步), Flash Through the Back (闪②通背), Forearm Fist (演手捶), Lazy-about Tying the Coat, Single Whip — twelve postures in all. Swing Kick is a horizontal foot strike; the Split Fall (跌岔) is a low body method; Golden Rooster and Kick to the Heavens are high body methods, methods using the knee. The seven postures below are repetitions and need not be repeated. These twelve small postures are likewise linked by one qi (一氣), uncuttable, and so are joined as one posture.

Posture 11: Cloud Hands left and right, Gao Tan Ma / High Patting the Horse (高探马), Cross Feet (十字脚), Fist to the Crotch (指裆③捶), Azure Dragon Comes Out of the Water (青龙出水), Single Whip — six postures in all. Cross Feet are the cross leaning strike (十字靠); Fist to the Crotch is the method of subduing life (the decisive blow); Azure Dragon Out of the Water is a leaping advance of the body, needing no turn — it is also a leaping method (縱法).

Posture 12: Paved Ground Brocade (铺地锦), Step Up to the Seven Stars (上步七星), Step Down and Ride the Tiger (下步跨虎) — three postures in all. Paved Ground Brocade is the seated body method with the right leg extended; Step Up to the Seven Stars is the forward-stepping method; Step Down and Ride the Tiger is the body method of watching above and below. These three small postures form one posture.

Posture 13: Swing Kick and Bombardment to the Head (当头炮) — two postures. The Swing Kick is a reversed-turning body method, warding off above and striking below; Bombardment to the Head protects the face and the heart. Combined, the above make thirteen postures in all, beginning with the civil image (文象) and ending with the martial image (武象).

The Diagram of the Wuji Image (无极象图)

The wuji (无极) is when not a single thing yet exists. Above the Great Beginning (太初), all was vast, dim, and undifferentiated, an unbroken chaos — this is what is called the Great Chaos (大混沌). When the learner steps onto the floor to practice the quan (拳), he stands upright and respectfully, closes the eyes, rests the qi, lets both hands hang down, keeps the stance upright, and sets both feet together; the heart and mind…

[Note ①: The original edition has both 招 and 昭; following the lecture texts on Front Zhaō and Rear Zhaō ("What is front zhaō?", "What is rear zhaō?"), 招 has been uniformly changed to 昭. Note ②: The original read "闷通背", clearly a typesetting error; 闷 is corrected to 闪. Note ③: For the 裆 of "fist to the crotch" and "the crotch must be open and round", the original often used 膪 or substituted 当; this edition uniformly corrects to 裆.]

Trang sách 96 · scan p129

[Figure] "Form of Wuji" (无极象形, Wújí xiàngxíng): a front-facing standing figure, eyes lowered, arms hanging naturally at the sides, enclosed in a large circle; dots run down the center line of the torso, with one dot at the lower dantian (丹田, dāntián) — showing the undifferentiated, primal qi (氣, qì) before all division.

Not one thing is attached to in the center, not one thought arises; solemn and majestic (穆穆皇皇), it is wholly like the great primal chaos — the image of Wuji (无極, wújí, the Ultimate of Nothingness). Therefore its form cannot be named; it is named "the Form of Wuji."

Diagram of Taiji (太極象圖)

[Figure] A front-facing standing figure, standing still and composed, arms hanging naturally at the sides, with no circle and no markings — showing the state of Taiji (太極, tàijí) as movement is about to arise but has not yet moved.

Taiji is born from Wuji. Might one say Taiji already has form and sound? The answer: not yet. Why is it not called "Wuji" but called "Taiji"? Although Taiji has as yet no form or sound, its first signs (幾朕, jīzhèn) have already sprouted — like the kernel of a great fruit: its living momentum is about to move yet has not moved, only its vitality is not yet sufficient, and it has not emerged beyond the shell. Otherwise, from what would heaven and earth be born? After the great primal chaos, although yin and yang were not yet divided, the mechanism of their division had already stirred; once the division came, the clear qi rose to become heaven and the turbid qi sank to become earth. But at this moment the clear qi has not yet risen and the turbid qi has not yet sunk — therefore it is called Taiji. The ancients, tracing back to the mechanism of yin-yang and the Five Phases (五行, wǔxíng) before heaven and earth were born, had no name for it and called it "Taiji" — yet in essence yin, yang and the Five Phases were already complete. When the boxer steps onto the floor, although hands and feet have not yet moved, within upright and respectful composure the mechanism of yin-yang's opening and closing, the reckoning of waning and waxing, emptying and filling, already resides within the heart and belly. At this moment the will is unified, the spirit concentrated, devoted wholly to reverence (敬, jìng); yin-yang's opening and closing, the messages of waning and waxing, are simply not yet given form. Since the moment cannot be named, it too is named "Taiji." These words are to show the learner: when first stepping onto the floor, first wash the heart and cleanse the thoughts, cast off deluded notions, calm the mind and settle the qi, and await movement — only after this can one learn the quan (拳, quán, the boxing).

Trang sách 97 · scan p130

Heading

Posture One: Jīngāng dǎo duì 金剛搗碓 (the Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Pestle), also called "Protecting-the-Heart Fist" (一名護心拳, yī míng hùxīn quán)

Upper text block (instructions for the upper body)

- The left wrist faces upward (左手腕朝上), aligned vertically with the nose-tip (與鼻準上下相照, yǔ bízhǔn shàngxià xiāng zhào).

- The left elbow sinks down (左肘沉下); it must be held up lightly and attentively (須得輕輕擎住注意).

- The heart-mind values equanimity (心貴和平); when the mind is calm the qì 氣 is still, and when the mind is calm the qì is harmonious.

- The left shoulder releases downward (左肩松下); it must not ride up (不可上架).

- The eyes do not look sideways (目不旁視); looking sideways scatters the mind and confuses the will (旁視則分心亂志).

- The spirit of the whole body is tied entirely to the crown of the head (周身精神，全繫于頂); therefore when the crown-force (頂勁 dǐngjìn) is drawn up, it should be as if present and yet not present (似有似無之間).

- The ears must not listen to anything (耳不可有所聽); by not listening the mind becomes concentrated (勿聽則心專).

- The neck is upright (項端直).

- The right shoulder releases (右肩松).

- The right elbow sinks down (右肘沉下); there must not be the slightest intention of raising it (不可稍有上架意).

- The right fist falls into the left palm (右拳落左掌中), five or six inches from the chest (去胸五六寸).

Lower text block (instructions for the lower body)

- All the qì returns to the dāntián 丹田 (氣皆歸于丹田).

- The left knee is slightly bent (左膝微屈); if it is not bent, the crotch (襠 dāng) does not open.

- The left foot and the right foot stand level and even, upright and flat on the ground (左足與右足并齊，端正平立). The acupoints Púcān 僕參, Dàzhōng 大鐘, Yǐnbái 隱白, and Dàdūn 大敦 all press the ground with slight force.

- The two sides beside the scrotum are called the crotch (腎囊兩旁謂之襠); it should be round and void (貴圓貴虛), never clenched (不可夾住).

- The right knee is slightly bent; if it is not bent, the crotch does not open.

- The waist-force (腰勁 yāojìn) should sink down and be firm and solid (腰勁貴下去，貴堅實).

Diagram

A line drawing of a standing practitioner: both feet together and flat, both knees slightly bent, the left palm on top supporting the right fist held five or six inches in front of the chest and abdomen; head and neck upright, gaze forward. Thin leader lines run from the text labels to the crown, both shoulders, elbows, wrists, waist, crotch, knees, feet, and the dāntián. The figure faces north with the back to the south, right side east and left side west.

Bottom explanatory note

In ordinary practice, adapt to the ground and the situation: one need not fix a direction, but one must keep to definite positions. Yet the North Star (北辰) and the Big Dipper (北斗) are both in the north; the learner should turn his mind toward them and receive the mechanism of Heaven, for only then does the central qì 中氣 of a person have its true master (真宰). Therefore the diagrams take facing north and back to south, right as east and left as west, as the fixed standard, to show the rule.

(Book title in the margin: Chén shì Tàijí quán túshuō 卷一. Printed page number: 97.)

Trang sách 98 · scan p131

Text (English translation)

The way of Heaven is established in yīn 陰 and yáng 陽; the way of Earth is established in the yielding 柔 and the hard 剛; the way of man is established in humaneness 仁 and righteousness 義. The feet should carry themselves with weight (足容重), the hands with respectfulness (手容恭), the head with uprightness (頭容直), the eyes with gravity (目容肅); sit like an impersonator at a sacrifice (坐如尸), stand as if fasting in reverence (立如齋).

Confucius said: "Do not look at what is contrary to lǐ 禮 (propriety); do not listen to what is contrary to lǐ; do not speak what is contrary to lǐ; do not move contrary to lǐ." When practicing the form, hold to reverence (執事敬) in what you undertake, and naturally the whole circle will accord with the compass (規) and every bend with the square (矩); and all your seeing, hearing, speaking, and moving will lie within the compass and square.

Before practicing the form, the mind is calm and the qì 氣 harmonious — a complete image of Tàijí 太極. As one is about to practice, the spirit is at ease and the qì still down to hands and feet; only then, when movement begins, can restlessness be dissolved and self-importance leveled, so that one exercises the natural mechanism of Heaven (天機) within our Tàijíquán and moves with composure right at the center of lǐ propriety (every movement of the form embodies the natural articulation of Heavenly principle 天理).

The Book of Rites (禮) says: the qi of indolence and arrogance must not enter the body. Once indolence and arrogance enter, the movements must overflow beyond the compass and square, and a hundred ailments will sprout.

Lǐ propriety is grounded in reverence 敬 (jìng); music 樂 is grounded in harmony 和 (hé). Only when one can be reverent and can be harmonious can one then learn to practice Tàijíquán.

Someone objects: Tàijíquán is but one art. By your words the rules you lay down are too strict — even sages in their labors go no further; for the tail end of a single art, why must it be so? I answer: it is not so. Practicing the form is also a study of cultivating the person (修身) and guarding the nature and life-destiny 性命. Mencius said: "Without compass and square, one cannot make circles and squares."

The way of practicing the form, from beginning to end, does not go beyond the single word "reverence" 敬. With reverence one concentrates mind and will, and naturally no wild swan (distraction, as in Mencius' archery story) circles to confuse the breast. As for the laws within the form, they are each detailed below.

Diagram

Diagram of Left-Yang and Right-Yin (左陽右陰圖)

Two concentric circles, an inner and an outer ring.

This is the diagram of hand-and-foot rotation in Posture One. The left hand belongs to yáng, the inner circle: this is the circle traced by the left hand and left foot. The right hand belongs to yīn, the outer circle: this is the circle traced by the right hand and right foot. They do not turn by turns; in fact they move together at once. The left hand is slightly earlier, the right slightly later; the left hand is inside, the right outside. In the initiating of the mechanism (機), yáng is first and yīn after; in the manner of the movement, yáng is inside and yīn outside.

This diagram is like the meaning of the mingling of yīn and yáng in the ten thousand things — the functioning of yīn and yáng at one end.

(Margin: Chén shì Tàijíquán túshuō 陳氏太極拳圖說. Printed page number: 98.)

Trang sách 99 · scan p132

(Margin title: Chén shì Tàijíquán túshuō 陳氏太極拳圖說 卷一 — Chen-style Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1)

Diagram of the Movement of the Qì Mechanism (運動氣機圖, yùndòng qìjī tú)

Mencius said: the will (志 zhì) is the commander of the qì 氣; the qì is what fills the body (體之充). The heart-mind (心 xīn) is like a general (將軍), the qì is like soldiers; once the general gives the order, the officers and men all obey. The clear qì 清氣 rises and moves to the hands; the turbid qì 濁氣 descends and moves to the feet; the qì moves all the way to the fingertips before it stops. The qì's rising and its descending seem like two separate segments, but in fact it is one qì threading through everything.

Diagram labels

- 心 (heart-mind; above it: 傳令 — transmits the order)

- 將軍 (general)

- 意 (intention, yì)

- 丹田 (dāntián)

- 會陰 (huìyīn, perineum)

- 清氣 (clear qì, rising to the hands)

- 左手 / 右手 (left hand / right hand, rising 上升)

- 濁氣 (turbid qì, descending to the feet)

- 左足 / 右足 (left foot / right foot, descending 下降)

- 全體之氣歸宿處 (the place where the qì of the whole body returns to lodge)

- 如兵馬屯處 (like the encampment of soldiers and horses)

The figure is a schematic human form: at the center a circle labeled 心 (heart) with 意 and 丹田 below it and 會陰 beneath that; curved lines rise from the center to the two hands (labeled 清氣, 上升) and descend to the two feet (labeled 濁氣, 下降), the feet marked as the lodging-place of the whole body's qì.

Diagram of the Silk-Reeling Force of Hands and Feet (手足纏絲勁圖, shǒuzú chánsī jìn tú)

Where the qì mechanism travels inside the upper arm (肱), it is all silk-reeling force (纏絲勁 chánsī jìn); in speaking of the hands the feet are already included.

Diagram labels

- 橫骨 (hénggǔ, the horizontal/pubic bone; pelvis)

- 尖襠圖 (diagram of the pointed vs. rounded crotch), with the note: 此是襠，貴圓，最忌尖 — "this is the crotch: value roundness, most avoid a point"

- （一）手背 (back of the hand)

- （二）手面 (palm side of the hand)

The left figure shows a leg/pelvis outline illustrating the rounded crotch; the right figure shows the arm and hand with the two coiling directions.

Bottom explanatory text

(1) In this shape the internal force (內勁 nèijìn) goes from the shoulder and arm out to the fingernails.

(2) In this shape the internal force goes from the finger-pads back in to the armpit and shoulder. The first diagram is outgoing force (出勁); the second diagram is incoming force (入勁).

(1) The force of the arm issues from the heart, moves to the shoulder, passes the elbow and reaches the fingers — this is the forward-coiling method (順纏法 shùn chán fǎ). From bone to skin and flesh, from shoulder to fingers: this is the issuing of essence (出精).

(2) From fingers to shoulder is the reverse-coiling method (倒纏法 dào chán fǎ). What is called the incoming of essence (入精) means drawing the opponent in, making him come near to me.

The force of both legs is led by the toes, coiling upward past the ankle, past the knee, to the thigh.

(Printed page number: 99.)

Trang sách 100 · scan p133

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated (陳氏太極拳圖說)

The root. The space between the roots of the two legs is called the kua (襠), that is, the huiyin point (會陰穴, huiyin xue). Move the heel of the foot, pressing the ground gradually reaching the toes — Tonggu (通谷), Dazhong (大鐘), Waifei (外腓) as well as Yinbai (隱白), Dadun (大敦), Lidui (厲兌) — firmly and solidly planted on the ground.

What is "Jin'gang Pounds the Pestle" (金剛搗碓, Jīn'gāng dǎoduì)? Jin'gang is the name of a deity. The "steel" (gang 鋼) is like refined gold, a hundred times smelted, hard and harder still; what the deity's hand holds is the demon-subduing pestle (降魔杵). "Pounding the pestle" (搗碓) is like grain in a mortar, pounded with a pestle. The right hand holds the fist like the demon-subduing pestle, the left hand is slightly bent like the mortar; this takes both its hardened heaviness and the fact that the two hands are gathered in one place to protect the heart — hence the name.

In practicing the form, the nose is the central dividing line: the left hand governs the left half of the body, the right hand the right half, and each foot moves following its own hand. The heart-mind and body must not force the qi (不可使氣); move gently and lightly, the hand leading the elbow, the elbow leading the arm. The qi in the hand merely leads up the hand and arm, no more; if it goes beyond, it becomes hard. When the upper body's hands move in a certain way, the lower body follows; upper and lower follow each other, and the middle naturally follows along with them — this is "one qi threading through" (一氣貫通, yī qì guàn tōng). When stepping onto the floor, one must stand upright and correct, the two hands hanging down, the two knees slightly bent; the kua jin (襠勁, kua energy) must be open and must be empty; only when the kua is open can the heart-qi then be aroused. First let the left hand lead up the left foot and step forward; then let the right hand lead up the right foot, the right hand from below circling a full turn upward around the outside of the left hand, the two hands interlocking like a turning ring for one circle; the right hand lands in the palm of the left hand, level with the heart, and together they halt. The right hand and right foot are all emptily, lightly enveloped (虛虛笼住); the left hand and left foot are all firmly, solidly planted on the ground, like earth settling into the earth. The Baihui point (百會穴, bǎihuì xué) leads the whole body: one must make the clear qi rise and the turbid qi descend. How does the clear qi rise? Only with a calm mind and stilled breath. The turbid qi must descend to the feet. Once a posture is complete, the clear qi of the upper body is all returned to the dantian (丹田, dāntián), for once the heart-qi sinks down, the qi of the entire body without exception sinks with it.

In Taijiquan, from beginning to end, this alone is the posture of the upright body (正身法): correct yet solemn, solid yet empty, soft yet hard; above, below, and on all four sides, whatever the opponent attempts, it is sufficient to respond to it — this is why it leads the multitude and stands at their head. The principle is real, the qi is empty; it revolves roundly and freely, vast and flowing, utterly without any mechanism of stagnation. Each time a posture completes, it returns again to the undivided atmosphere of a single Taiji (渾然一太極气象), with no trace to be found, no thread-end to be pointed at. Outwardly it seems to stop, yet within there is no interruption — this is why Taiji is Taiji.

The roots of the two thighs must open. The opening of the kua does not depend on size: even a breadth as slight as a single thread counts as open. For once the heart-intent opens, the kua opens at once. Those who cannot open the kua may spread their legs three feet wide and it is still not open. This lies in the student's careful discernment.

The way of practicing the form is nothing other than a circle. Circles may be straight or slanted, left or right, slow or fast, yin or yang, visible or invisible — all because of the posture presently being moved, cycling without end. For man receives the qi of yin and yang to be born; this body of ours is itself the body of Taiji. That the formless Taiji governs the visible Taiji, everyone knows. But to take the visible Taiji and make it carry out our formless Taiji, and instead to force and contrive it, not letting it be natural of its own accord — why is this? It is because one only knows to refine the qi, and does not know natural movement and rest. Furthermore —

*Translator's note: Pure text page, no diagram. Content: opening the kua (襠, the huiyin point), the meaning of the posture Jin'gang Pounds the Pestle (金剛搗碓, Jīn'gāng dǎoduì), the principle of "one qi threading through" (一氣貫通), opening the hips by intent rather than posture, and the fist way being only a circle.*

Trang sách 101 · scan p134

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated — Volume One

Like a man's eyes: open by day, closed by night — one opening, one closing, all the natural opening and closing of Taiji. In the form, one rise and one fall, one closing and one opening — how are these not all drawn from Taiji? People merely practice without perceiving it.

How could practicing the form not use qi? Without qi, how would the whole body move? But it uses the qi that is supremely great and supremely hard (至大至剛), nourishing it straight and without harm (以直養無害). People of the world do not know this and all consider it a soft art. They little realize that since one began training, through a thousand hammerings and a hundred refinings, hardness returns to softness and softness arrives again at hardness; the hard and the soft leave no visible trace. Viewed only from the outside, it appears soft, and so it is named soft — but is that really so? Moreover, "soft" is only spoken of in contrast to "hard." This art can be called neither soft nor hard; it can only be named Taiji. Taiji means that hardness and softness are both fully attained yet blended without trace. Its effort is abundant, therefore its achievement is certain. One must simply "be ever about it, but do not force it — do not forget it, do not help it grow" (勿忘，勿助長), and then one gains it.

From the first posture to the last, what is diagrammed is the visible form (有形之拳). Only by passing from the visible to the invisible, when the heart's mechanism enters the subtle, returning at last to no-mind (无心), may one then speak of the fist art. One can see that the fist art lies in my heart; when the heavenly mechanism in my heart flows, alive and brimming, then every place one touches is the fist — not like those in the world who take the fist to be merely the fist. This is an art that a whole lifetime cannot exhaust. It is not knowing that is hard; it is doing that is hard. The postures diagrammed are all the natural mechanism within Taiji. Qi and principle (理, lǐ): qi without principle has nothing to carry it; principle without qi has nothing to move it. Qi does not leave principle; principle does not leave qi. Principle and qi are one and yet two, two and yet one; their thousand changes and ten thousand transformations interweave without limit, so that a lifetime of practice cannot exhaust them. Let the student strive.

The heart is the master of the whole body; the kidneys are the source of life and nature (性命之原). One must purify the heart and lessen desires, cultivating the ground of the root, never letting it be harmed. When the root is firm, the branches and leaves flourish, and all things can be done — this is of utmost importance.

General Discussion (總論)

Pure yin without yang is a soft hand; pure yang without yin is a hard hand. One yin and nine yang is a root-end stick; two yin and eight yang is scattered-hand; three yin and seven yang still feels hard; four yin and six yang clearly shows a good hand. Only with five yin joined to five yang, yin and yang without bias, is one called a marvelous hand (妙手). The marvelous hand — every single move is a Taiji; emptily, its traces transform and return to nothingness.

For each posture of the form, often several thousand words cannot exhaust its subtlety; yet once it is demonstrated in person, it feels easy. What is difficult is the gongfu (工夫); what is especially difficult is long, sustained gongfu. The proverb says: "Do the form ten thousand times, and the spirit-principle appears of itself" (拳打万遍，神理自现). Truly so!

Taking the Image (取象)

The posture Jin'gang Pounds the Pestle (金剛搗碓) — yin and yang united in virtue; within the chest is a reservoir of the Great Harmony primal qi (太和元气), pervading the four limbs, supremely soft and supremely hard, truly possessing the virtues of Qian and Kun in their smoothness. When it is still —

Trang sách 102 · scan p135

What yin and yang store has no trace to be sought; when they move, they seem utterly soft yet are in fact utterly hard, seem utterly hard yet are in fact utterly soft — hardness and softness both complete. This is called yin and yang uniting in virtue (阴阳合德, yīnyáng hé dé), hence it draws on the trigrams qian and kun (乾坤).

Jingang Daodui 金刚捣碓 (Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Pestle)

First: Jingang Daodui gathers in the spirit (already concealing stillness without movement, the intent of the whole body as one undivided whole); above, below and on all four sides bending and stretching are harbored. Transformation without fixed method while still unlaunched (speaking of opening-closing, seizing-releasing); the undivided taiji is complete though there is no (fixed) body.

Second: A whole life free of affairs nourishing great harmony (太和, tàihé); brocade flowers in clustered abundance (a metaphor for the liveliness and interest of the quan). The Jingang of heaven carries a jade pestle, skilled at subduing the great demons of the human world.

Third: If it were not the Jingang's demon-subduing pestle, what demon or sprite would dare be stopped? Great opening and great closing return to tracelessness; only the beautiful, the great, the sage, the divine may be granted.

Fourth: Outwardly protecting the sovereign, inwardly protecting oneself — all depends on the true spirit of taiji. Having tasted through all the sweetness and bitterness herein, one is worthy to be called an unmatched person of today.

Fifth: First left then right is nothing remarkable; one movement, one stillness — it is a game of weiqi. When the surrounding play reaches where mountains end and waters are exhausted, suddenly one posture decides victor and vanquished.

Baihui 百会 (GV20, at the crown of the head), Yinbai 隐白 (SP1, at the big toe), Dadun 大敦 (LR1, at the second toe), Lidui 厉兑 (ST45, at the third toe), Qiaoyin 窍阴 (GB44, at the fourth toe), Zhiyin 至阴 (BL67, at the little toe), Tonggu 通谷, Dazhong 大钟 (both at the heel — all eight are acupoint names).

Trang sách 103 · scan p136

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated — Volume One

Second Posture: Grasp the Robe (揽擦衣, Lǎn cā yī)

The whole body joins its jin (合住劲) at once, and every joint and part of the body answers and joins with every other, as hand with foot, for instance. Each posture has its own host and guest (賓主): in this posture the right hand and right foot are the host, the left hand and left foot the guest. Every move rests wholly on the heart and chest. Using the heart-mind too much errs into constraint; not using it errs into slackness. It lies between having-mind and having-no-mind; only by governing everything with reverence (敬) can one attain the middle way and move the jin appropriately in every case.

The buttock bone turns up; the front kua joins closed, and the rear buttock naturally turns up.

The waist is the pivotal hinge of the greater body: it must be neither soft nor hard; striking the mean between them is what works.

The rear elbow forms the inner circle; its tip joins slightly forward with the left elbow.

The jin must not run counter; the spirit and expression must join — that is what works.

The shoulders press down and must not flip upward.

The neck stands vertical and must not tilt.

The top jin rises; the intention is like pushing through the sky with the crown — but do not use qi to excess.

The eyes fix on the middle finger of the front hand. It is the middle finger that serves [as the target], so one must look at it and must not glance aside, which would scatter and leave one without anchor. A man's whole body is used entirely through one heart, and the transmission of spirit resides wholly in the eyes; hence one must gather the spirit and fix the gaze. In this posture the right hand is host and the left hand guest; since the right hand initiates, the eye must look at it. The eye follows, pointing rightward as it travels; when the right hand halts, the gaze beforehand must fix on the middle fingernail of the right hand, and the pads of the five fingers must have their settling. At this moment the hand's movement — the hand seems stopped, yet the nimble qi of movement within it truly does not stop; if it stops, its breath is extinguished and it is cut off from the next posture. This is just like the ceaseless revolving of heaven's and earth's yin and yang: the qi grows ever more full the farther it goes, and when it has moved to the point of complete fullness, the next posture then issues forth. This is the meaning of "at yang's extreme, yin is born; at yin's extreme, yang is born" (陽極陰生、陰極陽生).

Notes around the diagram (one diagram):

The front hand first circles one full turn from the right ribs, moving around from the outside, extending until nine-tenths open and stopping. In between, the arm seems straight yet is not straight, seems bent yet is not bent; it rises and falls together with the rear hand, the middle finger being primary, joining its jin together with the rear hand.

The palm faces sideways.

The elbow tip sinks down, turning slightly outward one or two fen, bending slightly to join its jin with the rear elbow.

The chest relaxes open. When the chest relaxes, the whole body is at ease; there must be neither deliberate intent nor absent intent.

From Huagai (華蓋, huàgài point) to Shimen (石門, shímén point) must be empty — emptyly held, not letting crosswise qi lie crosswise in the chest.

The right knee joins its jin with the left knee; the waist bone joins together with the rear leg; the front leg is like a prop, the rear leg like a push.

This joining differs greatly from before; thus the stance holds its position steady.

Neither a T-stance nor an eight-stance ("ding bu ding, ba bu ba" — neither one thing nor the other), yet the rear toes should point slightly inward.

The feet are about one and a half chi apart.

The heels press firmly on the ground.

The five toes of each foot press with force to grip the ground.

As for this, the upper body sways [unclear].

The bend of the left leg must not go soft.

Labels on the diagram: 腰 (yāo — waist), 石门 (Shímén — the Shimen point), 膝 (xī — knee), 腰骨 (waist/hip bone), 腿骨 (leg bone), 裆要圆，圆则稳 (the kua must be round; round, then stable).

Trang sách 104 · scan p137

Diagram of the internal strength (neijin) as it travels from start to finish through Lan-Ca-Yi

This diagram is described with the practitioner facing north.

The left hand is in the west; as the right hand first begins to move, the left hand follows it. It first turns a small circle, then turns a large circle from below upward, sinks down to the left ribs and forks/holds at the waist. Most people use the right hand from childhood and use the left hand little, so the left hand is somewhat clumsier than the right. In practicing the quan, one should pay attention so that both hands move together and turn circles together; only then can the qi be gathered and settled.

In the middle it seems bent, like the shape of a new moon, winding with silk-reeling jin (chan sijin) outside the bones, between muscle and skin. The right hand uses the same method as the left; the right hand, right foot and left hand move in three places, while the left foot alone does not move.

The shoulder sinks down, the elbow tip presses forward to join, the back of the hand faces upward, the four fingers are in front of the ribs, the thumb behind the ribs.

- "At this point it moves forward, i.e., the east"

- Shoulder

- Turn

- "The left hand moves downward"

- Waist

- "The left hand arrives here"

- "Held fast here"

- Turn

- "The right hand sends out (forward)"

- "The left hand returns"

- Turn

- "The right hand returns"

Diagram labels (lower diagram — right-hand internal strength)

- "This is the diagram of the right hand's completed internal strength"

- Right shoulder / Right hand

- "Shoulder and upper arm join the force inward"

- "Start of the right foot" / "Stopping point of the right foot"

- North / South / Inner / Outer

- "The bend of the elbow faces north, the arm bent slightly three or four fen; do not let it scatter. The elbow tip faces south; the four fingers of the right hand gather the force inward, near in echo with the right shoulder, far in echo with the left hand. The internal strength seems to stop yet does not stop, led by the central (qi)."

Main text

What is Lan-Ca-Yi (揽擦衣, "Gathering and Brushing the Robe")? Lan means to gather as with the hand grasping a thing; ca means to brush close against; yi means the upper garment. The form is like gathering a thing with the left hand, close against the clothes. It means the left hand forks and holds at the waist, the elbow slightly joins forward, the thumb and the four fingers behind it spread apart, the hand slants down from the upper wrist, with the intention of pressing downward, the palm turned back, forking and holding at the waist. The left hand belongs to yang, while the bent elbow seems yin — this is called "yin concealing yang" (yin zhong cang yang, 阴中藏阳). The right hand, from the upper wrist, goes from above to the right, downward and around to the left in one circle, then upward, then slowly sends out to the right — the slower the better, no higher than the nose, no lower… (continues on next page).

(Page: 104)

Trang sách 105 · scan p138

(Continuing from the previous page) …no higher than the shoulder; the hand travels to nine-tenths and stops. The internal strength (neijin) goes neither forward nor back but travels along the center: if it falls behind it splits apart, if it rushes ahead it collapses — both miss the center. The strength is governed by the middle finger: when the strength of the middle finger arrives, the strength of all the other fingers arrives too. It all issues from the heart/mind, passes the breasts and crosses the armpit, enters the bone within the shoulder and upper arm, and from the bone marrow fills the muscles and skin, moving slowly along. When the strength has traveled to the pads of the fingers, then hand matches hand, elbow matches elbow, shoulder matches shoulder, knee matches knee, foot matches foot. When we speak of matching, both halves of the body, above and below, match together all at once; meanwhile the crotch strength in the middle opens open, and yet must also be held in the match. Thus the closing strength (he jin) resides within the opening strength (kai jin); it is not that opening is merely opening and closing merely closing, as though opening and closing were two separate strengths.

When the right hand moves, the right foot sweeps a circle, moving together with the right hand, starting and stopping together. As the right hand is about to halt, the heel of the right foot lands first, then from the instep to the five toes touch down in sequence, placed in a figure-eight stance (ba zi shi). Speaking of the two feet: the right foot in front is the host — the host within the guest; the left foot behind is the guest — the guest within the host, because the right foot remains behind as the left foot's stationary guardian. The right foot is empty (xu), the left foot full (shi): this is "empty in front, full behind" (qian xu hou shi). Speaking of a single foot, it too is empty in front and full behind. The toes and ball of the foot must claw the ground, while the Yongquan (涌泉, "Gushing Spring," sole point) must be empty; if it is not empty the toes will not grip the ground and strength cannot be applied — this is "full at front and rear, empty in the middle." The leg strength rises from the big toe, spirals inward from the outer ankle, travels diagonally upward past the Sanli point (三里), crosses the knee and passes the Xuehai point (血海, "Sea of Blood") up to the thigh root. The winding method of the left leg's internal strength is the same; both legs' strength winds upward together to the Huiyin point (會陰, "Meeting of Yin," the point between the two yin organs) and stops at the central seam between them. The two strengths meeting head-on is their settling point; here is where the leg strength comes home to roost. The waist strength descends slightly, down to the thigh root; spread open the crotch and the strength naturally becomes round. The waist must be empty — once it is empty, above and below are both agile.

Arms and thighs must all use silk-reeling winding strength (chan sijin); never go straight out and straight back — straightness lacks the sense of winding continuity and bending turns. Without that sense of continuity, not only do bending and extending lack force; even when crossing hands with an opponent one cannot adapt to circumstances and excel in turning and spiraling. If the pivot turns sluggishly, one is a notch behind in awareness — how then to gain victory? Even if hard force could beat someone, the other is unconvinced in his heart.

The diagram above has already been drawn and described most fully; but if the learner does not practice, nothing happens — as soon as he practices, his mind forgets it. Therefore I do not shrink from drawing it again and using many words to make it plain, wishing him to silently remember and understand in his heart, keeping it in mind without lapse. Winding from the shoulder outward to the middle fingernail is advancing strength (jinxing jin); winding from the middle finger across the back of the hand inward back to the shoulder is drawing strength (yin jin), drawing from far to near. At the initial issue use advancing strength; when gathering inward use retreating strength. In the lower body the leg strength runs from the toes to the thigh root, advancing and retreating both through the foot. Where its ascent differs from the arm's is a matter of one's own practice — certainly unalterable. Likewise, in crossing hands with an enemy: when the enemy comes to invade, first draw him in, then advance — this too is certainly unalterable. Remember: the right hand stops only after traveling nine-tenths, and the spirit-energy then fills to the full ten-tenths — this point is the hardest to put into form (continues on next page).

(Page: 105)

Trang sách 106 · scan p139

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated (page 106, Volume One, no diagram)

...capacity (容). From start to finish the movement must run slowly; slow as you can, keep it slow — slow to the fullness of ten-tenths gongfu, and you will be nimble to ten-tenths. Only when the nimbleness reaches the full ten-tenths of ripeness can the opponent fail to keep up with me, and will instead find my art strange — such is ordinary human feeling. Little do they know this is the effect of the work of "first the difficult" (先難之功). Again: the whole body is first slanted, then upright — slanted outside, upright within. The slant is its shape; the upright is its essence. With the central qi (中氣, zhōngqì) of the heart moving through the four limbs — this is a place others do not see and I alone know; one must constantly apprehend it spiritually, and with time it becomes clear of itself. When the hands and feet move, they must be gathered, not scattered: gathered, the spirit collects and congeals; scattered, the spirit disperses and grows sluggish. In short, the sense-organs and frame all take orders from the heart; once the heart is reverent and careful, the hands and feet naturally move according to the method.

The shoulders must press down, the elbows must sink down; the right hand leads the left hand, forked at the waist, the arms bent — this is this move's most essential configuration. The gaze of the eyes follows the right hand's movement: in this move the right hand gives the commands, so the eyes follow only the right hand; when the right hand arrives at its place, the gaze fixes at once on the right hand's fingernail; this middle finger is the standard the eyes look at. The bone seam of the shoulder must open. At first it will not open; do not force it open. If the gongfu has not arrived, when it seems to open and you think it has opened, in truth it has not; only after bitter, long-attacked practice will it open naturally — only then does it count as open. Once this place opens, all the arm's coming and going, bending and stretching, like wind blowing willows, with heaven's mechanism stirring, alive and brimming without any stagnating gear — all depends on this. This is the pivot of the arm's nimbleness; one must not fail to know it.

The right hand is level with the shoulder: not too low, not too high. If low, the central qi has difficulty moving through, perhaps failing to arrive; if high, the raised arm is without strength. In all cases, hitting the mean is prized. The top jin leads up (頂精 — the leading is the central qi of the heart lifting like something drawn up). Where does the top jin reside? At the Baihui point (百會穴, bǎihuì xué). Its intention need only lead slightly — "leading" means leading the spirit of the whole body so that it is neither biased nor leaning — and that suffices; it must not go too far, for then one is stretched and suspended above and stands unsteadily. This is the key point of the whole body, that through which the central qi passes; one must not fail to know it. The central qi connects above to the Baihui and below to the twenty-first vertebra. Once this place connects, above and below all connect; the body's whole qi-channels and bones connect, and there is naturally no defect of toppling or leaning. The two sinews behind the head are the aides of the central qi; the sinew-less space between those two sinews is the road along which the central qi circulates up and down. It descends through the middle of the spine, stopping at the twenty-first vertebra. Even the front-and-back Ren and Du mai (任督二脈, Rèn/Dū èrmài) are all aids to my central qi. The central qi is hardest to name, and even the road along which it travels is hardest to name. It is formless and soundless; without long gongfu one cannot know it. Thus "neither biased nor leaning" does not refer to bodily traces; it means the spirit naturally attaining the center. Even the central qi moved within the four limbs is merely a side-flow of this same central qi — there is not another central qi besides. When this place does not lean, the central qi in the four limbs all does not lean either. Though the shapes of the four limbs may take many leaning postures, the central qi flowing within the limbs is naturally unbiased. This meaning can only be apprehended spiritually and then understood. The neck must stand upright and correct, like a pillar in midstream (中流砥柱), neither forward nor back, neither left nor right, so as not to topple — then this posture is achieved. In the right hand's movement, the right is primary, because it responds to the opponent rightward. The right hand belongs to yin; what moves in it is yang within yin; and that by which it moves is that which governs it. The left hand makes a fist and bends; the left foot does not move; the turn...

Discusses slowness producing nimbleness, slanted-outside/upright-inside body method, opening the shoulder seam, the top jin at the Baihui point, the central qi's route from Baihui down the spine, the Ren/Du mai as aides to the central qi, and the upright neck.
