Trang sách 107 · scan p140

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Juan 1 (Volume 1)

Continued from previous page: ...to the right hand, which is the guest (賓), yet in truth it is the master within the guest, serving as the foundation that guards the whole body. The left hand belongs to Yang; its manner of movement resembles the yin within yang, and the reason it can be yin-within-yang is that there is something that governs it. In terms of principle, Yin and Yang take each other as their root and cannot be split into two severed halves. Consider the right arm: the right half of the body is all yin, and its internal jin (勁, intrinsic force) coils outward from the shoulder to the fingernails, then coils from the fingernails back inward. Yin and Yang appear to be two forces, but in fact they rise and fall simultaneously — this shows the marvel of Yin and Yang interbeing as mutual roots. How is this seen? When facing an opponent, if the opponent's hand comes in, I use my hand to draw it in; drawing and striking happen at once, not drawing first and striking afterward — this proves the reality of Yin and Yang as each other's root.

The way of quan (拳, the fist/boxing) is endless advance and retreat, with shen-qi (神氣, spirit and energy) threading through without the slightest interruption. I have seen people practicing quan: before the previous posture is finished they want to stop. Once they stop, the qi is broken and the spirit scattered. Or if not that, before this posture is finished they want to strike the next one; and by the time they strike it it is still unfinished, and they already want the one after that. With such an impatient mind, how can one carefully ponder and understand the rising and falling of the internal jin and the fullness of spirit? Those in a hurry always suffer this fault, and so they never succeed. In practicing quan, one must not only deeply ponder the principles within each posture: even the end of the previous posture and the beginning of the next — the place where bones join and mortise fits (接骨斗筍) — is the "transitional vein" (過脈). At the transitional vein one must consider how to keep the blood-vessels continuous so no gap arises. At the end of the previous posture, one must wait until shen-qi is completely full before it can be concluded. When concluding the previous posture, its form is complete and the surplus spirit flows beyond the boundary — this means the trigger of the next posture has already stirred at the end of the previous one, and then the next posture arises joined to the previous one: this is the "joint" (構). The joint means the mortise of the next posture connects and fuses with the mortise of the previous one. Not only must they be joined in form (勢); one should first join them in spirit. When the shen-qi has no gap with the previous posture, that is skillful beginning. As the saying goes, "in gaining the stance, contend for the incoming vein" (得勢爭來脈); once the incoming vein gains the stance, what follows is like splitting bamboo — no part fails to gain the stance. This is the most crucial point. The reason the above has been repeated three or four times, and rambled without stopping, is fear that the reader has not grasped its meaning.

Specially Marked: The Left Hand's Reverse Rotation (特標左手倒轉)

In the left hand's rotating circles — as in Lansao Yi (攬擦衣, Lǎn Zā Yī / Llam Tσα Yı, "Lazy Tying the Coat"), Lou Xi Ao Bu (摟膝拗步, Brush Knee and Twist Step), Chu Shou (初收, First Withdrawal), Zai Shou (再收, Second Withdrawal), Pishen Chui (披身捶, Wrap-body Fist), Zhou Di Kan Quan (肘底看拳, Watch the Fist Beneath the Elbow), Zhi Dang Chui (指襠捶, Fist to the Crotch), and Xia Bu Kua Hu (下步跨虎, Step Down and Straddle the Tiger) — all are methods of the reverse-rotating circle (倒轉圈). However, in Chu Shou, Zai Shou, and Zhou Di Kan Quan the left hand is near the top, so rotating the circle is somewhat easier; in Lansao Yi the left hand sits at the middle, level with Jiuwei (鳩尾, the sternum point) at the middle of the left ribs, so its rotation is rather difficult; in Zhi Dang Chui the rotation is very slight; in Xia Bu Kua Hu, the left hand's rotation is the same as in Lansao Yi. All the above are executed with the essence of chansijin (纏絲精, coiling-silk force), and being reverse circles they are difficult — though it is the same hand. The right hand's reverse rotation is very easy, which is why the left hand's reverse rotation is specially marked. As for the left hand turning rightward, one naturally finds it easy and there is no need to repeat it; study closely the earlier scattered diagrams and it will be self-evident.

Taking the Image (取象)

Take Lansao Yi as a posture: Yang on the left, yin on the right; Yang bends, yin extends; there is inner yang and outer...

Trang sách 108 · scan p141

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Continued from previous page: ...yin; inner strength with outer compliance (內健而外順) — therefore it takes the image from Tai (泰, the hexagram "Peace"): this is the image of the right hand. As for the left hand: first draw a small circle; then the left hand goes from above downward, to the left, upward and to the right, and down to the waist, bending like a circle — square outside and round inside — the hand propping at the waist, with the image of "the hidden dragon does not act" (潛龍勿用, from hexagram Qian 乾). Judging by the image formed from the left hand's own position, it is a still image. When stillness reaches its extreme it must move: this is the natural principle. Thus the left hand already conceals the trigger of "return after seven days" (七日來復, from hexagram Fu 復). The right foot moves with the right hand; the left foot does not move, in order to secure the root.

Four-Character Folk Verse (四言俚語)

One yin, one yang; the images of the law shine clear.

That which bends is yin; that which extends is yang.

Yin and yang in fivefold function — the Way of Heaven is stored therein.

When motion and stillness are without bias, that is your strength.

Seven-Character Folk Verse (七言俚語)

People of the world do not understand Lansao Yi (攬擦衣, "Lazy Tying the Coat"):

left bends, right extends, shaking the tiger's majesty.

Who knows the bending stored within the extending?

Rare are those who perceive the extending hidden within the bending.

The crotch divides and stands like the Sword Pavilion (劍閣 — taking its roundness);

the central peak above the head is like the Xuanji (璇璣 — a metaphor for zhongqi (中氣), central energy).

A thousand changes and ten thousand transformations move at my command;

the two feet of the lower body fix the foundation.

Trang sách 109 · scan p142

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 1

Third Posture: Dan Bian (第三勢 單鞭, "Single Whip")

Diagram: A single male figure in traditional woodcut line style, facing the reader, wearing a short buttoned jacket and loose trousers. The left arm extends up and outward, open palm facing outward at roughly head height; the right arm extends sideways with the fingers pinched together into the hook hand, wrist bent. Wide stance: left leg bent forward with knee over the foot; right leg straighter and extended, foot pointing north and hooked slightly northwest. The only label written on the body itself is 胸 ("chest"); all other annotations attach by leader lines to the eyes, neck, shoulders, elbows, hands, hips, knees, and feet.

Diagram annotations (translated):

1. The finger-pads exert strength.

2. The left hand's joints must not be limp.

3. The inner elbow is like a new moon, like a drawn bow — slightly bent.

4. The eyes fix on the left hand's middle finger. Ding jin (頂勁, crown energy) is led upward.

5. The neck stands erect.

6. Front and rear shoulders sink down; they must not be hunched up.

7. The right hand's joints must not be limp.

8. All the right fingers pinch together, gathered to one point; the front half spreads, the rear fingers bundle — this is the rear hand.

9. The left hand, in exhalation, must pair with the right hand.

10. The palm's outer front edge exerts force.

11. The rear of the palm and the thumb exert force.

12. The crosswise qi of chest and diaphragm should be discharged down to the soles of the feet; if that is not possible, it should at least be discharged to the dantian (丹田).

13. The right hand must respond and pair with the left hand.

14. Chest (label on torso).

15. The space between the arms forms a crescent shape.

16. The buttock bones tuck up slightly; the lower belly closes over the crotch, and then the Huantiao (環跳, hip) points rise naturally.

17. The front knee braces across; the front knee shows five or six tenths.

18. The left foot's five toes must grip the ground with force, the big toe especially.

19. The left foot is slightly more empty than the right foot.

20. The rear heel touches down first, gradually moving forward onto the left toes.

21. The crotch-step opens round, all closing inward — naturally mutual in attendance.

22. The right knee shows two or three tenths; it must not be limp, and must also brace.

23. The right foot points north, hooked slightly northwest.

24. Sink into the Yongquan (湧泉, "Bubbling Spring," sole point).

25. The great heel presses firmly on the ground, stable and secure.

26. The right foot must be solid — this is called "front empty, rear solid" (前虛後實).

27. The two great thighs wrap and close from the outside inward.

Bottom commentary:

Ding jin and zhongqi (中氣, central qi) are a stream of upright qi. The mind's intention leads it up, and it immediately travels to the crown of the head — the central qi is naturally led upward thereby. Nothing physical lifts it: it is purely the intention that does so.

In practicing quan (拳), the heart is the master. The spine is the pivot of the body's left and right; the waist is the pivot of the body's upper and lower. Above the waist the qi travels upward; below the waist it travels downward — like two opposing pulls, yet in truth one qi threads through, moving in parallel without conflict. Let the left hand lead the left foot and the right hand lead the right foot; however the hands move above, so the feet move below. Rising, they rise together; landing, they land together. Upper and lower follow each other, naturally in rhythm.

Trang sách 110 · scan p143

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

The skillful use of the hand lies wholly in the palm; the fingers lead the movement of the whole body, and the feet following the hands is especially crucial. The zhongqi (中氣, central energy) must travel slowly through the middle of the arm; do not rush or neglect it, but follow what is natural of itself. Then move according to what is natural, letting nothing lean to one side, and let the heart's qi travel through the middle of the two arms — this is the central qi. The back of the left hand faces north by one or two parts (fen 分), the back of the right hand faces north by four or five parts; when the central qi travels to the fingers, ten-tenths complete, all close together at once.

In ordinary practice of quan (拳) one need not rigidly fix the facing, but a fixed standard is drawn for the diagrams: the Northern Dipper is in the north, governing heaven's transformations, so north is taken as the primary orientation. Thus all the diagrams take facing north as the standard — right is east, left is west, facing north, back to south — thereby fixing the directions.

Diagram 1 (hand-movement diagram): a drawing of circles and lines tracing the hands' paths, labeled: the hand rises (手起); the arm relaxes and extends (舒肱); going down toward the west (下西行); left circle (左圈); turning with (以轉); left hand (左手); stopping together (並住); opening out (展開); the left hand's fingers (左手指); the left hand leaves the waist and travels upward (左手離腰上行); closing together (合住); small circle (小圈); first turns one (先轉一); this is not yet moving (此是未運行); the right hand moves (右手運); movement only like (行只如); the right hand stops (右手止); this is the arm's jin (此是胳膊勁); circle, turning fully one (圈，轉夠一); the back slightly forward (背微向前).

Text between diagrams: This diagram follows the one above in facing north; it is drawn with the right hand as primary. This is the left and right pairing together — the transitional vein (過脈) between the end of the previous posture and the beginning of the next.

Diagram 2 (upper-body movement diagram): labels: left shoulder (左肩); left hand spreads (左手展); left hand stops (左手止); this is the left hand's movement diagram (此左手運行圖); the left arm bends at the elbow and pairs with the right arm (左肱以肱彎與右肱合); in the middle the chest and belly join (中間有胸腹合); from the Tiantu point (天突穴, at the throat hollow) to the navel (臍); below, at Yinjiao (陰交), the qi...; ...Hai (海), Shimen (石門), Guan (關) [acupoint names along the midline of the abdomen]; like the form of bowing (如鞠躬形); this is called gou (鞲), holding-contained (含住); the chest is called contained (胸，謂含住); contained, the jin must be empty (勁要虛); the right arm reversed (右肱反); its back posture pairs with the left hand (背勢與左手相合); the line inside the circle is the right hand's point of departure (圈內線即右手發端); the right hand's departure first turns a small circle, the right hand gathers (右手發端先轉一小圈，右手束).

Text below diagram 2: This diagram is still drawn facing north, so left and right are as above.

Diagram of the Legs' Coiling Jin (足纏絲勁圖)

The great thigh coils from the foot up to the thigh root; its jin coils from the outside inward, coiling to the root of the leg; the two legs' jin brace against each other head-on, held fast like a gourd (瓠住), without using hard qi; the two knees close inward, and the five toes of the foot all point...

Diagram 3: Diagram of the Left and Right Thighs' Method of Joining (左右股合法圖) — a line drawing of the legs and knees in stance, labeled left knee (左膝) and right knee (右膝).

Trang sách 111 · scan p144

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated — Volume One (page 111, no diagram)

...joins inward; the legs above and below naturally join closed, and the kua opening (裆口) is of itself round.

The method of the feet's movement: the left foot first comes to the right foot's side, the toes touching down, then steps out toward the west; the two feet are about one and a half chi apart.

The method of twisting the heel: as in Single Whip (單鞭), the left foot first withdraws to the right foot's side, the toes touching the ground, then travels out toward the west — this need not be repeated. As for the right foot: in Grasp the Robe (揽擦衣) its five toes originally press the ground pointing northeast; when the left foot opens a step to the west, at the landing the toes point northwest. At the moment just before landing, the right foot does not leave the ground: the heel, pointing northeast, twists on the ground slightly toward north, northwest, and west — because it inclines toward the western side of north, hence the phrase. The right toes and the left toes come down together, pressing onto the ground. The left foot likewise lands heel first, gradually reaching the toes, joining its jin with the right foot, so that nothing scatters.

Question: What is Single Whip (單鞭, Dān biān)? Answer: The two hands are not in front of or behind the chest, but on the left and right of the ribs. The two arms spread open — the posture seems singly weak, yet its force is like the venom of a whip; and with the two arms spread it is again like a single whip, hence the name. In this posture the left hand is primary: the left hand rises level with the navel and turns a small circle from outside to inside; the right hand, from back to front, also turns a small circle; left and right join closed together, the spirit and qi answering each other like two people talking face to face. Then the left hand leads up from where they joined; the left half of the body turns from below to above toward the west, gradually traveling west until eight or nine tenths, and then stops. While the hand has not yet spread and not yet stopped, the gaze of the eyes follows the left hand; when the left hand stops, the gaze fixes on the middle finger of the left hand, without glancing sideways. As for the central qi's chansijin (缠法, coiling method) and Grasp the Robe: when the right arm joins with the kua, the left foot on the left first draws in to the right foot's side, the toes touching the ground, setting the position in advance for the lower foot's movement. When the movement begins, the left foot travels west together with the left hand; as the left hand above is about to stop, the left heel lands first, advancing step by step until the movement reaches the left great toe, stopping together with the left hand (the shape seems stopped, but the spirit is not stopped). Thus the left half of the body, above and below, follows along; the left foot extends according to the size of each person, in general within no more than two chi. As for the right hand when it joins: the right hand first turns one circle; as the left hand rises and issues, traveling west, the right wrist is behind, and the right hand turns again one circle (轉, pronounced shàng tone) from back to front, the arm slowly taking the reversed-back posture (反背勢). It is not like the left hand's spreading open with the flow: the moving hand not only reverses the arm's jin backward, but travels slightly eastward, and the back of the hand must again close forward. The right hand travels east while the left hand travels west — seemingly a posture of two divide, but in truth it lodges the spirit of two joining. The reason the hand takes the reversed-back form is that it plants a hidden thread (伏脈) for the next move. As for the right hand's five fingers being gathered and not spread: this is for fear that someone behind might suddenly seize the fingers and bend the joints backward; the eyes look to the front and cannot attend to the rear, so gathering them guards against this harm. The arm's coiling jin (缠劲) coils from back to front, ending at the right fingertips. Though the right foot does not move, it watches the right hand's movement and twists in front accordingly, the toes pointing northeast. Now...

*Translator's note: Page 111, no diagram. Covers the footwork and heel-twisting method connecting Grasp the Robe (揽擦衣) to Single Whip (單鞭, Dān biān), the question-and-answer on the name "Single Whip," the left hand as primary, the gaze following the middle finger, and the reversed-back right hand with gathered fingers guarding against a rear grab. The text breaks off mid-sentence at the page's end.*

Trang sách 112 · scan p145

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen Family Taijiquan)

Then as soon as the right hand moves, the right heel touches the ground, and the toes twist and settle pointing northwest (it says "northwest" because there is more north in it and less west); in this way the upper and lower of the right half of the body follow one another. In explaining the method for others, one cannot avoid analyzing it point by point, but one must not view the body as split into five fragments; when it comes to joining, the whole body must lock together as one — that is best. As for the joints of the whole body, such as the left and right elbows, left and right shoulders, and all places above and below, those with the same names each lock in place in the same way; no need to repeat this. If anything is unclear, look at the diagram and it will be self-evident; afterwards, imitate this body method, always keeping it upright, never leaning to one side. The joints are relaxed and open, the arms as if hanging from the shoulders; the movement seems soft but is actually hard, the spirit is stored within and not revealed — this is the highest level.

A boxer takes personal practice (躬行, gongxing) as primary; but it is difficult at first, and one must not expect quick gain. If idle thoughts fill the chest, the art of quan (拳) cannot advance. When skill is achieved, however the enemy comes, so you respond — without waiting for thought, there is naturally a method. At the moment of crossing hands, who knows from where the enemy comes! Who knows where the enemy will strike me! But wherever he relies on, use that very place (this is the "local scenery" [of the moment] — the hardest thing of all) — draw him in and strike; the timing always fits, and it is marvelously beyond comprehension — truly attained without thinking, hitting the mark without trying. But one who has not yet achieved this cannot do it. Ask: when may one be considered accomplished? Answer: the levels within this are more than a lifetime can exhaust; speaking only roughly of the present stage, great accomplishment takes nine years, small accomplishment seven. As for refinement and subtlety, it is learning that a lifetime cannot exhaust. Students who study one or two years, or three or four, stop after a shallow taste; they remain outsiders, never having tasted the flavor! Those who harbor a mind set on quick gain, I know, will never find the ford — for they cannot advance steadily step by step, just as one cannot make squares and circles without compass and square. In learning, perseverance is precious; personal practice is the hard part.

Seven-character verse on Dan Bian (單鞭, Single Whip)

Dan bian — this posture is the most powerful; a long snake in a single line, moving between west and east. Strike the head and the tail moves, spirit threading through; strike the tail and the head moves, the channels open. Strike the middle and both head and tail move; above, below, and the four sides lock like a drawn bow. If you ask the true secret within (that is, the guiding thread), you must seek it in the joints of the spine and back.

Verse of mixed long and short lines

An unmatched whip under heaven — strike in, unhurried. The enemy, seeing my left hand at my waist and my elbow bent, takes advantage of my unpreparedness, invades the western frontier, and insolently flaunts his strength. How could he know that when my bowstring is released, what is bent must extend — I strike him so that he has nowhere to hide, and instead meets disaster. It is no other extraordinary method, but relying on the whole body's empty nimbleness (空灵, kongling), a single thread of central qi (中气) rising with the momentum. No matter how he comes (this is what my long cultivation has prepared), seeking victory he meets defeat, not knowing his own measure (this speaks of the enemy coming to strike) — openly displaying his strengths and weaknesses before me. This is what is called "yin concealing yang" (bent is yin; the extended arm is yang).

The image taken by Dan Bian (單鞭取象)

In practicing quan, if the mind attaches to nothing at all, it is clearest; the chest contains everything…

Trang sách 113 · scan p146

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 1

Outside it is empty and open; within, refined and yielding outwardly — this has the image of the trigram Li (離) with its emptiness in the middle. The qi (气) returns to the dantian (丹田); the principle is solid, the qi empty; empty above, solid below — the image of Kan (坎), fullness in the middle. The four limbs are extended and open; the central qi (中气) circulates to full satisfaction, the momentum abundant — the image of Taiji. When the qi is full it is hard to shift, change is not easy — the image of Pi (否, stagnation). Yet the accomplished hand deals with it without anxiety: this is the great man dwelling in stagnation yet prospering. The second line changes and becomes Kan — the central qi is stored within; the fifth line changes and becomes Li — the empty nimbleness is contained within. Nourishing the firm central qi with the empty, nimble mind, what harm even in stagnation! This is what is called "in times of hardship, walk through hardship." Not greedy to strike people; respond as things come — hence the image of stagnation at rest. Possessing these four virtues, the art of quan (拳) is complete!

Lan ca yi (攬擦衣, Lazily Pulling the Robe) and Dan bian (單鞭, Single Whip) are the Two Polarities (兩儀). When the two polarities interact, the Four Images arise. From Taiji arises these two polarities; from then on generation is inexhaustible, the myriad images in their profusion cannot all be given shape — everything depends on the practitioner embodying it, pondering it carefully; with time one knows it oneself. That the quan is named Taiji — how could this be empty talk! It is truly the natural movement of heaven's mechanism, the natural opening and closing of yin and yang, not a thread forced or contrived. Whatever is forced violates the natural principle of Taiji and may not be called Taijiquan (太极拳).

Fourth Posture: Jin Gang Dao Dui (金剛搗碓, Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Mortar)

Here in the form this is the second Jin Gang Dao Dui; in the sequence of the form it is the fourth posture.

Correct Body Method (正身法)

In this quan, only this posture together with Dan bian, Lou Xi Ao Bu (摟膝拗步, Brush Knee and Twist Step), Ye Ma Fen Zong (野馬分鬃, Parting the Wild Horse's Mane), Dao Juan Hong (倒捲肱, Reverse Reeling Forearm), and Shang Bu Qi Xing Chui (上步七星捶, Step Up Seven Stars Fist) are Correct Body Methods facing east, west, south, and north.

Seven-character vernacular verse

The second Jin Gang faces west; the whole body turns (a "turn" is half a rotation), hands and feet in unison. Clearly distinguish empty and solid — you must remember this; do not let the horse's hooves fall into confusion.

Left empty, right solid — this sets the posture for Bai E Liang Chi (白鵝亮翅, White Goose Spreads Its Wings).

The subtlety within: do not sever the spirit-qi (神气); if the spirit-qi is unbroken, the blood channels naturally circulate.

The first Jin Gang Dao Dui faces north; this Jin Gang Dao Dui faces west. How does one facing north turn to face west? As in the previous posture Dan bian: the left hand is in the west, the right hand in the east. When the central qi has circulated to the full, seemingly stopping yet not stopping, the left hand leads, the intent seems to go upward; the right hand also leads, the intent seems to go downward; meanwhile both shoulders relax downward. As the shoulders relax, the left toes point north. In the previous posture the toes pointed due north; in this posture they point due west — because the drawing is not easy to make, I state it expressly; readers should know this and forgive it. As for the left and right feet…

[Diagram]: A line drawing of a man standing upright, feet shoulder-width apart, knees slightly bent, hands together in front of the lower abdomen (dantian), facing the viewer — the Correct Body Method (正身法) stance. No labels on the diagram.

Trang sách 114 · scan p147

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說)

This is not an "eight-character" foot shape. Now the toes rise slightly, the heel stays on the ground without leaving its place; give the big heel a twist (ning, 拧) toward the west so that the left toes turn around to point west. When the left foot has been drawn in from outside to the inside, at once the left hand circles downward from outside to inside in one arc, arriving at the chest; the hand cups itself like a mortar (碓臼), the wrist facing up, awaiting the right hand. At the moment the left heel twists (ning means to twist, to turn — like a boat turning its rudder), as the left hand first rises inward the right hand simultaneously launches, passing downward and across the right side of the body, stroking up from below (lü, 捋) to grip, then the fist drops down, landing on the Laogong point (劳宫) in the middle of the left wrist. When the left toes twist to the west, the face and body have turned to face west — thus the one facing north has turned to face west. Why strike another Jin Gang Dao Dui (金剛搗碓)? "When a person reaches the limit, he returns to the root" — like the first posture Jin Gang Dao Dui, this is the original image of Taiji. Since the momentum was exhausted at Dan bian (單鞭, Single Whip), it therefore returns to the original posture, so that the movement and transformation of the body in the following postures can arise; hence Jin Gang Dao Dui is used again to continue.

Seven-character vernacular verse

Already the Jin Gang posture has been set before; now it is repeated — could it be the same?

There, the face pointed straight to the north; here, the spine turns to the east.

It links above to Single Whip not without reason (exhaustion returns to the root; this is also the connecting vein between the preceding and following postures),

and opens below to the White Goose (白鵝亮翅), uncommonly mighty.

Whoever can grasp this method of turning the body will find within it the spirit and mechanism of transformation.

(No diagrams on this page.)

Trang sách 115 · scan p148

Volume 1 · Fifth posture: Bái é liàng chì 白鵝亮翅 (White Goose Spreads Its Wings)

Diagram annotations

- Dǐng jìn lǐng qǐ 頂勁領起 — the crown jin (leading energy at the crown of the head, dǐng jìn) is raised.

- Yǎn shén kàn zhù 眼神看住 — the gaze is fixed forward; bù kě páng shì 不可旁視, one must not look sideways.

- This posture takes the right hand as the anchor. First the right hand leads upward; the right foot draws a large circle toward the right side — that is, toward the north — and the right foot steps out to the right, no more than four or five cùn 寸 (about 13–17 cm).

- The right elbow sinks down; the right knee bends three or four cùn.

- The left elbow sinks down; the left knee likewise bends three or four cùn.

- The waist jin settles firmly (yāo jìn 塌住); the jin in the chest also seems to follow the right hand and left hand: first from the right, downward, then to the left, then upward, and around to the right in one large circle.

- The left hand follows, led up by the right hand; the left leg also draws a large circle. The left foot follows the right foot — one may say the right foot is three or four cùn away, its toes touching the ground; the two hands are about one chi 一尺 and one or two cùn apart. This is the hidden link (fú mài 伏脉, "concealed thread") between the movements.

Vertical text below the figure

The right foot arrives at the right side and plants on the ground; before the foot touches down, the foot circles once following the hand.

Carrying the movement through, the fresh form advances until it is beside the right foot, some two-three or three-four cùn from it. The five toes of the right foot press the ground — this completes the posture (chéng shì 成勢). Bái é liàng chì is the upper half of the posture; lóu xī 摟膝 (brush the knee) is the lower half; only when the two are joined does one whole posture form.

Horizontal text

This is the drawing jin (yǐn jìn 引勁).

The left hand rises and stops following the right hand. When the right hand rises, the left hand rises too; when the right hand circles, the left hand circles too; when the right hand stops, the left hand stops too — like a wife following her husband (fū chàng fù suí 夫唱婦隨).

The right hand, from in front of the right breast...

- Right-hand diagram: the left hand sets out · not pausing · pausing and...

Trang sách 116 · scan p149

*Chen shi Taiji quan tushuo 陳氏太極拳圖說 (Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan)* — continuation of Bái é liàng chì 白鵝亮翅 (White Goose Spreads Its Wings).

Main text

...seven or eight cùn 寸 from the breast, going down and then up toward the left, from left to right — the start and finish are set by the size of the step taken by the right foot. Upper and lower run together as one, and so each part watches over the other. The single line in the middle, where yin and yang, the two qì 氣, merge into one — that is the central qi (zhōng qì 中氣). It too sets out on its own, going down, then left, then up, then right, stopping only when its jin meets the right side head-on. Its jin takes the rising and stopping of the right hand as its own rising and stopping. This is exactly the Great Harmony primordial energy (tài hé yuán qì 太和元氣) circulating without a break. Look at this diagram and quietly absorb the meaning of how the internal jin runs.

Foot-movement diagram (足運行圖)

Around the circle: 循環 circulation · 陰陽 yin-yang · 五行 five phases · 心氣由右行 the heart-qi travels from the right · 心 heart. In each of the two lower foot circles is the character 發 fā "issue"; a line joins each circle to a drawn foot, showing the direction the force issues from the foot.

Foot movement

In Bái é liàng chì the right hand is host (zhǔ 主) and the left hand guest (bīn 賓); so too with the feet. The left foot moves rightward with the left hand; the right foot stands to the right and fixes the stance, then the left foot circles toward the right. In moving, the hands can move in unison but the feet cannot move in parallel — to try would be to fall. The toes of the foot touch the ground in an empty stance. The step opens to the right, moving rightward together with the jin of the right hand, and the toes of the right foot rest flat pointing to the northwest.

If we speak of striking (如以打人論)

If an opponent controls my right elbow, I lead it leftward with the elbow, then return and strike; if he controls my left hand, the left hand being on the left, I lead it rightward with the left hand and strike; if he controls both my hands, I lead both rightward in this posture. Here only the drawing jin (yǐn jìn 引勁) is shown — the striking has not yet been discussed.

This marks the boundary with the next posture: this posture ends where the drawing (引) ends. Learners are mostly impatient — before putting in the work they already love to strike. The principle is: wherever the opponent presses in, from there you draw and strike; no fixed frame confines it. I cite one or two examples merely as illustration. I do not speak of it lightly, fearing to stir impatience in learners so that they skip the groundwork — that is why it is not spoken of casually. The preceding diagram should be absorbed with quiet attention; do not dismiss it.

Meaning of the name

What is "White Goose Spreads Its Wings"? It is like the white goose spreading its feathers — a picture-shape (xiàng xíng 象形). The right hand leads the left hand, first descending to in front of the left ribs, seven or eight cùn from them, first completing one circle; then the left hand leads the right hand in a diagonal line, moving upward against the flow toward the right side, circling only a little more than half a circle — the shape of a goose spreading its wings, seeming to stop yet not stopped; the right foot travels rightward, also circling a little more than half a circle, the step being a bit more than one chǐ 尺. The toes of the right foot point north...

(Page 116)

Trang sách 117 · scan p150

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated — Volume One (page 117, no diagram; conclusion of the posture White Goose Shows Its Wings 白鵝亮翅)

...flatly and solidly on the ground; the left foot follows the right foot, arriving at its side, the toes touching and standing on the ground — also making the next open-step easy. The left foot is empty, the right foot solid; the chest in the middle faces north-west (explanation given earlier). The inner jin within the chest revolves like the primal qi of the Great Harmony (太和元气): first from the right downward, moving leftward in reverse and upward, turning from left to right a full circle, stopping together with the hands and feet. The right hand passes the head on the right, about a chi from the right ear; the left hand moves along with the right hand to in front of the nose, the left hand slanting upward, seven or eight cun from the face. The left and right hands are a bit more than a chi apart. The eyes fix on the two middle fingers. The crown leads, the neck is straight, the elbows sink, the shoulders press, the chest is held in, the knees bend, the kua opens; the left foot stands empty and inverted, the right foot flat on the ground. Though this posture is named one posture, it is in fact half a posture, for its quality is blunt (钝); it is the intent of "leading in to land on emptiness" (引进落空, yǐnjìn luòkōng), and it is entirely opening-intent with no joining-intent; it must combine with the next posture to make one whole, and only then does the overall position come to a halt.

Taking the image (取象): this posture carries the meaning of the hexagram Bi (比, Holding Together). The left hand moves along with the right hand, and the chest also turns its closing with the right hand — "holding together from within, soft, compliant, and centered" (比之自内，柔顺中正). The left foot moves along with the right foot; viewed together, there is the image of "holding together outward," and the good fortune of "bright holding-together" (显比之吉). This posture takes left following right, above and below alike; but it only leads and does not strike, gaining the softness of the lower trigram Kun and the centered hardness of the upper trigram Kan — hence it takes from Bi. Above and below follow each other; hardness comes and dwells beneath softness; movement and joyousness in following — hence it further takes from the hexagram Sui (隨, Following). "The noble man, as dusk falls, enters and rests" — to only lead and not strike is fitting.

Seven-character vernacular verses (七言俚语)

The first: In leisure, with nothing to do, I watch the white goose; its right wing spreads out in yet another wave. The two hands lead in and shift the peak posture — how unlike autumn waters issuing from the Tai'e sword (太阿)!

The second: Whence does the primal qi know the Great Harmony? The right hand wheels half a turn (辗 — 辗 is half of turning) and the two hands play the autumn snail. In the north the leading-in and the spirit-mechanism are complete — that the wing shines has ever been the white goose's way (a man's nourishing of the primal qi is like the goose crouching motionless, nourishing its spirit).

Former title, five-character quatrain: It is not the crescent moth-brow moon, yet rubbing it makes a perfect likeness (the two elbows bend, the chest like a bowing man); why should the bent bow not shoot? Once shot, it is doubly spirited.

*Translator's note: Page 117, no diagram; the conclusion of the posture White Goose Shows Its Wings (白鵝亮翅, Bái é liàng chì). Contains the final body-method description, the Yijing "Taking the Image" discussion (hexagrams Bi 比 and Sui 隨), and two seven-character vernacular verses plus a five-character quatrain on the posture's imagery.*

Trang sách 118 · scan p151

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated (page 118) — Sixth Posture: Brush Knee and Twist Step (搂膝拗步, Lǒu xī ào bù), with a body-method diagram (a standing human figure, each part connected by lines to marginal notes)

Diagram notes (pointing to the parts of the figure):

- Crown (頂): the top jin (頂勁) leads the whole body up by the central qi (中氣)

- Left shoulder (左肩): the left shoulder sinks down; do not hoist it up

- Right shoulder (右肩): the right shoulder sinks down; the eyes look toward the middle finger of the right hand

- Right hand (右手): the right hand spreads open, square outside and round within (外方內圓), the side-waist holds the hand, landing in front of the chest. The middle finger takes the nose-tip (鼻準) as its target; use chansijin (纏絲勢 — the coiling-silk jin, 缠丝劲) coiling from the shoulder to the hand; only when the central qi arrives at the middle fingertip is the movement complete. The right hand is four or five cun from the chest; hold the jin softly — do not retain the slightest hard qi

- Left hand (左手): the rear hand gathers the wrist upward, square outside and round within, using chansijin. The hand lands on the rear spine

- Waist (腰): the waist jin reaches down; the kua bone (尻骨, coccyx) lifts slightly, containing it from all four sides

- Left knee (左膝): the left knee spreads open, rising by chansijin; the lower abdomen naturally joins its jin closed

- Right knee (右膝): the right knee props outward and joins inward; the coiling method of chansijin is the same as for the left leg

- Feet (足): the left foot steps solid; the right foot steps solid

- Legs/kua (腿/襠): the kua bone's Huantiao (環跳) lifts up; the thigh roots inside spread open and the kua opens of itself; the two knees join closed and the kua is naturally round

- Chest and abdomen (胸腹): the chest and abdomen are broad and grand, joining closed forward, the central qi threading through, full spirit above and below — truly bearing the air of "a countenance at ease and a back full of abundance" (睟面盎背)

Main text: This posture faces due west, bearing the image of the Great Harmony primal qi (太和元气), and conforms with the upright positions of the four directions — Kan, Li, Dui, Xun, Qian, Kun, Kan, Li. The left elbow twists slightly into the reversed-back form; the rear hand gathers the wrist upward, square outside and round within, using chansijin. The hand lands on the rear spine. The left shoulder sinks down; do not hoist it up. The top jin leads the whole body up by the central qi. The right shoulder sinks down; the eyes look toward the middle finger of the right hand. The right hand spreads open, square outside and round within; the side-waist holds the hand, landing in front of the chest. The middle finger takes the nose-tip as its target; use chansijin coiling from the shoulder to the hand; only when the central qi arrives at the middle fingertip is the movement complete. The right hand is four or five cun from the chest; hold the jin softly — do not retain the slightest hard qi. The waist jin reaches down, the kua bone lifts slightly, containing it from all four sides. The left knee spreads open, rising by chansijin; the lower abdomen naturally joins its jin closed. Coiling — coil to the thigh root and join the jin closed. The left foot steps solid. The kua bone's Huantiao lifts up; the thigh roots inside spread open and the kua opens of itself; the two knees join closed and the kua is naturally round.

The chest is like a man bowing, bending slightly forward, bearing the image of containment from all four sides. With long practice, when the gongfu reaches this state, one naturally knows its spirit and expression. Even having arrived at that state, it can only be grasped by the mind and cannot be transmitted in words.

The chest and abdomen are broad and grand, joining closed forward, the central qi threading through, full spirit above and below — truly bearing the air of "a countenance at ease and a back full of abundance." With long practice, when the gongfu reaches this state, one naturally knows its spirit and expression. Even having arrived at that state, it can only be grasped by the mind and cannot be transmitted in words.

Explanation beside the diagram: for the left hand, for fear the reader might not see the shape it moves along its path and the circle it turns in reverse, it is also drawn in front. This makes people see that in truth the left hand is behind, at the waist — it must not be mistaken as being in front.

Citing the Lingshu, chapter "The Circulation of Defensive Qi" (《靈樞·衛氣行篇》): "The course of the defensive qi (衛氣, wèiqì): in one day and one night it makes fifty circuits of the body. By day it travels in the yang for twenty-five circuits;"

Trang sách 119 · scan p152

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated — Volume One (page 119, no diagram; continuing the Lingshu quotation from the previous page)

...by night it travels in the yin for twenty-five circuits. "At daybreak (平旦), when the yin is exhausted, the yang qi issues from the eyes; when the eyes open, the qi travels upward through the head (following Jingming 精明), then down the Foot Taiyang Bladder meridian (足太陽膀胱經), the Hand Taiyang Small Intestine meridian, the Foot Shaoyang Gallbladder meridian, the Hand Shaoyang Triple Burner meridian, the Foot Yangming Stomach meridian, and the Hand Yangming Large Intestine meridian — this is what is meant by governing the exterior for one day. By night it travels the Foot Shaoyin Kidney meridian, pours into the Hand Shaoyin Heart meridian, the Hand Taiyin Lung meridian, the Foot Jueyin Liver meridian, and the Foot Taiyin Spleen meridian, likewise making twenty-five circuits like the yang course, and then returns to join at the eyes. That which is called 'human qi arising at daybreak' is precisely this: traveling upward through the head and returning to join at the eyes." In each posture of the form, when the yang qi stirs once, it makes one circuit of the body; when reaching stillness, one stillness is one circuit of the body. That is, when one thought of the heart stirs, the yang qi immediately makes a circuit of the body; when one thought stills, the yin qi immediately makes a circuit of the body, without a moment's interruption. Considering the whole body: the back is yang, the chest is yin; the arms and backs of the hands are yang, the inner hand and elbow bends are yin; the face is yang, the back of the head is yin; the upper half of the body is yang, the lower half is yin; the knees are yang, the calves are yin; the left hand and left foot are yang, the right hand and right foot are yin; the top of the foot is yang, the sole is yin; qi is yang, blood is yin; the six bowels are yang, the five organs are yin. This is the discrimination of yin and yang among the sense-organs and frame.

Speaking of the central qi moving within: drawing-in jin (引勁) is yin, issuing jin (出勁) is yang; bending is yin, stretching is yang; opening a step is yang, withdrawing a step is yin; the toes touching down is yang within yin; the foot flat on the ground is yin within yang. Again: the back of the arm originally belongs to yang; in Grasp the Robe (揽擦衣) the jin is drawn from the right fingers up to the shoulder — the shape is yang, yet the jin is on the contrary yin; the belly of the arm originally belongs to yin, yet its jin is moved from the heart to the fingertips — the shape is yin, yet the jin is on the contrary yang; and they move together at once. This is what is called "yang hidden within yin, yin hidden within yang; one and yet two, two and yet one." That is, the arm's drawing-in jin is yin; with one turn it becomes yang jin — this is what is called "yang is rooted in yin" (陽根於陰). The jin of the arm's belly moving outward suddenly contracts — yin rooted in yang; yang hidden in yin, yin hidden in yang: this is what is called "they are roots of one another" (互為其根). Again, half-drawing and half-advancing, drawing while advancing, drawing that is advancing, taking drawing as advancing — yin and yang applied together at once: this is what is called "the Way goes forward in parallel without conflict" (道並行而不悖); without yin and yang united in virtue it could not be done. The heart's mechanism stirs and the hand arrives at once — nothing is faster than this.

As for the saying that the central qi returns to the dantian (丹田, dāntián): do not cling rigidly to it; it merely means making the qi descend to the lower abdomen below the navel. If one investigates finely: if the dantian is not the source of qi, why is returning to it alone spoken of? This is only a rough statement of the general idea. If one traces the source: the body's whole primal qi issues from the kidneys (腎); when kidney-water is sufficient, the qi is naturally strong. It is nourished in the stomach (胃); when the stomach receives nourishment, the qi is also strong; it is stored in the liver (肝) — when liver-qi stirs, rebellious qi springs up crosswise and the qi loses its balance; it is steeped in the heart (心) — when the heart has no idle thoughts the heart is calm, and where the heart is calm the qi is naturally harmonious with the lung (肺). The lung governs the voice; in truth it is sounded by the heart — where the heart's mechanism goes, even before the voice issues from the mouth the heart has already signified it. It is strengthened in the gallbladder (膽); the gallbladder knows no hesitation, and the qi follows its movement into the spleen (脾). This meridian has much qi and little blood; hearing a sound it stirs, and stirring, its transport-transformation does not cease — the moment the heart stirs, the spleen stirs too. It is aided by the large intestine (大腸), which has much qi and little blood and is the official of transmission...

*Translator's note: Page 119, no diagram, continuing the Lingshu "Circulation of Defensive Qi" quotation from the previous page. Content: the twelve meridians' day/night circulation, the yin–yang division of every body part, the yin–yang character of drawing versus issuing jin (with examples from Grasp the Robe 揽擦衣), yin and yang as mutual roots, and the beginning of the discussion that the body's primal qi issues from the kidneys and is nourished through stomach, liver, heart, lung, gallbladder, spleen, and large intestine. The text breaks off mid-sentence at the page's end.*

Trang sách 120 · scan p153

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated (page 120, no diagram; continuing the discussion of the organs from the previous page, then the sixth posture Brush Knee and Twist Step 搂膝拗步)

...official [of transmission]; and further aided by the small intestine (小腸), which in front lies above the navel and behind assists the spine; the dregs and filth are not retained — the turbid qi departs and the clear qi arrives. All the meridians above are of benefit to the fist art, hence they are mentioned. If one speaks solely of the kidneys: the kidney is the official of strength and skill (作強之官), from which craft and ingenuity issue. This meridian has little blood and much qi; it stores essence in the will (藏精於志) — the abode of essence and spirit, the root of nature and life (性命之根). Man has two kidneys, each with two attachments: one attaches to the heart, one travels upward to connect with the brain. Where qi is generated truly begins here, and its final homecoming must return to this. As for the mingmen (命門, Gate of Life): it is in truth the gate through which the qi between the two kidneys goes out and in, hence it is called the Gate of Life. When the fist art speaks of the kidneys, it speaks of the source of going-out and coming-in, so there is no need to speak again of the mingmen.

The previous posture, White Goose Shows Its Wings (白鵝亮翅), has both hands raised and above. When the inner jin has moved to complete fullness, the two hands immediately part level and descend; the right foot twists to point due west, and the left foot opens a great step leftward, more than two chi, the toes likewise pointing due west and pressing solid. While parting level and descending, the right hand brushes past the right knee, traveling from below upward to the right, turning left, turning one great circle slantwise upward to face the nose; the left hand brushes past the left knee, traveling downward, then upward from the left, and again turns down and back, completing one circle — this is reversed-back jin (反背勁) — the hand landing at the middle of the rear spine, level with the waist, and halting; the right hand likewise turns one circle in reverse, the right and left hands moving together and stopping together. Though the right hand lands in front of the chest and the left hand lands behind the body, with abdomen and back between them like a mountain range apart, when the right hand calls (一呼) the left hand answers (一應), and the spirit of front and back naturally threads and joins. The right hand is in the west — west is Kan's (坎) upright position; a man's qi and blood circulate like Kan-water flowing around without gap. The right hand in front guards the front so that no one above or below dares intrude; the left hand is in the east, guarding the back, so no one dares intrude suddenly from behind. The reason is this: the east belongs to Li (離), and Li is empty within (離中虛). For the practitioner of the form, the left hand behind is supremely empty and supremely nimble; if anything touches it, it knows at once and can respond according to the occasion. Not only the hand — the back and the whole body are all empty and nimble; thus the left hand behind is not without anchor. The left hand behind gathers its fingers, for fear someone might seize the fingers and snap the joints backward; it gathers them to guard against harm. The left elbow is in the south. South is Qian's (乾) upright position. Qian is vigor (健); like Qian, centered and hard, awesome, none dare offend. The left elbow resides in the south, the right hand in the north. North is Kun's (坤) upright position. Kun is compliance (順); according with principle and following the situation, it responds to the ten thousand things. The Book of Changes says: "Beauty dwells within, and flows freely through the four limbs, issuing into affairs — this is beauty at its utmost" (美在其中，而暢於四肢，發於事業，美之至也). The right elbow has deeply attained this meaning. Though a strong enemy be in the north, what is there to fear? As for the head and ears: the ears can hear the sound of the enemy's approach, the eyes can see the enemy's expression as he issues, the head can make contact front, back, left, and right, and moreover the two hands can rise to assist it — this is not unworthy of Qian's image as head and as roundness. As for the feet: if he comes from the left, sway left; from the right, sway right; kick to defend the front, stamp to defend the rear. The foot belongs to Zhen (震); Zhen is thunder — one lifting of the foot is like sudden thunder that cannot be covered by the ear. Of all enemies invading my lower body, the foot's contribution is the greatest — great indeed is the foot's use! The proverb calls this posture "Six Sealing and Four Closing" (六封四閉) — truly so! The six sealings: above, below, and the four sides are all sealed, leaving no door to enter; the four closings: left, right, front, and back are strictly closed, leaving no...
