Trang sách 121 · scan p154

*(Pure text page, no diagrams — continuation of the discussion of Brush Knee and Twist Step (搂膝拗步, lōu xī ào bù) following White Goose Shows Its Wings (白鹅亮翅, bái é liàng chì).)*

…(the hands) joined can strike; no matter whether the opponent comes empty, comes full, comes from an oblique angle (meaning the enemy advances with a slanted form), or comes straight on (meaning the enemy advances with an upright form) — in every case there is nothing to fear.

The previous posture, White Goose Shows Its Wings, is purely opening energy (开劲, kāi jìn); the present posture, Brush Knee and Twist Step, is purely closing energy (合劲, hé jìn). The previous posture is the first half of the following posture, and the following posture is the second half of the previous posture: one opening and one closing, together forming a single posture.

The former, White Goose Shows Its Wings, is movement; where it halts is stillness — that is stillness within movement. The latter, Brush Knee and Twist Step, is movement; where it halts is stillness. Viewed together they are one movement and one stillness. The former stillness is the end of the first half-posture, and is the "guest"; the latter stillness closes the whole posture, and so is the "host." The upper move stores power for the lower move — it is purely opening, hence the guest. The lower move immediately receives the upper move: after circling once left and right, the whole body closes together in one piece; upper and lower moves are connected in a single breath of energy, with no interruption of spirit and qi. In the upper move — the movement and stillness within opening — one is temporarily the guest, and the qi still rests in the four limbs; one must wait until the following posture closes, and only then does the qi return to the dantian (丹田, dān tián). This is what is meant by "movement generates yang, stillness generates yin; one movement and one stillness are the root of each other."

This posture takes the four trigrams Qian, Kun, Kan, and Li (乾坤坎离).

Their positions have already been discussed above. This posture obtains the upright qi of Qian and Kun to circulate through the whole body: soft outside, firm within, truly in accord with the strength of Qian and the compliance of Kun. The heart belongs to Li, the kidneys to Kan. The heart's empty clarity is like the emptiness in the center of Li; the fullness of qi is like the fullness in the center of Kan. Qian and Kun, through their central lines interacting, transform into Kan and Li; heart and kidneys exchange their qi, not harming but aiding one another — thus returning to the root (Qian gives its middle line to Kun, obtaining Kun's middle line and becoming Li; Kun gives its middle line to Qian, obtaining Qian's middle line and becoming Kan; returning to the root they again become Qian and Kun, their original unexchanged forms). Now, since "Heaven-one generates water, earth-two generates fire," one might say water comes before fire; yet although the will (志, zhì) is stored in the kidneys, it is actually issued from the heart. When the heart's mechanism stirs, the intent first exists in the heart; then the will, as commander, leads the true yang of the gate of life (命门, mìng mén), with the true yin following. Thus the will is the dwelling, the qi, and the commander of the heart. In this move, when yang qi should be aroused, once the heart's intent is issued, the will immediately leads the true yang of the gate of life, passing from the kidneys through the sea of qi (气海, qì hǎi) to the heart and circulating to the four limbs. When movement reaches its extreme and stillness arises, once the heart's intent grows still, the will leads the true yin of the gate of life back from the kidneys to the sea of qi — this is speaking from the standpoint of function. Therefore Li comes before Kan: Li is the master, Kan the function. Moreover, before Qian and Kun were split apart, yin and yang lay mingled together, substance and function both complete: its use should move when it ought to move and be still when it ought to be still — one must not cling or stagnate. If this move is discussed together with the previous one, it is a closing; closing means gathering the spirit of the four limbs, not merely the completed form. As to the completed form, the right hand is in front, the left hand behind, and the left and right feet are several feet apart — so it seems one cannot call it simply a closing. Yet what closes is its form and spirit; what does not close is the placement of the four limbs. Without this, the closing of the next posture would have no "incoming dragon" (source of momentum)! And if it is named a closing, without opening how could there be a closing? This is called "opening within closing" — the closing being the closing within opening — and so the following connecting posture continues from this one. If the posture were set up for the sake of the next move, yet truly is not for the sake of…

Trang sách 122 · scan p155

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chen Style Taiji Quan Illustrated Explanation)

The next posture is set up in advance. After White Goose Spreads Wings (白鵝亮翅, Bai'e Liang Chi), the two hands divide evenly, whether inclining forward or backward; where the feet should open, they open; where the stance should be wide, it is wide. This change of form and the positions of hands and feet seem as if they ought to be so, but in fact they are the operation of the natural mechanism of Heaven (tianji ziran, 天機自然), not something human force can make. A person merely follows the natural mechanism and moves accordingly. At the moment of closing (he, 合), the qi (氣) must return to the dantian (丹田); when movement reaches its extreme, stillness is naturally due. The principle of the taiji cycle cannot be forced or altered — just as when a person's strength is spent, rest is due; where the momentum leads, it cannot be otherwise. This is what is meant by "one opening, one closing — nothing but what is natural." All the forms that follow likewise follow the natural mechanism, opening and closing, only the forms of opening and closing differ in each case.

Although in practicing the form one must not first ask how each posture strikes an opponent — for unknown to most, every posture in the form can strike in all four directions and eight angles; it is only that one cannot yet do so before the moment arrives. What I have always meant by quan (拳, fist/boxing) is "to weigh" (quan, 權): thereby one weighs things and knows their lightness or heaviness. When the moment of application comes, my whole body is the fist. I weigh the lightness or heaviness of what comes at me and respond with measure, not confined to any direction, not concerned whether the incoming force is great or small. There is nothing in what the opponent brings that is "wrong"; the only question is whether my own foundation is prepared or not, whether my gongfu (功夫) is accomplished or not.

Practicing the form is fundamentally preparing the body methods (shenfa, 身法). The body methods include upright and slanted, straight and bent, compliant and reversed, inclining forward or backward, left or right, upward or downward; sometimes sitting on the ground, sometimes flying in the air, sometimes gathered in, sometimes dispersed — body methods beyond enumeration. But all must be moved by the central qi (zhongqi, 中氣). Even the most inverted, most crooked body method is threaded through by the central qi. If at this point you apprehend it with the mind (yi, 意), you will know yourself that Brush Knee and Twisting Step (摟膝拗步, Lou Xi Ao Bu) is an upright body method, while the previous posture was a right-inclining body method. One need only seek the principle within the present body method, and it is obtained.

As for the movements of hands and feet, they are nothing outside a circle (quan, 圈); there is absolutely no coming straight or going straight. Circles may be upright or slanted, compliant or inverted; the various methods of turning also differ. One should turn according to what is natural to each posture. For example, the previous posture White Goose Spreads Wings is a forward (shun) turn; Brush Knee and Twisting Step is an inverted (dao) turn. Raise one to infer three — this is shown as the ford and bridge (jinliang, 津梁).

Chang Duan Ju Li Yu (長短句俚語, Colloquial Verses of Long and Short Lines)

The quan shi (拳势) fundamentally has no fixed direction; no need for great opening and great closing — like the next posture, the slanting twisting step (xie xing ao bu, 斜行拗步), in the same stance. The right hand is in the northwest, the left hand in the southeast, the left foot in the southwest, the right foot in the northeast; in the middle, the kua (裆, crotch) opens wide, the arms extend, one twisting step and only then is it settled. I too have weighed it carefully: the two hands divide evenly, brushing the knee together; the right hand, like a side lattice, descends to the chest; the left hand hides behind the back; both feet aligned neatly facing west — the banner orderly, the ranks dignified. Even from this posture one may see in self and other a shining of auspicious light. Why insist on clinging to the ancient forms or only following the modern forms to be strong? I once heard that of the marvelous arts of sword-dancing, none surpasses Sister Sun (referring to Gongsun Da Niang, 公孫大娘). How could the mysterious and subtle have a constant form? Only follow what is good — nothing is hindered thereby. This is what is called "variation without fixed direction, finding the central path" — this is the ford and bridge.

You (又, Again)

"Out of the kidneys, into the kidneys" (chu shen ru shen, 出腎入腎) is the true secret (zhen jue, 真訣); the reeling-silk force (chansijin, 纏絲勁) requires that the body's stance be set upright and not swaying —

Trang sách 123 · scan p156

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo*, Juan Yi (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一, Book One)

The feet stand firm, the kua (裆, crotch) opens round, the hard qi (硬氣) softens below. Not one element lacking, the original qi (yuanqi, 元氣) not escaping — like the two halves of a tally meeting (ruo he fu jie, 若合符節).

Qi Yan Li Yu (七言俚語, Colloquial Verses in Seven-Character Lines)

Qi Yi (其一, The First)

两手平分两足开，右前左后护胸怀。

中间只要身法正，何怕周围一齐摧！

The two hands divide evenly, the two feet open; right in front, left behind, guarding the chest. In the middle, only the body method (shenfa, 身法) need be upright — then why fear the surroundings crushing in all at once?

"Dividing evenly" (ping fen, 平分) means the two hands divide evenly downward from the renzhong (人中, the philtrum); "the two feet open" means the left foot opens into a step, and with one twist of the right heel the body method becomes upright. "Upright" (zheng, 正) means the body's stance is square and correct, leaning toward nothing, containing the insubstantial spirit (xuling, 虛靈) within — hence one does not fear being pushed over by another. This speaks of "preparedness averts peril" (you bei wu huan, 有備無患).

Qi Er (其二, The Second)

太和元气运周身，普护两膝前后心。

眼神看住中指甲，四面八方任人侵。

The qi of Grand Harmony (taihe yuanqi, 太和元氣) circulates through the whole body, universally guarding the two knees and the centers before and behind; the eyes' spirit (yanshen, 眼神) fixes on the middle fingernail — the four sides and eight directions may be invaded at will [by the opponent, who can do nothing].

Qi San (其三, The Third)

自从白鹅展翅开，扣合周身护官骸。

只有一点真命脉，无妨出入任君裁。

Ever since White Goose Spreads Wings (白鵝展翅, Bai'e Zhan Chi) opened, closing and uniting the whole body guards the organs and frame. There is only one point of the true life-artery (zhen mingmai, 真命脈) — its going out and coming in may be judged freely, without hindrance.

The "life-artery" (mingmai, 命脈) is the kidneys (shen, 腎), from which the central qi (zhongqi, 中氣) originates. In movement it goes out, in stillness it enters; it has fixity yet no fixity — meaning that because the posture is constantly changing, the changes of the two qi, yin and yang (陰陽二氣), are likewise without fixity.

Qi Si (其四, The Fourth)

一气旋转自无停，乾坤正气运鸿濛。

学到有形归无迹，方知玄妙在天工。

One qi revolves, never stopping of itself; the upright qi of qian and kun (乾坤, Heaven and Earth) moves through the primordial vastness (hongmeng, 鴻濛). When your study reaches the point where the formed returns to the traceless, only then do you know the mystery lies in the work of Nature (tiangong, 天工).

Trang sách 124 · scan p157

Seventh Posture: First Close (第七势 初收, dì qī shì — chū shōu)

Central figure: a practitioner in a half-seated (squatting) posture, left hand wrapped behind the back, right hand raised beside the right ear; leader lines connect the annotations to each body part.

Figure labels (by body part):

- (Eyes) The eyes watch the finger pads (眼看住指肚).

- The top energy (顶劲, dǐng jìn) must be raised all the more, the body held in: make the spirit of the whole body collect in one place, allowing no scattering; if it scatters, the body method grows too small to resist the enemy; if gathered, front, back, left and right can all be protected. Moreover, since it is named "close/collection" (收, shōu), it is the momentum of drawing the opponent to advance.

- The left elbow sinks down; the left hand whips up beside the left cheek; the left armpit is braced open, not pressed against the left ribs; the back of the hand faces outward, the wrist turned inward.

- The more the right elbow sinks down the better — it must not be clamped; it moves toward the center of the right chest. The right hand lands before the center of the right chest, seven or eight inches from the chest, back of the hand facing out; the two hands are about a foot apart.

- The right knee is bent, toes pointing west; the crotch must be opened round; the 【?】 closes forward; the waist energy sinks down and must not go limp.

- The left knee is bent: brace the energy open and close it inward; do not use stiff qi, but do not be too soft either — just hit the middle.

- The left foot draws in beside the right foot, the two feet four or five inches apart, the toes touching the ground — this is the empty foot, a hidden seed (伏脉, fú mài) for the following posture.

Movement instructions at the bottom:

The left hand, behind the back, unfolds the hand that was wrapped in; press the fingers tight, likewise turn left and forward through a full circle, and land in front of the left chest, back of the hand facing out, facing the right hand as if opposite it, only slightly offset in height — left hand high, right hand low.

The right hand leads upward from in front of the chest, turns left and across to the right side, the elbow sinking; the fingers are beside the right ear and whip upward, slightly slanted toward the face, the palm about a foot from mouth and cheek.

Small diagram labels (lower right): right hand / left hand / right hand starting point / issue (发) / turn from left / toward right.

Trang sách 125 · scan p158

*(Continuation of the Seventh Posture, First Close (初收, chū shōu). Upper right: two schematic foot drawings — the left foot with toes touching the ground, the empty stance; the right foot flat on the ground, the solid foot.)*

The left foot's toes touch the ground: this is the empty stance (虚步, xū bù), a hidden seed for the start of the following posture. The right foot rests flat on the ground: this is the solid foot (实脚, shí jiǎo).

"First Close" (初收) is so named to distinguish it from the "Second Close." In the previous posture the right hand is in front and the left hand behind — scattered and unordered. Here the two hands are gathered in front of the chest: the bodily frame is collected into one place and the spirit gathers together without scattering, hence it is named "close/collection" (收); it is also the connecting link that receives what is above and opens what is below. The right hand goes from below upward along the left, turns a circle from left to right, and when it lands the hand is slanted inward and turned upward. If this man attacks me with his forearm, I meet it with my right hand; when the two hands and two forearms make contact, I use my right hand to lead in his right hand, drawing it close to my body. If I do not do this, he will not advance and I cannot strike him. The right foot lifts, turns a small circle to the right, shifts three or four inches toward the center, and comes down flat and solid on the ground. As soon as the right hand begins to move, the left hand follows it and moves too: the left hand goes from behind upward along the left, turns a circle from left to right, and lands outside the left chest, seven or eight inches from the left nipple and about a foot from the right hand, and stops. The left fingers slant toward the right shoulder, the back of the hand also facing outward — thus the left hand follows the right hand. This is leading in his right forearm, letting him advance close to my body to strike me; only when he believes he has the advantage will he step in to attack. I use both hands to lead him in and make him fall into emptiness — this is the winning calculation of "to take, first give" (欲取先与). As for the left foot, it is collected on the right side with the toes touching the ground, an excellent ambush: not only can an upward kick strike the opponent, it also sets up the connection to the next posture most advantageously. Though the form of First Close is small, its breadth of spirit is vast, like the open sky and wide earth, beyond measure. Moreover, the top energy leads upward, the turbid qi descends, and the central qi (中气, zhōng qì) is stored and enters the dantian: though the shape is small, the bearing of qi is grand and unimpeded — a posture hard for others to fathom. Speaking of the present, it is stillness within movement; speaking of what is to come, it is also movement within stillness. Do not despise it because the form shrinks like a hedgehog.

Four-character folk verse (四言俚语)

The image of First Close: great qi spiraling. Like a cat pouncing on a rat, it folds its fur and feathers; like a tiger biting a man, it first binds its body; like a lion wrestling an elephant, the whole body's spirit (全休【?】— likely 全副). Do not, because the form is small, think it shriveled like a hedgehog. One spirit, numinous and subtle: heart, eyes and hands follow one another; release the moment release is called for — hard to withstand its keen edge. From of old the hunched caterpillar has never failed to stretch; I know its intent but can hardly depict its spirit. Wait for a master hand to trace its truth in full; this drawing is lifeless and cannot transmit it to others.

Seven-character folk verse on the above (前题七言俚语)

The whole body shrinks like pure yin; within the yin, yang hides, letting others invade. Slowly, slowly leading in — none perceive it; little by little lingering, the intent deepens of itself. Right solid, left empty, concealing the 【?】 strike; lifting above and striking below, embodying release and capture (纵擒, zòng qín). If one can truly grasp the principle within, with an empty, masterful hand one crowns the present age.

Five-character folk verse (五言俚语)

Writing prizes stored momentum; practicing the fist is just the same. If one truly wishes to win over others in the end, first, from within defeat… (continues on the next page)

Trang sách 126 · scan p159

(continuing from the previous page) ………reached/consummate.

Seven-character vernacular verse

To close from openness (the previous posture is opening) and gather (this posture is gathering) well, the only way is to keep the circles drawn by the two hands small. One gathering instantly shows the spirit collected; with one sudden turn, no one understands. Without gathering one never sees the ingenuity within releasing; once gathered… released, how soaring it is. People say this posture still seems green; I say the gathered form is the most seasoned technique. The right palm turns inward, fingers pointing to heaven; the left hand also gathers near the breast. The fierce tiger descends the mountain with an empty belly; outwardly appearing empty, yet its cunning is lofty. Half empty, half solid — who can predict? If you would catch mice, watch the clever cat. The clever cat stalking a mouse first gathers its body; with one gathering the whole spirit collects. When spirit gathers, the whole body is strong; to turn heaven and earth is held within the hands. Solemn and august, learn this one thing: great bravery appears as timidity — its mechanism is hard to fathom.

Taking the Image (from the Yijing)

The two hands are above, like the second line of Xun (巽, Wind). With yang above, Kun (坤, Earth) is the belly; with yin below, this is the image of Kun. Kun below, Xun above: the hexagram Guan (觀, Contemplation). Guan — "sincerity, reverent gaze": the central qi of the boxing dwells in sincerity within. The Image says: "The great contemplation is above; compliant and gentle, correct in the center to contemplate all under heaven." The two hands gather and restrain to store up the momentum, observing how the enemy comes to strike me, calmly examining the moment to respond. The lower trigram is Kun — compliant; compliance follows where the eyes look (Xun is "many white eyes," good at observing opportunity and momentum). Xun inverted is Zhen (震, Thunder); Zhen is the eldest son; the eldest son leads the army — the firmness of the heart leads the four limbs; lines two and four combine as Kun: likewise the lower body compliantly follows the upper body, moving as opportunity dictates. Kun takes the form of Qian (乾, Heaven); though seemingly yielding, strength truly dwells within. Gathering the body, the method stores momentum — this also carries the meaning of the hexagram Da Xu (大畜, Great Accumulation). The principles of the Changes are the most fluid, and the images taken are the broadest and most fitting. In taking the Changes for taijiquan, one may take the meaning of the hexagram's name, or the Image commentary, or the line statements, or the Great Image, or the Small Image, or a single phrase within a hexagram, or sentences from Confucius's Xici ("Great Commentary"), Shuogua ("Explaining Trigrams"), Xugua ("Sequence of Hexagrams"), or the ideas of Bo (剝, Splitting), Fu (復, Return), Pi (否, Standstill), Tai (泰, Peace), the waxing and waning of yin and yang — the meanings taken cannot be enumerated; the ten thousand principles are all complete, the myriad images all present. Whatever I take, each fits its intent exactly. That taijiquan accords with the Way of the Changes is not forced, far-fetched interpretation; it truly originates from the Supreme Ultimate itself. Observe the one opening and one closing of the whole body: it is truly the natural closing and opening of yin and yang, not forced — one acts without knowing why it is so, and yet it is so. Profound indeed is boxing as an art!

Seven-character vernacular verse

In the hands, day by day, draw the Supreme Ultimate — yet no one recognizes this Way. Yin and yang wax and wane with their own truth; all depends on a attentive mind searching within the hands. The circles drawn may be upright or slanted, yet each is nothing but one circle, one Supreme Ultimate. Straight and oblique, joining and parting, full of feeling — it all depends on whether one can understand. The mechanism of heaven is active, subtle and deep; the pivot turns and turns within one mind. If indeed you can taste the delight within the boxing, then the thirty-six palaces are all spring.

(Printed page number: 126. Spine: Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo.)

Trang sách 127 · scan p160

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo*, Volume 1

Eighth Posture: Xie Xing Ao Bu (Oblique Walk, Twisted Steps)

The right hand is at the northwest — Fuxi's *gen* (艮) direction.

The left hand is at the southeast — Fuxi's *dui* (兌) direction, King Wen's *xun* (巽) direction. This posture takes the *dui*, *zhen*, and *xun* directions of Fuxi's Eight Trigrams: it requires a strong twist of the body, so that above and below, hands and feet face different directions — this accords with the meaning of *ao bu* (twisted steps), and accords with the corner directions of Fuxi's Eight Trigrams. This is another type of body method in the fist, like the "Airs of the States" (*bian feng*) variations in the *Book of Songs* — not the ordinary pattern of facing east or west.

Figure: A person in a low horse-stance: the left hand raised to the mouth/chin, palm facing inward; the right hand held behind the body at the waist. Directional labels: northwest (top), southeast (left), northeast (bottom left), southwest (bottom right).

Notes beneath the figure:

- The right foot is at the northeast — Fuxi's *zhen* (震) direction, King Wen's *gen* (艮) direction.

- The left foot is at the southwest — Fuxi's *xun* (巽) direction, King Wen's *kun* (坤) direction.

- Once the waist twists, the upper body twists naturally and does not align with the lower body: the waist is the pivot between upper and lower body.

At the start of the set one faces due north; by the second Jingang Dao Dui (Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounds the Mortar) one has already turned to face the west. By the first Closing (shou) the body has already turned to the southwest. Now in this Xie Xing Ao Bu, the chest faces southwest while the eyes watch the right hand at the northwest — this is the "twisted body" method of this posture. Although the body method changes, the joints above, below and on all four sides still respond to one another, and the opening and closing of the whole body is threaded through by the *zhong qi* (central energy, 中氣), so the whole body naturally flows as one breath. The physical form changes but the principle does not — this is what is meant by "different yet the same."

Xie Xing Ao Bu (verse)

"Oblique walk" means from northeast toward southwest: the left foot first takes one large step, and the right heel...

(Page: 127)

Trang sách 128 · scan p161

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (page: 128)

Figure: A martial artist in a low, twisted stance: the left foot forward (southwest), the right foot back (northeast); the body faces southwest, waist twisted. The left arm (right side of the figure) reaches forward and slightly up; the right arm (left side of the figure) is bent with the hand near the chest/shoulder.

The body method of this posture is the hardest to depict in a drawing. On paper it is not easy to draw its twisting form — how much harder to depict its *nei jin* (internal energy, 內勁)!

The internal energy winds the left arm first: the upper arm turns in reverse, and although this is against the direction of the force, the energy still coils from the elbow to the fingertips and joins in front. When the right hand turns through a reverse circle and hooks inward, the internal energy coils from inside outward to the fingers and also joins in front. The chest and the two hands join together as one.

Notes at the right of the figure:

When this hand is formed, it folds behind the back.

The left hand is behind, fingers gathered. The left hand faces southeast. The left hand initiates: it brushes past the knee and turns one circle, following the momentum moving toward the southeast, the back of the hand toward the southwest; once past, the back of the hand also turns that way. The upper arm bends like a new moon — slightly curved, not straight; the circle is reversed.

The left foot at the southwest is "front."

The right foot at the northeast is "rear."

Notes at the left of the figure:

The body faces southwest. Twisted waist. Movement issues through the middle.

The right hand is at the northwest; the right hand in front spreads open, fingers held together and not allowed to scatter. When this hand is formed, it folds to the front of the chest.

Text at the left:

The right hand turns in reverse; when the hand hooks inward, the hand moves with conforming energy toward the northwest while the body travels toward the southwest. The circle is reversed. The right hand also initiates by brushing past the knee and turning one circle; following the momentum it hooks once inward, then moves toward the northwest with the right middle finger. The left hand brushes the knee first, the right hand brushes the knee after. The back of the right hand also faces southwest; the gaze fixes on the right hand. The right hand is in front and the left hand behind; at initiation the left goes first and the right after, but once the posture is formed the right is in front and the left behind.

Final paragraph:

...step, placed in front of the foot. Before it settles, the left foot again opens one large step toward the southwest, about two *chi* (roughly two feet) or more, and only then the foot stops, without stepping further forward. Two shorter steps on the left and one on the right — three steps in succession, walking obliquely. "Twisted steps" (*ao bu*) means: left foot at the southwest, right foot at the northeast, right hand at the northwest, left hand at the southeast — hands and feet twist in one posture, left and right hands and feet in different directions. After the initial Closing (shou), the right hand leads, the left hand and left foot lead back slightly by half an inch; then with the left hand above and the left foot below, one large oblique step opens toward the southwest, about two *chi*...

Trang sách 129 · scan p162

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo*, Volume 1

...permit. The left hand turns in reverse toward the back; it has turned barely half a circle when — swift as rainwater streaming off the eaves of a house. When the left hand has turned in reverse, brushing the knee toward the rear, the right hand immediately follows the momentum, turning in reverse from above downward, and also brushes the knee going up toward the rear, then to the rear and then to the front. When the right hand goes to the rear, the left hand has already arrived in front. The right hand completes a full circle, using conforming energy (*shun jin*, 順勁) to coil the arm from inside outward and extend it to the northwest. The back of the right hand faces up, slightly toward the southwest, joining in front with the left hand. When the full circle is completed, the energy issues from the armpit, coiling from outside back inward on top. The hand leads the arm; the bend at the back relaxes and opens; the hand joins forward, the fingers are gathered, the fingers hook down a little, and the eyes fix on the right middle finger. The right hand spreads open, the four fingers leaning on one another — do not let them scatter.

The thumb cannot quite rest against the second joint of the thumb, but it should lean slightly against the index finger; then above and below join together as one. For the two hands each do their own work — it is not that the left hand finishes its movement before the right hand begins. When the left hand arrives behind, the right hand starts from the front; when the left hand turns to the front, the right hand also turns from behind to the front — the interval between one and the other is very small. As for extending the arm, the right hand turns one more conforming circle, and only then the arm spreads open; the left hand spreads toward the rear, likewise along its path slowly turning a small circle with the back of the hand and arm; the back-bend relaxes and opens; the crown energy (*ding jin*, 頂勁) and crotch energy (*dang jin*, 襠勁) are full and refined — all as before.

The internal-energy diagram is given below.

Both right and left hands turn two circles; the previous diagram did not show this fully, so it is diagrammed again to make it easy to understand.

Diagram: Xie Chan Fa (Oblique Coiling Method, 斜纏法) — two hand positions with movement trajectories: the upper hand an open palm with fingers pointing down; the lower hand a hook (*gou*) with fingers curled inward; curved lines mark the paths.

- Vertical title at top of diagram: Oblique Coiling Method

- Horizontal annotations above the diagram (fragmentary lines): "...circle, just enough for one..." "...from below to the inside..." "...outward and downward..." "...up, toward..." "...from inside toward..." "Conforming energy is energy..." "...toward, from inside..." "...turning over..."

- Horizontal notes below the diagram: "The right hand reaches up and hooks inward"; "The right hand is first led up from behind."

- Vertical title at right edge of diagram: Diagram of the Right Hand's Internal Energy (右手內勁圖)

- Vertical note beside the upper hand: "This hand issues from the right side, descending like water flowing downhill, very fast."

- Vertical note at the left of the diagram: "Coils again a small circle, hooking inward."

- Inside the upper loop: "hook inward."

Trang sách 130 · scan p163

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Xie Xing Ao Bu (斜行拗步, Diagonal Circling Steps) — taking Gen, Dui, Zhen, Xun as images

*Figure of the left hand's internal force (左手内劲图) labels:* 此随 ("this follows"); 左手发端 ("left hand initiates"); 右手向右领寸许 ("right hand leads an inch or so to the right"); 左手没到上够一圈 ("the left hand, not yet arrived above, completes one circle"); 由下行 ("moves downward"); 下流倒转 ("the downward flow reverses and turns").

The force this left hand employs: elbow forward, back of hand forward, the posture like walking along a roof ridge, carrying winding (缠, chan) and extending along the way; the chest is the reverse-winding force. Reverse winding means the force goes from the armpit outward, then from outside to inside, downward and again along the arm to complete one circle — this is also the chest-winding method, winding inward at the belly, downward and again along the arm to complete one circle, also the chest-winding method. The diagonal-winding method.

This is the left hand, which also reverses through a small circle of force; the five fingers bind together.

*Figure of the left foot's force (左足劲图) labels:* 起 ("rise") — 落 ("land").

Gen (艮) is the hands. The right hand is in the northwest; the changed hexagram (文卦, King Wen's line-image) is Qian (乾). Qian means vigor; Gen means stopping. Using the hand to stop things so the enemy cannot invade me is actually accomplished by the qi of centered strength. The left hand is in the southeast: Dui (兑) is the youngest daughter and means breaking and destroying — like the impetuous temperament of a young girl, able to smash things, so the enemy dare not offend, fearing injury. The King Wen image is Xun (巽): as wind, the hand moves as swiftly as wind; as work (工), the hand is skilled in answering the enemy; as "many white eyes," the hand is behind while the mind watches over it, just as the hand can perceive things — knowing the moment they come, seeing even without looking — not merely using the white eyes to be arrogant, but truly having surplus in responding to the enemy. As "gains near the market, threefold profit": when people come near my body they gain much advantage; in the end it is impetuousness (躁). The left hand is behind; once it moves it cannot but be impetuous, and once this spot is impetuous the whole body's hundred bones come to aid it — how much more can one impetuous hand repel the enemy? The left foot is in the southwest: Xun means advance and retreat; the left foot is in front and knows advancing and retreating. The King Wen image is Kun (坤), meaning "devoted to service" — the left foot can follow the upper body as its servant, and gathering much it has great force: one foot like the strength of many, because the few serve the many. The right foot is in the northeast: Zhen (震). Zhen is thunder — striking a person like sudden thunder too fast to cover the ears; it is the dragon — the right foot changes unpredictably like a dragon; it is decided impetuosity — the foot is not content with stillness; it is "treading feet" — this foot constantly wants to move; it is vigor — the right foot's strength is extremely strong. Its King Wen image is Gen: Gen is the mountain — the right foot dwelling in the northeast is stable as a mountain; moreover it is hands and fingers, able to stop a strong enemy's pursuit from behind; it is the "black-beaked one" — the foot trampling a person like a bird's beak pecking, because it has bony strength. In this posture the hands and feet occupy the four corners, each holding one angle, while my mind uses the central qi (中气, zhongqi) to move through the four limbs, each attaining its proper role. It is like commanding troops: when the general has strategy and courage, the three armies naturally win victory. Xie Xing Ao Bu is the surprise force (奇兵): surprising yet never departing from the straight, and therefore it wins.

Trang sách 131 · scan p164

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

Figure labels:

- 左手手背朝上束 ("left hand, back of hand facing up, bound together")

- 伏脉余同 ("hidden pulse; the rest as before")

- 住指，此为下势 ("fingers come to rest; this foreshadows the Lower Posture")

- 右手手背朝外 ("right hand, back of hand facing outward")

- 下腰劲尻微翻起，裆劲自然合住 ("the waist's lower force: the coccyx tips up slightly, and the crotch force (裆劲, dang jin) naturally closes and holds")

- 左足趾点地是虚步，为下势伏脉 ("left toes touching the ground make the empty step — the hidden pulse for the coming Lower Posture")

- 右足正脚平实踏地，是实脚，是主 ("right foot placed squarely, flat and solid on the ground — the solid foot, the principal one")

*The figure shows a man in a low bow-like stance: right leg straight and planted flat (solid foot), left leg bent with only the toe touching the ground (empty step), both hands raised near chest level.*

The Ninth Posture: Zai Shou (再收, "Gathering Again")

The internal force (内劲, neijin) runs; we want it to be fully, ten-tenths sufficient. When the force is not yet sufficient, it still runs like moving one foot — and thus the mechanism of the coming Lower Posture (下势) is set in motion. This is the natural tendency and natural principle of yin at its extreme giving birth to yang. The gaze fixes on the right hand; the crown force (顶劲, ding jin) leads the chest so they unite — this is a key point of the boxing arts; the bone joints above and below each correspond to one another.

In the previous posture, Initial Gathering (初收), the face was toward the southwest, the feet also toward the southwest, left first and right after, stepping three steps diagonally, the chest settled toward the southwest as this posture halts. After Xie Xing Ao Bu (斜行拗步), in this Gathering Again, the face is toward the northwest, yet the spirit attends to the west. The fist strikes along one line; the intention is that the coming Lower Posture must correspond in position with the earlier Brush Knee and Twist Step (搂膝拗步), and only then is it correct. The right hand revolves smoothly; the left hand revolves smoothly along with it in one circle; the right fingers spread then draw together, coming to rest in front of the right ear, seven or eight inches from the body, and stop. The left fingers bind together and come down above the left knee, five or six inches from the knee, seven or eight inches from the body, stopping together with the right hand — appearing to stop yet not solidly stopped.

Verse in Seven-Character Common Speech (七言俚语)

斜腕吊踝真难看，此中自然有高见。

妙手空空从何来，太极图中贵善变。

善变神机无滞形，功夫归根在百炼。

百炼真金金有光，金光闪闪如震电。

此是功成最后境，当初谁是真先难。

日日先难难无尽，难尽易来小神仙。

(Steep wrists and hanging ankles truly look awkward, yet within this there is naturally lofty insight. Whence comes the marvel-hand, empty and free? In the Taiji diagram the excellence lies in skillful change. Skillful change: the spirit's mechanism has no sluggish form; the root of gongfu lies in being tempered a hundred times. A hundred-tempered true gold has its glow; the golden light flashes like startling lightning. This is the final realm of accomplished skill — at the start, who truly met the hard first? Daily the hard comes first and hardness is endless; when hardness ends and ease arrives, one becomes a minor immortal.)

Verse in Seven-Character Common Speech (continued)

初收转圈自然好，未若此圈十分巧。

(In Initial Gathering the circling is naturally good, yet not equal to the subtlety of this circle — the circle is the whole body turning, not merely hands and feet; but hands and feet, being on the outside, are easily seen, hence the turning is described in terms of the hands.)

前所转圈犹嫌大，

(The circle turned before still seemed too large,)

Trang sách 132 · scan p165

Verse on "Second Gathering" (Yan Shou) — linking what comes before and after

As this circle turns, it grows ever smaller. When it diminishes until there is no circle at all, it returns to the true divine marvel of Taiji.

People say this art depends on secret formulas, and often will not reveal them to others. I say this art is nothing strange:

From youth one must practice until old age. Practicing into old age, one naturally awakens — suddenly unified, the spirit and principle are marvelous.

Looking back on times of laziness, it was not that the wise teachers had not spoken. It was spoken, but it never entered my heart,

and my heart instead felt only much vexation. Day after day it was said, day after day forgotten — if one does not apply the mind, when will one ever understand?

If one day you can exert effort in seeking, the waxing and waning of yin and yang will show their truth. Each day study the Taiji diagram carefully:

one opening, one closing, is within my own body. Advance step by step, and the work grows long; in time you will naturally smell true fragrance.

As long as your effort is long and uninterrupted, Taiji everywhere reveals its circular radiance. This is the true formula within the fist —

try measuring it carefully with a calm mind.

Second Gathering (Yan Shou zai shou 再收) — instruction

The First Gathering (chu shou 初收) originally follows Brush Knee; the Second Gathering tightens, connecting with the Diagonal Posture (xie xing 斜行). Once the body turns to face forward, the fist is raised and the right hand performs its role.

Taking the Image (qu xiang 取象)

In this posture the whole body is bent/contracted; only the right hand is up, the fingers still at a slant, the elbow also bent. Thus the many yin harm the yang, and only one breath remains — many petty men harming one noble man, the situation nearly perilous. This is like the Bo 剝 hexagram of the Yi Jing: from foot to belly only one great fruit survives, yet the living potential is not extinguished; therefore the image is taken from Bo 剝 (Splitting). In the Bo hexagram, the inner lines: the second and fourth (lines) form the pattern of Kun 坤 opposed to Qian 乾; the third and fifth likewise form Kun opposed to Qian — all retain the virtue of central firmness within. Since the lower body is all strength, why fear a dangerous situation? The Xiang says: "The mountain rests upon the earth — Bo (Splitting). Those above thereby thicken the base and secure their dwelling." This means the noble man does not prey upon his people, and thereby secures his own position.

Trang sách 133 · scan p166

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

The Tenth Posture: Qian Tang Ao Bu (前堂拗步, "Front Hall Twist Step") — first half

"Front Hall" (前堂) means the foot moves toward the front face of the hall. The previous posture was diagonal, its step-position not square; now it turns to face squarely forward.

Figure labels:

- 左手由里而上、而外转过来，五指向地，如水就下势 ("the left hand comes from inside upward, then turns over to the outside, five fingers pointing to the ground, like water taking its downward course")

- 右手亦是由内向下、向外上行，由上下向里转一圈，手往下刺，如瀑布下锐流势猛不可当 ("the right hand likewise goes from inside downward and upward along the outside, turning one circle from above to below and inward; the hand thrusts downward like a waterfall's sharp descending current, fierce and unstoppable")

- 腰贵大弯，随左足开步，上随左手运转，侧棍肩带，上体往下刺，亦如瀑布往下流，又如猛虎下山势 ("the waist prizes the great bend; as the left foot opens the step the upper body follows the left hand's turning; the torso thrusts downward like a waterfall flowing down, and again like a fierce tiger descending the mountain")

- 前开第二步 ("second step opened forward")

- 点住地，必待左足趾地，而后向 ("touching and holding on the ground; one must wait until the left toes reach the ground before turning")

- 右足先是平实踏地 ("the right foot first presses flat and solid on the ground")

- 点住地，待右足至至而后平踏，如前有深渊，说回即收回，至虚至灵 ("touching the ground; wait until the right foot arrives, then tread flat — as if before it were a deep abyss; when told to return it withdraws at once, utterly empty and utterly spirit-like")

- 左足抬起顺转半圈，开步先使足趾 ("the left foot lifts and turns smoothly through half a circle; in opening the step first employ the toes")

*The figure shows a man in a low semi-squatting stance, left hand low with fingers pointing downward, right hand raised near the chest as if thrusting downward.*

The left foot follows the left hand around one circle inward and outward; the foot opens one step toward the northwest; the right hand follows the left hand around one circle as well; the left foot lightly touches and holds on the ground; both hands are like a fierce tiger descending the mountain.

The previous posture took Bo (剥, "Splitting Apart") as its image; this posture takes Fu (复, "Return") — yin at its extreme gives birth to yang. If the great fruit does not remain, yang cannot be born of the great fruit: when it perishes above, yang is born below. Moreover, within Bo all the lines are Kun (坤); Kun reversed is Qian (乾) — yang has not yet ceased. Thus in the previous posture the hand was above like the great fruit; Kun reversed into Qian is like the central qi (中气, zhongqi) held within. When the left hand and the right hand turn over together, yang is born — this is exactly the image of "return after seven days." This diagram is yang's first stirring, having the images of "return not far" and "resting return"; the second step carries the image of "repeated return."

The left foot opens the step, and the left side of the body revolves with the left hand through one great circle, like a wheel; the right hand revolves like the left hand.

Here the left hand is in front, revolving smoothly like waterfall water. The left foot opens the step as the hand circles —

Figure of the internal force (内劲图)

Trang sách 134 · scan p167

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

Moreover, the left foot likewise follows the left hand in circling — it is not a step straight forward. Here the right hand and left hand move together: this one below, that one above, mutually rising and sinking without pause, alternating in their movement. The left foot opens one step, the left hand brushes the knee, the hand circling; the right foot follows one step, the right hand brushes the knee, the hand turning smoothly in a circle.

Qian Tang Ao Bu (前堂拗步, Front Hall Twist Step) — second half

The third step: from the second step, the right hand has only just come down brushing the knee, when the left hand turns at once from behind to the front, using the back of the forearm (背扦) with reversed-turning force to spread open behind, reversing through a small circle. The left foot, with the left hand, opens one great step toward the west; the right hand stays behind, gripping with the head hanging (搦住垂头).

At this point the rear hand has not yet been lifted — this is depicting the world of Front Hall Twist Step. The moment it is lifted, one enters the Lower Posture (下势); the momentum within this movement has reached the point where it cannot but lift.

Figure labels:

- 右足在后如蹬 ("right foot behind, like a pushing kick")

- 左足在前如撑 ("left foot in front, like a brace")

*The figure shows a man in a low wide stance: left foot extended forward bracing, right foot back pressing; left arm raised high with open palm, right arm extended at shoulder level with open palm.*

This is the second step of Front Hall Twist Step. With the Lower Posture's Finished-Hand Fist (演手捶, yan shou chui) it runs as one qi, never halting — carrying the images of "the solitary return of the middle way" (中行独复) and "sincere return" (敦复). In the fist there is purely the vast flood-like qi (浩气, haoqi), flowing through the whole body, its momentum unarrestable. The Image says: "Thunder is within the earth" (雷在地中). The left hand is in front — whether spreading open or hooking the fingers together, either is acceptable. The right hand behind grips the fist (捶); before the hand is finished, it already has the posture of wanting to finish the hand and strike a person — its form like thunder lying hidden within the earth.

Song says (歌曰)

前堂须与金刚照，亦是拳中最紧要。缠法一切皆如前，观图自然会其妙。

(Front Hall must correspond with the Buddha's Guardian (金刚, jingang); this too is among the most essential in the fist. The coiling method (缠法, chanfa) — all is as before; observing the diagram, one naturally grasps its subtlety.)

Second verse (其二)

二次收来不须长，提回两足在一方。上从下行开三

(Gathering a second time needs no length; lift back so both feet are on one side. Above, from the downward movement, open three…)

Trang sách 135 · scan p168

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

…step, and connect below to the Finished Hand at the Front Hall (前堂). The Front Hall pressed step resembles the Diagonal (斜行), turning to stand at the northwest center. The right below joins with what is above; the spirit is concentrated; the foot presses flat on the ground like a wall of bronze.

The left hand brushes the left knee, having just turned smoothly backward half a circle; the right hand leads while the right foot follows one more step, passing beyond the left front — the right hand makes a reversed-turning circle; the right hand brushes the right knee. This is the second step.

Diagram of the Second Step, Left and Right Brush Knee (第二步左右搂膝图)

Figure labels:

- 右手起 ("right hand rises")

- 右手搂膝 ("right hand brushes the knee")

- 左手起 ("left hand rises")

- 左手搂膝 ("left hand brushes the knee")

*The figure shows two semicircular arcs: the left, wider arc (opening upward) traces the right hand's path — rising, then brushing the right knee; the right, flatter arc traces the left hand's path — rising, then brushing the left knee.*

Eleventh Posture: Punch with the Forearm (第十一势 演手肱捶, yǎnshǒu gōngchuí)

Figure labels (the drawing shows a low bow stance — front left leg deeply bent, rear right leg straight with foot planted; left arm extended forward with open palm, right arm bent with fist at the ribs):

- 前手是左手展开，即抠住亦可 ("The front hand is the left hand spread open; hooking-gripping with it is also acceptable.")

- 顶劲领住 ("The crown force (dingjin) is led and held.")

- 背用中气贯住劲，微扭一势 ("The back is threaded and held by the central qi (zhongqi), with a slight twist of the posture.")

- 前胸合住 ("The chest is closed and held.")

- 在后是右手，手背朝上合住劲，用缠丝法 ("Behind is the right hand, back of the hand upward, holding the force closed, using the silk-reeling method (chansifa).")

- 左膝屈住不可软 ("The left knee is bent and held; it must not go soft.")

- 左足趾向西北，足后踵不掀起，要塌实地 ("The left toes point northwest; the heel must not lift — press solidly into the ground.")

- 裆尤要虚，虚则回转皆灵 ("The crotch (kua) must above all be empty (xu); when empty, all turning is agile.")

- 右膝微屈，愈不可发软 ("The right knee is slightly bent, and even more must not go soft.")

- 右足蹬住地，如用力蹬重物 ("The right foot presses against the ground as if forcefully kicking a heavy object.")

- 腰劲要下去，下去腰劲，两膝撑开，裆合住要圆、要虚，自然下体又虚又灵又稳当，摇撼不动 ("The waist force must sink down. With the waist force down and both knees braced apart, the kua held closed — round and empty — the lower body is naturally empty, agile and stable, unmoved by shaking.")

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

Diagram of the Step Positions of Xie Xing Ao Bu (Diagonal Twist Step) and Qian Tang Ao Bu (Front Hall Twist Step) (斜行拗步与前堂拗步步位之图)

Figure labels:

- 西 (west), 东 (east), 南 (south), 北 (north)

- 地位 ("ground position")

- 初收 ("First Gathering" — the First Closing)

- 再收 ("Second Gathering" — the Second Closing)

- 左足开步至此止 ("The left foot opens a step, stopping here.")

- 右足次开步至此 ("The right foot next opens a step to here.")

- 再次仍是左足开步，开一大步约一尺余 ("Again still the left foot opens a step — one great step of about a chi and more.")

- 左足向西北开步至此 ("The left foot steps open toward the northwest to here.")

- 西南方第三步左足至此止，初收步位在此，再收地位亦在此 ("Third step southwest, left foot stops here; the First Gathering step-position is here, and the Second Gathering ground-position is also here.")

- 再次又是左足开一大步，二尺有余 ("Again the left foot opens one great step of over two chi.")

- 次右足开，开到左足边 ("Next the right foot opens, opening to the side of the left foot.")

- 初收地位第一步开步一尺有余，向西南方左足先开 ("At the First Gathering ground-position, the first step is a chi and more, opening southwest with the left foot first.")

Hand-posture diagram (two hands in a pressing position with curved movement lines):

- 左手不丢 ("The left hand is not lost/abandoned.")

- 右手领劲 ("The right hand leads the force.")

- 左臂屈收 ("The left arm bends and gathers in.")

- 右臂屈收 ("The right arm bends and gathers in.")

- 左手 ("left hand"), 右手 ("right hand")

The left hand is in front, slightly hooking-gripping, the wrist facing west to serve as the counterpart posture for the right hand. The right hand rises from below, going upward, then backward, then forward again, striking into the middle of the left wrist. The back of the right hand faces upward, closing downward to strike, with force. Yet if the right hand greedily strikes forward, it is not as good as having both hands outside the left flank, some seven or eight cun from the ribs: then the right hand holds the force closed, uses the whole body's total force, and strikes with seizing force (najin), revealing no roughness — only then does it accord with the rules. Asked: whence does this force issue? Answer: the right foot is planted well; the force goes from the heel, over the calf, up along the spine, threading to the right shoulder and arm as it turns, moving from the back of the arm across to the back of the hand — hence closing the force in makes the strike powerful. Yet although the force rises from the heel, its use fundamentally lies in the heart-mind: the moment the mind stirs, the central qi (zhongqi) at once goes from the dan… [continues]

136

Trang sách 137 · scan p170

*Chen shi taiji quan tu shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan), Juan 1 (卷一, Volume One).

From the moment the force issues until it reaches the hand, the whole body's strength gathers there. As for striking an opponent, it depends on his distance. At a distance you can extend the arm to reach him; up close the arm cannot extend, so use the bent elbow with a combined chui (捶, fist/thrust) — this is extremely restrained (hanxu, 含蓄), showing no outward trace at all, and the one struck simply falls down. This is the highest grade. Striking from afar is easy, striking up close is hard, so one must put in much extra work to accomplish this.

Qu xiang (取象, Taking the Image)

The previous posture took Fu (復, Return) — yang at its first arising. This posture takes Zhen (震, Thunder/Arousing): "thunder within the earth" must in the end burst forth outward; thunder above, thunder below — the whole body becomes like thunder that cannot be approached, and the hand is its most conspicuous expression. If it does not strike, nothing happens; once it strikes, "thunder startles a hundred li." Further: the right hand in advancing can strike, in retreating can defend, and externally can serve as the body's chief force. It is said: "Startling a hundred li — the far are startled and the near are afraid." Going forth, one can guard the ancestral temple and the altars of state, acting as the master of sacrifice — is this not precisely the image of Yanshou hong chui (演手肱捶, Wrapping Hand Forearm Fist)? It is on this basis that the image is taken.

Qi yan liyu (七言俚语, Seven-Character Folk Verse)

炼就金刚太极尊，浑身合下力千斤。

劝君智勇休使尽，剩下余力扫千军。

Forged into the Diamond (Jingang, 金刚), the honored one of Taiji — the whole body, when combined below, has the strength of a thousand catties. I urge you: do not exhaust your wisdom and courage; keep the remaining strength to sweep away a thousand armies.

Posture 12: Jingang Dao Dui, third time (金刚捣碓第三, Diamond Pounds the Mortar)

First withdraw the right foot level with the left foot, facing west, then turning north; the two hands circle and wheel as if flying — left empty, right full (音足也 [as given in the text]). You must remember this: only when the Diamond pounds three times can it be called marvelous. Lift up Kunlun (昆仑, the sacred mountain), part open the abyss's depths; standing dignified and alone, the countenance of the spirit solemn and serene, the bearing august and grand; chaos and void-spirit possessed in one heart, the myriad images stored in the five skandhas (五蕴), still and unmoving like a simpleton. Who knows that yin and yang are united in this body? Let people from the four sides and eight directions approach — it is hard for them to get near. Even if the brave and fierce, superior in strength, suddenly come to invade, some topple, some fall — none can fathom this spirit. And further: to go is hard to go, to advance is hard to advance, like standing on a round stone where one cannot stand steady — truly perilous. Regret inevitably follows a fall; is there another method? Only that the gongfu (功夫) be pure — everything depends on one opening and one closing, one stroke sweeping away a thousand armies.

*[Diagram: a standing martial artist in traditional Chinese attire, hands clasped in front of the body, feet together. No text labels on the diagram.]*

(Printed page number: 137)

Trang sách 138 · scan p171

*Chen shi taiji quan tu shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說).

Upper caption: "The left hand, in front of the chest, circles one loop from outside to inside; the hand is slightly bent, the wrist facing upward." — "Diagram of the paths followed by the left and right hands in Posture 12, Jingang Dao Dui (金刚捣碓, Diamond Pounds the Mortar)."

Vertical annotations beside the upper diagram (right to left): "The right chui (捶, fist) also comes from the east… circles left, returns to the chest, from the outside coils one large loop… in the left wrist the right fist lands."

Upper diagram: an arched label reads "Supplementary diagram of the movement path of the second Jingang Dao Dui" (补第二金刚捣碓运行图). Labels on the figure: zuo shou (左手, left hand) and you chui (右捶, right fist) on the upper coil; you shou (右手, right hand) on the middle coil; zuo zhang (左掌, left palm), you chui (右捶), and zuo zhang (左掌) on the lower coil.

Lower diagram: an arched label along the upper coil reads "Diagram of the hand's movement, returning through four loops" (四圈些回运行手图). Labels: xiong (胸, chest), zuo shou (左手, left hand), wai (外, outside), the directions nan (南, south), bei (北, north), dong (东, east), you chui (右捶, right fist), and zuo shou (左手, left hand).

Explanatory text at the bottom:

Jingang Dao Dui has already appeared twice — why repeat it again? Because the second Jingang follows Dan Bian (单鞭, Single Whip), with the forearms spread wide open; to close what has been opened, nothing is easier than closing with Jingang Dao Dui. Therefore using Jingang Dao Dui to combine the combination also takes the meaning that "things reverse when they reach their extreme" (wu ji bi fan, 物极必反). This Jingang Dao Dui follows Yanshou chui (演手捶, Wrapping-Hand Fist), in which the spirit-energy (shenqi, 神气) has all scattered outward. Yet although the stance is spread wide, fortunately the two hands are gathered in one place. As soon as the heel of the left foot shifts slightly to face due north, the two hands go from above downward and from inside outward, then rise again, circling together; the right chui lands in the hollow of the left wrist, four or five cun (寸) from the body, and the right foot also turns along with what is above. The foot and the hand come down together…

(Printed page number: 138)

Trang sách 139 · scan p172

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation, Volume 1 (陳氏太極拳圖說卷一)

…held closed with essence (jing); compared with before this is somewhat easier. What has long been scattered must gather; what has long opened must close — this is truly the natural closing and opening (hepi) of yin and yang. Therefore it is followed by the Buddha's Guardian Pounding the Mortar (金刚捣碓, Jingang Dao Dui): on the one hand, the complete spirit is gathered so that when one wishes to change the posture one naturally feels it agile; on the other, it returns to the original image of taiji, to show that the myriad differences all issue from one root. As for internal force and outward method, all has been said before — no repetition.

On the Image (取象)

The preceding posture — "thunder startles for a hundred li, nothing can withstand it" — what exhilaration! Hence Jingang Dao Dui takes the image of Yu (豫, Delight/Enthusiasm). If a person can preserve and unite Supreme Harmony (太和), he is at ease wherever he goes — this is the great attainment of "following Yu." How much more so "as firm as a rock, not waiting the whole day." In all affairs, follow the moment and move with it, harboring no impulsiveness or recklessness, and one can always abide in one's joy. If the time has not yet arrived, quietly nourish one's natural innocence. That this fist still returns to the original image of taiji — vast and unfathomable — what joy can match it?

The first Jingang faces north; the second Jingang turns to face west; the third Jingang again faces north. The body method is upright and settled, never leaning. Gather in the spirit; there is no other secret — a calm mind and harmonious qi and it is attained.

Trang sách 143 · scan p176

Posture 13: Bishen Chui, first half of the posture (庇身捶前半勢圖 — Bìshēn chuí qiánbàn shì tú)

Volume 2, *Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen-style Taijiquan).

This figure continues from Jingang Dao Dui (金剛搗碓, "Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Mortar"). After stepping, first open the right foot in a large step of about four to four-and-a-half cun (四四五寸); then sink the right shoulder forcefully downward, passing the right shoulder beneath the right knee — only then is the form correct. This is what is called the "seven-inch lean" (七寸靠, qī cùn kào), extremely difficult, extremely difficult. Nowadays there is no one who can do it — even this figure is only approximate: the right shoulder has already passed beneath the knee and is rising again, not the moment when the shoulder passes directly under the knee. As for the hands: some first brush the knee with the right hand, circling a great circle from the east, gripping the fist (搦捶) to land beside the forehead; then the left hand brushes the left knee, reversing through a great circle, the hand landing at the waist, propping the waist — this is one variant. In another, the right and left hands separate downward together, the right hand forward, the left hand back, the hands landing as described above — this is another variant; this figure follows the latter.

This figure shows the body method (身法, shēn fǎ) in the first half of Bishen Chui; the movement must not pause the qi mechanism (气机). Because the seven-inch lean technique is included, a figure is given to show it.

With the upper body down low, the spirit at the crown (頂勁, dǐng jìn) and the central qi (中气, zhōng qì) must all the more not be lost; the crotch (襠, dāng) and waist sink down, the foot presses the ground with force to fix the root. The lower the body method goes, the better — the right shoulder almost touches the ground, only seven inches high. If an enemy in front presses down on my head, put the right foot inside the enemy's crotch, place the right shoulder against the enemy's lower abdomen, and use shoulder force to lift upward once (挑, qiao, departing tone); the enemy will fly up and fall down.

Section explanation (節解, jié jiě)

- The left elbow and right elbow join their jin (合住勁).

- The elbow tip points yet further forward.

- The gaze watches the toes of the left foot.

- The chest faces north; the back faces south.

- The crown jin is well led; the central qi must sink down through to the tailbone (尻骨).

- The right fist and the left hand join their jin between the left knee.

- The chest must be contained; use joining jin (合勁) to join it.

Note: the page has two figures. The upper shows the low body position of the first half of Bishen Chui — upper body sunk, right shoulder near the ground at about seven inches (the "seven-inch lean"). The lower figure (jie jie) carries the labels transcribed above. One or two characters were unclear in the scan and are noted as such in the Chinese file.

Trang sách 144 · scan p177

Internal jin figure of Bishen Chui (庇身捶内勁圖) — this is the completed form of the standard Bishen Chui (庇身捶成式圖)

Bishen Chui (庇身捶, Bìshēn chuí, "Body-Protecting Fist") is a method of looking back: the body is in the west, the eyes in the east, the head up, the eyes below. This was already discussed in the first half of Yinmeng (引蒙); there is no need to repeat it. This is the body method: the body inclines to the east, the waist folds to the west. The front is easy to watch over, so the shoulders and arms need no discussion. Below the shoulders and arms, everything depends on the mind's numinous awareness to watch over them. The left foot joins with the right foot, toes also pressing firmly toward the northeast. The crotch (襠, dāng) must be all the more round and empty-alert (虚灵, xūlíng), in readiness for nimble turning at the transitions (转关, zhuǎn guān). Below the knees everything is rigidly fixed (死煞); therefore turning depends entirely on the waist and crotch. The right foot joins with the left, toes toward the northeast, flat and solid on the ground. The right knee joins with the left and props well. In practicing the fist, north is taken as "up," so north governs throughout; this figure faces north.

Figure labels (second figure: internal jin of Bishen Chui)

- The central qi (中气) passes from the crown through to the spine.

- The right elbow bends forward, joining jin level with the left elbow.

- The tip points forward.

- The left hand circles westward; the left hand props the waist (叉腰); the place where the left hand rises.

- The right hand circles eastward and lands at the forehead; large circle of the right hand; small circle of the left; circling back east; circling back west.

- The right shoulder lands squarely on the enemy's chest; this is the right shoulder folding back; this is the right shoulder going outward; the rise of the shoulder.

Bottom text

This figure is named Bei Zhe Kao (背折靠, Bèi zhé kào, "Back-Folding Lean"); it is the latter part of Bishen Chui, carrying on the remaining intent of Bishen Chui — it is not a separate posture.

Trang sách 145 · scan p178

(Continued) Application of Bei Zhe Kao (背折靠)

One must use the strength of the whole body to fold back and strike with the shoulder — not merely shoulder strength, for the shoulder is where the jin gathers (肩其聚勁地也). Application: if the enemy uses both hands to brush my arm and draws it toward his body, his posture nearly leaning forward, my shoulder happens to be close to his chest; I fold my shoulder outward and counter-strike. Because there is this method, it is not feared. A figure is further given to reveal its inner meaning.

Posture 13: Bishen Chui (庇身捶, also called Pishen Chui 披身捶)

What is Bishen Chui? Using the fist to protect the body. What is Pishen Chui (披身捶, Pīshēn chuí, "Draping-Body Fist")? Both hands split evenly down from the center; it is also called Pieshen Chui (撇身锤; 撇 piě, rising tone, means "to fold"). Why is it so called? It is named from the folding-back of the body. This posture continues from Jinang Daodui (金剛搗碓, Jīngāng dàoduì): the right hand leads the right foot, the hand moves downward, the right foot opens a large step to the east, the body follows the step sinking down and then rising up, the body is held in the draped position (i.e., folding the waist), the head turns back to look — left toes, right breast, half forward and half back. The body is slightly bent; though the body inclines, the central qi (中气) must be straight. The right toes point northeast, the right knee closes inward, the left foot hooks its toes toward the northeast, and the gaze rests there. Body method: as the right foot opens the step, the right shoulder sinks down; bend the waist where it can bend — bend as much as you can bend. Even if the shoulder cannot reach below the knee, level with the knee is acceptable; failing to come within seven inches of the ground need not be rigidly forced. Hand method: when the right foot opens the step, the right hand goes from above downward, turns to the east, slightly back two or three fen, reverses and winds around to the front, landing the fist on the forehead to guard the head, back of the hand facing up. The left hand likewise goes from above downward, reversing backward through a great circle, comes forward and props the waist where there are no ribs: the second, third, fourth, fifth fingers at the front of the waist, the thumb behind the waist resting against it, to assist the waist's strength. Bend the elbow, the elbow tip forward, joining jin with the bend of the right elbow; right hand joins with left hand, right elbow joins with left elbow; twist the neck backward, lift the head up, contain the chest, the thigh roots must not collapse, the crotch opens round. Knee joins knee, foot joins foot, the whole body joins into one piece; when the spirit-energy does not scatter, only then can the one qi flow through and protect the whole body.

Bishen Chui is for when the enemy controls me from behind: I use shoulder, arm, and forearm to face the enemy with my back, and wherever he touches me, I fold back from there and strike. Also, if someone comes from the east, approaching my body, I withdraw slightly backward, the right shoulder turns its jin around, the right arm bends, and the right hand thrusts the fist suddenly toward the enemy's lower abdomen, striking with the fist. This Bishen Chui — using the fist to guard the body and the fist to strike the person — is yet another variant.

Seven-character vernacular verse on the Yanshou Chui following Bishen Chui (庇身捶后演手捶七言俚语)

The right shoulder draws back a few fen, / turning the jin around comes another fist. / This fist strikes especially at the lower abdomen, / one blow to the mark and the man is hurt.

Seven-character vernacular song of Bishen Chui (庇身捶七言俚语歌)

The Bishen Chui posture is hardest to transmit, / both feet spread open three chi wide, / both hands split apart, both reversed… (continues on next page)

Trang sách 146 · scan p179

English

(Continuation of Bishen chui 庇身捶, the "Guarding-the-body fist")

...turning; both legs' combined jin (精) coil entirely diagonally. The right fist lands above the Shenting (神庭, above the Shangxing point 王星穴, below the fontanel Ximen 囟门), while the left fist forks (去声, a departing tone) at the left waist. The body is like lying on its side with a slight twist; the gaze fixes on the tip of the left foot. The crown jin (顶精) lifts up, diagonal yet holding straight; the crotch (裆) is braced open (knees spread) and closed (jin held together) like a half moon (the shape of a half-crescent moon). The right shoulder strikes downward (下 = downward) with a seven-inch lean (七寸靠); the back-fold lean (背折靠) is still perfectly centered. The right hand withdraws and delivers another punch — this is the "variable middle fist" of Taiji (太極變中拳).

The back of the body is yang; chest and abdomen are yin. The left and right hands use reverse-coiling jin (倒转精); this is yang closing into yin. As for using the folded-arm jin (臂折精) to strike an opponent, that is right-turning jin — from yin turning to yang and striking with yang jin. Once the Bishen-chui posture is complete and the jin is held closed, that is stillness; using the arm to fold back is movement — from stillness into movement. In sum, from the shoulder downward, the right hand reverses its circle and the body follows it in reverse turning — this is the back-fold lean (背折靠). When the right hand turns its circle in the normal direction, the body also follows it in normal turning — this is a single qi (一氣) above and below. In the back-fold lean, the right hand turns forward circles while the left hand goes from behind, from below, up and around to the front — reverse-coiling jin. Without this, it cannot run in one smooth sequence with the right hand. This is the inevitable configuration, also the natural order of principle; otherwise the two hands would oppose each other and be unable to help one another, and the qi would likewise be unsmooth. As for the shoulders and arms withdrawing so as to strike with the fist — using the back of the hand to send the fist-wrist, striking the opponent downward in a closing blow — that is yang closing into yin-yang jin. Before the shoulders withdraw, there is stillness; when the fist strikes the lower abdomen downward toward the east, there is movement. This too is movement arising from stillness, and after movement arises, stillness is born again. Movement and stillness cycle round — how could there be a gap between them! What I call one movement, one stillness, one opening, one closing, fully exhausts the subtlety within the quan (拳). Unless the mind has its measure and balance, it will not easily stand on ground where defeat is impossible; responding according to how the enemy comes, one wins in every case.

Taking the Image (取象)

In this posture the right hand is above, the left hand at the waist; the tip of the right elbow points east, the tip of the left elbow north; the right foot is in the east, the left foot in the west; the Qihai point (氣海) faces north; the Huagai (華蓋) twists toward the northwest; the eyes are above yet look below; the Tianting (天庭) faces west; the toes all point northeast — above and below all have a tendency of separation. Therefore it takes the Li hexagram (離卦). Outside the body is strong, within empty; hands and feet are all strong, yet the heart's substance is empty and clear, able to illuminate the whole body — the image of Li. Second Nine: "Yellow radiance, greatly auspicious." The Image says: "Yellow radiance, greatly auspicious — it attains the middle way." If the quan can illuminate the principle of centrality and correctness, where would it go that is not fitting? Top Nine: "The king sends out on a campaign: he breaks the heads and captures those who are not his own kind — no blame." (The back-fold lean is the image of going out on campaign.)
