Trang sách 148 · scan p181

English

(Posture 15 — Zhou-di kan quan 肘底看拳, "Fist under the elbow, guarding")

The chest must hold the jin (精) in, and must also be empty. The right elbow is bent; the five fingers spread open and support each other; the eyes watch the right-hand fist below the left elbow. When the crown jin (顶精) leads once, the jin and spirit of the whole body are all roused.

When an enemy comes, there is first wind. If he is fast, his wind is great; if slow, his wind is slight — even with no wind there must be an omen beforehand. If the enemy is in front, the eyes can see him; but if he is to the right, to the left, or behind, that too is the omen. Consider: he is not in front but at the sides and behind, his mind unfathomable — one can only rely on the ears to listen and the mind to guard.

Diagram labels (attached to the figure): Crown jin leads once and the whole body's jin and spirit are roused / The chest must hold the jin in and be empty / The right elbow is bent, five fingers spread and supporting, eyes watching the right fist below the left elbow / The left knee is bent, braced open, jin held closed / The toes touch the ground — this is the empty foot, lying in wait for the incoming pulse of the next posture / The crotch opens round / The right foot treads flat and solid on the ground / The right knee is bent, braced open, closing with the left knee / The waist jin sinks down / The elbow braces open, square outside and round within / The right shoulder sinks; the lower hand lü's the fist (下手捋捶).

Caption below the diagram: Zhou-di kan quan, the old form (肘底看拳老式).

This is the shape traced by the hand as it withdraws from the east along its path.

Small diagrams: Left: the elbow; the place where the left hand forks at the waist (左手叉腰处). Right: the right-hand fist beneath the elbow; the grasp (拿); this is the completed configuration of the arm.

Final paragraph: What my late father transmitted to me: he always required left and right to rise together and move together, the right turning forward and the left turning reverse, turning circles together, closing together, and together stopping — the hands snapping up and held in place.

Trang sách 149 · scan p182

English

(Printed page 149; spine: Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo 陳氏太極拳圖說, Book Two 卷二)

Left diagram labels (an upright arm with a looping trajectory, open hand with spread fingers on top):

- "This right hand reverses through one circle and reaches upward" (此右手倒转一圈涉到上)

- "The right hand is at the eastern starting point" (右手在东起端处)

- "The left elbow is below; the elbow sinks down; the shoulder presses down" (左肘在下，肘沉下，肩压住)

Right diagram labels (a large loop with the fist at its lower end):

- "The right hand is at east" (右手在东)

- "The right hand goes from east downward, then travels upward, rotating forward one circle, landing below the elbow" (右手由东向下再上行，顺转一圈，落在肘下)

New-style internal-jin diagram (新式内精图)

What is "Zhou-di kan quan" (肘底看拳, fist guarding beneath the elbow)? Because the right hand lets its fist land beneath the left elbow, hence the name. First, with the right toes pointing northeast, use the heel — without leaving the ground — and with one twist turn the toes to the west, deviating one or two fen to the north, treading flat and solid. The left hand comes obliquely down from above, first from north to south, then turns back from south to north, reversing through one circle. The arm is bent; the hand spreads its fingers, supporting each other and facing up, the elbow below. The left foot withdraws from the west, drawing in beside the right foot, five or six cun from it; the left knee is bent, the kneecap and the elbow tip facing each other above and below; the knee opens outward while the jin closes inward; the toes touch the ground — first lying in wait with the incoming pulse for the following Dao-juan gong (倒卷肱, reverse reeling forearms). The right hand turns one forward circle from south to north and returns again to the south. Lü-ing the fist, it lands below the left elbow, the eyes fixed on the fist. The right knee is bent, opening outward, jin closing inward; the secret lies in rounding at the hip joint, the inner thigh closing inward from above. In this way not only is the jin held closed but the crotch (裆) is also round; the crown jin (顶精) holds up, the head inclines slightly to the northwest, the fontanel (囟门) tipping down one or two fen; the chest is held closed. The trace seems to halt the qi yet does not halt it: one must wait until the internal jin (内精) slowly arrives, fully complete. When the mechanism of the next posture is eager to stir, only then can the preceding and following postures be joined through, without barrier, the qi flowing in one stream. Not only this one posture: from beginning to end of the quan, the end of every posture is like this.

Zhou-di kan quan: the left hand is yang, the right hand yin; the back of the hand is yang, the right wrist yin — everyone knows this; why belabor it! But the left hand reverses from below upward, winding from outward to inward: this is movement turning to stillness (由动之静), not merely circling. The right hand withdraws from the east up high, turns one forward circle sweeping down, and the fist lands beneath the elbow — this too is movement turning to stillness, not an empty circle. For the left hand's reverse turn, its jin arises from the finger pads, coiling obliquely downward and outward and turning back, regardless of the number of circles. Coil obliquely to the armpit, then from outside the armpit coil obliquely inward, again regardless of the number of circles, winding to the left fingers... (continues on the next page)

Trang sách 150 · scan p183

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (marginal title)

...stopping at the belly (du zhi 肚止); only thus can it join (he 合) with the right hand's jing (精, essential energy). The right hand turns in the forward direction (shun zhuan 順轉): its jing goes from the fingernail downward to the inner side, from the inner side outward in a slanting coil (xie chan 斜纏) to the right armpit, then turns back from the armpit, from inside going up, outward and down, coiling slantwise until it stops at the fingernail, joining the jing with the left hand. To join the jing, one must use the coiling method (chan fa 纏法); without the coiling method the outward form appears joined, but in truth the inner jing has never been joined.

Therefore I say it is not merely the hand turning circles: it is truly the xin-qi (心氣, heart-energy) moving and coiling in the left and right hands without a single moment of stopping. As for what is called stillness (jing 靜), in the boxing it only means the breath is somewhat quieter than in movement — it does not mean stopping. When have yin and yang of heaven and earth ever stopped? At the summer solstice, one yin is born — yin is stillness. Yet after that yin is born, when has there been a moment of not growing! Likewise when a person sits, lies, or sleeps — the body's time of stillness — yet how could one breath ever be interrupted? When the gong reaches this point, the rules are roughly worth looking at; they are just not yet lively. If one can advance without ceasing until it becomes lively and active, one enters a further level. How could the mysteries of the chamber be hard to glimpse! Mencius said: "A great master craftsman teaches others only with compass and square (guiju 規矩)." The compass and square are the perfection of square and round. Teaching with them is what a great craftsman can do. As for skill (qiao 巧), the great craftsman cannot confer it; it rests with the learner. If one reaches skill, then one follows the rules without being stuck in the rules, leaves the rules yet naturally hits the rules — but one's resolve must never be full, for fullness invites loss. The proverb says: "Beyond the sky there is still sky; once full, one invites loss." Able to follow the rules and not lose correctness, one counts success, failure, gain and loss as nothing.

Taking the Image (Qu xiang 取象)

This posture's body seems unconnected: the hands have some extended, some fisted (lü quan 捋拳); the feet, though several inches apart, have some flatly planted, some standing on tiptoe. Moreover the five sense-organs and hundred bones all have a constricted form — truly bearing the image of repeated Kan (xi kan 习坎, "double abyss," Hexagram 29) and entering the abyss, so the image is taken from Kan. Yet it says: "Hardness and softness in mutual aid — in the end there is strategy; at the time of leaving danger, Kan is full within." A person's psycho-physical constitution is complete; the central qi (zhong qi 中氣) returns to the dantian (丹田), bearing the image of upper Kan and lower Kan. The Classic text: "Xi Kan: if one possesses truthfulness (you fu 有孚), the heart-mind penetrates (wei xin heng 維心亨); action has distinction." The Image says: "Xi Kan means doubled danger." It is like my body entering heavy danger: the water flows yet does not overfill (like my humility being able to receive benefit); moving in danger yet not losing trust; the heart-mind penetrates because of the firmness at the center (meaning there is true principle in my heart, and the central qi commands it). "The danger of earth — mountains, rivers, hills" (meaning what others despise, from any terrain one can defend). "The kings and dukes set up barriers to guard their states" (meaning that in the boxing, preparedness averts calamity — what danger is there?). "The use of danger in its season is great indeed!" (meaning in the boxing, every deployment is fitting — nothing is impossible!) The middle lines and Lü (履) speak of the yang qi lying hidden below; Zhen (震, Thunder) is the dragon — the transformations of the hand are like a dragon; Zhen is inverted by Xun (巽). Xun means compliant: "dwell amid trouble and walk within trouble," moving with the times. The second change: Kun (坤) inverted by Qian (乾) — outwardly soft and inwardly firm. Though the boxing's form appears cramped and constrained, the upright qi of Qian and Kun is ever naturally at ease — how fear that Kan cannot be exited? Box the fist so that on the narrow road you can keep yourself firm without loss; on the wide road, move about freely without obstruction. This hexagram is Kan above and Kan below, saying the central qi is real and again real; its inversion is Li (离, Fire), speaking of the heart...

(printed page number: 150)

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, juan 1-2 (Volume 1, Chapter 2)

...clear and clear again (fragment continuing from previous page).

Elbow-Bottom Guarding the Fist (Zhou di kan quan 肘底看拳) — four-character verse

Left elbow above, right fist below; the chest holds reserve, the head turned aside, looking down. Left foot touches the ground, right foot flatly planted, both knees bent and held, the crotch broad and open. Spirit complete, qi full — the true and nothing false; it links what is above and opens what is below, the image archaic and elegant.

On the preceding topic — five-character verse

It also resembles an image of a macaque monkey; the immortal peach hangs beneath the elbow (the peach is a metaphor for the fist). Dare to look, dare not to eat (meaning: the fist must not be touched, lest one be struck); quietly nourishing the heaven within one's nature (bending the body, keeping to oneself, awaiting what comes).

The Sixteenth Posture: Dao Juan Hong (倒捲紅, "Rolling Back the Red / Reverse Reeling Silk")

(Diagram labels)

- Right hand arrives on top; the elbow slightly bent, the fingers slightly curved.

- The spine is held up and led (ling 領); the body spreads down yet must also be led upward — a great bending of the waist while stepping backward.

- Right foot flatly planted.

- This is the great spreading body method; the crown jing (ding jing 頂精) must be all the better led.

- The eyes' spirit fixes on the left foot; otherwise one may fear stepping where one should not step, and thus standing would be unstable — hence the eyes' spirit stays here.

- Left hand behind; the arm bent one or two degrees, the fingers slightly hooked as if digging.

- Method of retreating: the feet step backward in retreat, opening into large strides.

- Left forefoot presses the ground with force.

(Diagram: a figure in a low, backward-retreating posture — left foot forward on the forefoot, right foot flat, waist deeply bent back, spine led upright, right hand raised with the elbow slightly bent, left hand behind with slightly hooked fingers.)

Trang sách 152 · scan p185

(Marginal title) Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo

This is the old style. The chest is two chi from the ground — people today can all do this no longer, so the posture is slightly altered, avoiding the difficult and taking the easy. Yet in liveliness of movement it surpasses the old style, so it is specially drawn here to show the old style's original form, lest it be lost to posterity.

Diagram 1: Cang shen zuo zhuan tu (藏身左轉圖, "Diagram of hiding the body, turning left")

- Left hand (左手)

- Right foot (右足)

- The left toes and sole touch the ground first (左足趾與足掌先着地)

Dao Juan Hong (倒捲紅) retreats from the position of Zhou Di Kan Quan (肘底看拳, Elbow-Bottom Guarding the Fist); from front to back it goes until Bai E Liang Chi (白鵝亮翅, White Goose Spreads Wings), and must stop only when the left foot is behind. This is the dividing line between Dao Juan and the next posture (xia shi 下勢).

Diagram 2: Shou jin, zuo jiao you zhuan tu (手進左腳右轉圖, "Hand advances, left foot, turning right — diagram")

- Upper left (上左)

- Moon-like bend (月如彎) — new posture

- The fist goes downward (拳往下倒)

- Turning, moving backward and down (轉往後下運)

- The left hand stops here (左手至此止)

- Right foot (右足)

- Left foot (左足)

Dao Juan Hong (倒捲紅, "Reverse Reeling the Red")

What is Dao Juan Hong? The feet retreat; the hand turns over from above downward, falls back and reels. "Red" (hong 紅) means striking with full force without sparing face — hence the name Dao Juan Hong. The jing (精) of the fingertip pad goes from inside to below, from below to outside, then coils from outside upward and returns inside — this is reverse-coiling jing (dao chan jing 倒纏精), a slanting coil method (xie chan fa 斜纏法). From the armpit it coils slantwise to the hand, then from the hand coils to the inner side of the shoulder, then from the inner shoulder from inside going down, out, and up to inside, coiling slantwise to the fingertip pad — this is half...

Trang sách 153 · scan p186

陳氏太極拳圖說 卷一二 (Chen's Illustrated Explanation of Taiji Boxing, Vol. 1, Part 2)

Quan shen fa (圈身法, Body-Coiling Method)

The quan shen fa (圈身法): the foot method (足法, zu fa) all follows the hand method (手法, shou fa) in reverse coiling — this is the appearance of retreating (倒卷, dao juan). When the left hand is behind and moves from rear to front, the right hand moves to the rear; when the right hand, from the rear, coils in reverse to the front (front means in front of the face, its position is above), the left hand immediately coils in reverse to the rear (rear means behind the body, its position is below). When the left hand arrives above, the right hand arrives below; when the right hand coils back to above, the left hand immediately coils to below. The hands follow the feet's backward stepping as their standard: the left foot retreats one step, roughly three chi (尺, ~1 meter), and the left hand likewise completes one reverse circle. With the left foot behind, the left hand is also behind. When the right foot retreats to the rear, then the left hand and left foot are both in front. When the right foot retreats to the rear, the right hand also, following the right foot, completes one reverse circle to the rear. With right foot and right hand behind, the left hand and left foot arrive in front; with left hand and left foot in front, the right hand and right foot go behind. The two hands alternate one after another, exchanging front and back, running in succession, the circles round like the turning of a wheel — only, the wheel turns all together forward, whereas here the two hands alternately travel backward.

Qu xiang (取象, Taking the Image)

This posture retreats, chest and belly in front. Kun (坤, the Receptive) is the belly hexagram, so the image is taken from Kun. Kun says "first loses the way" (先迷) — the foot retreats backward, not knowing where it will land: this is "first losing the way." It says "afterwards gains the lord" (后得主) — the foot, retreating backward, finds its footing on the ground: the foot has a master. The Classic (《易经》) says "benefit," says "behind, compliant, attains constancy" (后顺得常): the foot has found the ground, the hand follows it — there is a constant measure. The First Line: "treading on frost; solid ice is coming" (履霜，坚冰至) — speaking of retreating: like treading on frost before solid ice, one should guard against future trouble. The Second says "straight, square, great" (直方大): in reverse-coiling retreat, the qi (气, khi) in the heart-mind is straight and square. "Without practice, nothing is not beneficial; the way of Earth is luminous" — though retreating, there is no harm. The Third Line: "holding inner beauty, one can remain correct" (含章可贞) — having the plan already formed in the chest, upright and firm. "Perhaps serving the king's affairs" — meaning, compelled by circumstance one retreats to hold one's position: "without accomplishments yet with completion" (无成有终). The Fifth Line: "yellow lower garment, greatly auspicious" (黄裳，元吉); the Image says "the pattern is within" (文在中也) — the ordering principles within the belly are clearly distinguished; beauty lies within. Though retreating in reverse coil, there is no injury. The Top Sixth: "dragons fight in the wild" (龙战于野) — speaking of retreating while the hands reverse-coil: this is combat. "Their blood dark and yellow" (其血玄黄) — if a strong enemy is in front, even with weapons, and one retreats and reverse-coils to fight — can one be certain of no injury? The Image of the Sixth says: "ending with the great" (以大终也). Yang is great, yin is small; Kun's opposite is Qian (乾) — by the way of firmness at the center, one brings the affair to completion. It says "benefit in lasting correctness" (利永贞): Kun is utmostly soft yet in movement firm, utmostly still yet in virtue square — hence retreat is without misgiving. The Sixth Five: "The noble person, yellow at the center, penetrates the pattern; occupying the correct position, dwelling in the body; beauty within, and flowing through the four limbs, issuing forth in achievement — beauty's utmost" — reverse coiling and retreating is beautiful in just this way. The left hand follows the left foot, the right hand follows the right foot — above and below follow one another: this carries the meaning of the Sui (随, Following) hexagram. Its Image-text says "movement and joy: following" (动而说随), hence it is taken again.

Yong dao juan hong chang duan ju li yu (咏倒卷红长短句俚语, Rustic Verse on Rolling-Back Red, in Long and Short Lines)

Watching through the curtain pearls roll back in reverse; upright qi (正气, chinh khi) threads through the center. Yin and yang change and alternate back and forth, following (continues on next page)

Trang sách 154 · scan p187

陳氏太極拳圖說 卷一二 (Chen's Illustrated Explanation of Taiji Boxing, Vol. 1, Part 2)

The 17th Posture: The Second Bai E Liang Chi (第二个白鹅亮翅, White Goose Spreads Its Wings, second occurrence)

The pivotal awareness (机, ji) looks left and glances right (左顾右盼). The retreat is straight, without deviation, one qi (气) threading through continuously, like two wheels turning. Do not raise the head to gaze afar; do not tilt the leg hanging high. Look carefully at both sides, the left and right hands — truly believe that the primordial qi of great harmony (太和元气) turns in reverse, entirely round.

Five-character rustic verse (五言俚语)

Raising the feet, all step forward; this posture alone retreats.

The two hands like sun and moon, turning in alternation without a sound.

The 17th Posture: The Second Bai E Liang Chi

Again spreading open the white goose's right wing; vainly raising the two hands to guard the chest. Sink the elbows, press down the shoulders, shaped like a moth's eyebrow; one spark of the living pivot lies in the heart's own design. (The left and right hands go north and must not be blunt and direct: the intent is like the curve of a moth's eyebrow, like the moon on the third or fourth day of the month. The right foot circles half a ring and steps out to the right; the left foot follows to meet the enemy. One can fix the principle in advance but cannot fix the situation in advance; hence one adapts to circumstances in the moment — where to lead (引) lies in one's own judgment.)

Five-character rustic verse

Above it receives Dao Juan Hong (倒卷红); below it links to the Brush-Knee posture (搂膝势). The living pivot is but one turn; leading from the right is by itself unimpeded.

The silk-reeling essence (缠丝精, chan si jing) above and below, and all the rules of method, are the same as in the previous White Goose Spreads Its Wings. The left foot, three to five cun (寸) from the right foot, touches the ground with its toes reversed — this is the empty stance (虚步), set up for the following Brush Knee Twist Step (搂膝拗步). The right foot steps out to the right, toes pointing northwest, planted flat and solid.

The left foot arrives behind; the right foot, beside the left foot with its toes touching the ground, immediately sends the right foot circling half a ring outward and steps out; the left foot follows the right foot to the right side, several cun from the right foot, toes touching the ground, settling the posture's incoming line (势脉). In the previous posture the left foot stood in the east unmoving and the right foot pointed at its side; hence in this posture it is very easy for the right foot to step out to the right. This empty standing of the right foot is precisely this posture's incoming line. In this posture the left foot is at the left①, toes touching the ground, making it easy for the following posture to step out to the left. Each posture receives from above and opens below — all like this. For the rest see the first White Goose Spreads Its Wings.

① The original edition reads "in this posture the left foot is at the right"; following the form of the posture and its principle, the character "right" has been corrected to "left."

(The right-hand page carries one drawing of the posture: standing with one foot forward, hands in the specific gesture, no caption.)

Trang sách 155 · scan p188

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo, Volume 2 — Qu xiang (Taking the Image)

In this posture the left hand follows the right hand in movement, the left elbow follows the right elbow, the left foot follows the right foot — just like the meaning in the *Dui* (兑, duì, Lake) hexagram of line two leaning on three, three leaning on four, four leaning on five; therefore the image is taken from Dui. Heart (xin 心) and kidneys (shen 肾) are within, like the central firmness of lines two and five; hands and feet are without, like Dui's third and top lines — softness outside. Meeting things with the sincerity of the heart through outer softness: though soft, within the delight there is real firm integrity — this is called "delight favorable through perseverance." The *Tuan* says: "Following Heaven and responding to humankind." Moving the hand with the heart, turning the circle with the flow of momentum — therein is the Way of Heaven: the upper Dui. The kidneys store will (zhi 志); the foot following the will also turns half a circle with the flow — therein is the Way of Man: the lower Dui. The first line is "harmonious delight," the second "sincere delight," the fourth "deliberated delight," the top six "leading delight": within, deliberating with a sincere heart; without, leading the advance forward with the qi of soft compliance — thus the firm qi lies concealed within softness. This posture is purely the essence of leading-in (yin jin 引进), so the image is taken from Dui, and above all from the "leading delight" line. Again, in a person the heart is the master and the four limbs follow it, like line nine-five of the *Bi* (比, bǐ, Holding Together) hexagram occupying the honored place, possessing firm virtue while all the other lines cling to and assist it. The *Image* says: "Bi means holding together; those below comply and follow... because of the firm center. The restless now come, above and below respond." In this posture the four limbs move from the heart and all the sense-organs and frame comply gladly; therefore the image is also taken from Bi — and in the end all is carried out with the upright qi of Qian and Kun (乾坤, qián kūn).

The Eighteenth Posture: Lou Xi Ao Bu (搂膝拗步, lǒu xī ào bù — Brush Knee and Twist Step)

Diagram

The drawing shows a person in stance: left leg forward with bent knee, right leg extended behind; the left hand circles to the side of the left knee, the right hand raised in front of the chest. Labels beside the figure:

- 左足手精皆倒转，外往里缠 — "The jing of the left foot and hand are all reversed, spiraling from outside inward."

- 左右足足精、腿精用包合法，皆是外往里缠 — "The foot-jing and leg-jing of both left and right use the enveloping method (bao he fa 包合法); all spiral from outside inward."

Text

In lou xi ao bu, the right hand winds around to the front, loosely enveloping (xu xu long zhu 虚虚笼住); the left hand winds around to the rear and gathers in the hand, also loosely enveloping; the right hand is a chi (尺) and more from the chest, the left hand six or seven cun from the back. The waist between is slightly bent, the chest joined and closed — there is the capacity to envelop the myriad phenomena, the air of possessing the upright qi of Heaven and Earth. The heart is calm and the qi harmonious; fix the gaze quietly on the tip of the middle finger of the right hand. The crotch jing (dang jing 裆精) is stretched round, yet must also be empty.

(Printed page number: 155)

Trang sách 156 · scan p189

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo, Volume 2 (continued from previous page)

Text (continued)

The knees join their jing; the big toes of both feet wrap inward; the sole of the foot, front and back, must exert force, treading the ground level and firm; all the remaining rules are as in the First Lou Xi Ao Bu (搂膝拗步, lǒu xī ào bù — Brush Knee and Twist Step).

The jing of the left foot and hand are all reversed, spiraling from outside inward. The foot-jing and leg-jing of both left and right use the enveloping method (bao he fa 包合法); all spiral from outside inward.

Qu xiang (取象 — Taking the Image)

The preceding lou xi ao bu took the directions of Qian (乾, qián), Kun (坤, kūn), Kan (坎, kǎn) and Li (离, lí), yet the meaning was not exhausted. When the boxing skill has matured, upright and respectful, reverent toward what one does, not daring to be self-satisfied — there is the meaning of humility (qian 谦); therefore the image is also taken from the *Qian* (Modesty) hexagram. Qian means to possess yet not dwell on it. The mountain is of the utmost height yet bends to dwell beneath the earth — the image of modesty. Stopping within, gathering in and not boasting; complying without, and humbling oneself below others — the meaning of modesty. The *Image* says: "The Way of Heaven descends to aid and is bright; the Way of Earth is lowly and moves upward. Heaven diminishes the full and augments the humble; Earth changes the full and flows to the humble; ghosts and spirits harm the full and bless the humble; the Way of Man hates the full and loves the humble." Humility brings benefit. If a person can shepherd himself with lowliness, he will naturally have a serene, tolerant bearing; this outwardly formed state matches line two, "sounding modesty, persevering brings good fortune." The right hand is in front, the left hand behind; the left foot slightly forward, the right foot slightly back, the two feet more than two chi (尺) apart. The heart runs the movement wholly with respectfulness; though the body has a divided form, the heart has its master — this matches line three, "toiling modesty," and line four (扐 — per Lai's note, "to divide") of modesty: not boastful, not expansive, composed and easy in manner. Though called practicing martial arts, the civil is within it. Line five, "it furthers one to invade and conquer," and top six, "sounding modesty, it furthers one to lead armies" — these are the meanings contained within the boxing. If one can truly be humble, where in dwelling of heart would it not be fitting — how could it apply only to lou xi ao bu! Yet lou xi ao bu all the more must not be let go, hence again the image of Qian.

The Second Lou Xi Ao Bu — Six-Character Folk Rhyme (六言俚语)

Before there was lou xi ao bu; now again lou xi ao bu. Not that one loves to make extra trouble — without it one cannot open the step. The White Goose hands (bai e shou 白鹅手) are both on the right; here the right is in front and the left behind. The crotch opens across a full chi, letting people assail from all four sides. This body relies wholly on the empty-spirit (xu ling 虚灵); every organ and limb is attended to. Moreover, humility unceasing — what worry, what fear has the gentleman? Let cunning and craft spring up as they may, firmness and softness are stocked from the start. The Qian hexagram: Gen (艮, gèn) below, Kun (坤, kūn) above. Gen is the hand — it can stop things with the hand. Gen inverted is Zhen (震, zhèn), the yang in the two feet. Kun is compliance; opposed to Qian, which is vigor — hence it is said firmness and softness are both complete. Zhen is the foot, hence the mention of feet.

(Printed page number: 156)

Trang sách 157 · scan p190

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo, Volume 2

The Nineteenth Posture: Shan Tong Bei, First Half (闪通背前半势, shǎn tōng bèi qián bàn shì — Flash Through the Back, first half of the posture)

Diagram: path of the right hand (右手运行图)

A curved-loop diagram with directional markers: North/above (北/上), South/below (南/下).

Text

The right foot steps open toward the west; the right hand immediately turns one circle from south to north, then the right hand, turned sideways, sweeps down toward the crotch; the crown and neck must be good and the central qi (zhong qi 中气) connected, running through the twenty-one... eleven segments of the spine below. The left hand follows the right hand and also winds one circle; the left foot retreats and steps open to the rear (left side); the left hand sweeps down from above and again sweeps up from below to behind the back. This is a half-posture diagram, not a resting posture.

Figure

A drawing of a person in a low, forward-leaning stance: right hand in front; labels — 眼看着右手 "the eyes look at the right hand"; 腰弯下 "the waist bends down"; 膝屈住 "the knees stay bent"; 左手到东 "the left hand arrives at the East."

Diagram: path of the left hand, initial rise (左手运行图·初起)

A loop diagram with West (西) and down (下) markers.

Text (footwork)

The right foot first withdraws without touching the ground, steps open westward six or seven cun (寸), and only then plants on the ground. The left foot steps open toward the East about one chi (尺) and plants level on the ground. The right foot is gathered back to the west — see the preceding diagram for how it is gathered. The left foot goes from west to east, the left foot lands in the east position.

Figure notes

This is due west; the right foot lands here, pointing to the northwest. Below the crotch is the interior. This is the foot of Bai E Liang Chi (白鹅亮翅, bái é liàng chì — White Goose Spreads Its Wings).

(Printed page number: 157)

Trang sách 158 · scan p191

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo, Volume 2

Shan Tong Bei (middle section) — diagram (闪通背·中截, shǎn tōng bèi zhōng jié — Flash Through the Back, middle interception)

Figure and labels

A drawing of a person in a left bow stance: the left hand raised overhead, the right hand extended forward, the eyes watching the right hand. Labels:

- 頂精不可失 — "The jing of the crown must not be lost."

- 左手在东 — "The left hand is at the east."

- 眼看住右手 — "The eyes fix on the right hand."

- 右手在东 — "The right hand is at the east."

- 右足拧转不动 — "The right foot twists in place and does not move."

- 左足至西 — "The left foot arrives at the west" (the left foot is at the west).

Text

The previous right foot was at the west; here the left foot turns a reversed step past the front of the right foot, passing the right foot by more than two chi (尺), and lands at the west without pausing. One posture is not yet complete — how could one halt even briefly? Readers, do not mistake the inner jing. In the preceding posture, when the waist bent down, the central qi (zhong qi 中气) rose in reverse from the twenty-first segment at the base of the spine, traveled up, passed in front of the crown and swept down to the dantian (丹田, dān tián); in this posture the central qi issues from the dantian, travels in reverse across the chest to the crown of the head, crosses the brain and descends, returns below, is caught at the twenty-first segment, still in reverse; going up it passes the crown and descends, returning again to the dantian. Here the Du vessel (督脉, dū mài — Governing Vessel) moves in reverse and catches the Ren vessel (任脉, rèn mài — Conception Vessel), goes down and turns back; the Ren vessel moves in reverse, catches the Du vessel, moves in reverse to the crown and descends, returning again to the dantian. In the previous posture the Du moved in reverse and the Ren forward, making only one circle; in this posture the Ren moves in reverse, catches the Du over the crown, descends forward to the twenty-first segment and turns back. The Du again moves in reverse, passes over the crown and sweeps down, catches the Ren, which descends forward and returns to the dantian — Ren first and Du after makes one circle, then Du first and Ren after makes another circle. This is the middle-intercepted posture, and the vessels, Du and Ren, turn two full circles back and forth.

(Printed page number: 158)

Trang sách 159 · scan p192

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation), Book 2 (卷二).

Diagram (final section of Shan Tong Bei 閃通背)

Line drawing of a practitioner in the Shan Tong Bei (Flash Through the Back) posture: left forward bow stance, left hand extended forward, right fist held at the chest, head turned to gaze at the left hand, torso slightly twisted. Labels:

- ding jing ling hao 頂精領好 — the crown's essence well suspended

- yan kan zuo shou 眼看左手 — eyes watch the left hand

- xin zai you chui 心在右捶 — attention in the right fist

- dang kai yuan 裆开圆 — crotch open and rounded

- you shou zhan zai hou 右手展在后 — right hand extended behind

- shan tong bei jie xian 闪通背界限 — boundary of Shan Tong Bei

- bu dong zuo zu 不动左足 — left foot unmoving

- you jiao dao zhuan 右脚倒转 — right foot rotates back around

- you zu dao xi 右足到西 — right foot arrives at the west

- right foot / left foot / dantian 丹田

Caption below the diagram: This is the final-section diagram of Shan Tong Bei; its boundary reaches only to here — what follows is Yan Shou Chui (演手捶, Deliver the Fist). The internal essence after passing the front crotch along the Ren meridian is received by the Du meridian, reverses direction with the central qi (中氣), slowly rises, crosses the brain to Baihui (百會) at the crown. The internal essence travels reverse — the boundary stops here. This diagram combined with the previous two makes three diagrams for one posture.

Commentary

The left hand, ready to answer the right hand behind, holds the potential of stepping forward and striking with the fist; the chest must be closed. This is still a non-stopped (transitional) posture: one must wait until Yan Shou Chui is finished, pause briefly, then begin the next posture. The left foot does not move; only the heel twists — the toes pointing west turn east, which is why the left foot ends at the east: the right foot steps behind the left.

This diagram shows the rear hand, already setting up the following Yan Shou Chui, so the stored fist has entered the launching frame.

Shan Tong Bei is a reversed circle. The left foot merely rises, first opens one step to the west and, staying at the west, only manages the twisting of the heel. The right foot rotates from west to east, then from east to west — completing one reversed circle; the right hand follows the right foot's path, rising alone. At first the right hand reaches down with forward-rotating essence (順纏); thereafter it all follows the right foot's reversal, using reversed essence (倒轉精). When bending at the waist in Shan Tong Bei, the left hand is behind (east): its internal essence coils in reverse from hand to armpit; when the left foot arrives west, the left hand's essence goes from armpit up the inside and outward in forward coiling (順纏) to the hand; when the left foot reverses to the east, the left hand is also east, still forward-coiled to the hand, lightly suspended (虛虛領住) awaiting the right fist's eastward strike to answer it. At the start, the right foot and right hand are west; the right hand sweeps down from above, the body rises, reversing half a circle; the right foot east, the right hand uses reversed coiling (倒纏) and is also east. When the right foot reverses to the west, the right hand follows the body to the west. Extending the arm to gather (捋) the fist, fist level with the waist, left hand in front at shoulder level — this is Shan Tong Bei, the standard pattern (正格) of the two hands, the fixed, unchanging boundary of the two hands in Shan Tong Bei.

What is meant by Shan Tong Bei? The central qi descends from the heart past the navel to the dantian, then from the dan— (continues on the next page)

Trang sách 160 · scan p193

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Book 2.

Commentary (continuing from the previous page)

…(dan)tian and the Ren meridian (任脈) reverse-travel upward, past the navel, Shangwan (上脘), Huagai (華蓋), Tiantu (天突), Lianquan (廉泉) to Chengjiang (承漿, below the lower lip). The Du meridian (督脈) receives it, reverse-travels through Shuigou (水溝), Renzhong (人中), Suliao (素髎, nose tip), past Shenting (神庭), Shangxing (上星), Xinhui (顖會), Qianding (前頂) to Baihui (百會); descending past Houding (後頂), Qiangmen (強門), Naohu (腦戶), Fengfu (風府), Yamen (啞門), Dazhui (大椎), Taodao (陶道), Shenzhu (身柱), Shendao (神道), Lingtai (靈台), Zhiyang (至陽), Jinsuo (筋縮), Jizhong (脊中), Xuanshu (懸樞), Mingmen (命門), Yangguan (陽關), Yaoshu (腰俞) to Changqiang (長強) — all the back-shu points of the spine — and again to Huiyin (會陰), the extreme (the starting point below where the front Ren and rear Du meridians both begin). The central qi (中氣) from Baihui descends and connects through Changqiang and Huiyin: this is called "passing through the back" (通背). "Flash" (閃) means: as if someone grabs you from behind at the waist, the front waist bends forward sharply, head and shoulders dip down, while at the rear Changqiang and Huantiao (環跳, the outer hip bone) forcefully lift the lower abdomen upward and flip it over; the opponent's hands come apart, is turned upside down, flashes over your head to the front, and falls backward before you. This is called Shan Tong Bei (閃通背, Flash Through the Back).

The body is a reversed circle; the right hand reaches the top and reverses with it, the right hand being the leader. The hand turns with the body — in truth it turns with the foot: wherever the right foot lands, the right hand follows. The left hand likewise turns with the body in a reversed circle.

Diagrams

Right Hand Movement Diagram (右手运行圖)

Compass: north top, south bottom, west left, east right. Path labels:

- the right hand begins facing west

- the right hand arrives east, then reverses southward

- the right hand arrives west

Left Hand Movement Diagram (左手运行圖)

Compass: north top, south bottom, west left, east right. Path labels:

- the left hand begins, arrives east

- the left hand arrives west, then reverses southward

- the left hand begins, arrives west

Symbolism (取象)

Originally the head is above yet moves downward; facing west it reverses, going north then east, then reverses from east, facing south completing one great circle. This turning — passing from this to that — is the great body-turning method (大轉身法) of the fist. Reversal belongs to yin: Da Guo (大過, hexagram 28, Greater Exceeding), Xun below and Dui above — eldest daughter and youngest daughter, both yin images — hence the symbol is taken from Da Guo. The first and top lines are both yin lines, like the hands and feet at the two ends, yielding and obedient. The middle lines two, three, four, five are all Qian (乾) way, like my heart moving the four limbs with firm, vigorous power. Moreover Qian inverted gives Kun (坤), firmness and softness completing each other: though the body makes a great turn, the four limbs obey without obstruction. Hence line four of the hexagram: "The ridgepole rises — good fortune" (棟隆，吉), speaking of the flood-like qi (浩然之氣)… (continues)

Trang sách 161 · scan p194

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Book 2 (卷二).

Commentary (continuing from the previous page)

…sufficing to fill one heart; hence the Great Image says the junzi (君子, noble person) "stands alone without fear," and meets no harm. The Xiang commentary says: "Da Guo (大過, Greater Exceeding): the great one exceeds" (in the fist we take only its general sense — the guo 過 of passing, as when a boat canopy passes an angle) — meaning the great turn of the body passes over. It says "the ridgepole sags: root and branch are weak" — a metaphor for hands and feet. It says "firmness exceeding yet central" (a metaphor for the upright qi of the heart: though the body makes a great turn it can still attain the middle way). It says "yielding and joyfully moving" (metaphor for hands and feet yielding in obedience). It says "beneficial to have somewhere to go, hence success" (metaphor for the fist: no turn is without advantage; it always succeeds). It says "Great indeed is the timeliness of Da Guo!" The principles of the Book of Changes are supremely supple, supremely great, all-embracing — what affair under heaven could fall outside them? That I take Da Guo is only because the meaning of the words fits the general idea; how dare I use Change theory to illuminate the fist!

Shan Tong Bei, Five-Character Vernacular Verse (閃通背五言俚語)

A bronze slab presses down the back;

the whole body stores up its qi.

The hip bone flips over sharply;

the skull plunges downward.

However heavy — a thousand catties —

it can be made to fall to the ground.

Shan Tong Bei, Seven-Character Vernacular Verse, First Set (第一閃通背七言俚語)

I.

The ancients left us Shan Tong Bei;

the right palm chops down in a great turn.

The right foot draws back to the geng-xin position (庚辛, the west);

the whole body gains the posture, outdoing mighty Qin.

II.

Why are shoulder and arm named "flash through the back"?

From the Du meridian to Changqiang is the exact center.

Rising from below is called reversed essence (倒精);

the enemy flashes to my front — I shoot within it.

III.

At first, Deliver the Fist (演手捶) went west;

why does this move alone turn east?

A word to you: if strong, do not use all your force —

guard against a cunning enemy attacking from behind.

IV.

Since ancient times worldly affairs have all differed;

how could the fist be done one single way?

Each move has its own posture;

don't let outsiders laugh that it doesn't make sense.

Up close, bend the elbow and use full effort;

at a distance, how can the arm not extend?

Moreover the enemy's attacks come without fixed pattern;

transforming lies entirely within one heart.

Since the great turn of Shan Tong Bei —

one wave, three folds, marvelous beyond telling.

The waters of Yu Gate rise in three-tiered waves (one change of Shan Tong Bei, one strike);

would any fisherman fail to ask the ford? (the enemy again comes from the east)

East comes, east strikes — originally no fixed form;

this single strike settles heaven and earth.

People say there are many marvels here:

the one flood-like qi (浩然一氣) moves with natural truth.

Trang sách 162 · scan p195

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說).

20th Posture: Yan Shou Chui 演手捶 (Deliver the Fist / Strike with the Hand)

Hand-Movement Diagram

Line drawing of the hands' trajectory with directional arrows and hand icons.

Labels:

- you shou qi chu 右手起處 — where the right hand rises

- xiang dong pian yi ying 向东偏以应 — angled east to respond

- zuo shou zai zhong wei 左手在中微 — left hand in the middle, slightly…

- right hand passes over the left wrist (cong zuo shou wan guo 从左手腕过)

- qu 去 — goes away

- you shou luo chu 右手落處 — where the right hand lands

- dong 东 — east

Main Figure (rear view)

Line drawing of a practitioner in a low stance facing east, wearing a long-sleeved jacket. Labels:

- zuo er ting zhu hou mian 左耳听住后面 — left ear listening to the rear

- ding jin ling zhu 顶劲领住 — crown jin (energy) suspended

- ci yan shou chui bei mian 此演手捶背面 — this is the rear view of Yan Shou Chui

- yan kan zhe you shou 眼看着右手 — eyes watching the right hand

- zuo zhou chen xia 左肘沉下 — left elbow sinks down

- dang yuan 裆圆 — crotch rounded

- xi cheng zhu 膝撑住 — knee propping up

- yao jing xia hao 腰精下好 — waist essence well settled down

- shen xiang dong si tan bu tan 身向东似贪不贪 — body toward east, as if reaching yet not reaching

- you shou you xi xiang dong ji 右手由西向东击 — right hand strikes from west to east

Footwork Diagram

Three foot positions in line drawing. Labels:

- you zu qi 右足起 — right foot rises

- you zu ru deng 右足如蹬 — right foot like a tread/push

- zuo zu ping ta; zuo zu you xi jin bu dao dong 左足平踏，左足由西进步到东 — left foot treads flat; the left foot advances from west to east

- zuo zu zai zhong bu dong 左足在中不动 — the left foot in the middle does not move

- you zu luo 右足落 — right foot lands

Trang sách 163 · scan p196

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Book 2 (卷二).

Opening Instruction (引蒙)

In Shan Tong Bei (閃通背) the right foot rests east and does not move; the left foot starts from the west, steps past the right foot and advances①, the step landing east of the right foot. In Shan Tong Bei the right hand is at the west; raised from below and closed, the fist strikes east. The left hand spreads open; the right fist sweeps past it and strikes east; the left elbow slightly wards west. The internal essence descends from the dantian (丹田), passes the crotch and rear, then reverse-travels from Changqiang (長強) up to Baihui (百會), descending to the shoulder, advancing and moving into the right fist. Only when the spirit of the whole body gathers into the fist is there power. Left and right feet press the ground, steady as a mountain, immovable on the earth — that is true power.

Symbolism (取象)

In this posture the spirit gathers in the right fist, like the Cui hexagram (萃卦) line one: "as if calling, one grasp" (the Lai note: speaking of a heart of sincerity 有孚). Facing forward and gripping the fist with the right hand — this is sincerity at its utmost. And the Classic says: "Cui brings success; beneficial to have somewhere to go" — hence this symbol is taken from Cui. In this posture the right foot advances one step from behind — one small exceeding (小過) — so the Small Exceeding hexagram is also taken. The right fist strikes from rear to front like thunder on a mountain, too swift to guard against; its advance is swifter than a bird's flight — this is the right fist's taking of Xiao Guo. The right fist is not soft or weak, so the image of Da Zhuang (大壯, Great Strength) "beneficial, correct" is also taken. The Tuan says: "Firmness in motion: hence strength (the right fist is pure central firmness filling the fist — hence strength)." The Xiang says: "Thunder above heaven: Da Zhuang (the right fist is like a thunderbolt in the sky — one strike, a clap of thunder, too quick to cover the ears)." Line one: "Strength in the toes — advancing brings misfortune (the right foot lands east and moves no more: this is the strength of the toes; to move again is unlucky, not to move is lucky)." Da Zhuang is Qian below, Zhen above: the qi of central firmness is moved into the fist, correct and solid, hence line two: "Correct — good fortune"; the Xiang says: "Nine in the second place: correct and lucky, because of central firmness."

Deliver the Fist, Second Set — Seven-Character Vernacular Verse (第二演手捶七言俚語)

I.

Suddenly an enemy comes from the east;

the right fist opens toward the east.

The right foot steps forward — do not slacken;

the attacker, in high spirits, returns face upturned.

II.

Lifting the foot, advancing to crush eastward,

fist-power like wind, the meaning like thunder (that is, its swiftness).

Ask why one more step is taken?

Because the next posture lies latent in embryo.

Note

① According to the requirement that the opening posture faces north, the original edition's "left" and "right" were reversed; "left" is corrected to "right" and "right" to "left."

Trang sách 164 · scan p197

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說).

21st Posture: Lan Ca Yi 攬擦衣 (Grasp and Wipe the Robe)

Diagram

Line drawing of a practitioner facing north in a bow stance (front leg bent). The right hand (viewer's left) extends forward toward the east in a grasping (拿, na) pose; the left hand (viewer's right) rests near the hip on the west side, also labeled na (拿). Direction labels: east (东) upper left, west (西) upper right. Diagram title: "Facing North Diagram" (面向北圖).

Five-Character Vernacular Verse (五言俚語)

The right arm extends, never bends;

the enemy dares not enter.

The left hand guards against a touch from the west;

one release — nothing so venomous.

So swift — who can withstand it?

The method belongs to me alone.

East is jia-yi wood (東方甲乙木);

seemingly straight, seemingly not,

the right hand spreads east,

the central qi moves from the heart —

how much more when advancing like wind.

The body is the same as other men's;

there is no special secret:

the clear spirit lies in one heart.

Only be able to move from the center —

among chickens, a crane stands out.

For those who study the fist

I have pointed out the whole way.

Commentary

The body methods up, down, left and right are all as in the first Lan Ca Yi; no need to repeat. As for the linking method: the right hand withdraws, then spreads again; the right foot draws in beside the left foot, toes touching the ground; then from the west it circles half a circle outward and spreads east. The left foot only twists on its heel, pointing north, staying in place. The right foot draws from east to west, then from west circles half a circle and lands east.

Method of the Right Hand (右手法)

The right hand withdraws from the east to the side of the body, circles toward the ribs, then spreads east; withdrawal and turning together make one full circle.

Trang sách 165 · scan p198

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo*, Volume 2

Taking the Images (取象 qǔ xiàng)

This image takes the general meaning of the two hexagrams Xiao Xu (小畜, "Small Accumulation") and Da Xu (大畜, "Great Accumulation"). The Xiao Xu hexagram says: "Bringing it about from my western suburbs" (this means the right hand moves from west toward east). The Image says: "In Xiao Xu, the yielding obtains its place and is responded to from above." The right hand belongs to yin; the fourth line, yin (六四), is a yin line. Qian below, Xun above — Qian within, Xun without; the yang strength is in the center, moved up and down to respond to the right hand, responding to the yin of the fourth line: this is Xiao Xu. The Image says: "Robust yet yielding; strong in the center and the will acts" (this means the right hand can extend only with the aid of yang; the right hand, being moved by the strength-centered second line, therefore extends). The Image says: "Wind traveling over heaven" (this means the right hand is like wind traveling over heaven — swift). The first line says: "Returning by one's own path" (this means the right hand, from below, turns and rises to settle in the east, also returning by its own path). The top line: "Rain has fallen, and resting, virtue is carried" (Xun is wind and rain, and is yin; the right hand is yin. Moving the central qi (中气 zhōngqì) with the right hand, its swiftness is like wind, and the yin disperses. When the top line changes, Kan becomes the cart below; the third line being yang, virtue is carried by the cart. This means the right hand moves the central qi within the hand). It says: "The moon is nearly full" (this means the central qi of the right hand is sufficient, like the moonlight of the sixteenth day — round and complete, visible). The second line: "Led back." The fifth line: "Possessing sincerity, linked together" (this means although the right hand belongs to yin, the yang qi is all linked and threaded through within the arm).

Da Xu: Qian within, Gen without. Gen is the hand; using the right hand to move and stop things, strong qi must act through its center. Qian's opposite (错 cuò) is Kun: hard and soft temper each other. Gen's reversed hexagram (综 zōng) is Zhen, and Zhen is the east. The right hand extends from the west toward the east. Zhen is the foot; the left and right feet tread the ground steadily. The Image says "Strong, robust, sincere, substantial" (this means the central qi of the fist is abundant); it says "The strong is above and honors the worthy" (this means the right hand's use is more convenient than the left's — slightly superior to the left hand); it says "It can stop the strong: great centrality" (this means the right hand can, with one hand, stop the enemy's strength — attaining the middle way); it says "Beneficial to cross the great river" (if one can cross a great river, then nothing is without benefit — the right hand is like this). The top line: "Where is the sky-road? Prosperous" (何 hé here read in the departing tone). The Image says: "The sky-road — the way greatly advances" (having accumulated for long, its way greatly advances). Moving the central qi within the right hand, attaining its way and greatly advancing, without the slightest obstruction.

Trang sách 166 · scan p199

Posture 22: The Second Single Whip (第二个单鞭 dì èr gè dān biān)

Four-Character Rhymes (四言俚语)

Where does numinous qi arise? It arises from the one heart/mind. Where does the central qi (中气 zhōngqì) return? It returns to the two kidneys. The heart-mind stirs and the will follows (the will is stored in the kidneys), moving my four limbs. The qi travels within the bones and fills the flesh. With long practice comes numinosity, a numinosity beyond compare. Touch and you instantly know; there is naturally a response. Neither separating nor clinging — sticking, connecting, adhering, following. Like a fly landed on glue: though it has wings it cannot fly away. The subtlety within this is subtle beyond subtlety.

Diagram: Facing North (面向北图)

Annotations around the figure (right to left):

- The left hand meets the right hand at the navel; once the joining is complete, it then unfolds from east to west, with the sense of a coiling line along the path — use the smooth coil (顺缠 shùnchán) energy

- West

- The foot momentarily taps and holds its place, joining with the right foot to unfold toward the west

- The toes of the left foot are drawn in beside the right foot

- The crown energy holds the head up

- The eyes look at the middle finger

- The crotch is open and rounded

- The right hand, not moving in the east, turns back in a circle from behind

- East

- The rear foot is in the east; the right rear heel twists and turns toward the west

- North

The Second Single Whip (第二单鞭)

The right hand turns from below and backward, circling a small circle toward the north; the left hand turns from within toward the north and then to the south, also circling a circle; then both hands join facing the face. The right hand unfolds toward the west with smooth-rotating energy; the left hand unfolds toward the east with reverse-rotating energy; gather and bind the five fingers together.

Trang sách 167 · scan p200

Taking the Images (取象 qǔ xiàng)

The first Single Whip took the four hexagrams Kan, Li, Pi, and Tai; the image taken for this posture is likewise so — one will understand by reading the earlier discussion of the taken images.

Seven-Character Rhymes (七言俚语)

The first Single Whip faces north; the second Single Whip still faces north. The former Single Whip follows Jingang (Buddha's Warrior Attendant); this one follows Yanshou (Cannon Fist) and the separation. Each posture's incoming channel differs of itself — not merely the hands and feet positioned toward the east. A point of numinous qi arises from the heart, ascending to the blue heavens above, entering the earth below. This qi travels within hands and feet, neither hard nor soft, naturally composed and at ease. What follows below is Cloud Hands — that is the outgoing path, and it is precisely the incoming channel (来龙 láilóng) of Cloud Hands. The eight bodily gates (顶 crown, 裆 crotch, 心 heart, 眼 eyes, 耳 ears, 手 hands, 足 feet, 腰 waist) and their crucial closings — you must remember them: human effort, perfected, rivals heaven's work.

Posture 23: Left and Right Cloud Hands (左右云手 yún shǒu)①

Annotations on the figure:

- Right hand (右手)

- Middle finger of the hand (手中指)

- The eyes attend to the right (眼着右)

- Crown lifted (顶起)

- Essence/energy (精)

- Both shoulders relax down together (两肩一齐松下)

Below the figure: In Single Whip, the left hand is in the west; it then leads the right hand up. The right hand moves to the east, while the left hand is drawn back from above down to in front of the left breast, five or six inches from the breast. When the left hand first leads, the shoulder immediately relaxes down. The right hand begins from the east (because in Single Whip the right hand is in the east, it starts here), withdraws to the right breast, turns upward in a smooth rotation, turns back toward the east, comes around again to the side of the right breast — one complete circle.

Left Cloud Hands (左云手)

The left foot is in the west; the left hand withdraws; the left foot, following the left hand, turns one circle and withdraws, together with… [text continues on the next page]

【Note】① The original edition names posture 23 "Right Moving Hands" (右运手); following the table of contents, it has been corrected here to "Left and Right Cloud Hands."

Trang sách 168 · scan p201

*(Continuation of Left and Right Cloud Hands — Left Cloud Hands, from the previous page)*

Annotations on the figure:

- The eyes look at the left hand (眼看左手)

- Relax (松) / right shoulder (右肩) / down (下)

- The elbow sinks down (肘沉下)

- The knee presses two or three inches (膝压二三寸)

- The crotch is open and rounded (裆开圆)

- Left foot (左足)

- Right foot (右足)

Vertical annotations (right to left):

- The left hand is in the west, leading the foot in the west. The left hand withdraws from below inward to the left breast, rises and turns westward to the western side — this is a smooth-rotating (顺转 shùnzhuǎn) circle. At this moment the right foot has just been withdrawn to beside the right breast.

- The left foot is in the west, waiting for the right foot to unfold; when the right foot withdraws and the left foot settles, it is drawn from the west to beside the right foot, turning half a circle, still…

Main text:

The left breast and it face each other above and below, pointing due north. The right foot is withdrawn to beside the left foot, then moves eastward one circle, alighting on the east and treading flat, pointing due north.

The left foot unfolds in the west; the right foot turns half a circle and withdraws to the west, facing the right breast above and below. The left hand moves half a circle to the west; then the right hand, from below, turning half a circle, withdraws inward to beside the right breast, five or six inches — or seven or eight inches — from the breast.

The right hand and right foot withdraw from the east to the right breast, then move again to the east — one full circle. The left hand rises from the west, turns downward half a circle and withdraws to beside the left breast; without pausing, the left hand from above the left breast smoothly rotates half a circle to the west — this is the left foot taking another step to the west. The right hand is immediately withdrawn from the east to beside the right breast; below, the right foot follows the right hand, turning half a circle from below and withdrawing to beside the left foot, six or seven inches from the left foot. When the right hand arrives in the east, the left hand has just arrived beside the left breast; when the left hand moves to the west, the right hand has just withdrawn to beside the right breast. The left foot bends slowly westward, stepping to the west; the right foot immediately turns from below and withdraws to beside the left foot; the right foot moves across above the left foot, advancing to the east, while the left foot moves down from the west and withdraws to beside the right foot. One replaces the other, back and forth, taking turns in circles, not bound to a fixed number — generally two or three circles (three circles: the left foot takes three steps westward, the right foot follows with three steps), stopping about a step and a half short of the position of the third Jingang Dao Dui (金刚捣碓 jīngāng dàoduì, "Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Mortar"), leaving room for the next posture, Gao Tan Ma (高探马 gāo tàn mǎ, "High Pat on Horse").

The two feet alternate, the turning mechanism never pausing. The left foot takes a step westward and the right foot follows it. Though it too takes a step, when the right foot is about to reach the left foot, it turns back five or six inches on the ground before finally alighting — only so does the movement show no straight steps. Every time the left foot steps out, the right foot follows it in the same manner. The two feet move westward, the face toward due north; the feet travel crosswise toward the west, not stepping toward due north. For instance, when the right hand smoothly rotates one circle: in the first half circle the central qi spirals obliquely from within the armpit outward to the fingers; in the second half circle, returning from the east, the energy spirals obliquely from outside back to the armpit… [text continues on the next page]

Trang sách 169 · scan p202

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume Two

(Continuing the preceding text) ...down; the left hand is likewise. As for the feet: the right foot's front half-circle coils from the leg root inside outward to the toes and returns, then coils from outside to inside back to the leg root; the left foot is likewise.

【Diagram】Diagram facing due north

Right-hand circle diagram:

- Above (north), East (right), Below (south)

- Inside the circle: the right hand is yin, like the shape of the moon

- Text around the circle: the right hand's half-circle turns the neck, the coiling silk applied descending, going east, returning from below, again five times by return; rising from outside, from the right hand

Left-hand circle diagram:

- Above (north), West (right), Below (south)

- Inside the circle: the left hand is yang, like the shape of the sun

- Text around the circle: the left hand rises from outside, moves upward, applies coiling; the center of the ground strikes, the left from the right descends, going west, returning from below, again five times by return

Together forming the upper circle.

Right-foot diagram:

- Above (north), West (right), Below (south), East (left)

- Inside the diagram: the right foot is east of the left foot

- Text around the diagram: the right foot advances east, returns from below, again five times by return

Left-foot diagram:

- Above (north), West (right), Below (south), East (left)

- Inside the diagram: the left foot is west of the right foot

- Text around the diagram: the left foot advances west, returns from below, again five times by return

Taking the Image (取象)

The left hand is yang; the right hand is yin, like the moon. Qian is heaven and is the head. The hands pass before the head, like sun and moon suspended in the sky. The Great Image says: "Sun and moon attain heaven and double their brightness, suspended in the sky" (日月得天重明，以麗乎天). The Great Image says: "The great man continues his brightness to illumine the four quarters (just as the fist's left and right hands illuminate the whole body)." First Nine: "Thrown into confusion, yet reverent — no blame (錯 means interlacing, east and west crossing as oblique movement; the opening step of the crossed fist is like this; one must take reverence as the master)." Join at the bosom; the chest must join empty — Li is empty within (離中虛); Li interchanges with Kan (離錯坎). The waist essence descends and the qi returns to the dantian (丹田, dāntián), matching Kan's fullness in the middle (坎中滿) — hence it takes from Li. The two hands being like sun and moon, they are also like thunder and wind (the hexagram Heng 恒, Xun below and Zhen above: Xun is wind, Zhen is thunder; the two hands move in alternation without cease, without interruption, having the meaning of duration. The hexagram Heng. The Great Image says: Heng means lasting. Sun and moon attain heaven and can shine long — this speaks of the Way of heaven and earth enduring without halt. Benefit in having somewhere to go: the end again has a beginning — hence it further takes from Heng (Nine in the second: regret vanishes, for one can long remain in the middle. Six in the fifth: constant in one's virtue. The merit is all like this).

Trang sách 170 · scan p203

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated

- The eyes look fixedly at the middle fingernail of the right hand

- The top jin (頂精) holds the central qi (中氣) leading

Five-character vernacular verses on Yunshou (运手五言俚语, Cloud Hands)

Two hands lead two feet, dancing left-right, east and west. First the left hand leads, the right hand follows going west. The right foot also gathers west; the two hands are level with the eyebrows. The hands a bit more than a chi apart, turning slowly inside and out (both coils go from inside outward). The central qi threads the middle of the spine — nowhere may it tilt. When the right foot withdraws (this is the final non-dancing moment), the left hand halts in the west.

Seven-character vernacular verses (七言俚语)

Two hands turn like a ring, east and back to west; two feet travel sideways — a strange stepping method. Coming and going the qi moves, unceasing and lasting; two suns suspended in pairs shine on heaven and earth (it has an intimate connection with Li 離 and Heng 恒).

The Twenty-Fourth Posture: Gao Tan Ma (高探马, High Pat the Horse)

【Diagram】Single-practice posture diagram: a man in a long robe, the left leg bowed in front and the right leg stretched behind, the body leaning slightly forward; the right hand extends straight forward, the palm slanting downward; the left hand is bent beside the left hip; the head turns toward the right hand, the eyes on the middle fingernail of the right hand.

Yunshou (运手, Cloud Hands) faces north; this diagram, facing north, turns to face south — this is the old style. The left hand leads and holds; the right hand circles one turn from the east and again leads and holds; the left foot draws back one step and lands to the east, and the face is then turned south. The right hand follows the left hand circling one turn eastward, turning back to the west; the body twists around, in front. The right hand is in front, the back of the hand spread open facing upward. As the left foot follows the right hand circling eastward, the right foot first opens one step back, its heel then shifting to point southwest; the left foot then retreats one great step, landing east of the right foot.

The right foot follows the right hand to the east. The right foot first retreats backward, the foot landing; with one twist of the right heel, the toes point southwest.

—— 170 ——

*Translator's note: Page 170 (book page). Contains the closing verses of the Yunshou (运手, Cloud Hands) posture — a five-character vernacular verse and a seven-character vernacular verse — and the opening of the twenty-fourth posture Gao Tan Ma (高探马, Gāo tàn mǎ, "High Pat the Horse"), with a diagram description of the single-practice pose and the initial footwork (the right heel twisting so the toes point southwest).*

Trang sách 171 · scan p204

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume Two

What is Gao Tan Ma (高探馬, High Pat the Horse)? It is like a horse grown tall and large: mounting it, one first reaches the hand out to explore its saddle-bow (鞍橋) — hence the name. In Yunshou (运手, Cloud Hands), both hands are in the west; the enemy sends his hand to invade my left arm; I immediately lead and hold with the right hand and draw him with the left hand, making him advance. To make the enemy advance, one must first step the right foot back one step to the east; after the drawn foot [comes], one then immediately folds the left hand back to the west and strikes — this is why the hand turns one circle. At the time of drawing him in, the right hand is in the east; the right foot follows the right hand, retreating one step and landing in the east, twisting with the rear heel so it points southwest. The left foot then retreats one great step, landing east of the right foot. The right foot① is the solid foot; the left foot② is the empty foot. Above, the left elbow strikes back, and the body twists around to face south; the left hand immediately withdraws, landing below the left nipple, the wrist facing upward, two or three cun from the chest, guarding the chest and body. Then, twisting from the west around to the south, the right hand and arm immediately spread open westward, the wrist facing down, joining with the left wrist — like the complete saddle-exploring horse posture. The right hand is forward-turning essence (顺转精); the left hand is reverse-turning essence (倒转精).

【Diagram】Diagram of the inner essence turning in the left and right hands (the face already turned south): the right hand goes west, arriving at the exploring face; the right hand in the south; the right hand in the west; west is front.

【Diagram】Left-hand diagram: the left hand comes out downward from the left shoulder, withdrawing to below the left nipple; the right hand extends forward from the right arm.

【Diagram】Footwork diagram (east/west): the left foot retreats one step east; the right foot retreats one step east; this follows the body method.

Taking the Image (取象)

In this posture the right hand is in front and also above; the left hand is behind and also below. The chest has...

① The original version reads "是实脚" (is the solid foot); according to the requirements of the boxing posture and principle, it is supplied as "the right foot is the solid foot."

② The original version reads "右脚是虚脚" (the right foot is the empty foot); according to the boxing posture and principle, the character "右" (right) is corrected to "左" (left).

*Translator's note: Page 171 (book page), Volume Two: the name-explanation for Gao Tan Ma (高探馬, Gāo tàn mǎ, High Pat the Horse), the drawing-in and counter-striking sequence, three diagrams (hand-turning, left hand, footwork), the textual-critical footnotes correcting solid/empty foot attributions, and the opening of the "Taking the Image" section. Trang sách 172 · scan p205

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated)

Reserved and contained, utterly empty (xu 虛). The hand outside is substantial (shi 實), the mind within is empty — this has the image of Li 離 (Fire). The two feet are empty in front and solid behind; the crotch (dang 裆) is rounded, the knees open yet joined. Zhen 震 (Thunder) is the foot: its upper two lines resemble the thigh and the lower leg, its lower line resembles the foot joined. Zhen below and Li above — this is the hexagram Shi He 噬嗑 ("Biting Through"), therefore it is taken from Shi He. In practicing the form one cannot fail to strike people; without striking people one cannot defend the body — of what use would it be? "There is something in the jaws: that is called Shi He" (the hand is above; before one strikes a person, there is first the posture of striking — like the image of the jaws in which there is not yet anything. When one comes to strike a person, there will be something in the jaws). The Great Image says: "Firm and yielding are divided" (this means the foot below belongs to Zhen — firmness is below; the hand above is mostly Li. Yet in Li the upper and lower lines are both yang, like the hand of the fist being below and above; the middle yin line is like the mind at the center, utterly empty and utterly alert), "moving and bright" (when the hands and feet wish to move, the mind first perceives clearly and commands it), "thunder and lightning join and become manifest" (the foot is like Zhen, thunder; the mind is like the brightness of Li — inner and outer, above and below, each becomes a coherent pattern in itself), "the yielding obtains the center and moves upward" (the two hands extend left and right, like Li obtaining the middle way and moving upward). The Great Image says: "Thunder and lightning: Shi He" (this means that a person's having a mind is like the brightness of lightning, and having feet and hands is like the firmness of thunder). The first line says: "destroying the toes" (meaning I destroy the enemy's toes); the second line: "biting the flesh, destroying the nose" (meaning I strike the person's flesh and also strike his nose flat); the fourth line: "biting dried gristle"; the fifth line: "biting dried meat" (meaning even if I meet a strong enemy, I need not worry). The image taken is Shi He: it means that I possess the equipment of biting-through; although I have not yet bitten through, within there is the mind of biting-through and without there is the form of biting-through — in time there will surely be an affair of biting-through; it is only that the biting-through has not yet happened. Affairs have what must come; principles have what is inherently so — all these are words of anticipatory resolution. That the boxing takes Shi He likewise carries the meaning of anticipating in advance. Moreover, the two legs are at the sides with emptiness in the middle, like the hollow center of Li; the right hand is above with the two ribs at the sides, like Gen 艮 (Mountain) as an overturned bowl. The hollow center of Li moves upward and penetrates to the heart — the image of the heart-fire (xin huo 心火). When this heart-fire stirs and is transported to the right hand, that is "fire beneath the mountain," so it is further taken from Bi 贲 ("Adornment"). The Great Image says: "Yielding comes to adorn the firm" (this means the yielding of the lower body comes to adorn — for what humans rely on is culture, ren wen 人文: resplendent with propriety in mutual greeting, which is where adornment comes to rest, and sharply distinct in mutual keeping of bounds — this is an analogy for the right hand above, able to stop and able to guard). "Observe the patterns of Heaven to discern the changes of the seasons; observe the patterns of humanity to transform and complete the world" (this means the boxing has the mind transporting to the hand, and can thereby win people's hearts). The first line: "adorns his toes" (Gen is the inverse of Zhen 震; Zhen is the foot, having the image of toes; Gen is the hand — meaning the boxing has the sense of hand and foot guarding one another). The second line: "adorns his beard" (on the chin it is called beard xu 鬚, on the upper lip mustache zi 髭, on the cheeks whiskers ran 髯; "adorning the beard" means even in small places one is compliant). The third line's image says: "The good fortune of lasting perseverance — in the end nothing encroaches upon it" (with the High Pat on Horse 高探馬 raised high, no one dares come near). The fifth line: "adorned at the hill garden" (the pat raised high is like the horse at the hill garden). The Great Image says: "The good fortune of the sixth line five lies in having joy" (no one dares encroach — what joy could match it?). The top line nine: "white adornment, no blame." The Great Image says: "White adornment, no blame — the highest attains its will." Between Heaven and Earth, form is emptiness and emptiness is form; form, form, emptiness, emptiness; emptiness, emptiness, form, form —

Trang sách 173 · scan p206

陳氏太極拳圖說卷二 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume II)

Non-being gives birth to being, and being returns to non-being — with what thing is it not so? How could it be so with the boxing alone? How could it be so with the High Pat on Horse 高探馬 in the boxing alone? When an art reaches this point, the heart is satisfied and everything is judged fitting!

High Pat on Horse — Seven-Character Folk Verses

The First

An eight-chi horse and above is called a dragon: standing alone on Wu Mountain, the highest peak. Only because one wishes to ride a thousand li at speed does he raise his hand high to pat — Zhao She enfeoffed as Lord of Mafu (Zhao She, descendant of Boyi, was enfeoffed as Lord of Mafu 馬服君).

The Second

North of Ji, picking the finest from the herd is hardest of all; a horse of eight chi in height is not easy to pat. If one can truly stand in a posture transcending the vulgar crowd, in a single day a thousand li can be freed from the saddle.

The Twenty-Fifth Form: Right Heel Kick (You Ca Jiao 右擦脚)

【Diagram】Single-person practice-posture diagram (Right Heel Kick), with labels beside the figure:

- jing 領 at the crown of the head (ding ding jing 頂頂精)

- the eyes watch the right hand

- jing joined together (合住精)

- just now with the left hand

- joined in jing with the right hand

- outside — going from outside to inside is the coiling direction (fang 外往內纏方)

- the left elbow sinks down

- the waist bends forward one or two fen

- the buttocks point downward
