Trang sách 174 · scan p207

Posture 26: Left Foot Rub (左擦脚 zuǒ cā jiǎo)

Annotations on the figure:

- 頂精領住 dǐng jīng lǐng zhù — the crown force threads upward and holds

- 眼看左手 yǎn kàn zuǒ shǒu — the eyes watch the left hand

- 左手腕打左足面 — the left wrist strikes the instep of the left foot

- 左足左屈膝一二分 — the left leg bends the knee one or two parts

- 右膝微屈 — the right knee is slightly bent

- 外纏往精用外順 — wrapping outward: use outer coiling (顺缠 shùn chán)

- 左手倒纏方与外往里纏 — the left hand coils in reverse, from outside inward

- 左手合住 — the left hand closes and holds

- 右肘沉下 — the right elbow sinks down

- 腰前就勢，臀往下稍就方能撑住左半身 — the waist leans forward with the momentum and the buttocks drop slightly, only then can the left half of the body be braced

- 右足平踏用力 — the right foot presses flat with force

Text:

The right hand strikes the right foot. First bring the right hand down and fold it back to the left ribs, then travel upward and westward, using the coiling method (顺缠 shùn chán) to strike the instep of the right foot. The chest bends forward, the buttocks press back and down; only following this momentum can the front and rear brace each other so the body does not topple. The left knee bends slightly, only then can the left foot stand stable. After striking the right foot, move the right foot a little forward, one foot's length (that is, move it after the right foot has landed), toes pointing north; move the left foot in front of the right foot, pointing northwest, set the foot down, then lift it again. Before striking the foot, the left hand likewise goes up from the left ribs and strikes forward at the instep of the left foot, also using coiling force; the waist bends westward, the buttocks drop, and following the momentum press toward the east — then left and right can both brace. The crown force must be well lifted; the crotch sinks with bent knees, and the foot on the ground must press solidly.

Annotations under the figures:

- 面向南方图 — figure facing south

- 右手图 — figure of the right hand

- 东 East — 西 West

- 左手在后亦往下暗助力 — the left hand behind also secretly assists downward

- 手在前打右足面 — the front hand strikes the right instep

- 左肘在后亦展开 — the left elbow behind also opens out

- 右肋 right ribs — 左肋 left ribs

- 右手从西屈肘回到右肋 — the right hand comes from the west, bending the elbow back to the right ribs

- 右肘展开 — the right elbow opens out

- 右前臂 right forearm — 左前臂 left forearm

- 左肘 left elbow — 右肘 right elbow

Posture 26 (continued) Left Foot Rub: Facing-North Figure, Image and Verse

Annotations on the figure:

- 面向北方图 — figure facing north

- 左手图 — figure of the left hand

- 西 West — 东 East

- 肱展开合手 — the forearm opens, the hands close

- 右肋在後亦展開 — the right ribs behind also open out

- 左手从左肋由外往 — the left hand goes out from the left ribs, from outside

- 左肋 left ribs — 右肋 right ribs

- 左足所落之西停住，再抬左足 — the left foot lands in the west and stops, then lifts again

- 左足在东右足在西，右手打罢右足落原位 — the left foot is in the east and the right in the west; after the right hand strikes, the right foot returns to its original place

- 扭转足踵使趾向北，然后左足自东越右 — twist the heel so the toes point north, then the left foot crosses from the east past the right

- 左足从此起 — the left foot rises from here

- 右足从此起 — the right foot rises from here

- 打罢仍落 — after striking it still lands

- 左足越之 — the left foot crosses over it

- 足时之前踵扭转，右足指亦向北 — when lifting the foot the fore-heel twists, and the right toes also point north

Right Foot Rub — Image (取象 qǔ xiàng):

This posture uses the right hand to slap the instep of the right foot. Zhen (震) is the foot: the right foot kicks the opponent; Gen (艮) is the hand: the right hand assists and wards off the enemy — this is the true intent. Practicing the quan is not truly meeting an enemy; slapping the right foot prefigures the authority of self-defense. The foot kicks upward, the hand strikes downward — the meaning of Increase (益 Yì); therefore the hexagram Yi is taken. The Image says: "Decrease above, increase below" (the hand assists the foot); it says: "From above coming down to below — its way is greatly bright" (the right hand descends from above down to the right foot — a compliant path, hence "its way is greatly bright"); it says: "It furthers one to have somewhere to go; correctness in the center brings blessings" (inside, move it with the central qi; the waist bends forward, the buttocks press back — gaining central correctness, hence blessings. The right foot kicks up — nothing is not favorable). First line: "It furthers one to undertake something great; supreme good fortune" (the right foot is honored by having accomplishment; kicking upward with it is a great deed — good fortune, the utmost good). There is the hexagram Guai (夬), the image of strength in the fore-toe; and the fourth line, the image of "no skin on the buttocks" (when the right foot kicks forward, the buttocks must follow; here one orders them to whip behind and drop slightly — "no skin" means not exposing the skin).

Right Foot Rub — Mixed Verse (长短句俚语):

The right hand draws out from the left ribs and circles once, the hand level with the heart; open the forearm, stand firm on the left foot, kick up the right foot — it lands below the jaw or in the crotch. It can rob a man of his life in an instant; if it can be spared, then spare it — one must not rashly raise and move at random.

Left Foot Rub — Image:

The right hand has just finished the foot-rub strike; turn straight back around, facing north, then kick up with the left foot, left palm facing down, and strike with the left hand. In the previous posture the left foot had just finished standing alone; now again

Trang sách 176 · scan p209

Left Foot Rub — Image (continued), Verse for Right and Left Foot Rub

Left Foot Rub — Image (continued):

Stand on the right foot alone. The right foot below is yin, Xun (巽) below being broken and thus yin; with Xun below and Gen above this is the hexagram Gu (蛊, Decay/Remedying), therefore Gu is taken. The right thigh stands alone, trembling and unsteady — yet in unsteadiness achieving steadiness, like a tree beset by insects, hardly able to sustain itself and yet forcing its support. The Image says: "Gu: firm above and yielding below" (firm refers to the left hand, yielding to the right foot); it says: "It furthers one to have somewhere to go" (this refers to the advantage of striking the foot-rub); it says: "The end has a new beginning" (this posture once finished, the next begins — this is the movement of heaven; the quan too is the natural movement of heavenly principle). First line: "He remedied the decay of the father" (the Gen hand above — there is a father occupying the honored place, devoted to non-action yet acting), "and the son is capable" (the right foot governs standing, the left governs kicking — serving and obeying like the compliance of Xun), "the father is without blame" (the foot can kick, the hand can strike — like a father without blame); fifth line: "He remedied the decay of the father and gains praise" (the left foot kicks up with achievement — that is gaining praise). In sum: though a matter may contain decay, one who remediates it dissolves the decay and even gains merit.

Left Foot Rub — Mixed Verse (长短句俚语):

The right foot stands firm facing north; the left hand likewise turns back from the right ribs, the hand level with the heart; open the forearm; the left hand closes its palms and strikes downward, the left foot kicks up swift as the wind. Neither leaning nor crooked, it kicks again into the enemy's crotch (the enemy is not one man) — face to face, the hero appears.

Right Foot Rub — Four-Character Verse (四言俚语):

Mark the positions clearly, the facing sharply defined; the left foot first crosses sideways, the right foot follows and plants; the right hand scoops from the left, striking level toward the foot.

Seven-Character Verse (七言俚语), the first:

First lay the left foot crosswise toward the south (the Horse-Exploring posture faces north; the left foot first twists toward the south, the right foot twists to face south — hence facing south); raise the right foot with the instep stretched flat. The right hand first circles over from the left; striking above and kicking below, the two meet.

Seven-Character Verse, the second:

Facing south, the left foot fixes the foundation; the right hand receives from below without troubling thought. The whole body closes like a drawn bow; glancing east, striking west, they follow each other.

Left Foot Rub — Four-Character Verse:

The face, from the south, turns to the north. The right foot stands firm, the left foot flies aloft. The left hand circles to the right, striking down without hindrance. The central qi threads through fully — such is your strength.

Seven-Character Verse:

Then twist the right foot toward the north; lock and close the whole body and strength arises of itself. The left hand circles rightward and strikes downward — thread by thread into the groove, only then is the form correct.

*(page 176)*

Trang sách 177 · scan p210

Left and Right Foot Rub — Combined Chant; Posture 27: Middle Single Whip

左右擦脚合咏长短句俚语 (zuǒyòu cā jiǎo hé yǒng chángduǎn jù lǐyǔ):

First commit the positions to mind; from north turn south with both feet crosswise. The left foot first stands firm; the right hand circles one full turn from the left; then the right foot kicks up and the right hand strikes level and true at the instep of the right foot. After the right foot has kicked, it lays crosswise toward the north, and the left foot follows forward and plants. The right foot first steps true; the left hand circles one full turn along from the right ribs, opening the hand, stretching out the forearm, and strikes once more at the instep of the left foot. The crown force lifts up, the crotch force sinks down; each posture, each foot stands distinct. Assailed from four sides yet unafraid — the tiger roars, the wind rises, hands and feet receive it; the primal breath of Great Harmony refined to perfection, alert as a rolling eye. Motion and stillness weighed together; keep the heart reverent, cultivate by real effort, advance step by step, and in ten years it is achieved. By then the breath no longer contends, the mind holds no excess — only then does one know the quan practitioner holds the scale of judgment.

Posture 27: Middle Single Whip (中单鞭 zhōng dān biān)

Upper-posture figure (上半势图) labels:

- 顶精领 — the crown force leads

- 右肘相合 — the right elbows join

- 眼神意注前 — the eye spirit and intention concentrate forward

- 听思聪 — hearing with clarity

- 右膝屈与左膝合 — the right knee bends and joins the left knee

- 右肘屈住与左肘合，腰精下去 — the right elbow holds bent and joins the left elbow; the waist force sinks down

- 左肘屈住与右膝合住 — the left elbow holds bent and joins the right knee

- 右足平踏 — the right foot presses flat

- 左足点住地 — the left foot touches the ground

Lower-posture figure (下半势图) labels:

- 顶精领 — the crown force leads

- 右手 — right hand

- 右肘节展开 — the right elbow joint opens out

- 腰精下去 — the waist force sinks down

- 右膝微屈 — the right knee slightly bent

- 右足平踏 — the right foot presses flat

- 身往后霸 — the body presses back

- 西 — West

- 能得正气右足亦立得住 — once upright qi is attained, the right foot too can stand firm

- 左脚东之蹬精惟如此，身 — the left foot's kicking force goes eastward in just this way; the body...

- 左手 — left hand

- 左肘节展开 — the left elbow joint opens out

- 眼看左手中指 — the eyes watch the middle finger of the left hand

- 身向南 — the body faces south

- 东横蹬一脚 — one horizontal push-kick toward the east

Trang sách 178 · scan p211

*Chen Family Taiji Boxing Illustrated Explained* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chén shì tàijíquán túshuō)

After the left hand finishes striking and the left foot kicks, the body turns around from north toward the west, the face turning toward the south. The left foot stands facing east, the toes pressing into the ground; both elbows are bent. Suddenly the left hand extends east and the right hand extends west, opening out together. This is called "Middle Single Whip" (中单鞭, zhōng dān biān), also called "Double Wind Pierces the Ears" (双风贯耳, shuāng fēng guàn ěr). It is so named because when the two arms open out, the hands quickly pass by the ears, as though wind were rushing into them. When the two arms open out, the left foot simultaneously kicks out eastward; the foot pushes east while the body presses west, so that the forces east and west balance each other and the body stays centered and upright, without falling; the right foot below keeps the stance stable. The crown energy (頂精, dǐng jīng) must be held up, the right knee slightly bent; only then can the hips sink slightly so the body can press westward. Neither leaning east nor leaning west, it stands firm in the center. Everything is like this: whoever can weigh things to hit the mean will naturally be fitting. Yet this weighing has no fixed rule — the body may be somewhat off-center, but if the applied forces balance, one can still be centered: this is called attaining the mean. The weighing is unfixed, yet there is in it something fixed and immovable; each person must grasp this for himself.

Taking the Image (取象, qǔ xiàng)

Qián (乾) is the head. The head is on top, the crown energy held up; the gaze fixes on the left hand and also on the spot where the left foot kicks. The two arms open out with the hardness of Qián. Zhen (震) is the foot. The right foot stands firm while the left foot kicks east, swift as thunder. Zhen below, Qián above — this is the hexagram Wu Wang (无妄, "Innocence / No Falsity"), so the image is taken from Wu Wang. Wu Wang means utmost sincerity without falsehood. In all things do your utmost in what is yours to do, and entrust good and ill fortune alike to fate — then naturally there is nothing to reckon. The Image says: "Hardness comes from outside," referring to the upper trigram of Da Xu (大畜); Gen (艮) comes and becomes Wu Wang. Zhen means movement (the left foot kicks east — the movement of Zhen); it says: "Movement and strength" (the kick is utterly vigorous); "the center is hard and has response" (the strong hands of Qián respond to the left foot); "great success" (both left hand and left foot are effective); "beneficial to be upright" (kick when kicking is fitting, not kick recklessly; the eastward kick strikes a vital point, and thus is correct); "this is the command of Heaven" (the eastward kick too follows principle — the situation arises naturally, nothing but Heaven's command); "there is somewhere to go" (the kick is not a mere kick; something comes after it — the classic's "going without falsity"); "whither?" (directly opposite is where to strike); "Heaven does not aid" — no: opposite there is a target, kick what should be kicked, strike what should be struck, and Heaven aids it; "go, then." Keep reverence in the heart, circulate the central qi (中气, zhōng qì) — where can one not go? First line: "Going without falsity — auspicious." The Image says: "Going without falsity attains one's aims" (sincerity can move things; nothing fails to follow one's wish — this is the meaning of striking with the hand and kicking with the foot). The Image of the fourth line says: "Steadfast, no blame — it is inherent" (Single Whip can kick or strike, and can also refrain, because it has been cultivated as a habit).

Internal Essence (内精, nèi jīng)

In Grasp the Robe (揽擦衣, lǎn cā yī) the right arm is extended and must first join with the left arm; only then does the left hand open out into Single Whip. In Middle Single Whip both elbows are bent, like a firecracker wrapped up (wrapping the opponent's arm with mine, opening outward to strike him). Suddenly use compliant energy (順精, shùn jīng) to open out all together — this differs only slightly from the former.

Trang sách 179 · scan p212

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation (Chén shì tàijíquán túshuō), Volume 1–2

When kicking with the left foot, one likewise uses shùn-chán (unwinding coiling) and *jing* (essence) to close it together. When kicking with the right foot, one likewise uses shùn-chán and jing to close it together — only then can one stand stably.

Seven-character verses on Middle Single Whip (Zhōng dān biān)

I:

The posture of the body stands dignified and correct, without the least deviation; the left and right (hands and forearms) spread evenly (extended, that is), arrayed on both sides. The left foot kicks out one kick toward the east, relying entirely on a single tree (zhen trigram = wood, = foot; the right foot stands independently below) thrusting up to pierce the heavens.

II:

The two hands suddenly gather and suddenly disperse; the vast, mighty *yuanqi* (original qi) threads through the center. The right foot sinks below like a foundation, supporting the left foot as it hangs in mid-air.

Posture 28: Strike-the-Ground Fist (Jī dì chuí)

Notes around the figure (right to left):

- "Striking the ground" is not literally so; it means that the enemy has been kicked to the ground, and one then advances two steps to strike him with the fist.

- Shang jiu (top line): "Dun gen, auspicious" — "dun" meaning the whole body is present within it; the left and right forearms, shoulders, and hands naturally need no mention.

- The rear vertex (hou ding), the back of the skull, must further rise upward and lead, so that although the face points downward, the heart/mind is at the back. The eyes look toward the right fist.

- Six in the fifth: "Gen its jaws." "Fu" is the outside of the jaw/cheek; from the stillness of the jaw one may know the closing of the mouth.

Right-side notes on the figure:

- The body inclines and halts; the right shoulder is below, the left shoulder above.

- The left hand is behind, above, guarding the back.

- Six in the fourth: "Gen its trunk." The trunk is the chest and back.

- Nine in the third: "Gen its waist (xian)." Xian is the waist.

- Second line: "Gen its calf (fei)." The fei is the belly of the leg; this is the right foot.

Left-side notes on the figure:

- The right hand must strike the ground with the fist.

- Bent and lodged beneath the left lower leg.

- Initial line: "Gen its toes." The left knee.

[Figure] Posture of Strike-the-Ground Fist (Posture 28): the body bends forward nearly to the ground, the right hand fisted and striking down close to the ground, tucked beneath the left lower leg; the left hand is behind and above, guarding the back; the right shoulder below, the left above; though the face points downward the rear crown lifts, eyes on the right fist. The six lines of the Gen hexagram (52) of the Yijing are matched to the body's parts: dun gen (top nine), gen its jaw (six-five), gen its trunk/chest-back (six-four), gen its waist (nine-three), gen its calf/belly of the leg — right foot (second line), gen its toes — left knee (initial line).

Trang sách 180 · scan p213

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation

In Middle Single Whip (zhong dan bian), when the left foot finishes kicking (deng), the left foot comes down and immediately opens one step toward the east. The right foot crosses over the left foot and opens another step east. The left foot again crosses over the right foot and opens another step east, until the right knee is only two or three cun from the left nipple. If not done this way, the waist has not bent down and the right hand cannot strike a fist toward the ground. The right hand strikes the ground with the back of the hand facing east: this is the closing together (he zhu) of the fist striking the yang jing (yang essence).

Hand method: when the left foot finishes kicking and opens its step and lands, the left hand immediately follows it, unwinding (dao zhuan) through one circle. When the right foot crosses the left foot and opens a step east, the right hand likewise unwinds a circle following the right foot. When the left foot opens another step east and the left hand unwinds a circle, as the left hand comes down to rest at the left ribs, the right fist immediately strikes a blow toward the ground — this is called the Low Performance-Hand Fist (xia yan shou chui). At the final opening step of the left foot, the knee bends greatly, the knee very near the chest; the waist bends far down, and the rear vertex (hou ding) must further be lifted and held up. Do not let the spirit court (shen ting) or cheng jiang (the groove below the lower lip) sink downward. Even with the rear crown lifted and leading, so the face does not point down, one is already seven-tenths downward — how much more if the rear crown is not lifted enough! This is of the utmost importance and must be understood. Below, the crotch (dang) must be rounded open, and the steps must be firmly planted. This is a greatly spread-out posture (da pu shen fa): the back is only a few cun higher than the buttocks (meaning that when the body bends down, the back being level, the difference front-to-back in height is no more than five or six cun). Otherwise, if someone presses on the back one topples over. If they lift the ear from the east, one likewise falls face-down. So the crotch must be spread open and the feet planted on the ground. As for the mind, it is used entirely on the head and back and the bend of the right leg.

When I was young, my late father once lowered his body in this posture and had us two brothers press on his spine, exerting force up and down with all our strength. We only felt him twist his body once, and we two brothers fell down together. At that moment I understood that the mechanism lies entirely in the waist: the crown essence above leads once, the crotch essence below opens round, the two feet truly plant on the ground, and the waist essence in the middle gives one twist — no matter how many people, they cannot hold you down, let alone us two brothers. This is what is called the zhongqi (central qi) threading fully: things come and one accords with them; nothing can oppose it.

Internal jin (nei jin)

Left-hand diagram: when the left foot kicks and opens the first step east, the left hand uses dao jin (inverted/reversing jin) to unwind one circle.

Right-hand diagram: when the right foot opens the second step east, the right hand likewise unwinds one circle following the step, using the reversing jin. When the left hand has completed one full circle, the right hand strikes the fist downward from the upper half of the circle, turning only one circle.

The left foot then opens another large step east and settles. The waist bends down, the left hand following the left foot turns one circle with the reversing jin; when the left foot lands, the right hand, taking advantage of the bent waist, strikes one fist toward the ground.

Footnote: the original edition reads "the right HAND" crossing over the left foot to open a step east; per the posture and principles, "right hand" has been corrected to "right FOOT."

Trang sách 181 · scan p214

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation

Three circular direction diagrams

- Upper half circle: left falls, hand rises (E, W, S, N)

- Right rises, hand falls (E, W, S, N)

- Left falls, right rises (E, W, S, N)

- Lower half circle

Left-hand diagram

Left foot lands third step; first step, second step, third step (E, W, S, N) — three steps in all.

Text

The left foot first kicks once and opens one step; when the right foot has finished opening the next step, the left foot then opens one large step further east — thus three steps in a row. When the left foot opens the third step, the right hand has just turned the lower half circle; once the left hand has arrived, the right hand fisted, level with the crown of the head toward the east, strikes downward past the head by seven or eight cun; it may also strike down from west to east. This is the right hand's upper half circle.

Taking the Image (qu xiang)

In this posture the whole body is directed downward; only the left hand is above, and still behind. The rest — the right hand and both feet — are all on the ground, stopped and unmoving; hence the image is taken from Gen (Keeping Still, hexagram 52), the passage from movement to stillness. When stillness reaches its extreme, movement arises again! Movement and stillness cycling round is the principle of nature. The fist art is just these two qi, one moving and one still, the wondrous operation of ceaseless cycling.

Gen below, Gen above — this is the Gen hexagram. (Gen means stopping. One yang halts atop two yin lines; the yang rises to its utmost and stops there — this is the meaning of stopping. Its image is earth below the mountain, the substance of the mountain. One yang overspreads two yin, the turbid below — also an image of stopping.)

"Gen its back, and one does not grasp its body; walking in one's courtyard, one does not see its people — no blame." (Lai's note: this hexagram is the inverse of Zhen, whose fourth line is the human body; therefore the Duke of Zhou's line-statements take the fourth line as the body. In the trigram, the second place is the human position. The courtyard is the front courtyard, the fifth line. Gen is gate and tower, so the space within the gate is the courtyard. Zhen means walking. Walking upward, the face is above and the back below, so the yang lines initial and fourth are taken as the back. Gen means stopping. Standing downward-facing, the face below and the back above, the yang lines third and top are taken as the back. The first two phrases speak by the lower trigram, the last two by the upper trigram: halting the back, the body is behind and one does not see the fourth line's body; walking in the courtyard, the back is before others and one does not see the second line's person — all from the one stopping. Neither seeing oneself nor seeing others. The wording is by nature profound and hard to grasp…)

Trang sách 182 · scan p215

*(Commentary on the Gen 艮 hexagram — hexagram statement, Image, first and second lines; part of the Yijing commentary linking to boxing principle. No figure on this page.)*

Confucius knew that King Wen formed the hexagram statement by means of the hexagram's paired counterpart (zong 綜) — one "walking" character, one "stopping" character, both joined to "timing" (shi 時).

The Tuan 彖 says: Gen means stopping (zhi 止). When it is time to stop, stop; when it is time to go, go. If movement and stillness each do not miss their proper time, one's Way will be bright and clear. "He stops at his stopping" — he stops in his proper place. The upper and lower lines "match as opponents" (di ying 敵應) yet have nothing to do with one another; therefore "he does not gain his body; he walks in his courtyard and does not see his people — no blame." *(This explains the hexagram name by the hexagram body, its zong-pair, and its virtue. The hexagram statement says: what is called Gen lies in its stopping. Yet the principles under heaven are inexhaustible and human affairs change in myriad ways — if one merely "stopped" and nothing more, how could that suffice to exhaust the principles of affairs! One must also look at its zong-pair to see how it is. Now what accords with the fitting is called "timing": when the time fits Gen's stopping, then stop; when the time fits Zhen's 震 going, then go. When the movement and stillness of going and stopping never miss their time, then nowhere is one off the impartiality of heavenly principle, and one's Way shines like sun and moon — far more than merely "no blame"! Yet Gen is named "stopping" not because of stubborn fixation without change, but because it stops where it should stop. Because it stops at what is inherently fitting, "when it is time to stop, it stops." The statement again says "he does not gain his body, does not see his people": if others engage with me, then I can obtain the other; if I engage with others, then the other can seize me. Here, the first line with the fourth, the second with the fifth, the third with the top: yin stays yin by itself, yang stays yang by itself, with no mutual engagement. Hence others cannot gain my body, and I do not see the other — one merely obtains "no blame." How could there be the "stop when it is time to stop, go when it is time to go," which is far more than no blame? Of the eight pure doubled hexagrams none respond to one another, yet this is said only of Gen — because Gen's nature is to stop, and stopping means stubborn fixation without change, hence not bright and clear, hence only "no blame." King Wen spoke wholly in images; Confucius spoke wholly in principle.)*

The Image 象 says: Doubled mountains — Gen. The junzi thinks without leaving his position. *(Doubled mountains: one mountain within, one mountain without — a two-layer mountain. The principles of the world exist in their positions: the father has the father's position, the son the son's; ruler and subject each their own; husband and wife likewise. The wealthy-honored have their position, as do the poor, lowly, the barbarians, hardship. So too in boxing: ears, eyes, hands, feet, head, and body each have their position. Where there is the inherent position, there is the principle of what ought to be. "Thinking without leaving one's position" is precisely how one stops at that principle; to leave one's position is to overstep the principle.)*

First Six (初六): Stopping at his toes — no blame, beneficial to be forever correct. *(Gen's zong-pair is Zhen, the image of foot and toe; the first line at the bottom is likewise a toe image. The Xian 咸 hexagram likewise moves up through the body gradually. First Six is yin and soft, without talent to act — one who can stop; it further dwells at the very bottom, lowly, one who must stop. Hence the image of stopping at the toes. If one divines thus, one must not act rashly or push forward blindly, and may be without blame and correct. Yet there is fear the correct one cannot remain so forever — hence this instruction to the diviner.)*

The Image says: He stops at his toes — he has not lost correctness. *(What principle requires one to stop is called "correct" — that is the zhen 貞 of the line statement. The line says "beneficial to be forever correct"; the Image says "has not lost correctness." This shows the first line's stopping is where principle requires it.)*

Second Six (六二): Stopping at his calves; he cannot rescue those who follow him; his heart is not glad. *(Fei 腓 means the calf. It is also the foot image of the lower Zhen. "Rescue" (zheng 拯) means to save; "follow" (sui 隨) means to comply. The second line adheres to the third and follows it; "he cannot rescue his follower" means he does not seek rescue from the third he follows. Whenever the yin-soft depends on the yang-firm it is called "rescue" — as in Huan 渙, first line, "rescue with a strong horse." The second line is central and correct; in the bagua the correct position is Gen. When it is a time of Gen's stopping, the second, stopping, does not ask of the third; the third, stopping, does not yield and defer to the second — hence the second's heart is not glad. The inner trigram Kan 坎 means added sorrow and heart-ailment: the image of "not glad." ○ Second Six dwells central and correct, adjacent to the third, stopping at the calves. With a yin-soft nature seeking the yang-firm third's aid would be possible; but Gen's nature is to stop, and not seeking rescue from its follower, its central-correct virtue has nowhere to be applied — hence the heart must be unhappy. Thus the image of the divination is as...)*

*(The sentence continues onto the next page.)*

Trang sách 183 · scan p216

*(Printed page 183, Book/Volume 2 — commentary on the Gen 艮 hexagram lines 9-3, 6-4, 6-5, upper-9, and the taiji posture 擊地捶 Ji Di Chui / Strike-the-Ground Fist)*

Nine in the third line: "Gen its limit — danger; it smokes the heart" (艮其限，厲薰心). (The "limit" is the boundary: the dividing line between upper and lower body is the waist. "Tendons" [夤] means connection — the waist's unbroken linking. The waist, in the body, stands at the very point of bending and stretching: it ought to move, not stop. If one stops at the limit, then upper is upper and lower is lower, no longer connected; separation cuts the body into two distinct halves. 薰 is the same as 熏, smoke rising. "Smoking the heart" means the heart is unsettled. The inner trigram is Kan 坎, water, which stands for heart-ailment — hence the second line's unease and the third line's smoking heart; Kan's opposite is Li 離, fire, the image of smoke. ○ The way of stopping lies only in stopping where principle resides. The third line sits above the calf, at the waist — the very place of change and movement, where one ought not stop. Stopping where stopping is wrong, one can neither bend nor stretch, and upper and lower are split apart and cut off from each other. Hence danger and an unsettled heart — such is the image for one who divines this.) *The Image says:* "Gen its limit — danger smokes the heart." (To stop where stopping is wrong is to cling to one thing and lose adaptability. Outwardly severed, the heart must be uneasy; hence danger and a smoking heart.)

Six in the fourth line: "Gen its body — no blame" (艮其身，無咎). (To stop one's body is to be quiet and withdrawn: when neighbors quarrel, shut one's door — the "tied sack, no blame" category. The fourth line is yin dwelling in yin, purely yin, hence the image of stopping the body. Having stopped one's body, there is nothing to undertake; one who divines thus is without blame.) *The Image says:* "Gen its body — stopping in one's own person." (躬 [gōng] is the body: unable to govern others or complete things, one merely stops in one's own person.)

Six in the fifth line: "Gen its jaws — speech in order; regret vanishes" (艮其輔，言有序，悔亡). (序 [xù] is sequence; "jaws" [辅] is explained in the Xian 咸 hexagram. Gen's opposite is Dui 兌, lake — mouth and tongue, the image of jaws and of speech. "Gen its jaws" is not speaking rashly; "speech in order" is speaking always in accord with principle. Regret arises when speech becomes glib and verbose, rambling, or perverse. The Xian hexagram mostly images the human face, while Gen mostly images the human back — because King Wen's judgment reads "Gen its back." ○ The fifth line is at the jaws, the place where speech issues out; yin dwelling in yang, it cannot avoid the regret of mis-speaking. Yet because it attains the center, there is also the image of stopping the jaws with ordered speech, and so the divination reads: regret vanishes.) *The Image says:* "Gen its jaws — because of central correctness." (正 [zhèng, "correct"] should be read as 止 [zhǐ, "stop"], the same "stop" as in "stopping in one's own person": stopping from central stillness, hence regret vanishes.)

Nine at the top: "Sincere Gen — good fortune" (敦艮，吉). (敦 [dūn] is the same "sincere/thick" as in "sincere in practice." The meaning: stop when it is time to stop, firm and unchanging. There is an image of inner solidity — hence "sincere approach" and "sincere return" both take earth as their image. The top nine, with yang firmness at Gen's utmost, stops from beginning to end without changing: solid in stopping. One who divines thus — what good fortune could match such lightness/clarity?) *The Image says:* "The good fortune of sincere Gen lies in ending with thickness." (To end with thickness is to be sincere and steadfast to the end without change. In the hexagrams Bi 賁, Xiao Xu 小蓄, Gu 蠱, Yi 頤, Sun 損, and Meng 蒙, the top nine is auspicious in all six because they carry this sense of a thick ending.)

Ji Di Chui (擊地捶, Strike-the-Ground Fist): The right hand draws back a fist resting near the ground, the arm spread open — this is the single yang line of Gen's lower trigram; the toes of the right foot press the ground and the five toes of the left foot press the ground, imaging Gen's two yin lines — this is the image of Gen's lower trigram. For the upper trigram Gen: above, the occipital bone continuing down through the great vertebra (Dazhui 大椎) and the twenty-one vertebrae below it images Gen's one yang line; the two halves of the buttocks image one yin line, and the ribs on left and right, in two branches, image the second yin line — this is the image of Gen's upper trigram. My taking of images does not stop at this Gen-stopping: the jing 精 at the crown is held up, the jing of the crotch (dang 裆) sinks down, the jing of the waist is well employed, and all the rest each stop in their proper place. For when stamping, the feet open… (sentence continues on the next page).

Figure: Posture 29 — Er Qi Jiao (二起脚, Double Kick): the practitioner is shown mid-leap, body leaning forward, right arm extended forward and slightly up with open hand, left arm extended back; left leg extended forward-downward, right leg bent and lifted behind — a kicking/jumping motion, head turned looking at the right hand.

Labels on the figure:

- 顶精更得领起来！ "The crown must be led upward!"

- 眼看向右手 "The eyes look toward the right hand"

- 他势肩要松下 "In other postures the shoulders must sink and relax"

- 身并提起 "The body lifts up together"

- 独此势肩要与 "Only in this posture the shoulders must…"

Main text (middle column):

…step, the hands reverse and turn. When movement reaches its extreme it must become still — this is the moment to stop. When it is time to stop, then stop: this is stopping where one ought to stop. When stillness reaches its extreme it must arise; this passage first sets up the rise for the next posture.

Seven-character verse on Ji Di Chui (击地捶, "Punch to the Ground") (the left foot kicks with a stamp, knocking the enemy flat on his back to the east; because he is now far from me, and I fear he may rise again, I must take three quick steps in a row and, before he gets up, strike his body another punch so he cannot rise again):

The first:

Turn the face around to face the south,

Stamp the left foot eastward and watch the remarkable man (these two lines continue the previous verse).

Three chasing steps in a row, waist and legs strong,

Deep into the tiger's den, probing with the hand (to seize).

The second:

Open the stride and greedily press forward,

Having finished, the eastern stamp leaves the left foot suspended ("having finished" means the foot has already landed),

Strike down one punch to control his fate,

Then turn the body as if about to fly to heaven.

Posture 29: Er Qi Jiao (二起脚, Double Kick)

When the right-hand lower "performing the hand" (演手) is finished, leap up from the ground, and with the right hand turn one circle following the movement, then slap the instep of the right foot with the wrist; or one may first lead the left foot up with the left hand, the left hand and left…

*Printed page: 184 (scan p217)*

Trang sách 185 · scan p218

*(Text-only page, no figure)*

Margin (right side, vertical): 陳氏太極拳圖説 卷二 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo, Volume 2)

Main text:

…foot about to descend, the right foot instantly flies up, while the right hand reverses through a small circle and slaps the instep of that right foot. In the preceding method the route is close to the right foot: as soon as the lower "performing the hand" (演手 yan shou) is finished, turn the head abruptly and, following the momentum, leap up with the whole body, instep level with the palm — the right foot is the host; the left foot, when the lower yan shou is finished, sets up the position for the right foot, leaping up first — this foot is the guest. In body method, the heart-jin lifts upward; the whole body's spirit rouses and soars into the air. The right foot must rise as high as the crown of the head to be correct; if it cannot match the forehead, it can still rise no less than level with the shoulders. When the lower yan shou of the left hand is finished, at the moment of turning the head sharply, the right hand turns one circle following (shun), slapping the right foot, and the left hand reverses through one circle to aid the right hand's jin — like a general deploying decoy troops to bolster the army's morale. When speaking of the hands, shoulders and feet are all included.

What is "double rise" (二起 er qi)? The left and right feet, one after the other, leave the ground together, leaping up four or five chi — hence the name. Kicking the er qi: the right hand's yan shou punch has just been cut down to strike an opponent, and on both sides enemies come attacking from behind; at once turn the head sharply, the right hand going from below upward, from south to north and down, the left hand likewise rising from below to above, moving from above downward. The left foot kicks up first to lead the right foot's rise. Just as the left foot is about to come down, the right foot rises following it, and the rising jin also lifts upward with all its strength (sheng, "rise," means going up; ti, "lift," means lifting the spirit). The kicking instep must be level. At this moment the right hand below does not stop: following the reversing jin it speeds from below upward, closing the palm, and slaps the instep of the right foot with the right hand. When left and right feet land one after the other, the form has not yet halted when the mechanism of the next posture already stirs. The boundary of er qi reaches here — this is the largest version. Because the circle turned by the right hand and body method is large, the posture is large; this is the lowest grade of kick. Yet learners must begin by this route as the entrance; ascending by grades, the right hand need not reach to the right side. When the lower yan shou is finished, the left hand rises up once, and the right hand goes from east to west, then down from the west, up toward the east, completing a westward circle, and the right hand slaps the left foot; the two feet leap up, again left first, right after — this is the middle grade of kick. Ascending further is the highest grade, quite difficult. When the right hand's lü-punch plants downward, at once use the right hand following the momentum with shun jin to turn one circle and slap the instep of the right foot; the right hand turns one circle shun, while the left hand uses side jin reversing one circle to aid the right hand's jin. As for the feet, again left first, right after; but the left foot must first kick upward fiercely with force, and only then can the right foot kick high. Er qi purely uses the whole-body lifting method. When er qi is finished, both feet stand facing south, the right hand in the west, the left hand in the east, as in the single whip (單鞭 dan bian) posture.

And unless one has personally entered that state, one cannot know it — because the natural scene of the place does not pre-set a posture."

Qu Xiang 取象 (Taking the Image):

In this posture the left foot rises first. Zhen (震, Thunder) is the foot: the lower Zhen — the right foot rises from behind; the upper Zhen — combining them gives the hexagram Zhen, therefore the image is taken from Zhen. Zhen means movement. Nothing long remains still without moving: when the two feet move, the whole body rouses itself in response. This is the first and fourth lines of Zhen, yang — one movement…

Trang sách 187 · scan p220

Margin (right, vertical): 陳氏太極拳圖説 卷二 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo, Volume 2)

Main text (continuing the Qu Xiang from the previous page):

…then the second, third, fifth and sixth lines also shake and move in response. The previous posture is stillness at its extreme; this posture bears the image of "thunder coming, perilous" (震来厉). The feet rise extremely high, so there is also the image of the Qian hexagram, "the flying dragon in the heavens." Once the heart-jin leads up, the five sense organs and the hundred bones all rise together with it, so there is also the Sui (随, Following) hexagram — Sui carries the image of gain. Moreover, rising up vigorously from the lower yan shou punch, like thunder amid the marsh — how could one not shake with alarm for a hundred li?

First: Er Qi, five-character verse

One foot rises in linked succession, the whole body leaps to mid-air.

If you do not kick from below the mouth, whence does the blood flow red?

Second: seven-character verse

Raise the central qi and the sinew strength of the back grows hard; linked, the er qi soars upward.

If it had not first lain hidden eastward in ambush, how could the western strike pass the bridge of the nose?

Third

The flying dragon in the heavens is nothing rare (the dragon's ordinary affair); thunder amid the marsh is hard to place with skill (rising high from the ground is hardest).

Rely only on this body long smelted in the forge; first learn Wei Ju's single bound.

Posture 30: Shou Tou Shi (兽头势, Beast-Head Posture)

Printed page: 187 (scan p220)

Trang sách 188 · scan p221

Margin: 陳氏太極拳圖説 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo)

Main text:

What is the Beast-Head Posture (兽头势 shou tou shi)? The right fist is at the forehead, the left fist above the left knee, and between them the eyes glare with a fierce look (chēn: to look hatefully). The form is fierce like the beast-head ornament on a roof — hence the name. When the rise is finished, the left foot first comes down; the right foot steps forward six or seven cun; the left foot then steps forward about one chi. Then the left and right hands divide downward on either side of the left knee, using reversed-winding silk-reeling jin (倒转缠丝精) to wind onto the fists. The right fist settles at the forehead, five cun from it, outside the direct line of the forehead. The left fist settles above the left knee, seven or eight cun from the abdomen and two or three cun from the knee. The left foot — of the two — is drawn in beside the right foot, five or six cun from the stepping foot, the toe touching the ground: this plants the hidden thread for the next posture.

Nei Jin (内精, Inner Jin)

Hand-movement circle labels:

- 右拳落额上 去额五寸 在正额外 "Right fist settles at the forehead, five cun away, outside the direct line"

- 左拳左膝上 去膝二三寸许 "Left fist above the left knee, two-three cun from the knee"

- 左右手从左膝两旁分下 用倒转缠丝精缠到拳上 "Left and right hands divide down beside the left knee, winding onto the fists with reversed silk-reeling jin"

- 左足在两者收到右足边 去行足五六寸 点住脚 "Left foot drawn in beside the right foot, five-six cun from the stepping foot, toe touching"

- 右手运行图 "Right hand movement diagram"; 左手运行图 "Left hand movement diagram"

Footwork diagram labels:

- 左足前进收回图 "Left foot advance-and-recover diagram"; 右足前进开步图 "Right foot advance-and-step diagram"

- 右足向西开一少步 落住不动 "Right foot takes a small step west and lands without moving"

- 左足再向西开一步 至此 "Left foot again steps west, reaching here"

- 点住脚 "toe touches"; 复收回 "then withdraw"; 边足右回 "beside the right foot returning"

Qu Xiang (取象, Taking the Image):

In this posture the spirit gathers entirely in the eyes, watching the enemy's expression come and go: observing his outside one knows his inside, therefore the image is taken from Guan (观, Observation). Guan means: he observes my fist as I observe him — watching the path by which the enemy comes and seizing the opportunity to respond. The Image says: "Great observation is above; compliant and yielding (xun), central and correct, observing all under heaven" (that is, the two eyes on the face — great observation above. "Compliant and xun": the Xun trigram has much white in the eye. Watch his general momentum and strike following it. "Central and correct": with a calm mind and even qi observe the enemy — this is observing all under heaven). It also says: "Observe the divine way of heaven: the four seasons do not err" (heaven is heavenly principle, heaven's mechanism; the divine way is the path. Observe the path by which another's foreknowledge reveals his coming, and respond with the four limbs according to the moment). Third line: "Guan wo sheng" ("observe my life" — sheng means life) — advance and retreat. The Image says: "He observes his own life in advancing and retreating, not losing correctness" (two people in combat: life is at stake; outwardly observing the other…)

*(text continues on the next page)*

Printed page: 188 (scan p221)

Trang sách 189 · scan p222

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陈氏太极拳图说, Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan), Volume 2 (卷二). No diagrams on this page; commentary on the Shou Tou Shi (兽头势, Beast-Head Posture), continuing the Guan hexagram (观, hexagram 20, "Contemplation") correspondence.

Interiorly observing oneself; knowing oneself and knowing the other, one wins a hundred battles out of a hundred, and all is governed by the zhong qi (中气, central qi), never departing from the way of great centrality and correctness (大中至正). Fourth line: "Contemplating the light of the kingdom." One skilled at reading appearances can repel small foes as well as great bandits — hence "contemplating the light of the kingdom." Fifth line, Image: "Contemplating my life means contemplating the people" (here "people" stands for the opponent). Top nine: "Contemplating his life, the noble one is without blame" (the noble one stands for the completed hand/strike); Image: "Contemplating his life, the will is not yet at rest" (meaning the quanjia (拳家, boxer) whose hand is complete can steady his will, so that he has no crosswise qi and nothing he cannot undertake). The upper two strokes of Xun (巽, hexagram 57, Wind) are yin — the left and right fists. Xun's "white eyes" signify observation. Xun's opposite is Zhen (震, hexagram 51, Thunder), carrying an air of roused spirit. The lower trigram is Kun (坤, hexagram 2, Earth), which is the belly. The upper body is yang, the lower body yin; Kun's opposite is Qian (乾, hexagram 1, Heaven) — softness meeting hardness. Kun below and Xun above matches the sense of the Beast-Head posture, so it is taken up. There is the Kuai/Kui hexagram (睽, hexagram 38, Opposition) — the image of meeting an evil man; the Yi hexagram (颐, hexagram 27, Nourishment) — "the tiger gazes intently, its desires pursuing one after another"; Da Zhuang (大壮, hexagram 34, Great Power, beneficial to be correct) — "power in the toes"; and Kuai (夬, hexagram 43, Breakthrough), fifth line: "The great man changes like a tiger, and his pattern is brilliantly visible." After all, though the images present many a warrior's form, the interior is truly soft, compliant, central and correct; there is also Ming Yi (明夷, hexagram 36, Darkening of the Light) — inner brightness and文明 with outer softness and compliance — so beyond taking images from the various hexagrams, Ming Yi is taken as well.

Seven-character verses on the Beast-Head Posture (兽头势七言俚语)

One

Glaring eyes and grabbing fists, the image most fierce; the mechanism nimble, hidden within the chest. The left foot points emptily, first storing up power — who could recognize this great hero?

Two

The two fists, above and below, are like a beast's head; the left foot goes west, then draws back east. Within the heart-protecting fist (护心拳) lies boundless meaning: to use hardness, first show softness.

Four-character verses (四言俚语)

The right thigh must bend; the left thigh must be gathered in; the left foot touches the ground, straight yet not straight. The right fist is at the forehead, the left fist at the knee; above and below watch each other, together with my heart and belly. The application is in the mind, the keenness in what is unique (独 — what others do not know and only I know). To be hard, first be soft; to rise, first press down. The primal qi of Great Harmony (太和元气) lies hidden in the whole; before the numinous trigger moves, one already knows there is no enemy.

Trang sách 190 · scan p223

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說)

The Thirty-first Posture: Ti Yi Jiao 踢一脚 (Kicking One Foot)

Shou tou shi (獸頭勢, Beast-head posture): the left foot touches the ground, and immediately following the posture the face turns to face north. With the left foot planted, [the body turns] toward the west, and one kicks upward with one foot into the enemy's crotch (襠中, dang zhong).

In the beast-head posture, the left hand is above the knee because, as the left foot kicks up, the left hand also spreads open together with the posture and the arm, to assist the power of the left foot.

The eyes watch the spot where the left foot kicks, so that it never misses its mark.

Ding jing ling zhu (頂精領住): the energy at the crown is held up.

The right hand is at the forehead because, as the left foot kicks up, the right hand also spreads open together with the arm, to assist the power of the right leg.

Annotations on the Figure

- Ding jing ling zhu (頂精領住) — the crown energy leads and holds.

- Yao jing wei wang hou xia qu er san fen (腰精微往後下去二三分) — the waist energy sinks slightly back and down two or three fen.

- You zu ping tou ta zhu di (右足平頭踏住地) — the right foot, flat on its sole, presses firmly on the ground.

- Xi yao wei qu, bu qu li bu wen (膝要微屈，不屈立不穩) — the knee must bend slightly; without bending, the stance is not stable.

The figure shows a one-legged stance: the weight is on the right leg, straight with a slight knee bend; the left leg lifts with a bent knee, kicking upward toward the opponent's groin; the left hand extends near the left knee, the right hand rises to the forehead.

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang 取象)

The previous posture contained the two meanings of "the tripod overturned" (鼎顛) and "the toes of the traveler" (趾旅即次). This posture kicks up the left foot, having the image of strength in the fore-toes (壯於前趾), and the image of Increase (益) in which the top is diminished to benefit the bottom (損上益下). All the power of the upper body is poured into the left foot; kicking a person with the left foot, one only knows to "advance one's horns" (晉其角) and "to attack the city" (維用伐邑) — "harsh but auspicious, no blame" (厲吉无咎) — without considering that there may be regret (吝). For Da Zhuang (大壯, Great Strength) says: "Strength in the toes; going forward brings misfortune" (壯于趾，征凶). It is also like the top six line: "The ram butts the hedge; it cannot retreat, it cannot advance; nothing is favorable" (羝羊觸藩，不能退，不能遂，无攸利). Though the affair is difficult, in the end it is auspicious.

(Printed page: 190)

Trang sách 191 · scan p224

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 2 (卷二)

When I kick a person with my foot, he naturally lifts my foot with his hand — how could I retreat or advance (退與遂)! Nothing is more difficult; yet in the end there is a way to resolve it, hence auspicious. At this moment there is the image of Ming Yi (明夷, Darkening of the Light): "darkening of the left thigh" (夷于左股); the only recourse is "to be saved by a strong horse — auspicious" (用拯馬壯吉). The "strong horse" means the following posture: Deng Yi Gen (蹬一跟, Kicking One Heel).

Ti Yi Jiao 踢一脚 — Five-Character Folk Verse (五言俚語)

Left foot kicks upward,

those outside cannot fathom it.

One strike to the crotch (兜襠),

and in an instant his life is gone.

Seven-Character Folk Verse (七言俚語)

Before the eyes a sheer wall rises to the mighty heavenly pass,

the Sword Pavilion hollow amid the mountain valley's mouth.

Should a hero first arrive at this place,

one kick topples ten thousand layers of mountain.

The Thirty-second Posture: Deng Yi Gen 蹬一跟 (Kicking One Heel)

Annotations on the Figure

- Hou ding di zhu (後頂提住) — the rear crown is lifted.

- Yao ji bu ke ruan (腰脊不可軟) — the waist and spine must not be soft.

- You xi bi wu qu deng (右膝必无屈蹬) — the right knee must not bend while kicking.

- You zu ping ta (右足平踏) — the right foot treads flat.

- You quan shou jie zhi (右拳手節執) — the right fist and hand joints are held firm.

- Ying quan suo jin (硬拳搠緊) — the hard fist grips tight.

- Jiang yi xia zhi yan hou (將以下至咽喉) — from below up to the throat.

- Deng wu jiu wei yu cheng (蹬吾鳩尾與承) — kicking my Jiuwei (鳩尾) and Cheng [jiang] points.

- Tui yi bu, yi fang (退一步，以防) — step back one step, to guard.

- Ji yi zuo jiao wang hou (即以左腳往後) — then with the left foot backward.

- Ren lai deng wu, wu (人來蹬吾，吾) — when the opponent comes kicking me, I...

- You shou na di (右手捺地) — the right hand presses the ground.

- Zuo shou na di (左手捺地) — the left hand presses the ground.

- Zhou jie yong li (肘節用力) — the elbow joint applies force.

Text Below the Figure

This figure shows the opponent's [attack] posture: he wants to lift my whole body with one hand and strike me with the left hand. Or, because his right hand has caught my foot, he uses his left hand as well to pull my foot, twisting it hard to injure my left thigh and vent his momentary fierceness. The plans of these two postures are not without cunning — but how could he know that when the body enters the place of death (死地), nature provides another way of life (生法).

In this figure, I kick the opponent with my left foot; he grips my foot with his right hand, wanting to twist it and make me fall to the ground in pain, or he lifts my foot upward, wanting my whole body off the ground so he can throw me down and strike me. I then follow the momentum and tumble over: both hands press against the ground, and with my right foot I slide upward along my own left leg against his right hand that grips my left foot — the difficulty is at once resolved.

(Printed page: 191)

Trang sách 192 · scan p225

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan) by Chen Xin, printed page 192.*

Paragraph (top column)

Or again, if the opponent blocks (擱 ge) my left foot, I immediately use my right foot to kick (蹬 deng) the tip of his right elbow, or kick his wrist joint — either can resolve the situation. Such is the general principle of "Kicking with the Heel" (蹬一跟 Deng Yi Gen). As for the differing circumstances of the moment, it is permissible — according to the advantage of my own position — to kick his vital spot (要害 yaohai) in order to break free. Applying it in the moment depends on one who examines the opening (審機 shen ji) and responds to the enemy according to what is convenient.

Heading

Nei Jing 內精 (Internal Essence)

Main text

When I kick a person with my left foot, he may seize my foot with his right hand. I then quickly sink the body down, both hands pressing (捺 na) the ground. Although the head faces downward, the back of the crown (後頂 hou ding) is lifted up by intention. The body and waist use the essence of "two opposing pulls" (兩奪之精). The strength of the shoulders is entirely employed in the hands: from the shoulders to the finger joints there must be no softening. Once it softens, not only can the lower body no longer kick the opponent, the upper body too will collapse to the ground — how then could one break free? From the navel (臍 qi) downward, spirit and strength are entirely devoted to the backward kick of the right foot. Whether the difficulty is resolved or not, whether danger emerges or not, depends entirely on landing this one kick. Kick to a vital spot: not only can the difficulty be resolved, the opponent can be injured. Fail to kick where it hurts: not only is the difficulty unresolved, the harm that follows — who knows where it would end! Can one afford not to be careful? But to be careful only at the moment of kicking is already too late; the care lies in the preceding posture. Just before kicking, look: if it can be kicked, kick; if not, do not kick. Do not use the kick recklessly. Even when there is an opening to exploit with a kick, value speed like lightning and not slowness; value kicking the crucial joints, openings and acupoints (關緊穴俞 guan jin xue shu), not kicking broad thighs or thick flesh where no pain or itch is felt. This is the essential secret (要訣 yaojue); the one who kicks must know it.

Diagram

Deng Yi Gen Xin Shi Tu 蹬一跟新式圖 (New-style Diagram of Kicking with the Heel)

Labels in the diagram:

- 頂精領住 — "The crown essence leads and holds" (spirit lifting the head)

- 西 — West (direction faced)

- 右手 — "Right hand" (the hand pressing the ground)

- 力不可吃 — "Strength must not be swallowed" (must not slack)

- 懈怠 — "Slackness" (to be avoided)

- 手不捺地，須要穩 — "The hand presses the ground and must be steady"

- 左足踏地 — "The left foot treads the ground"

Description: the figure leans forward with both hands pressed to the ground, right foot kicking up and back, head facing down while the back of the crown stays lifted; the toes point west.

Paragraph (bottom column)

This posture is harder than the preceding one. Wishing to avoid the hard and take the easy, another method has been established for the learner: have him first learn this, and afterwards practice that — also bending down into it, easier to perform — lest the fear of difficulty overcome him and he abandon the work halfway. Kick one foot, the face turning west in the inverted turn.

Trang sách 193 · scan p226

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo 陳氏太極拳圖說, Volume 2 (卷二), printed page 193.*

Main text

From West to North and to East, the head faces East and downward. Having finished the kick with the left foot, it travels from West through North and lands to the East of the right foot; immediately kick back one heel (蹬一跟 deng yi gen) with the right foot, kicking the opponent's chest, and the body swiftly inverts — the faster the better. The crown essence (頂精 ding jing) is led upward; both hands use the inverted-reeling silk essence (倒轉纏絲精 dao zhuan chan si jing) and the uniting essence. Both hands may press the ground, or may not press the ground; the foot need not kick upward in inversion — exert force only in kicking backward. Behind are both sides; the eyes watch the left and right hands, the mind settles on the right foot.

Qu Xiang 取象 (Taking the Image)

Taking the image: I kick a person with my left foot and he seizes my left foot — this body has entered the mansion of Kan 坎 (Water); therefore the image is taken from Kan. Yet there is "stripping the bed to the flesh, a calamity close at hand" (剝床以膚 Bo — hexagram Bo 剝, Splitting Apart); therefore the image is also taken from Bo, and Tai 泰 (Peace).

The first line says: "Pulling cogon grass, the roots intertwined" (拔茅茹) — [hexagram Pi 否, Standstill; the first line of Tai reads the same]. The enemy wishes to lift my whole body with one hand — like the image of pulling cogon grass with its tangled roots; therefore the images are taken also from Pi and Tai.

[Kun 困, Oppression.] The first line says: "Entering the dark valley, for three years unseen" (入于幽谷，三年不覩) — [meaning my head faces downward and sees nothing]. The third line says: "Trapped by stone, grasped by thorns" (困于石，據于蒺藜) — [Gen 艮 is the hand and is stone: it figures the tightness of the enemy's grip, like being seized amid brambles].

The top line: "Trapped by tangled vines, in perilous unease; it says: movement brings regret, there is regret — yet marching brings good fortune" (困于葛藟，于臲卼，曰動悔有悔，征吉) — [the foot is entangled like deep peril among vines; to move brings regret, but once the heart resolves, marching is auspicious, and not marching is inauspicious]. Therefore the image is also taken from Kun.

Were it not the great fruit (碩果) — [meaning the right foot] — that is not eaten, how could one reach the islet (祗)? Once leveled, the cup of wine and the pair of vessels; counsel admitted through the window — escaping danger, and gaining the divination of "the wheel-strap comes off" (脫輻).

By the time one heel has kicked back, the overturned Standstill (傾否) has passed and the great difficulty is Resolved (解); the bent mulberry puts forth shoots — is this not a great rejoicing? People say "Heaven aids him"; I say in truth it comes from man: if one attains the Way, one divines good fortune of oneself [hexagram Fu 復, Return].

Deng Yi Gen Qi Yan Li Yu 蹬一跟七言俚語 (Seven-character Rhymes on Kicking with the Heel)

One: The left foot kicks west and upward; both hands press the ground with tiger strength. The inverted body kicks at the hand [the enemy's hand]; turning over, the hand plays out striking toward the chest.

Two: Then send the right foot kicking up to heaven; follow along the left leg, sweeping without deviation. When matters reach their hardest there is still a method — who would know the body can undo the inverted hang.

Trang sách 194 · scan p227

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo 陳氏太極拳圖說, printed page 194.*

Heading

Posture 33: The Fourth Yan Shou Chui 演手捶 (Play-the-Hand Punch)

Diagram

The figure stands in a bow stance facing West, right fist striking forward, left hand near the body. Vertical labels beside the figure:

- 眼看右拳 — "The eyes watch the right fist"

- 頂精領住 — "The crown essence leads and holds"

- 周身力氣俱聚于右拳，尤須用其膀力 — "The strength and qi of the whole body gather in the right fist; in particular the shoulder power must be used"

- 腰精下去 — "The waist essence sinks down"

Caption beneath the figure: "The right foot follows the body's inversion turning West, opening one more step without moving; the left foot lands and opens one step."

Main text (right column)

The face is toward West, the body toward due North. The right hand comes from behind and travels upward, advancing past in front of the face; grasp into the fist (捋 lu), using the reeling-silk essence (纏絲精 chan si jing) to reel from outside inward, reeling to the fist, uniting the essence to strike forward. The left foot first lands, opening one step; the right foot inverts, from behind opening one more step toward West, and stays without moving; the left foot then opens one large step toward West, and stays without moving. The right foot presses firmly on the ground behind; the heel force travels in reverse up the leg to the twenty-first vertebra of the spine, then in reverse from the twenty-first vertebra up to the shoulder, and from the front of the shoulder to the right fist, to aid the force of the fist.

Main text (left column)

When the heel kick (蹬一跟 deng yi gen) is finished, the left foot first lands and inverts, coming from East through South to West. The right foot opens one more step toward West, the chest toward North. The left foot then performs Yan Shou Chui toward West. The left hand is in the West, using the uniting essence (合精 he jing) to answer the right hand. The left hand uses the inverted-reeling essence toward West, back of the hand facing up; united, the punch strikes the enemy — no enemy can withstand it. Facing the enemy's fist, it falls into the left wrist in playing the hand (演手). The left and right arms need not spread wide; judge by the enemy's distance. If the enemy stands far from me, spreading the arms is permissible; if the enemy is very far, advancing in consecutive steps is permissible — one step, two steps, or three steps — the right hand grasping into the fist, the arm extended to strike. In this way...

Trang sách 195 · scan p228

陳氏太極拳圖說 · Volume II

"Linked through three steps" — that is the meaning. If the enemy clings (li 麗 means to attach) too close to my body, there is no harm in bending the arm, the hand grasping into a fist, and using the strength of the whole body to strike with a努 (努, explosive force). This, however, still depends on the state of one's gongfu, one's strength, and one's issuing jing (chu jing 出精). If the gongfu, strength, and issuing jing are all abundant and flowing freely, then using this explosive force is far better than extending the arm. For the moment this place moves, the other person falls several bu 武① away. If not, then striking with a bent elbow is still not as effective as striking with an extended elbow — for although extending the elbow may not fell a person, it beforehand has none of the hindrance of a constrained arm. The jing at the crown (ding jing 頂精), the jing at the waist (yao jing 腰精), the gaze, and the jing at the crotch (dang jing 襠精) — where they should rise and where they should join — all follow the previous method.

As for the footwork: after the backward-leaning kick is finished, the face is toward the East; from East one turns leaning toward South, then West. The left foot lands first, twisting on its heel, going from East to South. The right foot turns around and takes a step from South toward West; at this point the chest already faces North. The left foot then takes another step toward West — this makes three consecutive steps of the left and right feet. Before the left foot has landed firmly, the right fist strikes toward the West. If there is no enemy in the West, the right fist may simply land outside the left shoulder and left ribs, some six or seven cun from shoulder and ribs. The empty space — in the end the boxing postures have no fixed frame; it would even be acceptable for the right fist to land just in front of the left ribs, only five or six cun from the left nipple. In ordinary practice of the form one must neither neglect to keep the established rules nor rigidly adhere to them — it rests on the learner's ability to make good use of the inner jing (nei jing 內精). As for the outward traces of movement, if the terrain imposes limits, adapt and apply them according to the lay of the ground; that is acceptable.

When performing the High Pat on Horse 高探馬, one stands and strikes a person. Upon meeting a strong enemy — if on the South side the hand cannot reach (xia, here read in the level tone), insert the right foot; or on the North side insert the left foot; or turn the head toward the East and perform the hand strike (yan shou 演手); or lean back, turn the head toward the West into the Double Punch (er qi 二起); or kick with the left foot toward the West; or turn around and use the Great Turning Body Kick (da zhuan shen 蹬) with the right foot. Above there are the images of Shi He 噬嗑 (Biting Through), the wooden cangue (he 校, to wear) on the neck, and the misfortune of "destroying the toes"; below there are the images of Da Guo 大過 (Great Excess), "wading through" (guo she 過涉), "destroying the crown of the head" (mie ding 滅頂), Shi He 噬嗑, "destroying the ears" (mie er 滅耳), Yi 頤 (Mouth Corners), and "toppling Yi" (dian yi 顛頤) — the Way turns back and forth; who knows when one can come out of layered peril and "cross the great river" to attain the "solitary return of the middle way"? Now, however, it is "return after seven days" (qi ri lai fu 七日來復); "treading the way, smooth and even" — who would not use the warrior's campaign, performing it with the hand to repay enmity! Confucius said: "To repay enmity with uprightness is not excessive."

Diagram Descriptions (Tu Shuo 圖說)

Diagram of the right fist (right fist posture): annotations — shoulder 肩 (vai), back of the elbow joint 肘節背面.

Diagram of the left hand (left hand posture): annotations — elbow crease facing inward 肘彎朝裡.

Footwork diagram (feet with curved arrows):

- the left foot in front (在前左足)

- the right foot behind (在後右足)

- unmoving (不動)

- the right foot 右足

- opening a step (開步)

- jing. The right foot also uses the reverse-turning jing (dao zhuan jing 倒轉精), then again toward the West — chân phải cũng dụng tinh đảo chuyển, rồi lại hướng Tây.

- The left foot lands first, then steps toward the West with the left foot; the left heel twists, using the reverse-turning jing — chân trái đáp trước, ngay lấy chân trái mở bước hướng Tây, gót chân trái xoay, dụng đảo chuyển tinh.

- The left foot in the West still uses a large step — chân trái ở phía Tây vẫn dụng đại bộ.

- The left foot, having landed in the West, also does not move — chân trái đáp tận phía Tây cũng bất động.

Inner Jing (Nei Jing 內精)

The central qi (zhong qi 中氣) rises from the dantian 丹田 up to the shoulder — (Trung khí từ đan điền đi lên tới vai,)

① shu wu 數武: several half-steps; the ancients held that "a half-step is one wu 武." (số vũ: vài nửa bước, người xưa có thuyết "nửa bước là một vũ")

Trang sách 196 · scan p229

陳氏太極拳圖說 (continued)

From the shoulder it goes down and outward, coiling obliquely from the outside until it reaches the lower edge of the third segment of the back of the fist. The force rises from the rear heel, travels counter-current up along the spine to the right fist; one must use the shoulder-arm force joined in jing (he zhu jing 合住精) — this is striking with the left hand. It is reverse coiling (dao chan 倒纏) arriving at the hand; the hand slightly hooks and holds the wrist toward the East. The jing at the crown (ding jing 頂精) and at the crotch (dang jing 襠精) are as before; the left knee bends and holds, joining with the right knee; the foot treads the ground level and stable; the right foot is behind as if kicking against something, to assist the force of the right fist. The right knee must not go soft — it joins its jing with the left knee.

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang 取象)

The previous five forms truly carry the image of Xi Kan 習坎 (Repeated Peril — layered danger). The third line says: "Coming and going, pit after pit; perilous, and leaning back one falls into the pit's hollow. Even if one wishes it, it cannot be used" — therefore it is taken from Kan 坎 (Water). Yet even though in the affairs of the world it is said "there is no level ground without slopes," there is also "no going without returning" ("no level without slopes" refers to the previous five forms: what is rooted in Heaven draws near to the top yet instead draws near to the bottom — this refers to the head; what is rooted in Earth draws near to the bottom yet instead draws near to the top — this refers to the feet. "No going without returning" means that those drawing near the top and those drawing near the bottom each return to their original positions). When Pi 否 (Standstill) reaches its extreme, Tai 泰 (Peace) comes; therefore Pi and Tai are taken again — now the time has become Tai. Thus, "advancing as if checked" and "walking alone in the correct way," one takes from Jin 晉 (Progress). The Tuan Commentary says: "Jin means advancing; the yielding advances and moves upward" (this means the force of the right foot's great heel also moves upward, assisting the right fist's force). Top line nine: "advancing to the horns, used only for punishing one's own city; danger, yet good fortune" (taking danger as good fortune). Who says "dense clouds but no rain, from our own western outskirts"? If one returns by one's own way, then "it has rained, it has settled" — what blame could there be? Therefore Xiao Chu 小畜 (Small Accumulation) is taken as well.

Fourth Performing-Hand Fist (Di Si Yan Shou Quan 第四演手捶) — Seven-Character Folk Verses

The First

The fourth performing-hand fist faces the West: entering peril and leaving peril, repaying others' bullying.

The right fist must use the strength of the shoulder (bang 膀); with one strike everyone's horse hooves are thrown into confusion.

The Second

The left foot landing comes first of all; the right foot turns and lands in front of the left foot (this refers to the reverse turn, taking a step toward the West).

Then take another step with the left foot — behold the spirit-force brimming in the empty fist.
