陳氏太極拳圖說 · Volume II

The Thirty-Fourth Form: Small Grappling Qinna (Xiao Qinna 小擒拿)

Diagram: a figure stands in a bow stance, left foot in front and right foot behind, both arms extended forward in a pushing/grasping posture; the left hand open, palm facing forward; the right hand in a palm-push posture. Arrows and dotted lines indicate the direction of force and the axis of the body.

Labels on the diagram:

- 眼看着住敵人之胸 — the eyes look, holding to the enemy's chest.

- 而以右手推之 — then use the right hand to push.

- 打拳全手推之 — strike/push with the whole hand in boxing.

- 精領好全身精神，為之一振 — the jing leads well, the spirit of the whole body rouses with it.

- 耳須聽住背後 — the ears must listen to what is behind.

- 在膀力須用上 — the shoulder (bang 膀) strength must be brought into play.

- 左手 左掌 — left hand; right palm.

- 膝撐住，與右膝合住精 — the knee props and holds, joining its jing with the right knee.

- 腰精要下好，腰無力則周身無力 — the waist jing must settle down well; if the waist has no strength, the whole body has no strength.

Footwork instructions (below the diagram):

The left foot — because in front the enemy is rather far from me and striking would not reach (ji 及), one therefore advances a step forward to strike him. But to advance the left foot, the footwork must first advance the right foot, with the left foot following it. The step is already large and cannot advance further, so first advance the right foot with the left foot following; then take one more step forward and one is at the West. If the jing props and rounds, then the whole body has strength; if left and right do not turn roundly, one is not nimble. Therefore the right foot takes up the position of the previous posture and opens forward four or five cun, then stops.

Main text:

From the rear crown of the head down to the thigh bone (bi gu 髀骨), all must be nimble and alive. Although the mind is in front facing the enemy, one must also keep attention on what is behind, fearing that another enemy may attack from the rear where one does not expect it.

When the enemy strikes at me with his hand, I use my left hand with compliant coiling jing (shun zhuan jing 順轉精) to lead away his hand, then use my right hand to push at the enemy's Jiuwei point (jiuwei xue 鳩尾穴, CV15, below the sternum). Palm force must be used — only when there is strength in the palm can one push a person down.

In the previous performing-hand fist, perhaps I did not strike the enemy where it hurts and he fights with me again; or he has been knocked down but rises again and comes back to fight; or this person has fallen and yet another enemy comes forward to oppose me — in every case he is rather far from my body and we cannot close and cross hands. Then the feet below must advance, left and right, bringing my body near the enemy; afterward the left hand deflects the enemy's hand aside, and the right palm pushes forcefully against the enemy's chest — all this is arrayed battle in the open field (lie zhen da zhan 列陣大戰). Here the enemy, slightly defeated, comes again; therefore cover above and strike below, seize and take him — there is no need for large body method. It is called "small" (xiao 小), meaning the body method is small.

Printed page: 197

Trang sách 198 · scan p231

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated · Book 2

Posture 34: Xiao Qin Na 小擒拿 (Small Seizing) — continued

Internal energy (nei jing 內精):

I use the left hand to deflect the opponent's hand; one must use forward-turning energy (shun zhuan jing 順轉精), either drawing it up and leading it, or deflecting it away from north to south. Both hands go out together, left hand above, right hand below, using reverse-turning energy (dao zhuan jing 倒轉精) from south to north, and afterward striking forward. This is the method of stealthy seizing beneath the elbow.

Taking the image (qu xiang 取象):

This posture is like Ma Wu capturing the bandits, nearly defeated by Zhou Jian and Su Mao (referring to the previous postures). Then Wang Ba came to the rescue; Wu fought with doubled strength and turned defeat into merit (referring to the preceding Yan Shou Quan 演手捶 — the "Performing-Hand Fist"). Having won, one must still pursue to the utter end, as Cangtou Zimi killed Peng Chong until his submission. Small seizing and striking is like Xiang Yu striking Zhang Han — nine battles, nine victories. Or again, after the Hong Canal marked the boundary, the King of Han was determined to drive Xiang Yu to his death at the Wu River before stopping. Or again, like the Jin hexagram's "advancing as if crushed" (crushing means pushing the enemy down with the hand) — the image of "advancing alone is upright," hence the Jin hexagram is taken. Again the second line: "advancing as if sorrowful" (sorrow is caution shown outwardly), "perseverance brings good fortune" (moving it with central qi, upright and firm). "He receives great blessings" (referring to returning in triumph with victory) "from his queen mother" (the Queen Mother dwells in the Yao Pool in the west; a series of postures all strike westward, and this one goes yet further west). Also the top ninth line: "advancing to the horns" (a metaphor for the right palm), "it is fitting to attack the city; danger" (li 厲 means severity, courage) "but good fortune, no blame." Again, like the Ming Yi hexagram's "hunting, one must obtain the great head before stopping." Again, like what Pan Geng said: "if there are those who are not auspicious, not obedient, who rebel and transgress, disrespectful, who in the moment commit treachery, I will cut them off and exterminate them, not allowing them to leave descendants in this new city" (referring to the principle of eradicating evil at its root) — the image of Family, top ninth: "having sincerity, majestic, in the end good fortune"; though one goes among evil men, though coming is obstructed, one returns to greatness. Hence the images of Family top ninth, Gui first line, the ninth line of Jian, and Jian top sixth are also taken.

Six-character folk verse on Xiao Qin Na:

The preceding posture's Yan Shou is most brilliant; moreover it wins by the unexpected (qi 奇 — stratagem: above covering, below striking, striking the man by stealth, hence called "unexpected"). Heart, hand, eye, and foot as one breath; the enemy is surely seized.

Diagrams:

1. Circular diagram — titled 右手掌前推图 ("Diagram of the right palm pushing forward"). Circular text: 此即 / 右手掌前推 / 自南而北 / 倒转精 / 左手拨开 / 自北而南 / 顺转精 / 左手拨开.

2. Standing figure diagram:

- Right side: 左手用引精引开敌人之手，须用缠丝精 ("the left hand uses leading energy to deflect the enemy's hand; it must use coiling silk energy 缠丝精"); 引之，令其脚立不稳 ("lead him so that his feet cannot stand steady"); 下体步法与前法相同 ("the lower-body footwork is the same as the previous method"); 东 ("east").

- Left side: 前即西方 ("in front is the west"); 步即速向前开步 ("step quickly forward").

- Feet: 左足随住右足开步 ("the left foot opens a step following the right foot"); 左足原位 ("left foot in original position"); 右足原位 ("right foot in original position"); 右足先向前开步 ("the right foot first opens a step forward").

3. Footwork diagram: 步即速向前开步 ("step quickly forward").

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated · Book 2

Posture 34: Xiao Qin Na 小擒拿 (Small Seizing) — folk verses

Seven-character folk verse

First:

The rear heel follows, the left foot forward ("forward" means advancing forward); the left foot lifts and steps again ahead. The left hand lifts up as if to block and parry; the right hand with one palm strikes straight at the strong point.

Second:

One palm to the belly — pain reaching to heaven; stealthily it passes through beneath the left elbow (piercing through). Even an immortal certainly cannot defend against it — how much less a mountain peak, entirely hao ran 浩然 (hao ran is the central qi 中气).

Five-character folk verse

The west is Geng-Xin Metal 庚辛金, the season when the ten thousand things bear fruit (when the myriad things reach autumn they all bear fruit); why speak of Xiao Qin Na — arriving here it is nothing remarkable (referring to the bearing of fruit).

Seven-character folk verse

One bout attacking east (all the postures lie west of this posture), one bout west (the Yan Shou Quan 演手捶 lies in the west); west and yet further west (this posture, Xiao Qin Na) — the unexpected lodged in the upright. Only here does one begin to know the Taiji Quan (the preceding postures are all small seizing-and-striking; when the postures arrive at Xiao Qin Da, that is a small moment of fruition for Taijiquan. Within the substantial there is the insubstantial, within the insubstantial the substantial — the natural wonder of Taiji. Only when the use reaches its fruition does one awaken to the refinement of this principle).

Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated · Book 2

Posture 35: Bao Tou Tui Shan 抱头推山 (Embrace the Head and Push the Mountain)

Diagram: a figure in a bow stance, left leg bent forward, right leg extended behind, both arms extended forward in a pushing motion.

Labels on the diagram:

- 上既有咸其辅颊舌，则咸其耳，咸其目，咸其头一头能触，咸其肩，咸其肘，皆在其中 — since above there is already "sensing the jaws, cheeks, and tongue," then "sensing the ears," "sensing the eyes," "sensing the head — the head can touch," "sensing the shoulders," "sensing the elbows," all are contained within it.

- 左手掌与五指俱用精前推，两肘要摘精，亦犹左足之咸其拇也，两肩用力 — the left palm and five fingers all use energy pushing forward; the two elbows must gather energy, just like the left foot's "sensing the great toe"; both shoulders apply force.

- 顶精颈好，眼看向右手 — the crown energy and neck are good; the eyes look toward the right hand.

- 咸其辅颊舌（辅口辅腮内肉颊颊颊舌口内舌也）— "sensing the jaws, cheeks, tongue" (fu is the inside of the mouth and cheeks; jia is the inner flesh of the cheek; she is the tongue inside the mouth).

- 右手掌用精亦犹右足之咸其腓也 — the right palm uses energy just as the right foot "senses the calf."

- 心为一身之主，后既咸其腹，前之咸其心者可知 — the heart is the master of the body; since behind it "senses the belly," that in front it "senses the heart" may be known.

- 咸其股膝可知矣 — "sensing the thigh and knee" may be known.

- 咸其腓（足肚也）— "sensing the calf" (the belly of the leg).

- 裆撑圆 — the crotch supports round.

- 后足蹬好 — the rear foot presses well.

- 大趾也（足咸其拇）— the great toe (the foot "senses the thumb").

- 腿弯直硬，一丝不可软 — the leg is straight and hard, not one thread may soften.

- 咸其腹（腹背内在心上而相背不能感物而无私系）— "sensing the belly" (belly and back are within, above the heart, facing away from each other; unable to respond to things, having no selfish attachment).

Main text:

My face is turned toward the man I have seized and his surrounding partisans who oppose me. Suddenly someone strikes at me from behind; fearing a blow to my skull, I instantly twist my face around, and with my left and right hands I part the enemy's left and right hands, then with both hands push against the enemy's chest, so that his two hands cannot come inside to strike me. The posture is like hands pushing a mountain, seeking to topple it. The right thigh, which was extended, suddenly bends; the left thigh, which was bent, suddenly extends; the left foot presses hard against the ground; the crown and neck are well led; the waist energy sinks well; the crotch energy supports round; the soles press hard into the earth; the strength of the shoulders is delivered into the palms; the strength of the whole body pours into both palms. In pushing, power values divine speed: even if one cannot push him over, one can make him retreat several paces.

Internal energy (nei jing 內精):

Both arms and both thighs all use coiling silk energy (chan si jing 纏絲精), coiling from outside inward, taking their combined forces joined for the attack. The nearer the enemy, the faster the push: from afar pushing is easy, from close it is hard. Advance like a fierce wind driving a man, like a lightning flash — the faster the better. The left and right hands first travel from above downward, rising from the two thighs, parting, then closing from outside inward, entering between the enemy's two... (text continues on the next page).

Printed page: 200

Trang sách 201 · scan p234

*(From Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo, juan 1, part 2.)*

...within the forearm, press down against the opponent's chest and push with force. If you are only a few inches from the opponent's body, pushing slowly will not topple him, and it will even give the opponent time to devise a clever trick — therefore swiftness is prized.

In the previous posture the left foot faced west and was in front; in this posture the body has already turned toward the east, so the left foot, formerly in front, now becomes the rear foot. The left foot need not leave its place; merely twist on the heel. Once turned, the right foot, formerly behind, now becomes the front foot: from its original position toward the east, step out once more toward the east. Step according to each person's foot length; the stepping must place the foot in a position of advantage, with strength applied above — the step goes no farther than one chi.

[Diagrams] Four small diagrams in a row: Diagram of the Right Hand Reverse Coiling Method (you shou dao jing chan fa tu), Diagram of the Left Hand Reverse Coiling Method, Diagram of the Right Leg Reverse Coiling Method, Diagram of the Left Leg Reverse Coiling Method — each showing silk-reeling (chan si) pathways. A large figure in a low stance with both arms raised: captioned Diagram of the Right Foot Opening Step (you zu kai bu tu), with notes "right hand from below upward" and a partially cut-off note on the left hand. A small sketch of the feet stepping.

Qu Xiang (取象) — Taking the Image

In the previous posture I was facing west, seizing an opponent, when suddenly someone comes from the east intending to attack me unawares — this is being "stimulated" from behind. I instantly turn around to face due east and push with both hands — this is my stimulating him. The two stimulate each other, just like the young man and young woman of the Yijing mutually stirring one another; hence the image is taken from Xian (咸, hexagram 31). Xian means "to stimulate/move." If someone comes to provoke me, I will not lightly let him go; and in my provoking him, would I lightly let him go? Since neither will lightly let go, it must come to using the strength of the whole body, pushing as if to topple a mountain. Hence "stirring the big toe," "stirring the calf," "stirring the thigh," "stirring the cheeks" of the Xian text are all appropriate. Moreover, the five sense organs and hundred bones all join in and are stirred together. Fearing that the strength of the two hands alone is small and cannot topple a man, it is better that the strength of the whole body is great and can overthrow the enemy. The Image says: "Two vapors respond and stimulate, and thereby unite" (speaking of man and woman); "thus benefit favors steadfastness." In the boxing, it is I who stimulate others with the upright qi (zheng qi) of heaven and earth — nothing fails to penetrate, nothing fails to profit, correct and firm. Thus in movement one does not lose constancy: even in sudden alarm one remains composed. If pushing is appropriate, push — not deliberately intending to push, yet naturally without losing...

Trang sách 202 · scan p235

...the peril of not pushing. Yet the head is not excessively heavy, so wrap around with the hands from left and right and push.

Bao Tou Tui Shan (抱头推山, "Embrace the Head and Push the Mountain") — seven-character vernacular verses

Verse 1

Fangzhang, Penglai, Yingzhou — mountains of the immortals; assuredly wondrous immortals dwell therein (a metaphor for the opponent). I now imitate a gallant man: with both hands push him into the great billows.

Verse 2

To push the mountain, why raise the arms to embrace the head? For fear of a chopping blow to the crown, take the upper position. Turn the body, embrace the head, and advance forward — push down Penglai and Yingzhou and cover the nine regions.

Verse 3

Both hands support the chest as if pushing a mountain, regretting not to topple him in one stroke. This body's strength must be united; pay careful attention further to the region of the spine (this calls up the intent of the following posture, Dan Bian / Single Whip).

(No diagrams on this page.)

Trang sách 203 · scan p236

Title and Section Explanation

Posture 36 — the fourth Single Whip (單鞭, Dān biān)

Jie jie (節解) — section explanation.

Labels on the figure (right to left)

- "The eyes watch the tip of the left middle finger."

- "The crown jin (頂精, dǐng jìn) leads and holds up."

- "Both shoulders relax downward — never raise them up."

- "The right arm uses reverse silk-reeling jin (倒纏絲精, dào chán sī jīn) from below the shoulder/armpit, coiling from outside to inward."

Yinmeng (引蒙) — Instruction

Having just thrown down the enemy attacking from the east with "Embrace the Head and Push the Mountain" (抱頭推山), suddenly another person strikes from the west. I immediately take both hands on the east side, coiling with outer-to-inner jin in one closing (合, hé); then, coiling with inner-to-outer jin, I chop out toward the west, while the left arm spreads open.

The positions of the body points have already been described under the previous method. The heart/mind must be empty (虛, xū); when the mind is empty, the four limbs are all empty. The dantian (丹田), the waist jin, and the soles of the feet must be solid (實, shí). When these three places are solid, what was empty in the four limbs all becomes solid — this is called "empty yet solid." When the crown jin is well led, the spirit of the whole body is roused. The right arm, in closing, uses reverse silk-reeling jin; in extending, it uses forward (順, shùn) silk-reeling jin. The left arm, in both closing and extending, uses reverse silk-reeling jin. Both legs, in closing, use reverse silk-reeling jin: coiling from the foot up to the back of the knee as the stance opens. The right foot does not move; the left foot steps out toward the west, using forward silk-reeling jin, coiling from the foot inside-outward, likewise coiling up to the back of the knee.

*(Chen Family Taiji Boxing Illustrated Explanation, Volume 2. Page 203.)*

Quxiang (取象) — Taking the Image

This posture faces east — it is said to face east, but the chest points due north and the face is only half-turned toward the east; this need not be argued again. But because this art is named Taiji (太極), from beginning to end images are taken from the *Book of Changes* (《易》, the Yijing); therefore for this posture as well one takes the images of the *Changes* that accord with the boxing…

*(Continued on the next page. Where small characters were unreadable this is marked; connections follow the sense of the text.)*

Trang sách 205 · scan p238

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (Chen's Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation), Volume 2*

...and take it from them. The *Yi Jing* (*Book of Changes*) begins the first part (upper canon) with Qian and Kun and ends it with Kan and Li, because Qian and Kun, by the intercourse of the central qi (i.e. the qi of the central line), beget in the second generation Kan and Li; hence the upper canon ends with Kan and Li. In taijiquan, by the fourth Single Whip (dan bian 單鞭) thirty-four postures have been completed, so it too can take Kan and Li as a conclusion. Yet the movement of qi has never stopped — this is only borrowing Kan and Li to close this passage for the moment. Speaking first of the Li trigram: Li is empty in the middle with one yang line above, like the yang line below the left and right upper arms; [the yin lines] are like the yin within the left and right legs; it resembles the heart's Li brightness, upper Li and lower Li shining upon each other in succession. The heart's empty clarity is like sun and moon, shining in succession without a moment's interruption. Moreover, once the heart is empty, the whole body is empty. Only emptiness is spirited (ling 靈), and spiritedness suffices to answer the opponent — hence this is taken from Li.

Speaking of the Kan trigram: the left and right upper arms at the two sides resemble the yin line at the top of Kan; the left and right feet standing apart resemble the yin line at the bottom of Kan; the central qi threading through the middle of heart and kidneys, reaching upward to the crown of the head and downward to the huiyin (perineum), is like the single yang line in the middle of Kan. For only when a person's solid principle and solid qi (solid principle is utmost sincerity; solid qi is the flowing mechanism of transformation) are full within can opening-closing, seizing-releasing proceed without obstruction — hence this is taken from Kan. To combine them: at first pulling Single Whip one attends to the circulation, which is all the central qi — that is Kan; at the moment of pulling Single Whip, the empty-spirited heart illuminates everything — this is Kan producing its image in Li. When the Single Whip posture is complete, the heart is calm and the qi harmonious, the central qi returns to the dantian (丹田) — this is Li in turn producing its image in Kan. Kan and Li join, returning again to the Qian and Kun trigrams, returning to the primordial qi of taiji's yin-yang. The heart belongs to fire, the kidneys to water — these are just the Kan and Li of the *Yi*. Thus once heart and kidneys interact, it returns to Qian and Kun, and the primordial qi of taiji in my body (primordial qi is the qi of yin-yang and the five phases) is restored. Qian and Kun, the firm and the soft, use yin and yang together, neither leaning to one side, neither exceeding nor falling short; when Kan and Li attain the center, this art is complete. Kan and Li certainly have their proper positions as Kan and Li, but to regard Kan and Li merely as Kan and Li is not to know whence Kan and Li come; it is moreover not to know that Kan is the middle son and Li the middle daughter, and that the middle son and daughter are themselves a small Qian-Kun within Qian-Kun. My body contains within it the principle and qi of yin-yang — in the midst of heaven and earth, does it not itself possess the upright qi of Qian and Kun! Since it possesses the upright qi of Qian and Kun, my body too has of itself the primordial qi of taiji. To move this primordial qi inherent in my body through my body — its bending and stretching, going and coming, gathering and releasing, seizing and letting go — is nothing but one opening and one closing, one empty and one full! So this posture may be called Kan-Li, or it may be called Qian-Kun (tracing it to its source), or even taiji — none of these is not permissible. And if from being it returns to not-being, calling it wuji (無極, the limitless) is also not impermissible. Provided the principle can be extended flexibly, any of these may be borrowed as a name. But the image of Single Whip is close to Kan and Li, so Kan and Li are used to clarify Single Whip — this is why Single Whip takes the trigrams Kan and Li.

Fourth Single Whip, Seven-character Folk Verse (第四單鞭七言俚語)

No. 1 (其一)

第四单鞭象坎离，闲拉无事不为奇。

抱头方向东边击，转向西方向更宜。

The fourth Single Whip images Kan and Li; pulling it idly when at leisure is nothing remarkable. Facing east to strike with baotou ("embrace the head"), yet turning to strike toward the west is even more fitting.

(Page number: 205; no diagrams on this page.)

Trang sách 206 · scan p239

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo — continuation of "Fourth Single Whip, Seven-character Folk Verse"*

No. 2 (其二)

双手抱头向东推，又有敌人自西追。

回头诸势来不及，惟用单鞭最相宜。

Both hands embrace the head and push to the east, while another enemy pursues from the west. For all the other postures there is no time to turn the head back — only using Single Whip is most fitting.

No. 3 (其三)

左足从容向西方，抱头东推力倍强。

庚辛（西方也）从后来相击，转（即回头也）用单（用，我用也）鞭一命亡。

The left foot unhurriedly points to the west; embracing the head and pushing east, the force is doubled. When Geng-Xin (i.e. the West) strikes from behind, turning (i.e. turning the head back) and using Single (yong 用, "use," means "I use") Whip — a life is lost.

No. 4 (其四)

忽然左耳听西方，若有人兮称刚强。

岂知太极元气转，为用全鞭孰敢当？

Suddenly the left ear listens to the west, as if someone there boasts of his strength. How would he know the primordial qi of taiji turns — when the whole whip is set to use, who dares withstand it?

No. 5 (其五)

声东击西计最良，此是平居善用方。

谁知实向东推毕，转脸四击一字长（一字，言我单鞭如一字长蛇阵）。

"Make noise in the east, strike in the west" is the finest stratagem — this is the method to use well in ordinary practice. Who would know that, having actually finished pushing to the east, I turn my face and strike in four directions with a "one-line" length (yizi 一字, "one line": my Single Whip is like the one-line long-snake battle formation)?

(Page number: 206; no diagrams on this page.)

Trang sách 209 · scan p242

Section 37: Qian Zhao 前昭 (Forward Gaze) (Volume 3, printed page: 209)

Diagram Labels

- Left elbow sinks down

- The five fingertips and the middle and lower segments apply force

- The arm bends slightly, three or four parts

- The gaze fixes on the middle finger of the left hand

- The jing 頂精 at the crown leads and holds up (dingjing)

- The ears listen to the back of the body, guarding against the enemy's stealthy attack

- The shoulders are compliant; the Jianjing 肩井 and Futu 扶突 points all relax and sink

- The elbow tip points upward

- The chest closes forward

- The left knee bends slightly, one or two parts

- The left foot touches the ground but must be empty

- The crotch jing 裆精 opens round

- The right foot steps flat and solid on the ground

- The right knee bends and holds forward

- Waist, body, and crotch all sink down together

- The five fingers of the right hand close together; the wrist draws back, but do not let it hang down excessively

(Figure: a standing figure with the left arm extended forward and leading upward, the right hand gathered behind at the waist, feet clearly divided into empty and solid.)

Explanatory Lecture (Yinmeng Jiangyi 引蒙講義)

What is "qian zhao" (forward gaze)? The eyes look forward, gazing at the left hand. Why gaze at the left hand? Suppose the enemy is to the west and comes to seize the hand or twist the forearm: I lead my left hand upward and toward the north, then turn a small circle from north to south (falling tone), striking with the back of the hand and the back of the forearm. This demands quick hands and sharp eyes; any delay and one risks being controlled. At the moment the left hand leads upward, the waist and crotch sink down together; the upper body revolves and feels lively, while the lower body does not stiffen and die — the right knee bends and holds, the left leg gathers in, and it becomes naturally easy. As for the right hand behind: when the left hand leads upward, turning a small circle from south to north, the back of the right hand, together with the arm, also turns a small circle from north to south. The left hand turns with the flow, the right hand turns in reverse; the back of the left hand faces south, the back of the right hand faces north. In sum, the whole body must be made to move upper and lower in mutual accord, with one qi 貫通 running through as one.

Trang sách 210 · scan p243

(Printed page: 210)

Inner Jing 內精

(The preceding diagram shows the completed form of Qian Zhao 前昭, but does not yet explain the stages of movement along the arm or the coiling of the true qi 真氣. Therefore further diagrams show how it rises in the arm, how it falls, and how the central qi 中氣 turns and revolves — thereby displaying the natural opening and closing of taiji 太極, achieved without forcing by human effort, and only thus according with principle and method.)

Diagram 1: Left Hand in the West, Turning with the Flow (順轉圖)

(Circle diagram. Center, vertical: "the left hand turns over from inside to outside." Outer ring, directions matched with the eight trigrams 八卦: North, Li 離, Kun 坤, Dui 兌, South, Kan 坎, Xun 巽, Zhen 震.)

Diagram 2: Right Hand in the East, Turning in Reverse (逆轉圖)

(Circle diagram. Center, vertical: "the right hand turns over from outside to inside." Outer ring: North, Qian 乾, Dui 兌, Kun 坤, South, Kan 坎, Gen 艮, Zhen 震.)

Text between the two diagrams: "Reverse hand, with-flow circle… the right hand must follow the left hand's turning" 〔the phrase appears garbled in the print〕.

Main Text

Diagram twelve shows the manner in which the left and right hand methods revolve. Practicing the quan 拳 depends entirely on using the heart-mind 心: the moment the heart's intent stirs and wishes the hand to lead upward and turn a circle, the hand transmits it just as the mind intends. Here the one issuing the command is the heart, the one transmitting the command is the hand, and the one observing the countenance is the eye. This is the teaching of the "three arrivals" — heart, hand, eye — none of the three may be lacking. When crossing hands with an enemy, observing the enemy's countenance, noting where on my own body he fixes attention, and how his hands and feet set up their position, advance or retreat — all of this lies in the eye. Once the eye has seen it, the heart knows it; how to prepare and respond, the hand arrives following the heart. The mechanism is supremely nimble, the movement supremely swift (movement here means the motion of hands and feet). Hence observe his hand and you know his heart.

Left Hand Clockwise Coiling Diagram (左手順纏圖)

The left hand leads upward and turns a circle; the circle drawn by the fingers and the coiling jing 纏精 of the arm are one and the same jing 精 — they must not be seen as two separate segments. Only for ease of seeing is it described by way of the hand.

(Figure: an arm extends diagonally upward; a spiral line runs along it, showing the coiling-silk motion.)

Right Hand Reverse Turning Diagram (右手倒轉圖)

In Qian Zhao the left hand is primary, so the gaze fixes on the left hand — indeed the spirit of the whole body attends to the left hand. The right hand in the east, with the forearm turned back, is not useless: should an enemy attack from behind, one reversal of its jing naturally leaves ample leisure to respond.

(Figure: an arm extends horizontally to the right; a spiral line runs along it.)

(Continues onto the next page)

This posture continues upward from Dan Bian 單鞭 (Single Whip). The arm has already extended to meet the enemy. Yet once the arm has already [continues on p244]

Trang sách 211 · scan p244

(Printed page: 211; Volume 3)

Main Text (continued from the previous page)

…extended. But should another enemy come, the situation will no longer permit extending again; therefore bending must be taken as the superior course. Yet if the forearm is bent, how does one meet the enemy? Thus the hand must be led upward, with the coiling method 纏法 used inside to meet the enemy coming from the left side. This too is the natural mechanism and moment inherent in the quan 拳 — no forcing is required. The left hand, in a person, is by nature not in an advantageous position, and moreover extended but not yet bent: should an enemy come, one has no choice but to lead the left hand upward. When the left hand is up, the jing-force of the entire body must be gathered and poured into the left hand before it will serve the purpose. This is "diminishing the lower to benefit the upper, its way moving upward" (損下益上，其道上行) — hence it is drawn from the Sun 損 (Decrease) hexagram.

Seven-Character Homely Rhyme on Qian Zhao (前昭七言俚語)

The eye watches the left hand — this is Qian Zhao 前昭;

Lead above, strike below, and invite the guest in.

Let him come from the four quarters to bully and encroach:

In a hundred battles, with success achieved, the empty hand is portrayed.

Posture 38: Hou Zhao 后昭 (Backward Gaze)

Diagram Labels

- The right fingers point upward, casting

- The eyes watch the right-hand fingers

- The chest is slightly concave like a qing 罄 (chime stone)

- The crown jing 頂精 leads well

- The ear listens behind the body

- The left shoulder relaxes down

- The five fingers of the left hand are bound together, as if with an intention to raise

- This is the state of being about to gather but not yet formed

- The right foot should withdraw back

- The crotch props open round and holds the jing 精

- The left foot has an intention of advancing

- The bend of the left knee is slightly flexed

- The body closes forward

- The waist jing 腰精 sinks down and unites

(Figure: a bow-stance figure — left leg bent in front, right leg straight behind — left arm extended forward, right arm bent at the elbow with the hand raised near the shoulder, the gaze on the right hand.)

Caption Beneath the Figure

Trang sách 212 · scan p245

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen Family Taijiquan)

Hou Zhao (後昭, hòu zhào — "Backward Shine" / defending the rear)

Diagram labels:

- 右手内精顺转图 (yòu shǒu nèi jīng shùn zhuǎn tú) — Diagram of the right hand's internal essence turning forward; 手背向北 (back of the hand faces north)

- 右足收法 (yòu zú shōu fǎ) — Method of withdrawing the right foot

- 右足用缠丝精图 (yòu zú yòng chánsī jīng tú) — Diagram of the right foot using silk-reeling essence

- 右手顺转缠丝精图 — Diagram of the right hand's forward-turning silk-reeling essence

- 左手倒转缠丝精图 — Diagram of the left hand's reverse-turning silk-reeling essence; 左手下垂，手背向北 (left hand hangs down, back of the hand faces north)

Yimeng Lecture Notes (引蒙讲义)

What is hou zhao? The eyes look to the right hand in order to defend against the enemy. This is a matter of ordinary solo practice — the empty frame one drills — not that an enemy truly exists; it supposes an enemy coming from behind and asks how to resist him. For example: just as qian zhao ends, suddenly countless enemies arrive from the east; the body abruptly turns around, the head faces east, the left and right feet also face east, and the right hand and forearm catch the enemy's hand, circling one round from south to north, then issuing out again from north to south. Before issuing, one must first bend the elbow, keeping the right hand about a chi (尺) from the chest. For if the elbow is not bent it cannot extend; if it cannot extend, how can one resist the enemy? Therefore bending the elbow and circling round are one and the same moment. During qian zhao, the left hand turned forward (顺转, shùnzhuǎn) and the right hand reversed (倒转, dàozhuǎn), the left hand being host (主) and the right hand guest (宾). But when an enemy appears behind and one abruptly turns the body around, the right hand becomes host and the left hand guest. Whereas before the back of the right hand faced north, the moment it engages the enemy the right hand turns around with the momentum, from south to north and back again to south, turning (forward-turning) one full circle to serve as inviting-in (引进, yǐnjìn) and striking momentum. The right hand settles down in the east, hand level with the waist, back of the hand facing north so as to sink into the lower posture, guarding against the enemy encroaching from the left front as one advances. The right knee is bent and held; the right foot also carries the sense of forward turning, planted flat and solid on the ground — yet within the utmost solidity utmost emptiness resides. The left foot is in the west, toes pointing forward, simply still and awaiting movement.

The line diagram drawn after the figure shows the internal essence (内精, nèi jīng) running along its path through the hands and feet from beginning to end of this posture.

Taking the Image (取象, qǔ xiàng)

This posture need not use the large body method (大身法) to turn the corner; it suffices to pass the corner with the small body method (小身法).

Trang sách 213 · scan p246

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3 (卷三)

Hou Zhao (後昭) — continued

Agility and quickness are what is prized. The eyes were just turned to the west when suddenly an enemy comes from the east; the body at once wheels around to face east and answers him with the right hand. This is the small corner-turning body method (小过角之身法) between the end of qian zhao and the beginning of Ye Ma Fen Zong (野马分鬃, Wild Horse Parting Its Mane) — hence the image is taken from the hexagram Xiao Guo (小过, Small Excess). Xiao Guo crosses over Zhong Fu (中孚, Central Sincerity) and images Li (离, Fire); Li is the pheasant — a flying bird. Judging by the hexagram body, the two yang lines of Zhen (震, Thunder) and Gen (艮, Mountain) image the bird's body, the four yin lines above and below image its wings, and the middle-line Dui (兑, Lake) is mouth and tongue — the image of the lingering cry (遗音). The enemy comes from the east, first moving with sound — there is the image of the flying bird leaving its cry behind; one who relies on deceiving others must fail. Hence the first line (初六) says, "The flying bird brings misfortune." The middle-line is Dui; Dui is west and Xun (巽, Wind) is east — I turn from west to east. Therefore the fifth line (六五) says: "I make the offerings in the western suburbs" (自我西郊). And again: "The lord shoots with a corded arrow and takes him in the den" (公弋取彼在穴). I use the right hand to draw him in and strike — like shooting a bird with a corded arrow, no different from taking an object out of a bag. This is the image of "taking him in the den." But unless one's sensitivity has reached the utmost, this cannot even be spoken of. This too is a turn of extraordinary difficulty. This posture cannot be let past — much less when attackers swarm in from left and right; how then shall one resist them? Therefore the boxing art overcomes hardness with softness (以柔克刚) and thereby attains the center. When softness can attain the center, the arrival at good fortune is only fitting.

Hou Zhao: Seven-Character Folk Verse (後昭七言俚语)

Suddenly one turn facing east — countless enemies come, countless attack. Were this body not agile to the utmost, nearly would the back of the head be done for.

Five-Character Folk Verse (五言俚语)

Turn the eyes to face east in zhao — none of them are small heroes. Only guard the head well; why fear that crowd of fellows?

Trang sách 214 · scan p247

The Thirty-Ninth Posture: Ye Ma Fen Zong (野馬分鬃 / 野马分鬃, yě mǎ fēn zōng — Wild Horse Parting Its Mane)

Shan Tong Bei (閃通背, Flash Through the Back), Er Qi (二起, Double Kick) and Dao Juan Gong (倒卷肱, Reverse Reeling Forearm) are body methods of major function in the boxing art. This posture is likewise a body method of major function.

Diagram labels (right side):

- 腰精愈要下去 — the waist essence (精, jīng) must sink down ever further

- 左手在下，五指、手背、肘亦要用精 — left hand is below; the five fingers, back of the hand and elbow must all apply essence

- 耳要听其身后 — the ears must listen behind the body

- 顶精领好则全身精神皆振 — when crown essence (顶精) leads well, the spirit of the whole body is roused

- 眼睛顾视左右要快 — the eyes must glance left and right quickly

- 右手直符 — the right hand holds the straight alignment; the right hand's five fingers and back of hand must all apply essence, and the left hand's straight alignment likewise

Diagram labels (left side):

- 胸合住精 — the chest closes and holds the essence

- 右肘尖沉下用精 — the right elbow tip sinks down applying essence

Diagram labels (bottom):

- 右足踏得十分稳当 — the right foot plants most steadily

- 十指头上握 — the ten fingers grasp upward

- 左手腕朝下 — the left wrist faces downward

- 裆精愈下愈好 — the crotch essence (裆精) sinks, the lower the better

- 胯不可软 — the hips must not be soft

- 左膝微屈腿 — the left knee slightly bends the leg

- 前左脚有欲往前之势 — the front left foot has the intent of moving forward

Yimeng Lecture Notes (引蒙讲义)

What is Ye Ma Fen Zong? The left and right hand methods are like a horse in the wilds whose mane falls in parted locks on both sides — it is an image taken from form. This posture is a great body-spreading, forward-bursting, escaping body method. Above, the crown essence leads and holds the whole body; below, both knees are bent and held; the crotch essence must be empty and round. As for the hands: if there is a crowd of enemies on the left, the left hand rises from below upward, facing outward toward...

Trang sách 215 · scan p248

Right margin (vertical title):

陳氏太極拳圖說卷三 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Scroll/Juan 3)

Top text block (continuation):

"...below to ward it off; if an enemy comes from the right, the right hand likewise goes from below upward, turning a large circle smoothly (shun, 顺转) to ward it off. Generally, whenever the two hands alternate reaching the top, they all fan outward and disperse; yet it is not mere deflecting (bo, 拨), for each carries both drawing-in (yin, 引) and striking (ji, 击). One must have this method of body and hands before one may go out amid a crowd of enemies and come to no harm. This is the art of withstanding ten thousand men, and it is by no means easy."

Diagrams section (right to left):

* Right diagram: 左手运行图 (Diagram of the left hand's movement)

* Curved text above: 左手自上而下 (left hand from above downward)

* Vertical text right: 下行转一大圈以挡之 (descends, turning one large circle to ward off)

* Vertical text right below: 左右肱缠 (left and right forearms coiling, chan 缠)

* Vertical text below: 丝内精图 (diagram of the jing/silk thread within)

* Title below: 左手顺缠图 (Diagram of the left hand's smooth coiling, shunchan 顺缠)

* Central vertical label: 左右手内精 (the jing-silk within left and right hands)

* Left diagram: 右手运行图 (Diagram of the right hand's movement)

* Curved text above: 右手自下而上 (right hand from below upward)

* Curved text below: 膝下止 (stops below the knee)

* Text below: 右手止处 (where the right hand stops) / 右手起处 (where the right hand rises)

* Title below: 右手顺缠图 (Diagram of the right hand's smooth coiling)

Middle text block:

If the left hand issues from below to the top, then the right hand arrives below; one replaces the other in a circle, alternating in rotation. Advancing footwork (bufa, 步法) and hand technique advance together; when the right hand reaches the top, the left hand is below.

Section heading:

野马分鬃象乾卦 六爻俱備图 (Ye Ma Fen Zong 野马分鬃 — Parting the Wild Horse's Mane — imaged as the Qian 乾 hexagram; diagram of all six lines complete)

Bottom text block:

The lines drawn in the jing-silk diagrams, front and back, are the formless momentum (shi, 势) left by the fingers' movement. When it moves, its speed has a sound that can be heard; its dance has a form that can be seen. When the posture finishes its rotation, form and sound both vanish, nothing to see or hear. Hence I deliberately preserve the sense of each posture's rotation to show it: this is called the form of the formless (wuxing zhi xing, 无形之形). The two diagrams above depict the left-right cyclic hand technique; this line diagram depicts the qi (气) circulating within the hand technique like Heaven (the qi of supreme strength cycling without cease).

Bottom circular diagram (following the Qian hexagram lines):

* Top: 群龙无首 用九见 (a host of dragons without a head — "use nine," line 9)

* Right: 飞龙在天 此以下是 (flying dragon in the sky; below this...)

* Bottom right: 用九见 群龙无首

* Bottom: 见龙在田 (the dragon appears in the field)

* Left: 潜龙勿用 (the hidden dragon, do not act)

Printed page number: 215

Trang sách 216 · scan p249

Vertical side title: 陈氏太极拳图说 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo)

Section heading: 取象 (Qu Xiang — "Drawing the Image/Symbolism")

Main text (columns right to left):

This posture moves the whole body purely with the intent of the strength of Qian 乾 (Heaven). The left and right hands and feet closely resemble the image of qian qian (乾乾) — ceaselessly active — hence it is taken from Qian. "Qian is strength": Heaven receives from the Taiji its pure yang, supremely great and supremely firm. Since Heaven opened at Zi (子, the hour of midnight), each day has been so, and through all antiquity it is so; its rotation never ceases, stopping not for a single moment. The ceaseless advance and retreat of Ye Ma Fen Zong 野马分鬃 (Parting the Wild Horse's Mane) is likewise like Heaven's image of qian qian. Moreover the two hands spread forward on both flanks, like the sun and moon in the sky alternating their light by day and by night without resting, so that nothing among the ten thousand things is not bathed in their radiance. It is again like sudden thunder and fierce wind: ahead no one blocks the road, behind no pursuing troops, left and right no obstruction — the wind bends the grass, and wherever it turns all is swept aside. This is what Ye Ma Fen Zong takes from Qian. Yet it is not done merely by great force; it truly works by the pivot of the original qi (yuan qi, 元气) at the center driving and pressing, so that the opponent cannot help but fall back. And because the art of drawing in (yin, 引) and striking runs within the hands and feet, the opponent also cannot come close to my body. Such is the natural subtle function of Ye Ma Fen Zong — and truly the natural subtle function of the strength of Qian. The Tuan Xiang says: "Great indeed is the origin of Qian!" (大哉乾元) — how true it is.

Section heading: 七言俚语 (Seven-character vernacular verses)

Sub-heading: 其一 (The first)

Verse:

两手握地转如飞，中间一线贯无倚。

任他千军围无罅，左右连环破敌欺。

Commentary:

"Two hands grasp the ground and turn like flying" means both hands sweep the ground upward, so that the whole body top and bottom is looked after. The left hand turns first, the right hand after, so that it can connect with the preceding posture Hou Zhao 后昭. "One line through the middle" means the central qi (zhong qi, 中气) runs up from the Baihui 百会 point down through the Changqiang 长强 point, as if strung on one thread. "Linked rings left and right" means the left hand goes from below upward, the right hand turns from above downward, the left hand again goes from below upward, the left [right] hand again turns from above downward; the two hands are like two circles, dancing in alternation, one above the other. Its hardness cannot be broken, its sharpness beyond compare, its rotation without gap — hence it can withstand enemies.

Sub-heading: 其二 (The second)

Verse:

一身独入万人中，将用何法御英雄，

惟有飞风披左右，庶几可以建奇功。

Right sidebar (vertical): 陳氏太極拳圖說卷三 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Juan 3)

Title: 第四十势 单鞭第五 (Posture 40: Dan Bian 单鞭 — Single Whip, the fifth)

Diagram labels (vertical columns, right to left):

- 指肚用力伴住指肚 (the finger pads press; the finger pads accompany/hold)

- 两手把一去声无软 (the two hands hold as one, without slack)

- 肘弯微屈似新月形 (the elbows slightly bent like a crescent moon)

- 眼看住中指 (the eyes fix on the middle finger)

- 顶精颈起来 (the crown jing/silk — the neck lifts)

- 耳听住后面 (the ears listen behind)

- 两肩松下 (both shoulders sink and relax)

- 两肘弯向前弯住 (both elbows bend, held forward)

- 右手五指密依撮住无伸开 (the five fingers of the right hand close tightly together, not spread)

Lower/left area labels:

- 腰精下好 (the waist jing-sink set well)

- 裆圆 (the crotch rounded)

- 右膝微屈 (right knee slightly bent)

- 右足向前钩住 (right foot hooked forward)

- 力抓住地 (strength grips the ground)

- 腓、足踵用 (calf and heel engaged)

- 形，足趾、足 (form; toes and foot...)

- 左足八字撇 (left foot angled in the ba character/splayed)

- 左膝屈住 (left knee bent and held)

- 胸向前合 (the chest closes forward)

Main text — Yin Meng (引蒙, "Guiding the Uninitiated"):

This posture echoes and answers the first Dan Bian (Single Whip). As in writing where rules and measure are observed, it must not scatter. The body method (shenfa 身法), hand method, footwork, and the inner-outer silk-reeling (chan si 缠丝) jing method are all the same as in the first Dan Bian; only its opening differs. The first opening came from the first Lan Ca Yi 揽擦衣 (Gathering and Rubbing the Clothes), with that body method. This Dan Bian comes from Ye Ma Fen Zong 野马分鬃. One must wait until Ye Ma Fen Zong's left hand and left foot are in front, just landed and not yet settled; then with the right foot leap eastward (in common speech, "stepping forward") one large step. First plant the right foot down, then step the left foot westward to draw out the Single Whip. When the right foot leaps eastward, the right hand immediately slants up from below, sweeping a large circle toward the east. Its inner jing uses the smooth coiling method (shun chan 顺缠): from below, inward, upward, outward, down and slanting coiled to the armpit — this is where it differs from the first Dan Bian in continuing from what precedes; the rest of the movements of the body's parts are broadly the same with minor differences.

The inner jing: the right foot advances forward, pushing to the utmost — as far as it can reach. This is flat/level...

Trang sách 218 · scan p251

Headings / lead-in

纵法也。("…this is the method of releasing/extension" — tail of the previous section)

Diagrams

右足前进图 (you zu qian jin tu — Diagram of the right foot stepping forward)

- 右足止处 (where the right foot comes to rest)

- 右足从右足后面落于此处 (the right foot lands behind the right foot at this spot)

- 右手起处 (where the right hand begins)

右手图 (you shou tu — Diagram of the right hand)

- 缠丝劲由东向西起 (the silk-reeling force, 缠丝劲 chansijin, starts from east and goes toward west)

- 按下膝 (press down toward the knee)

- 左手从右足后至此 (the left hand comes from behind the right foot to here)

Notes on the diagrams

- 手往前进须用缠丝精方不直率 (for the hand to advance it must use silk-reeling essence, otherwise it is crude and straight-lined)

- 五指往来勿令散开 (the five fingers move to and fro — do not let them spread apart)

Qu xiang 取象 (Taking the Image)

膻中、鸠尾、气海、丹田，其象与第一单鞭同，皆取乎坎离。右足向东开步，有取乎晋。晋，进也，从后往前也。又取乎震之六五，震，往来厉之象。且震为足，震，东方卦也。右足向东，方锐不可当，故厉。

Translation: The danzhong (膻中, center of the chest), jiuwei (鸠尾), qihai (气海, sea of qi) and dantian (丹田) take the same image as in the first Single Whip (单鞭 danbian), all drawing on the trigrams Kan (坎, Water) and Li (离, Fire). The right foot steps out toward the east, drawing on hexagram Jin (晋, Advance): Jin means advancing, going from rear to front. It also draws on the fifth line of Zhen (震, Thunder): Zhen is the image of danger in coming and going. Moreover Zhen is the foot, and Zhen is the trigram of the east. The right foot goes east with an edge nothing can withstand — hence "danger."

Qiyan liyu 七言俚语 (Seven-character folk verses)

Verse one:

右足急蹦向东方，右手一齐往东趟①，只要顶精提得

(The right foot springs swiftly toward the east, the right hand in unison sweeps eastward①; provided only that the essence at the crown is lifted…)

① The original edition reads "右手一齐往东汤", clearly a copying/compilation error; the character "汤" is corrected to "趟".

(The verse continues onto the next page.)

Trang sách 219 · scan p252

Verse continuation (from previous page)

…好，连身带肘似鹰扬。

(…good — body and elbow together like a hawk soaring.)

Qiyan liyu 七言俚语 (Seven-character folk verses)

Verse two:

左手在左左皆顾，右手随现月光圆，从下往上须斜势，平地飞腾第一山。

(The left hand guards on the left and all is watched on the left; the right hand appears in turn, round as the moon. Rising from below to above must be on the slant — leaping from level ground, the foremost mountain.)

Posture heading

第四十一势 玉女穿梭（第一图） — Posture 41: The Fair Lady Works the Shuttles (Yunü Chuansuo), first diagram

Instructional text

顺转平纵法，青龙出水是直进平纵法，二起是上跃法，此势是大转身法。上承野马分鬃，下来右手趁其在下之势，不容少停，即以右手用缠丝精，从下握上，沿路斜形飞风向东去，指如钢锥。亦全赖右足在前，随住右手，亦用顺缠精。就住上势，大铺身法，尽力向东连进三大步，方够一大圈，约八九尺许。此是右足先蹿一大步之势，尤在顶精提好，裆精不得满足。身随右手，如鸷鸟疾飞而进，莫能遏抑。步落粘地即起，以启左足进步之势。此其三步之第一。下两步得势不得势，设势机关全在于此。此处圈转过一下，破竹不难矣。此玉女穿梭第二步（左足）进步姿势。面已转过向南，身已转过一半矣。此不算成势，是中间运行之形，亦是方转不停，莫误看。左足进步，足趾向东者，亦随右足趾向西，切莫停留。手法、步法、转法，愈快愈好。身方倒转，右足随住身

Translation: The "level leap with conforming turn" (顺转平纵法): Green Dragon Emerges from the Water is a straight-ahead level leap; Double Jump (二起) is a jumping-upward method; this posture is the great-turning method. It follows upon Parting the Wild Horse's Mane (野马分鬃 yema fenzong). From below, the right hand seizes the moment while it is underneath, without the slightest pause: with the right hand using silk-reeling essence (缠丝精 chansijing), it coils up from below and, along its path, slants like flying wind toward the east, the fingers like steel awls. This relies entirely on the right foot in front following the right hand, also using conforming reeling essence. Holding the preceding posture, spread the body wide, and drive forward three large steps toward the east with all your might — only then is one great circle completed, about eight or nine feet. This is the momentum of the right foot first springing one large stride; it depends above all on lifting the crown essence well, with the crotch essence never slackening. The body follows the right hand, advancing like a bird of prey darting swiftly, nothing able to check it. The instant the foot lands it springs off, to launch the left foot's advance. This is the first of the three steps; whether the next two steps gain the momentum or not — the pivot of the whole maneuver lies here. Once the circle turns through here, splitting bamboo is not hard. This is the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, second step (left foot) advancing posture. The face has already turned toward the south, the body already half turned. This does not count as a settled posture: it is the form in mid-transit, still turning without pause — do not misread it. The left foot advances, toes toward the east, following as the right foot's toes point west; do not linger. Handwork, footwork and turning: the faster the better. As the body turns around, the right foot follows the body…

Diagram labels

First diagram (玉女穿梭第一图)

- 顶精领住 (crown essence held aloft)

- 松右肩 (relax the right shoulder)

- 右手仄 (right hand on its side)

- 楞手 (braced hand)

- 前身 (front of the body)

- 贪往

- 右手自转大圈为式 (the right hand turns a large circle as its form)

- 功久自然小方好 (with long practice it naturally becomes small, and that is best)

- 左肘与左手平，去胸六七寸 (left elbow level with the left hand, six or seven inches from the chest)

- 左足此一脚仍是野马分鬃末一步 (this left-foot step is still the final step of Parting the Wild Horse's Mane)

- 右步足，前进 (right foot, advance)

- 左足有争往前进意 (the left foot has the intent of racing forward)

- 大前进步开足初 (at the start of the big forward step, open the foot)

Second diagram

- 玉女穿梭左足进图 (Diagram of the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, left foot advancing)

Trang sách 220 · scan p253

Instructional text (continuation from previous page)

倒转过来，面仍向北，右足再向东开一大步，似停不停，唤起下势起势之脉。本势似与揽擦衣大同小异，然其实大不相同。彼则身不动专用心运其右手、右足，其气恬，其神静。兹则连转身带运手、右足，其气猛，其神忙，非平素实有功夫，临事以中气贯其上下全体者，不能获万全。何也？盖出入广众之中，以寡敌众，旁若无人，惟天生神勇，其胆正、其气刚，其练习纯熟，故披靡一切裕如也。

Translation: Turning back around, the face again faces north; the right foot once more opens one large stride toward the east — seeming to pause yet not pausing, awakening the pulse that launches the next posture. This posture seems similar in the large and small to Lanche Yi (揽擦衣, "Brushing the Robe"), but in truth it is greatly different. There, the body does not move and the mind alone works the right hand and right foot — the qi is tranquil, the spirit calm. Here, turning the body is joined with moving the hand and right foot — the qi is fierce, the spirit urgent. Unless one has genuine skill in daily practice and, at the moment of action, threads zhongqi (中气, central qi) through the whole body above and below, one cannot achieve complete safety. Why? Because going in and out among crowds, meeting the many with the few as though no one were beside you, only inborn divine courage — upright in gall, firm in qi, thoroughly practiced — sweeps everything aside with ease.

身法内精 (Body Method and Internal Essence)

玉女穿梭，非再三图之，不足见转身全像。然三图，以第三图为主，自起势以至终势。右手足虽是顺缠法，而身法皆是倒转精。连三赶进皆是进步，绝无退步之说。至于内精，自顶精以至足五趾，法皆与前同。始终以右手右足为主，而以左手左足佐之。右手顺转，左手必是倒转。此是天然呈象，非人力所能为也。缠丝精即道也者，不可须臾离也。不必再赘。

Translation: In the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, unless it is diagrammed several times the full image of the turning body cannot be seen. Of the three diagrams the third is the primary one, from the start of the posture to its finish. Although the right hand and foot use conforming reeling (顺缠 shunchan), the body method is entirely inverted-reeling essence. Three advances in a row are all forward steps — there is no such thing as stepping back here. As for internal essence, from the crown essence down to the five toes, the method is all the same as before. Throughout, the right hand and right foot are primary and the left hand and left foot assist them. When the right hand turns conformingly, the left hand must turn inversely. This is a natural image, not something human effort can produce. The silk-reeling essence is precisely the Dao, "which cannot be departed from for an instant" — no need to say more.

取象 (Taking the Image)

乘乾健之后，宜取诸离，离中虚象也。心中虚，万理毕具，应敌不难。离本中女，宜属坤，何以舍坤而言乾？盖阳极生阴，又得中气，故取诸乾。且离错坎，坎中满，有理实气空之象。不但此也，玉女穿梭，其进如风，巽为风，故又取诸巽。巽错震，震为足，此势上虽凭手，下尤凭足，足快尤显手快之能。然中女、长女皆带父生之性。故吾谓此势虽以女名，实乾道贯注其中也。故莫或御之。

Translation: Following on the vigor of Qian (乾, Heaven), it is right to take from Li (离, Fire): Li is the image of emptiness within. When the heart is empty, the ten thousand principles are complete within it, and facing an enemy is not hard. Li is originally the middle daughter and should belong to Kun (坤, Earth) — why set Kun aside and speak of Qian? Because when yang reaches its extreme it generates yin, and the central qi is obtained — hence it takes from Qian. Moreover Li inverts to Kan (坎, Water): Kan is full within, the image of principle solid while qi is empty. Not only this: the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles advances like wind; Xun (巽) is wind, so it also takes from Xun. Xun inverts to Zhen (震, Thunder); Zhen is the foot. In this posture the upper body relies on the hands, but below it depends even more on the feet — the swiftness of the feet sets off the power of the swift hands. Yet the middle daughter and eldest daughter both carry the nature of their father. So I say: though this posture bears a woman's name, the way of Qian truly courses through it. Therefore nothing can resist it.

转引转击出重围，宛同织女弄织机；此身直进谁比

(Turning to draw in, turning to strike, breaking out of the encirclement — just like the Weaving Maid plying her loom; this body charges straight forward — who can compare…)

(…swiftness — a whole body of spirit in motion, rare since antiquity.)

七言俚语 (Seven-character folk verses)

Verse two:

天上玉女弄金梭，一来一往织绫罗，谁知太极拳中象，兔走鸟飞（如日月运行之快）拟如何。

(In heaven the Jade Maiden plies her golden shuttle, coming and going, weaving silk gauze; who knows the image within taijiquan — the rabbit runs, the bird flies (as swift as sun and moon in their courses) — how to liken it?)

Posture heading

第四十二势 揽擦衣 — Posture 42: Brushing the Robe (Lancayi 揽擦衣)

此第三个揽擦衣，与第一个揽擦衣相呼应。(This is the third Brushing the Robe, echoing the first.)

Diagram labels

(The figure stands in a left bow stance, left hand extended forward, right hand at the hip; labels in vertical columns:)

Right side

- 此红线即后图之左手叉腰 (this red line is the left hand on the hip shown in the later diagram)

- 之黑线先图于肘沉下去 (the black line is first drawn at the elbow, sinking down)

- 上，以明右手运行肩松下去 (above, to show the right hand's path of movement, shoulder relaxed and sinking)

- 路自何起，至何落耳听身后 (where the path starts, where it ends; the ears listen behind the body)

Upper middle (on the crown)

- 顶精者，是中气上冲于头顶者也，不领则气塌，领过不惟全身气皆在上，足底不稳，病失上悬，即顶亦失于硬，扭转不灵，亦露笨象。是在似有似无，折其中而已

(The crown essence is the central qi surging up to the crown of the head. If not held aloft the qi collapses; but if held too hard, not only does all the body's qi stay up top and the soles become unsteady — losing the suspended-from-above quality — the crown itself becomes stiff, twisting turns clumsy, and clumsiness shows. It lies in seeming to exist yet not exist — strike the mean and stop there.)

Left side

- 眼视中指勿斜 (the eyes look at the middle finger, not aslant)

- 肚住五指束住，指 (the belly settled, the five fingers bound together)

- 胸要虚含如罄 (the chest must be hollow like an inverted bell)

- 右膝露出膝盖 (the right knee — kneecap exposed)

- 力着地 (force reaches the ground)

- 腓、足趾、足 (calf, toes, foot)

- 趾肚俱用 (the balls of the toes all engaged)

- 裆撑圆虚实合住 (the crotch propped round, full and empty joined)

- 左膝微屈 (left knee slightly bent)

- 左足用精蹬 (left foot pushes with essence)

- 腰胯下去 (waist and hip sink down)

- 腿弯莫软 (the bend of the leg must not be soft)

- 手把无软 (the hand-grip not soft)

- 肘沉下 (the elbow sinks down)

引蒙 (Yinmeng — Guiding the Uninitiated)

揽擦衣上下身法、步法，一切皆与第一揽擦衣同。但彼自金刚捣碓来，手足运动似觉稍易；此从玉女穿梭来，较彼似难。盖玉女穿梭，我虽出乎重围，而边贼未

Translation: In Brushing the Robe, the body method above and below and the footwork are all the same as in the first Brushing the Robe. But that one comes from Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounds the Mortar (金刚捣碓 Jingang Daodui), so the movement of hands and feet feels somewhat easier; this one comes from the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, and by comparison seems harder. For in the Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, though I have broken out of the heavy encirclement, the bandits at the edges have not… (continues on next page)

Trang sách 222 · scan p255

...still, therefore, having turned the body around, one immediately uses the right hand to defend toward the east. Look sideways at the third diagram of Yünü Chuansuo (玉女穿梭, "Fair Lady Works the Shuttles") and you will know that in the earlier Lan Cai Yi (揽擦衣, "Brush the Clothes"), the right hand first circles from beside the body before issuing out to form the Lan Cai Yi posture. In this Lan Cai Yi, the right hand is still outside defending against the enemy; you must wait until this enemy has been struck down and another enemy comes, then bring the right hand diagonally downward, from outside to inside, drawing it in beside the right ribs; then from below upward, together with the right hand collected in from outside, altogether making one large circle — only then does the hand begin to move toward the east, forming the Lan Cai Yi posture. The right foot likewise must be drawn from the east in beside the left foot, standing on the ball of the foot; then the right foot follows the right hand, also circling half a circle, gradually bending slowly, stepping out toward the east: the heel lands first, gradually becoming solid forward, placed in a splayed "eight-character" shape, all five toes gripping the ground; the right heel and left heel face each other east–west, not permitted one in front of the other. As for the method of chanshi jing (缠丝精, silk-reeling energy): the right hand uses smooth reeling (顺缠, shun chan), the left hand reverse reeling (逆缠, ni chan), both reeling upward from the finger pads to the armpit and stopping there. The right foot likewise, from inside to outside, coils diagonally upward to the root of the right leg and to the huiyin (会阴, perineum) as far as the left foot. In all the world only the practitioner of pushing (推手, tuishou) can use silk-reeling energy; in stillness it is very hard to use. Yet its intent has never ceased to reside within the thigh. Therefore once a posture is formed, above and below are joined in accord. And the left foot likewise coils from inside to outside diagonally upward to the huiyin — not only joining together with the right thigh, but joining with the whole upper body as one, not slightly lagging behind. Hence I say: even in stillness, silk-reeling energy is never absent from within the thighs — this can be tested at the moment of closing (合, he). If the closing does not reach the huiyin, there is no crotch energy (裆精) and the frame cannot be propped round. This is why silk-reeling energy must never be set aside.

Nei jing (内精, internal energy)

This diagram is divided into two sections: the front half is the completed Yünü Chuansuo (玉女穿梭) posture; the rear section is the path of movement of Lan Cai Yi (揽擦衣).

[Diagram] A figure in a stance (bow-like), with curved lines tracing the circular paths of the hands and feet.

Labels beside the diagram (vertical):

- Along the upper arc: gradual advancing must use silk-reeling energy in its turning movement

- The right hand stops only here

- The hand and forearm are (from) Yünü Chuansuo

- Along the middle arc: the red line is the right hand's drawing-in

- Along the lower arc: smooth turning first follows this path — the left hand

Caption below the diagram: the arm and the "red line" are the front section; the "red line" is (the path) for drawing the enemy in.

Trang sách 223 · scan p256

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (Illustrated Chen Family Taijiquan), Volume 3.

Diagram annotations:

- "The red line is the path of the right foot withdrawing" (紅線是右足收回之路)

- "It is nevertheless also the completed posture of Yünü Chuansuo" (然亦是玉女穿梭成式) — *Yünü chuansuo* 玉女穿梭, "Fair Lady Works the Shuttles"

- "The hand wishes to go right" (手欲向右)

- "The foot wishes to go forward" (足欲向前)

- "The silk-reeling path of the hand" (纏絲的手路) — *chansi* 缠丝, silk-reeling

- "The silk-reeling path of the foot" (纏絲的足路)

…path, which is what is meant by "in order to extend, first bend"; the "black line" is the method of striking — bending yet necessarily extending, a fixed method. The reason one first turns a full circle is that otherwise the arm and hand would both be without power.

That *chansijing* 缠丝精 (silk-reeling force) must be used within the boxing: the method of sticking and connecting (*zhanlian* 粘連) lies entirely here. The method of drawing the opponent in and leading them forward (*yinjin* 引進) also lies here — it must not be neglected. With long practice, one can make others not dare to advance. If they advance, strike them; if they retreat, strike them too.

Taking the Image (取象)

This posture follows Fair Lady Works the Shuttles; again an enemy comes to attack — there is the image of difficulty. Using the right hand to ward him off carries the image of restraining. Combining danger and correctness, these two meanings, there is the image of Kan (☵) below and Gen (☶) above, hence the image taken is Meng (蒙, "Youthful Folly," Hexagram 4). Why take Meng? It means: the man has already failed to understand how to break my Yema Fen-zong (野馬分鬃, "Parting the Wild Horse's Mane"), and cannot defend against my Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, yet still wishes to exploit my danger and block me in front. How could he know that I, with the virtue of strength at the center (*gangzhong* 剛中), move within it. Like the second line of Kan, the central strength embraces all the lines above and below without exception. "Embracing" is precisely the meaning of drawing the opponent in. Making a man know my intent so he dares not advance recklessly — this is the way of "nurturing the immature through correctness." If he does not know to desist, it becomes the posture of the top line's "striking the foolish" (击蒙) — this too is what warding off bandits makes unavoidable. Moreover, Kan is the middle son — his strength is truly strong. Gen is the hand — it has the power to restrain. Employing this centrally, operating with the essence of strength-at-center, how could it be merely a matter of stopping things with the hand? "Stripping the bed to the flesh" (剥床以肤, from Hexagram 23) — injury to the enemy cannot always be avoided. Thus obtaining "the son who can manage the family" (克家之子, Meng's second-line omen) is fitting. As for what lies beyond strength-at-center — all unknown — that is the image of the youthful and ignorant; the youthful mind is single-focused.

Seven-Character Vernacular Verses (七言俚語)

First:

玉女穿梭步向东，轻身直出众人中，

虽有小贼来相犯，中气一击判雌雄。

*Yünü chuansuo bu xiang dong, qingshen zhi chu zhong ren zhong,

sui you xiao zei lai xiang fan, zhongqi yi ji pan ci xiong.*

Fair Lady Works the Shuttles, stepping toward the east; the light body darts straight out among the crowd. Though petty thieves come to offend, one strike of the central qi (*zhongqi* 中气) decides who is master.

Second:

破围全赖揽擦衣，屡次分疏识者稀，

即擒即纵缠丝精，须于此内会天机。

*Po wei quan lai lan ca yi, lüci fenshu shi zhe xi,

ji qin ji zong chansijing, xu yu ci nei hui tianji.*

Breaking the encirclement depends wholly on Lan Cao Yi (揽擦衣, "Grasp and Rub the Robe," i.e. Lazily Tying the Coat); time and again it disentangles, though few recognize it. Seizing and at once releasing — that is the essence of silk-reeling; one must grasp within this the workings of Heaven.

223

① Note: the "red line" and "black line" — in the original manuscript, red and black lines respectively marked the paths of hand and foot movement; these cannot be shown in ordinary black-and-white printed images.

Trang sách 224 · scan p257

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo*, Volume 3.

Posture 43: The Sixth Dan Bian (第六單鞭, "Single Whip")

This corresponds to the Single Whip below Shan Tongbei (闪通背, "Flash Through the Back") earlier. That one was a reversal, leaping out from difficulty, then forming Single Whip to protect the body. This one is likewise a reversal, bounding out (蹦 — pronounced with a rising tone, meaning to leap forward) from difficulty, then forming Single Whip to preserve one's life — slightly harder than the former. Because the enemy's numbers differ, the posture, though the same, comes at a somewhat different time and place.

Diagram Annotations

- Fingers press down; the finger pads exert force.

- The bend of the arm faces forward (that is, due north).

- The eyes look at the middle finger.

- The head and neck are held up as if suspended (顶颈领起来).

- The ears listen behind — must be alert.

- Both shoulders sink and relax.

- Though the elbow cannot bend forward, the intent is as if bending forward in a reversed back-forearm posture.

- The back of the hand easily faces forward, so it is slanted forward with the fingers pointing back.

- Make a "containing" (包含, bāohán) posture.

- The chest is slightly closed.

- The left foot presses and braces.

- The left knee shows.

- The toes of the left foot grip the ground.

- The crotch (裆, dāng) is rounded and braced, yet must remain empty/alive.

- The right foot presses the ground.

- The right heel shows one or two tenths.

- The bend of the arm curves forward; above and below, every part echoes the closed points, mutually encompassing and closing.

- The waist's essence sinks down; the intent closes forward.

Yinmeng (引蒙, "Guiding the Beginner")

The body method, footwork, and all the methods of moving above and below are no different from the earlier Single Whip. That earlier Single Whip has already appeared repeatedly in layers. Now another Single Whip follows — is that not too many? Of the whole body, the two hands are the most convenient to use. Shoulder, back, and elbow are used when the enemy presses against the body; foot and leg are used for what the hands cannot reach. The hands alone can block left and ward right, screen in front and guard behind — every command executed as one wishes. Precisely because they are the most convenient, they are used most often. Moreover, of enemies coming from the front — whether from the left, the right, straight at the center, or from above and below…

224

Trang sách 225 · scan p258

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo*, Volume 3.

(Continuation of Yinmeng, "Guiding the Beginner")

…all of these can be met with the two hands — either together at once, or alternately as they come — and this seems somewhat easier. But when the enemy is at left or right, or comes from both sides at once, then use the central Single Whip (单鞭, dānbiān) to break him; if he comes from the right, use Lan Cao Yi (揽擦衣, lǎn cā yī, "Grasp and Rub the Robe") to ward him off; if from the left, use Single Whip to strike him. Within the boxing there is no method better than this, hence it is used again and again, never tiring of its repetition. Why is it good? Generally, when an enemy comes to bully me, his mind seeks victory, and fierceness predominates. He knows advancing but not retreating — and once he does not know retreat, his mind has already entered my snare (彀中, gòu zhōng). How does he enter the snare? He only knows to advance, so I first respond with retreat. Retreat is precisely drawing-in (引, yǐn). He does not understand my method of drawing-in — I deliberately let him advance, as a plan of ambush. When his wits are exhausted and strength spent, when he realizes the difficulty of striking me and urgently thinks to retreat, it is already too late. At that moment his hands are powerless and his feet without root. So if I do not wish to strike him, fine; but if I do, one turning-around converts defeat into success. This is exactly the method of "to raise, first press down; to extend, first bend" (欲扬先抑，欲伸先屈) — what extraordinary art is there in it! This is still only a principle ordinary people all know; they merely forget it the moment affairs arise. Therefore the gongfu must become thoroughly ripe. And yet within this there is purely and simply the method of chansijing (缠丝精, silk-reeling force), which must not go unknown.

Neijing (内精, "Inner Essence") — Diagrams

- Diagram of the movement path of the left hand in Single Whip

- Diagram of the movement path of the right hand

- This elbow bends forward, not downward. The elbow tip points back; the hand is level with the shoulder — do not mistake this.

- The rear right hand moves forward to meet the front left hand.

- The chest and back fold to close with the rear hand.

- The left hand meets the front right hand.

- The front left hand slants to the right rear.

Trang sách 226 · scan p259

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (Illustrated Explanation of Chen Family Taijiquan)*

Main text

The right foot stays unmoving in the east; only twist the heel so the toes point slightly northwest. When the left foot is drawn in to join it, the feet still do not move.

The left foot plants here, toes pointing northwest. The toes do not first settle fully; above, together with the left hand they rise together and set down together — the intent is again "stopping without stopping, stopping without ceasing," and only this is correct form.

Diagram labels (upper diagram):

- 左足从此收 — the left foot draws in from here

- 左足收至此 — the left foot is drawn to here

- 左足和右足与十字 — the left and right feet form a cross

- 右足从此收（从右足到左足）— the right foot draws from here (from the right foot to the left foot)

Diagram: 左足收回图 (Diagram of drawing in the left foot) — a curved arrow shows the left foot's path, labelled 初发 (initial launch) and 终止 (end point), 由发至止约一尺 (about one chi from launch to stop).

Diagram: 左足开步图 (Diagram of opening a step with the left foot) — a curved arrow with a circle at center, labelled 终止 (end point) at the left end.

取象 (Taking the Image)

This posture embraces the myriad phenomena within the chest and carries the image of Li (離, Fire) hollow at its center: the void is sufficient to hold all principles, so the image is taken from Li. The second line, "Yellow Li, greatly auspicious — it attains the middle way." The top ninth: "The king employs: marching out on campaign is honored; beheading the leaders; capturing the bandits' crowd; no blame." Li, first line changing, becomes Gen (艮, Mountain), paired with Dui (兌, Lake). Dui is joy; Gen is the hand and is stopping. To stop others through joy, nothing serves but the hand. The second line changing becomes Qian (乾, Heaven), paired with Kun (坤, Earth): firm within, yielding without. The third change is Zhen (震, Thunder) as the foot, paired with Xun (巽, Wind), "bringing gain to the market, threefold." In opening a step the foot does not advance recklessly unless it benefits oneself. In this posture the central qi (氣) threads through the foot: the principle solid, the qi hollow — again like the image of Kan (坎, Water) filled at its center; so the image is also taken from Kan. Kan gains Qian's firmness at its center, hence "moving meets with esteem, going achieves merit; entering doubled peril without fear, leaving Kan's hollow with accomplishment." The middle lines: Kan's second and fourth joined form Zhen, paired with Xun, opposed by Gen; Li's second and fourth joined form Xun, paired with Zhen, opposed by Dui. Zhen, the eldest son, presides over sacrifice; Xun, the eldest daughter, carries out the command. The left hand belongs to yang as Zhen, positioned below — yin within yang, of the same kind as the right hand's Xun. This is "the eldest son commanding the army, the younger son accompanying": firm within, yielding without, and therewith one meets the enemy. Gen is stopping, yet nothing fails to follow the way; joy and compliance. Kan's third and fourth joined form Gen, paired with Dui, opposed by Zhen; Li's third and fifth joined form Dui, paired with Gen, opposed by Xun — meaning that when firmness and softness aid each other at the right moment, the disposition is everywhere fitting, naturally according with the natures of the four trigrams Gen, Dui, Zhen, and Xun. As for the subtlety of the movement, it also secretly connects with the hexagrams Jian (漸, Gradual Progress) and Jin (晉, Advance).

七言俚语 (Seven-character folk verses)

First verse

六子用事各有长，皆于乾坤耀精光（乾坤是个阴阳，震巽坎离艮兑六子皆是一阴一阳），果能悟得真主宰（太极之理以御气），人生何处不阴阳。

The six children, each in office, have their strengths; all shine their essential light through Qian and Kun. (Qian and Kun are one yin and one yang; the six children Zhen, Xun, Kan, Li, Gen, Dui are each one yin and one yang.) If one truly awakens to the true Master (the principle of Taiji governing the qi), then in human life where is there not yin-yang?
