*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo — Volume 3*

Second verse

遥承玉女弄金梭，中间揽擦①漾轻波，忽然一字长蛇亘，宛似清秋舞太阿。

Distantly receiving the Jade Maiden playing with her golden shuttle; amid it, the Lan Ca (攬擦, Grasp-and-Brush)① ripples light waves; suddenly a long snake in a single line stretches across, like dancing the Tai'e sword in clear autumn.

Notes:

- ① In the original edition "攬擦" (lan ca) was misprinted as "懶插" (lan cha); corrected here.

- ② The original read "攪插衣"; evidently an error, corrected to "攬擦衣" (Lan Ca Yi).

Diagram title: 揽擦衣②下单鞭上夹缝中左右手足图 — Diagram of hands and feet, left and right, in the central seam of Lan Ca Yi (Grasp and Brush the Robe)② above, with Dan Bian (單鞭, Single Whip) below.

Labels of the three figures:

- The right hand inserts obliquely like flowing water, silk-reeling (纏絲, chan si) joined from outside to inside.

- Joined in mutual response with the left foot; the right foot treads flat, the big toe turned inward.

- The left foot's toes touch the ground, in mutual response with the right foot's toes.

内精 (Internal Essence)

This is the posture of Lan Ca Yi above with Dan Bian below, at the central seam. From where does the internal essence (內精, nei jing) issue, and where does it travel? It issues from the one heart-mind and travels through the marrow of the four limbs, filling the skin and flesh of the four limbs. As the incoming line of Dan Bian enters the preceding posture: Lan Ca Yi has already used opening essence; here, when the heart delights and the intent unites, the whole body, upper and lower, hands and feet, joins together at once. The right hand uses joining essence from outside to inside, inserting obliquely downward, force in the finger pads, while the elbow and the rear of the palm use no force. The left hand, from the left ribs to below the right breast, likewise uses outward-to-inward reeling essence, force in the finger pads corresponding with the right fingers. The right foot, in a figure-eight shape, treads the ground unmoving. Drawn back from the left side, some seven or eight cun from the right foot; the five toes touch and hold the ground. The right foot follows the right hand, the left foot follows the left hand; when the heart's intent wishes to join, then upper and lower, hands and feet all join from outside to inward at once. What joins are the hands and feet — but that by which they join is the heart's essence. Once the heart's essence issues, the sinews, vessels, and joints of the whole body all follow it. What manifests without all issues from what is released within. Thus it is said: after the internal essence has joined, the left hand circles a small coil, extending from right to left; the arm extends seven or eight tenths, stopping without stopping. The left arm uses inward-to-outward reeling essence; the right hand, from below to outside, up, and inward, also circles a small coil, corresponding with the left hand, stopping without stopping. The right arm, however, uses reverse reeling essence, moving through the arm. Between right and left arms the essence is one smooth, one reverse, responding to each other front and back. Below, the left foot and right foot are likewise. After the two feet have joined, the left foot, its toes touching the ground, opens a step to the left of no more than a chi and four or five cun, then stops. When about to open the step, the left foot too first circles a small coil before opening. Otherwise there is not only no posture; the foot...

Trang sách 228 · scan p261

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo*

*(No diagrams on this page.)*

Main text

...it also has no force. Therefore one must first circle a small coil, as the cause that sets the posture for opening the step.

Heaven and earth move entirely by the two qi of yin and yang. We, born with heaven-and-earth's qi, likewise rely wholly on their numinous qi for the body's ceaseless revolving of opening and closing. Thus the movement of our body is the same as the movement of heaven and earth. Yet what moves is the body's frame, while that by which it moves is the principle (理, li) of Taiji. Only because principle governs the qi does our movement — high or low, reversed or upright, now slow, now swift, now hidden, now manifest, greatly opening and greatly closing, now moving, now stopping — all present one piece of numinous qi displayed in visible form, truly like kites soaring and fish leaping: the mechanism of transformation alive. One who observes the art well will never bestow deep admiration merely on ears, eyes, hands, and feet dancing among appearances. Therefore the learner must first study the principle; when the principle is clear, the qi naturally grows lively and nimble. It is not that the qi can by itself become lively — it is the principle that makes it so.

Only after knowing this may one speak of it. Internal essence (nei jing) as merely "issuing within to manifest without" — such an account is still shallow. Someone says: this boxing cannot strike people. Not being able to strike people is only a matter of practice not yet arrived; if the practice is pure and ripe, from the coil whose greatness has nothing outside, reaching the realm whose smallness has nothing inside — meeting no enemy is one thing, but if one meets a strong enemy, the internal essence erupts suddenly, like sudden thunder and fierce wind snapping rotten timber — who could withstand it? Speaking of the prior joining in this very posture: the ignorant say it merely sets the posture for Dan Bian (Single Whip), not knowing it is not so. The preceding Lan Ca Yi has already repelled the aggressor; if someone then presses and seizes my elbow, my shoulder goes from below backward, turning a small coil upward, then inserts obliquely forward and down, and the "guest" is escorted out beyond the main gate. If at this moment he seizes the elbow, strike with the elbow; seizing the shoulder is the same. If he seizes my hand, the hand flips from front to rear, again turning a small coil, striking with the back of the hand. After striking, if another comes to invade my left half-body, then after the left hand has joined, I follow the momentum to defend leftward. Such is Dan Bian: both left and right hands contain methods of striking. The one who first joined strikes by joining; the one who later opens strikes by opening. Nowhere are the hands and feet not turning a coil; nowhere are they not a place for striking. For I move my qi by my principle; when the principle has no obstruction, the qi naturally has no blockage. Do I intend to strike anyone? I simply practice my boxing, acting as though nothing were happening. When the art reaches this point, the skill is more than half attained.

取象 (Taking the Image)

The preceding posture, Lan Ca Yi, used opening essence at completion; this posture, at its opening, first uses joining essence — the sense of turning opening into joining. Moreover, things at their extreme reverse: the principle of nature. Opening at its extreme must join; joining at its extreme must open — likewise principle following naturally. Hence at the opening the image is taken from Ge (革, Revolution). After joining, the wrist faces downward, gradually turning over the hand so the palm faces outward; the left hand rises from below the right breast, gradually traveling past the jaw, past the front of the nose, beyond the front of the left ear, gradually spreading toward the left hand. The hand leads the arm to extend seven or eight tenths; the form seems to stop, but the intent does not stop, gradually filling the internal essence, so that the essence must fill from within the bones to the skin and flesh, and onward...

Trang sách 229 · scan p262

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo — Volume 3*

*(No diagrams on this page.)*

Main text

...finger tips. When the internal essence is fully satisfied, the mechanism beneath the posture stirs of itself. After the right hand has joined, the wrist, facing downward and to the right, is likewise gradually gathered in. The fingers face downward and backward, rise, go forward, and return backward. Here is the hardest thing to describe. The arm bends forward; the right hand moves together with the left. Yet the arm's essence must turn a full coil before "stopping without stopping," in mutual response with the left hand. Joining the essence and advancing by degrees, the movement between is imaged by Jian (漸, Gradual Progress). As this posture nears completion, the essence prizes fullness. The Yi says: "Fit to be like the sun at mid-heaven; when the sun is at mid-heaven its light shines over all under heaven." So at the posture's end the image of Feng (豐, Abundance) is again taken — speaking of the sufficiency and fullness of the inner essence, imaged by the light of the sun at noon.

Sixth Dan Bian (Single Whip), seven-character folk verses

First verse

一开一合妙入微，上下四旁泄化机。纵使六子俱巧舌，也难描尽雪花飞。

One opening, one joining — subtle into the infinitesimal; above, below, and on four sides, the mechanism of transformation leaks forth. Even if the six trigram children were all clever of tongue, they could hardly describe the snowflakes' flight completely.

Second verse

一片灵机写太和，全凭方寸变来多。有心运到无心处，秋水澄清出太阿。

One piece of numinous mechanism portrays the Great Harmony, relying wholly on the square inch (the heart) for its many changes. When intentional movement reaches the place of no-intention, autumn water, clear and still, yields the Tai'e sword.

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo*

Posture 44: Second Yun Shou (雲手, Cloud Hands), Left and Right ①

Note ①: The original edition read "中運手" (Zhong Yun Shou); according to the table of contents' title it is corrected here to "Second Yun Shou, Left and Right."

Figure: A practitioner in the Yun Shou (Cloud Hands) posture, in long robe with standing collar, left foot forward, left hand extended forward at shoulder height, right hand near the lower abdomen; vertical lines from head and hands indicate alignment. Labels:

Closing text

Though this posture repeats an earlier one, there were meanings not yet fully set forth before, so it is specially supplemented (it is not another, separate posture). At the start, first move the right hand, then the left hand. There is no fixed count to the hand-circling; the left hand first leads upward. If the left hand does not lead, the right hand cannot rise; even if it rises, there is no posture, and it is not one continuous breath. With this one leading, the blood vessels of the whole body are all summoned into motion.

Previously the right hand moved to the top; now the left hand moves to the top — this is one full cycle of left and right. When one cycle is complete, still move with the right hand; when the right hand finishes, still move with the left. It must continue until the hands reach the position of Gao Tan Ma (高探馬, High Pat on Horse) ahead before stopping. When the right hand moves, the right hand is the master; when the right hand finishes, immediately move with the left hand. When the left hand moves, the left hand...

Trang sách 231 · scan p264

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo — Volume 3*

Figure: A woodblock-style line drawing of a practitioner, standing collar, weight low with the left leg bent forward and right leg extended back; the left arm extends forward and slightly up, the right arm down and back. No labels on the figure.

Main text

...is the master. When the left hand is the master, the spirit of the whole body pours into the left hand, and the eye's spirit is especially critical — it must fix on the left hand. Next, moving with the right hand, the eye's spirit at once follows the right hand's movement; do not look sideways. Sideways looking scatters the spirit, and the will too loses focus.

Movement is rooted in the one heart-mind, while spirit lodges in the eyes. The eyes are the official that transmits the heart; that the eyes do not glance sideways is proof the heart is not applied to two things at once.

引蒙 (Guiding the Uninitiated)

Q: With what does the hand-circling begin? A: It is led up by the tips of the left-hand fingers. Q: What moves first? A: The pads of the right hand's fingers and the little finger's palm. Q: Where is the pivot of the movement, where does the motion begin? A: Once the left hand has led the right hand into motion, the right hand, level with the right arm, goes from above down, turning smoothly (順轉, shun zhuan, forward reeling) to outside the right knee, rises past the heart's gate (chest), moves to the nose, crosses the right temple corner, passes above the right shoulder, and returns to the original starting position — only then is one coil complete. The right elbow sinks, the right shoulder presses down, the right foot also turns smoothly following the right hand. When the right hand reaches outside the knee, the right foot, following the right hand, is withdrawn to the left foot's side. Now, as the right hand rises and moves outward, the right foot likewise rises and moves rightward — but the right foot lands five or six cun nearer the inside than its original position: this is called "opening a step unintentionally." When the right hand reaches outside the right knee and is about to rise, the left hand descends from above — this is the smooth-turning method. As the right hand rises and moves rightward back to its position, the left hand descends to the knee, and the left foot is likewise withdrawn to the right foot's side. When the right hand descends to outside the knee, the left hand and right hand move together. The left hand also rises, past the chest and up to the nose tip, crosses the left temple corner, passes above the shoulder, and moves to the left hand's original starting position. The left foot, from outside the right foot, opens a step leftward — also the smooth-turning method. But only because the right foot moved five or six cun less to the right can the left foot open five or six cun more to the left. When the left hand reaches its position, left and right have each completed one cycle. After one left-right cycle, the mechanism does not stop: the right hand rises again from below, the left hand continues from above, ending and beginning again, moving in alternation, cycling without cease, like sun and moon alternating in brightness.

Q: What governs the movement? A: The master is in the heart. When the heart intends left and right to move in alternation, the left and right hands and feet immediately move in alternation; when the heart intends the silk-reeling essence (纏絲精, chan si jing) to turn coils smoothly, the left and right hands immediately turn smooth coils with the reeling essence; when the heart intends the elbow to sink and the shoulder to press, the elbow sinks and the shoulder presses; when the heart intends the chest and abdomen to join forward, the waist essence sinks and the crotch opens round. And as the chest joins in front, the waist...

*(Text breaks off at the page's bottom after 腰 "waist.")*

Trang sách 232 · scan p265

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chen Style Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation)

...below the *jing* (精, essence), the crotch (dang, 裆) immediately opens round and everything follows one's wish; if the xin (心, heart-mind) wishes to bend the two knees, the two knees bend at once. The right foot moves with the right hand, the left foot with the left hand; and the knees follow the respective feet — otherwise many defects arise. This is why the organs and skeleton (guanhai, 官骸) cannot but obey the heart-mind. Hence I say: the xin is the master (zhuzai, 主宰) governing the movement of the whole body.

Q: Where does the key (guanjian, 关键) of practicing the quan (拳, boxing form) lie? A: Below the Baihui point (百会穴), running from the Dazhui (大椎) at the back of the head down to the Changqiang (长强); its movement lies in the Ren and Du meridians (任督二脉). Where does its spirit (jingshen, 精神) reside? A: In the pupils of the eyes (mouzi, 眸子). The moment the xin stirs, the eyes convey it, and never err. Someone said: the general outline of the quan I have now heard; yet why can one not express its spirit (shenqing, 神情)? A: This lies in ordinarily removing the heart that desires haste — as Mencius said: "One must be attending to it, but not forcing it; let the heart not forget it, yet not help it grow" (必有事焉，而勿正，心勿忘，勿助长焉). Before practice, first rid yourself of any air of frivolity and panic; purify the heart and lessen desires, calm the heart and settle the qi (气). Move through each posture (zhezhe, 着着) in accordance with the rules; over long accumulation the gong (功) ripens. Then, layer upon layer of twists and turns, having passed through every difficulty, bitterness gives way to sweetness and interest springs up irresistibly. When the heart holds real scenes and feelings, the spirit will naturally be expressed in the form. In short, all this is within human effort. As for achieving transformation without deliberate intention (wuxin chenghua, 无心成化), that depends on cultivation (hanyang, 涵养): over long days remain at ease and await its self-arrival, and you will attain it. Mencius said, "I am good at nourishing my flood-like qi" (我善养吾浩然之气) — these words are truly no falsehood. The questioner bowed assent and withdrew, and I thereupon took up the brush to record this.

The middle transporting hand (zhong yun shou, 中运手) and the front and back transporting hands echo each other from afar, yet clearly divide into the three realms of upper, middle, and lower — and those three realms are nonetheless penetrated by one single principle (li, 理).

Neijing (内精/内勁, internal essence)

Diagram of the paths traveled by the left and right hands (左右手沿路所走之图)

Two circles; texts arranged around and within each circle:

- Right-hand diagram: "right hand rises and stops" (右手起止), "right hand descends" (右手下行), "left hand ascends" (左手上行), "method of leading in" (引进来行之), "ascending stops here" (上行至此即止), "descending just moves" (下行即行), "together with the right hand rises together and stops together" (与右手齐起齐止), "left hand / right hand", "rise together" (一齐起), "stop together" (一齐止).

- Left-hand diagram: "left hand starts from here, at the end stops here" (左手从此起，临终至此止); the rest as above.

- Between the circles: "spirit leads — one inhale, turn and return" (神领一吸翻至乃回).

Although left and right hands rise and move together, the left hand starts from the left side, hand level with the shoulder, beginning downward; while the right starts from beneath the left nipple, arm bent, beginning upward. Therefore when the left hand reaches the bottom the right hand is at the top; the left hand, coming beneath the right nipple, should bend, and the right arm should extend to the right side.

Trang sách 233 · scan p266

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), juan 3 (卷三)

In practicing the quan (拳), movement is entirely led by the hands, and turning (zhuanguan, 转关) depends entirely on relaxing the shoulders (song jian, 松肩). This figure specifically depicts the method of relaxing the shoulders in the hand movement of Single Whip (Dan Bian, 单鞭). With long practice the bone seams (gufeng, 骨缝) of the shoulders open by themselves and cannot be forced. If the left and right shoulders will not relax downward, the turning is not agile. Moreover, relaxing the shoulders is not hunching the shoulders. When the joints (gujie, 骨节) open, the shoulders relax down of themselves.

Supplementary Diagram: Song Jian in Dan Bian (补单鞭松肩图)

[Figure: line drawing of the Single Whip posture — left bow stance, left arm extended forward at shoulder height, right arm extended to the side; no labels inside the figure.]

Qu Xiang (取象, Taking the Image)

The human heart (xin, 心) belongs to fire. Fire has no constant form; it attaches to the movement of hands and feet, and only then does the brightness of the heart-fire appear — like the Li hexagram (离卦) of the Yijing. Li means "to cling/be bright" (丽) and "brightness" (明). The two hands turn left and right like sun and moon clinging to heaven, alternating in brightness, moved by the qi (气, i.e. shenqi, 神气, spirit-energy). The two feet move below like the grains and plants clinging to the earth, alternating in succession, form clinging to form; doubled brightness clinging to the correct (重明以丽乎正). Upper and lower, hands and feet move by the middle way (zhongdao, 中道), turning without cease. When selfish desires in the human heart are utterly stilled and the barrier of principle (lizhang, 理障) is emptied, the substance of the heart is constantly bright. Bright, it shines on everything: if something comes from the left it shines on the left; from the right, on the right. When a person cannot deceive this brightness, it is numinous (ling, 灵), and numinous enough to respond to the ten thousand affairs. So if an enemy comes on the left, strike the left; if on the right, strike the right — prepared, there is no peril (有备无患). The image resembles Li; therefore it is taken from Li.

Middle Transporting Hand — Five-Character Folk Verse (中运手五言俚语)

The two hands move through the middle (of the three transporting hands — upper, middle, lower — this one is the center), left and right like a circling ring. Borrowing this tangible thing, I paint the sky within the water (utterly empty, utterly numinous: every single movement is the round image of Taiji, 太极圆象).

Seven-Character Folk Verse (七言俚语)

One forth, one back, completing one round; the qi mechanism (qiji, 气机) above and below never pauses. From antiquity Taiji (referring to the principle of yin-yang, the qi of hard and soft) has been thus — what need to vainly seek outside the body? (The Zhongyong says the Way is not far from man; Mencius says all things are complete within me. One need only seek within oneself — 返求诸己.)

(Page number: 233)

Trang sách 234 · scan p267

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Posture 45: Bai Jiao (摆脚, Swinging Foot)

Figure labels

Right side (top to bottom): "left elbow bends and holds" (左肘屈住); "eyes watch the right foot" (眼看右脚); "jing at the crown leads and holds" (顶精领住, i.e. dingjin 顶劲); "ears listen behind" (耳听身后); "shoulders relax down" (肩松下); "right arm extends open" (右肱伸开).

Left side: "palms face down" (掌朝下); "waist sinks down" (腰下去); "left leg bends but must not go soft" (左腿弯不可软).

Bottom: "right foot level with the leg, knee bent seven or eight parts; left knee bent two or three parts" (右脚与腿平，膝屈七八分，左膝屈二三分); "the left foot stands solid on the ground — the whole body depends on this foot, exert full strength" (左足实地踏地，周身全凭此脚，当十分用力); "hands slightly upward, circling from left toward the right, placing the swinging foot into this posture; what follows is the front view of Bai Jiao" (手微向上，从左绕而向右……).

[Figure: line drawing of Bai Jiao — standing on the left leg bearing the full weight, right leg raised and extended sideways with knee slightly bent; left arm reaching forward-downward palm down, right arm folded across the chest.]

Main text

What is Bai Jiao? The right foot lifts, level with the right hip, sweeping horizontally toward the north (the right side), using the foot to strike the opponent (摶, tuan, means "to strike", 击). It requires the right arm to extend open to the right, the left arm bent and held, hands toward the right, both palms facing down; the left and right hands sweep horizontally toward the south to meet the right foot. The right foot sweeps horizontally toward the north, meeting the two hands at the center, like two men facing each other in combat. The hands and the right foot brush past each other (modang, 摩荡, grind and surge). Hands and foot face off; when they have passed, the right hand is crossed by. The left hand levels off toward the left, the left arm extends open; the right hand also levels toward the left arm and bends and holds. The right foot levels with the arm; the right knee bends slightly — stopping yet not stopping, on the point of a downward-falling impulse (ji, 机), though in fact not yet fallen. The boundary of Bai Jiao ends here.

Yinmeng (引蒙, Leading the Beginner)

The completion of the preceding posture is already clear from the figure-by-figure explanation, and need not be repeated. But between the hand movement ending below and the swinging foot above — at this seam, what do the hands and feet do? It is said: as the hand movement is about to finish, the left foot shifts slightly toward the right by two or three parts, to clear the path for the narrow base of the following posture. The upper body's left hand leads and holds...

(Page number: 234)

Trang sách 235 · scan p268

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), juan 3 (卷三)

The right hand first goes left, rising from below and turning a small circle, then bends the arm to the right; the left hand drops to rest in front of the right shoulder. The right hand follows the left hand, likewise rising from below, turning a large circle, extending the arm open and stopping at the right. The two hands going left are leading (yin, 引) the opponent; turning to the right, the right hand strikes the opponent; turning again to the left, the left hand strikes. The right foot is originally at the right yet moves left: without first going left, a strike to the right has no power. Therefore one must first go left and only then strike right. With the two hands striking horizontally left and right, the right foot also lifts above, swinging right to strike — then of the four limbs only the single left leg remains below. Yet this one foot is what the Yijing (《易》) calls "the eldest son carries the vessel" (长子主器). It must be made as stable as a rock (盘石之安), as firm as moats of metal and walls of boiling water (金汤之固); only then, though attacked from all around (环而攻之), can it not be shaken — otherwise defeat! Q: How to make it stable, how to firm the thigh bone (bigu, 髀骨)? Sit down slightly, one or two parts; bend the left knee one or two parts; the upper body's crown-jing (dingjing, 顶精) well led; in the middle close and hold the chest; the left foot grips the ground, the sole's center clawing (kou, 抠) into the earth; the upper body propped and held front, back, left, and right, letting no side be over-weighted — then the sole of the foot is naturally steady, stable and firm.

Neijing (内精, internal essence)

Bai Jiao — Starting Position of the Left and Right Hands (摆脚左右手起端式)

[Figure 1: line drawing of the starting position; the curved movement paths carry the following annotations:]

- The right arm rises from below turning a small circle and bends to the right (右臂由下而上转一小圈向右屈住)

- The left hand drops to stop in front of the right shoulder (左手落在右膀前停住)

- The right hand follows the left hand, rising from below in a large circle, extending open and stopping at the right (右手随住左手自下而上转一大圈展开向右停住)

- The two hands going left lead the opponent (两手向左者引敌人也)

- Turning to the right, the right hand strikes the opponent (转而向右者以右手击敌人也)

- Turning again to the left, the left hand strikes (复转而向左者以左手击人也)

- The right foot is originally at the right yet moves left: not going left, a rightward strike is powerless (右足本在右而向左者……)

- Therefore first go left, then strike right (故必先向左而后击右)

- Both hands strike horizontally left and right; the right foot also lifts above, swinging right to strike (两手左右横击右脚亦抬起在上向右摆而击人)

Bai Jiao — Completed Position of the Left and Right Hands (摆脚左右手已成图)

[Figure 2: line drawing of the completed position; annotations:]

- The right foot swings from right to left — this foot movement is named Bai Jiao (右足从右向左摆右脚名摆脚)

- The left hand strikes from left to right — named the left strike (左手从左向右击左手名左击)

- The right hand strikes from right to left — named the right strike (右手从右向左击右手名右击)

This posture is a variant pattern (biange, 变格) within the quan (拳). Foot techniques with the foot below — kicking forward (前踢), pushing back (后蹬), stamping down (下蹴) — are the standard pattern (zhengge, 正格). Here the right leg lifts and the foot sweeps horizontally across to strike, hence it is called a variant. With the hands, forward strikes and back strikes, or a single hand splitting left and leading right, occur often. Here the two hands strike horizontally left and right in answer to the right foot — also within the quan a left... [text continues on next page]

(Page number: 235)

Trang sách 236 · scan p269

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說)

...a variant pattern (biange, 变格) of the right hand [aspect of Bai Jiao]. Practice it with the flood-like qi (haoran zhi qi, 浩然之气) and nowhere will it be unfitting. In the lower body the left leg stands alone like a pillar in midstream (zhongliu dizhu, 中流砥柱).

Qu Xiang (取象, Taking the Image)

Bai Jiao resembles the hexagram Gen (艮, Mountain). Gen is the hand (艮为手): with the left hand and the right hand one stops things on either side. Gen means "stopping" (止). The first line: "Gen in its toes" (艮其趾) — not yet losing correctness. The third line: "Gen in its body, stopping in one's own person" (艮其身，止诸躬). When the foot is stable the body cannot be shaken. The top line: "Sincere Gen, good fortune" (敦艮吉). The Xiang says: "The good fortune of sincere Gen lies in ending with substance" (敦艮之吉，以厚终也) — meaning the strength of the sole of the foot is great and the upper body hard to break down; therefore the image is taken from Gen. It also resembles the hexagram Lü (旅, the Traveler). Heaven and earth are the inn of the ten thousand things; time is the passing traveler of a hundred generations. The left and right hands go from left to right and again from right to left, like a traveler coming and going on the road — one passing, that is all. The right leg's horizontal sweep is likewise one passing. The left foot stands without moving: when it should stop it stops, when it should move it moves (referring to the right leg). All are passing travelers coming and going, leaving no lingering trace; therefore the image is also taken from Lü.

Bai Jiao — Seven-Character Folk Verses (摆脚七言俚语)

One: A single beam can prop up a great mansion's collapse (the single beam figures the left leg, the mansion figures the whole body); raising the right leg is a sword held level. Left and right hands strike left and right; first set yourself in the death-ground, then seek life (先置死地后求生).

Two: Of all postures Bai Jiao is hardest; standing alone, trembling (kui kui, 夔夔), like a chill in the gall. Who would know Taiji has wondrous use — the hands like a balance scale, the ten thousand affairs at peace.

Mixed-Length Folk Verse (长短句俚语)

One thread of heart's blood moves my flood-like qi (浩然之气). Front and back in proportion, all follows one's wish. Let him attack from the four sides — how can he withstand my hands and feet striking horizontally. Left, right, front, back — every affair is carried through.

(Page number: 236)

Trang sách 237 · scan p270

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), juan 3 (卷三)

Posture 46: Yi Tang She (一堂蛇), also named Die Cha (跌岔, Falling Split)

Figure labels

Upper section (right to left):

- The left hand passes beneath the right leg, extending open together with the left leg, gradually advancing forward (左手从右腿下去，与左腿一齐展开，以渐前进)

- Eyes watch the left hand and left foot (眼看左手左足)

- The crown-jing (dingjing, 顶精) must not be lost (顶精不可失)

- The right ear listens to the right side (右耳听住右面)

- The right arm extends open (右胳膊展开)

- The right hand seems to have an intention of going forward (右手似有欲前之意)

Lower section (right to left):

- The left leg extends open, resting level on the ground (左腿展开，平落地面)

- The left foot stamps hard against the opponent's shin bone (左足用力蹬敌之廉骨)

- The left knee must not bend (左膝不可屈)

- The body must be led and held, the qi gathered forward (身要领住，气往前合住)

- The right knee bends and holds, must not press heavily down (右膝屈住，不可踏实)

- The thigh bone (bigu, 髀骨) sits yet does not sit — solid yet empty (髀骨似坐非坐，实而虚)

- The right instep faces down, the shoe sole faces up (右足面朝下，鞋底朝上)

[Figure: line drawing of the single falling split — left leg extended left, level on the ground; right knee bent down to the right, right instep down; left arm reaching left-forward, right arm extended right, ear listening right.]

Main text

What is Die Cha? The body falls from the air with the two legs spread apart — that is Die Cha. In this figure the left leg extends open and the right leg bends and holds: this is the single falling split (dan Die Cha, 单跌岔). With the double falling split (shuang Die Cha), one cannot rise without the leaping method (zongfa, 纵法). Not so the single falling split: with only the left heel closing forward once, the right knee opening outward once, and the right heel flipping over with force, one rises as soon as one falls. Compared with the other it is somewhat easier, so it is used, and it can also secure victory. Moreover today's quan masters all do it this way; for now I follow them.

Yinmeng (引蒙, Leading the Beginner)

Die Cha answers back to Er Qi (二起, the double kick). Er Qi goes from below upward, flying into mid-air; this one comes down from mid-air, both legs reaching the ground. A natural correspondence, not a forced one — such is the strictness of the laws by which the ancients created the quan. When Bai Jiao finishes, bend the right arm and extend the left arm, both hands at the left. The two hands then from the left side, rising from below, turn toward the right. The right arm extends open, the left arm bends and holds, both hands extended. At this moment the right foot falls down to the ground; the left foot, following from the right foot, with its heel resting on the ground gradually kicks away toward the southwest. The intent bends upward like a new-moon crescent; the left hand moves just like the left leg. It is from the right waist slowly bending down, and with... [continues on next page]

(Page number: 237)

Trang sách 238 · scan p271

Chen Taijiquan Tushuo (Illustrated Explanations of Chen Style Taijiquan)

The left foot moves along together, pushing out toward the southwest. First use finger strength, then palm strength. The right hand is behind; although the arm is extended, the hand is still held inward (han). From top to bottom there is the intent of pressing forward — only the posture has not yet manifested (the Diěchà / 跌岔 falling-split section ends here).

Nei jing (内精 — internal essence)

Right leg diagram (右腿图 you tui tu)

In this posture the left foot kicking forward is primary. The kick is not an empty kick — it kicks the opponent. Therefore the heel at the back of the foot cannot fail to use strength, and the left hand pushing forward assists the left foot. The right hand being at the right likewise has great significance: it assists the intent of the left foot.

Qu xiang (取象 — taking the image)

In the falling-split posture (diecha), although one prevails by kicking with the left foot, the hip bone sits on the ground — it is no different from practicing Kan and entering the abyss of Kan, danger of the greatest degree. Without fu (孚, sincerity/faith), "only the heart penetrates" (wei xin heng), one cannot advance with attainment. If one can, with the principle of taiji, be honest and true within, not reckoning gain and loss, fortune and misfortune, and can, with the vast flowing qi (haoran zhi qi), act according to what the heart rests content in, then in due course Kan will not overfill but merely reach the islet (zhi 祗 is to be read as chi 坻 — chi is a small islet in the water; the Odes say: "as if standing on an islet in the water" — that is chi). Having entered danger and become level, one who entered danger can emerge from danger, hence "no blame." The posture of diecha in its movement resembles practicing Kan, therefore the image is taken from Kan. A person standing with both feet on the ground, with left and right hands stirring above, can still defend against the enemy with ease. As for diecha, the body enters layered danger — difficulty of the utmost. The hexagram Gen above and Kan below is Jian (蹇, Obstruction). The obstruction lies in the northeast (the Gen direction). In King Wen's circular diagram they are all in the northeast; the southwest has no obstruction, hence "favorable to the southwest." This is why the left foot of diecha kicks toward the southwest: because the southwest is the auspicious direction, kicking there has merit. Line Six-Two: "The king's servants obstructed and obstructed again" (jianjian). The king is the fifth line, the head; the second line is the servant, the thighs and arms (gugong). The outer trigram Kan is the head's obstruction; the middle-line Kan is the thighs-and-arms obstruction. The Yi takes the second and fifth as within the two Kans, saying "jianjian." Man's head and thighs-and-arms both sit in the lowest places — so it is likewise. The Yi speaks of "the cause of not sparing oneself"... (text continues on the next page).

Diagrams

Right leg diagram: a leg and foot drawn in a seated/low posture, labeled: 跌岔顶精 (essence at the crown of diecha), 提好，心精 (lifted well, essence of the heart), 提足，胸合 (lifted foot, chest closed), 髀骨 (hip bone), 无实坐下 (sitting down without solidity), 足底朝上 (sole facing up).

Curved line (middle of page): the trajectory of the left leg reaching forward to kick. Above: "this is the idea of the left leg advancing forward to kick"; below: "this is the form of the left leg extending to kick"; along the curve: "the danger of Kan is hard to travel — as at a deep pool, treading on thin ice" (approximate).

Foot diagram (bottom right): "the big toe curls, all the toes grip the ground; the knee gathers toward the heel."

*(Printed page number: 238. Some in-diagram annotations are partly illegible in the print.)*

Trang sách 239 · scan p272

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Juan 3 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanations, Volume 3)

...the image of seizing his body (this speaks of the calamity of destruction — none is greater than this). There is also the idea of what is not brought on by oneself. When the enemy's aggression is not exhausted, that is why one is brought into it; diecha (跌岔, falling-split) is the same. There is also the idea of not possessing one's own body. The head is placed low, the left foot dare not spare itself, kicking urgently forward — hence "jianjian" (蹇蹇). Therefore line Nine-Three: "going is obstruction; returning brings reversal" — this speaks of a person turning inward to examine himself, having the means to dissolve the obstruction. Though obstructed, he may still go. Line Six-Four: "going is obstruction; returning brings union" (lian). "Returning brings union" means the left foot joins with the right leg and the left and right hands, using the strength of the whole body to rush to the difficulty — those in multitude are strong. King Wu said: "I have three thousand ministers, all with one heart." Though there is obstruction, treading on danger is as if on level ground — the obstruction can be gone through. Line Nine-Five: "great obstruction; friends come." Though the head sits low, so long as the spirit of the whole body is intact, all can assist. Line Top-Six: "going is obstruction; returning brings greatness (shuo)." "Going is obstruction" speaks of my having the real capacity to bear hardship and great tasks. When it arrives suddenly, with reason upright and qi strong, no obstruction can block it. In the great battle of Kunyang, Xiu (Liu Xiu) finally won — this is the proof of "coming brings greatness." In sum: with this one kick one is not reduced to being trapped; when the gongfu is thoroughly ripe, its effect can be reaped — therefore it is also taken from the hexagram Jian (蹇). Further, Li below and Kun above — brightness entering the midst of the earth — is called Ming Yi (明夷, Darkening of the Light); it too is encountering hardship of the utmost degree. Line Initial-Nine: "the darkened light in flight, drooping its wings" — this speaks of diecha: left and right below, like a bird's wings drooping. Line Six-Two: "the darkened light wounds (yi means to level) the left thigh" — this speaks of the left foot kicking forward: the leg should extend level and relax. "It saves with a strong horse: auspicious" — this speaks of: if the foot is not strong as a horse, it cannot rescue from difficulty. Line Nine-Three: "the darkened light in the southern hunt." The inner trigram Li is due south; the outer trigram Kun is the southwest. "Southern hunt" means kicking the enemy toward the southwest and capturing his great head — that is, defeating the enemy. Line Six-Four: "entering into the left belly" — the left foot strikes the enemy's left belly. "Capturing the heart of Ming Yi" — this speaks of the enemy's pain. "Going out of the gate and courtyard" — one can now emerge from the difficulty. Though the place and time are hard, "know yourself, know the other, and in a hundred battles you will win a hundred" — this is because within there is cultured brightness (nei wen ming); therefore it is taken from Ming Yi. The hexagrams Kan, Jian, and Ming Yi — all three are borrowed forms for situations of the greatest difficulty, and without abundant virtue one is not equal to them. This matter has no great or small — its principle is one. Diecha in the boxing is likewise a situation of the greatest difficulty: without great gongfu one cannot prevail over a man with a single foot. It is not a fondness for difficulty; it is also being pressed by circumstance from which one cannot escape.

Diecha five-character vernacular verse (跌岔五言俚语)

The right foot swings down from above, the left foot brushes the ground below. One kick of the foot to the southwest — this again is attacking the unprepared.

Using a bow with the back facing down, the essence like a crescent new moon's upward curve. The left hand and left foot go from top to bottom, and at the bottom kick forward. Then again from bottom to top. Going from top to bottom is the beginning affair of diecha; going from bottom to top is the ending affair of diecha. Without this there is no way to rouse the rising momentum of the low posture (xiashi).

Seven-character vernacular verse (七言俚语)

One:

"Frighten above, take below — you must remember this; the left foot brushes the ground, and the kick brings its own advantage. The right thigh bends and the knee touches the ground; within the coiled roots lies hidden the intent of sinking."

*(Printed page number: 239. No diagrams on this page.)*

Trang sách 240 · scan p273

Verse, second stanza

右脚一摆已难猜，又为两翼落尘埃。

不是肩肘能破敌，一足蹬倒凤凰台。

"The right foot sweeps once — already hard to fathom; again the two wings fall to the dust. It is not the shoulder or elbow that can break the enemy — one foot kicks over the Phoenix Terrace."

Verse, third stanza

阴阳变化真无穷，只说英雄遇匪躬。

谁料妙机难预定，解围即在一蹬中。

"The transformations of yin and yang are truly inexhaustible; one says the hero meets hardship. Who could foresee that the subtle opportunity is hard to predetermine — the breaking of the siege lies within a single kick."

Verse, fourth stanza

果能太极（言太极拳）仔细研，绝处逢生自不难。

天下凡事皆如此，非徒拳艺令人观。

"If one can truly study taiji (meaning taijiquan) carefully, then finding life in a deadly situation is not difficult. All things in the world are like this; it is not merely the boxing art that is for show."

Verse, fifth stanza

一缕浩然然下行，坐中能令四座惊。

此身若非成铁汉，掷地何来金石声。

"A thread of vast moral force (haoran zhi qi 浩然之气) flows downward; within the stance it can startle all present. If this body were not an iron man, how could it strike the ground with the sound of metal and stone?"

Posture 47: Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg (Jinji Duli 金鸡独立)

What is called Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg? One leg stands alone, like a rooster standing on one leg with the other leg raised — it is an image (xiangxing 象形). This posture answers to the earlier Right Foot Sweeping (you ca jiao 右擦脚).

Jie jie (sectional explanation)

Jie jie (jie jie 节解 means: the joints of the whole body, unraveled joint by joint).

Yin meng (guidance for beginners)

From the fallen split (die cha 跌岔), the xin jing (heart essence) is lifted upward; the big toe and heel of the left foot press together with force forward; the right knee is lifted up; the toes and heel of the right foot close forward; both legs are firm; both hands gather forward; the jing of the crown and the jing of the crotch lead upward together; hands and feet exert force all at once, and one naturally rises. Just as one is about to rise, the body springs forward, the right heel kicks back. Once the body has left the ground, the left hand slowly bends and travels up to...

Figure label

- Figure: the Jinji Duli posture (standing on one leg, the other raised).

- Right column label: 右膝猛 (the right knee thrusts fiercely).

Trang sách 241 · scan p274

...ear. The jing (essence) is in the hand; it travels from the left shoulder and arm down to the heel of the left foot. The left hand descends in front of the shoulder, down to the left ribs, level with the breast. Then the right hand and right knee rise together; the right palm rises from below, past the outside of the right ribs, level with the left hand. Without stopping it goes straight up, the arm fully extended, the palm facing the sky; the right knee rises level with the lower abdomen, while the left hand has already hung down below. The right palm thrusts up against the enemy's bone below the Chengjiang point (承浆, the hollow below the lower lip); the right knee rises to strike the enemy's testicles (shenzi 肾子). Both are painful spots of the human body — they must not be used lightly. The left foot stands on the ground like a mountain; the right hand holds up the sky, as if it would break the heavens.

Nei jing (internal essence) — figure labels

- Right palm diagram (you zhang tu 右掌图)

- Left hand diagram (zuo shou tu 左手图)

- Large figure, label along edge: "and passes over shoulder, over ribs, over belly, then rises up again to here"

- Right foot diagram: "the right foot falls from the air to here"

- Left leg diagram: "the left leg stands straight and should be rigid; the bend of the leg is slightly bent, must not be soft"

- Right knee diagram: "the knee pushes upward"

- "The sole steps flat"

- Left hand diagram: "rises up to above the left ear"

Qu xiang (taking the image)

In terms of yin and yang: the right hand is yang, the image of the sun; the left hand is yin, the image of the moon. Kun (坤) is the belly. The right hand not only passes the belly but rises above the head, with the palm pushing up under the enemy's jaw — this is "advancing while attacking" (jin ru cui ru 晋如摧如, hexagram Jin 晋). The right hand above the head is "advancing to its horns" (jin qi jiao 晋其角). Li (离) is fire; fire's qi flames upward. The right hand and right knee both rise, like fire's qi ascending, not stopping until the utmost. Li above and Kun below — "brightness emerges from the earth" — is called Jin. Jin means to advance. The right hand rises past the crown with the sense of ascending, hence the image is taken from Jin. Below the mountain there is fire: Bi (贲). Gen (艮) is the hand and is the mountain. The hand raised is like a mountain peak towering. The second line: "adorns the beard." If the right hand can protect the beard, the head is safe. The first line: "adorns the toes" — the left foot must naturally exert force. Li dwells in the lower trigram, its brightness shines on everything, hence the image is taken from Bi. In Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg one has already escaped danger and subdued the foe — it carries the sense of "when Pi (否, standstill) reaches its extreme, Tai (泰, peace) comes; in seven days comes the return." The Yi says: "Thunder is in the midst of the earth: Fu (复, Return)." Kun is earth...

Trang sách 242 · scan p275

(Qu xiang, continued) ...is the belly. Zhen (震) is thunder, is the foot — it is movement. The right foot moves like thunder, the knee rising to the belly swift as thunder — hence "in the center one alone returns" (zhong xing du fu 中行独复, hexagram Fu 复). "In the center" means the zhong qi (central qi 中气) moves through the middle; "alone returns" means alone able to come out of danger and return to the ground of peace. In the earlier foot sweep, the left leg stood alone until the fallen split (die cha 跌岔), where the right leg could not stand①. But at Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg, the left leg still stands alone. Earlier the right foot swept the man and in the split the leg coiled bent on the ground. At Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg, the right knee again knees the man, again able to strike his painful spot and make him cry out. This is precisely "from Pi (否) comes Tai (泰); in seven days it naturally returns" (qi ri lai fu 七日来复). As the kernel of the great fruit has not ceased, so the heavenly mechanism of the fist has not ceased — there is in the end a principle of return; hence the image is again taken from Fu (复, Return).

Seven-character folk verses (qi yan li yu 七言俚语)

First

耸身上手擎天，左手下垂似碧莲。金鸡宛然同独立，不防右膝暗中欺。

"The body rises, the hand holds up the sky; the left hand hangs down like a jade lotus. The golden rooster stands just like on one leg — beware the right knee deceiving from the dark."

Second

一条金蛇拖玉堂，忽然飞起似鹰扬。只说右手冲上去，谁知膝（上膝盖之膝，下膝字是上顶之意）也难防。

"A golden snake drags along the jade hall, suddenly flying up like a soaring hawk. One only says the right hand charges upward — who would know the knee (the character 'knee' above means kneecap; the 'knee' below means pushing up) is also hard to guard against."

Jinji Duli (Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg)

This is the one-sided moving body method (pian yun shen fa 偏运身法). The right side of the body governs motion, the left governs stillness. In Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg, the standing is in the left jing (精); the movement is in the right, hence the right is taken as the primary. This posture rises from the fallen split. The right hand's jing travels up the right thigh, past the right ribs to the armpit, past the bend of the elbow to the right palm, turns from the five fingers to the back of the hand, passes the elbow to shoulder and arm, and descends to the right heel. The right leg's jing rises from the right toes to the knee-wrist (kneecap, xi gu 膝骨); the zhong qi rises from the dantian (丹田) past the crown of the head, turns to the back of the head, and descends to above the Changqiang point (长强). The zhong qi of hand and foot moves all at once together — it must not be done one after another. The left hand's jing travels from the left thigh up to the left ear, down past shoulder and arm, from the left hip to the Weizhong point (委中), ending at the left heel; the left foot stands without moving②. Front, back, left, right — the jing used is even and balanced, and one naturally stands stable. As for the later "facing-left heavenly kick" (chao zuo tian deng 朝左天镫), the left hand and knee move as the right did.

[Editorial notes]

① The original edition read "the right leg stands alone until the fallen split, then the left leg cannot stand"; according to the form's execution and principles, "right" was corrected to "left" and "left" to "right."

② The original edition read "the right foot stands without moving"; according to the form, "right" was corrected to "left."

Trang sách 243 · scan p276

Posture 48: Chaotian Deng (朝天镫 — Lamp Facing Heaven)

What is Chaotian Deng? The left palm faces upward, like today's "heaven-facing lamp" — it is an image (xiangxing 象形). This posture answers the earlier Right Brush-Foot (you ca jiao 右擦脚).

Jie jie (sectional explanation) — figure labels

- The left kneecap pushes upward (zuo xi gai wang shang ding 左膝盖往上顶)

- The chest must be held in reserve (xiong yao hanxu 胸要含蓄)

- The left palm faces up (zuo shou zhang chao shang 左手掌朝上)

- The crown jing is suspended upward (ding jing ling qi 顶精领起)

- The ear listens behind (er ting shen hou 耳听身后)

- The right shoulder sinks down (you jian song xia 右肩松下)

- The right hand hangs down (you shou chui xia 右手垂下)

- The right knee is bent only one or two tenths (you xi wei qu yi er fen 右膝微屈一二分)

- The right foot presses flat with force (you zu yong li ping ta 右足用力平踏)

- The whole body relies entirely on this one right leg to carry it — therefore it must not be soft

Figure: a man standing on the right leg alone, head tilted back gazing upward, the left knee raised high, the left arm arched over the head with palm facing the sky like a lamp held to heaven, the right arm hanging naturally.

In the human body the waist is the middle boundary: the qi of the left hand and left knee travels upward; the qi of the right hand and right thigh travels downward①. The waist is the dividing line in the middle.

Yin meng (guidance for beginners)

When the palm has finished pushing upward, the jing (精) is conducted from the fingers to the back of the hand, descends past the shoulder and arm, straight... (the sentence continues on the next page)

[Editorial note] ① According to the posture diagram, the original edition here had text and figure disagreeing, with "left" and "right" both reversed; this has been corrected.

Trang sách 244 · scan p277

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (Illustrated Explanation of Chen-style Taijiquan)*

...down to the right heel, passing the Yongquan (湧泉, KI-1) point, reaching Dadun (大敦, LR-1) and Yinbai (隐白, SP-1); only then has the qi (氣, qì) completed one full circuit. This is precisely how it should be circulated — it is the intention of the movement being run in the mind. As for the trace of the right hand: the thumb points downward from the right shoulder at the Jianyu (肩髃) point, passes the right ribs, and reaches the right thigh, the fingertips hanging down like an awl. The right foot moves downward; before it touches the ground, it shifts toward the northwest. The toes of the right foot point northwest, the heel lands first, a bit more than a chi (尺) away from the left foot. The left hand slowly bends; the momentum rises along the front of the left thigh, passes the left abdomen, the left ribs, the front of the left shoulder, the side of the left ear, rises over the left temple, the arm extends and thrusts straight upward, the palm facing the sky. The left knee bends and holds as it rises; the knee stops in front of the lower left abdomen.

Question: the left-side circulation is the same method — why not run both together, and instead run them separately, alternating? Answer: it cannot be done. The two hands can be raised together, but if the two feet rise together, that is the "leaping" (纵) method. If this leaping method were applied to this posture, the heart-qi (心氣) would lead upward, and the qi of the whole body would gather in the chest; although the foot of the lower body rises, the palm of the upper body would be without strength. Not entirely without strength — but the strength could not gather in the palm, and likewise the strength of the lower body could not all gather in the two knees. Once the heart-qi is lifted, the qi all gathers in the chest and has no time to distribute to the four limbs; it disperses and leaves each part to proceed step by step, slowly performing its own rotation. This is why the left and right are divided into two stages. When the left foot is grounded, the vital energy of the right half of the body can be carefully circulated one full round; when the right foot is grounded, the vital energy of the left half of the body can be carefully circulated one full round — without running into danger or unforeseen mishap. Moreover, this method of circulation also avoids the defect of neglecting one side. This is why the alternating method (迭運法) and sequential method (遞運法) are used, and not the method of running both together. That is the reason. So I say: it cannot be done. The student should examine this in detail.

Neijing (內精, Inner Essence)

What previously faced southwest now turns to face northwest. The preceding posture, Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg (金雞獨立), took the right hand and right knee as primary; this one is its reverse.

Diagram notes:

- Diagram of the left hand's circulation (左手運行圖): head, shoulder, ribs; straight up, straight down; left passing, right moving; palm facing the sky, with the left hand as primary and the palm exerting force.

- Diagram of the right hand's circulation (右手運行圖): head, ribs; down the thigh; the right hand descends from above, passes the head, passes in front of the left ribs, and reaches the thigh, the hand hanging down.

- Use the strength of the knee; here the left knee is primary.

- The left leg stands straight as in the previous left-leg method.

( Printed page: 244 )

Trang sách 245 · scan p278

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Volume 3* (陳氏太極拳圖說 卷三)

Quxiang (取象, Taking the Image)

The preceding posture rises out of the Falling Split (跌岔, Diecha), lifting up while striking, and would seem harder than this one. Yet in the human body the right hand and right foot are used most, while the left hand and left foot are used less, for the left limbs are not as handy as the right. Viewed this way, the Chaotian Deng (朝天镫, "Lamp Facing Heaven" / kick toward heaven) is harder still than the Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg (金雞獨立), because the left hand and left knee cannot find their proper position. Yet the Golden Rooster first wins victory with the right hand and right knee; the Chaotian Deng then follows, winning with the left hand and left knee — the fullest flowering. When the quan (拳) reaches the Falling Split, obstruction (否, pǐ) has reached its extreme. When obstruction is at its extreme, peace (泰, tài) must come. The Golden Rooster and the Chaotian Deng, winning victory alternately left and right, may be called the extreme of peace — hence the image is taken from the Tai hexagram. The principle of heaven and earth is cyclical: nothing goes without returning. When obstruction peaks there must be peace; when peace peaks there must be obstruction — even heaven and earth cannot escape this reckoning, how much less the small art of boxing. Even so, it depends on one who handles it well. In obstruction, if one can maintain a single, steadfast heart, even obstruction will surely pass; meeting peace, if one can keep a heart that preserves fullness and guards peace, then peace will not suddenly turn to obstruction. Students of the quan should understand this.

Chaotian Deng — Seven-Character Vernacular Verses (朝天镫七言俚語)

I.

Again the palm faces heaven on high, the knee between follows the method as before; still one bend divides left and right, and once more the hero spares his whip (the crotch cannot straddle a horse — what use a whip?).

II.

The right foot comes down, the left foot hangs; the left palm stretches upward, the lamp facing heaven. If a man of talent grasps the meaning within, he laughs in turn at how one lineage runs through the Golden Rooster.

III.

The right knee at the crotch — people are not convinced; who expected the left thigh to come forth again. Do not use it until real necessity; this move can make one weep (on it hangs the fate of life and death — be careful).

IV.

The teeth and the jaw below have nothing to do with it; what is feared most is the palm held up level (borrowed word). The reckless man does not know the hardship within — it can cost him three meals a day.

( Printed page: 245 )

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo* (陈氏太极拳图说)

The 49th Posture: Dao Juan Hong (倒卷紅, Reeling Silk in Reverse / Reverse Reeling Red)

This is a retreating method, the same as the "Pearl① Reverses the Curtain" (珍珠倒卷帘); hence the name. This posture echoes the earlier Reverse Reeling Red, and also faces the Parting the Wild Horse's Mane (野馬分鬃): that one advances forward, this one retreats. When you have finished watching the left hand, turn the face to watch the right hand; the right hand's circulation is likewise watched from front to rear.

Diagram notes (a retreating stance: the left hand raised high, the right hand near the waist as if holding a pole, the head turned right watching the right hand, the left leg stepping back):

- The crown essence lifts upward (頂精上提).

- The eyes watch the hand; the left hand goes from front to rear, the eyes likewise from front to rear.

- The waist essence sinks down (腰精下去).

- The right knee exerts strength, slightly bent.

- The right foot exerts strength, planted flat.

- The belly must be full; the hand as if sweeping in.

- The perineum (會陰) must be empty, the ...

- The toes of the left foot touch the ground first.

- The left leg, if it can extend, extends fully.

Yinmeng (引蒙, Guiding the Uninitiated)

This posture is a fully spread-out body method, the hardest of all retreating movements to perform. By what channel the left hand of the Chaotian Deng went up, by that same channel it comes down. The fingers slightly hook, sweeping backward as if gathering in (in the level tone). By what channel the left foot went up, by that same channel it comes down. Before the foot touches the ground, retreat backward and open a large step. Once the step is complete, if an enemy on the right comes wielding a weapon, the right hand sweeps to the right to parry the weapon (a weapon such as a spear or staff), sweeping it back as it goes. The right foot likewise spreads out, the leg retreating, opening one large step. When the right foot's retreat is complete, it again joins with the left hand in the reverse... (text continues on the next page).

① The original edition reads 真珠; per the table of contents this has been corrected to 珍珠.

Trang sách 247 · scan p280

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Volume 3* (陳氏太極拳圖說 卷三)

...reeling (倒卷, "reverse reeling" means reversing the circle); the left foot retreats. First left, then right, two or three times on each side, until the left hand and left foot have both arrived at the rear.

Neijing (內精, Inner Essence)

Diagram notes (four figures: right-hand, left-hand, right-leg, left-leg diagrams):

- Right Hand Diagram: the right hand arrives at the rear; the right hand arrives below.

- Left Hand Diagram: arrives above; the left hand; the left hand arrives at the front; the right hand arrives at the front; left and right are both reverse-winding essence (倒纏精); if the left hand arrives at the rear, then...

- Right Leg Diagram: if the right leg is at the rear, then...; the right leg is below.

- Left Leg Diagram: if the left leg arrives at the front, then...; the right leg to the rear; the left leg is at the front; the right leg has turned to the front; if the left leg arrives at the rear, then...; if the right leg is at the front, then...; if the left leg turns to the rear, then...; the right leg is at the front.

Both legs use silk-reeling essence (纏絲精, chansijing); both wind from outside inward — this is called the reverse-winding method (倒纏法), that is, reversing the circle.

Quxiang (取象, Taking the Image)

The earlier Reverse Reeling Red (倒卷紅) took its image from Kun (坤, the Receptive); why take it again now? Let me put into words what was not fully said before. The left hand and foot should each reverse-reel and retreat — this is the meaning of the six broken lines of Kun. One might ask: why not advance, but retreat backward — is this not cowardice? Answer: no. It is like conducting a campaign: if you can attack, you attack; if you cannot attack, you withdraw and defend. Attacking is hard, but defense is not easy either; to be able to withdraw and defend in order to prepare ground for attack is harder still. In this posture it is not that one does not wish to advance; but with thousands of men and ten thousand horses, spears and blades all at one's front with no gap through which to enter, one cannot but retreat — and with the two hands, left and right, reverse-reeling to avoid their sharp edges, watching for... (continues on the next page).

( Printed page: 247 )

Trang sách 248 · scan p281

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated)

To advance only after there is a gap is not too late. Moreover, if there is an opening to exploit, then one arrow hits the mark (striking the chief culprit, or hitting a vital point) — better by ten thousand times than killing nameless rank-and-file soldiers. This is the reason for retreating. The intent lies here — what is there to fear? Moreover, Kun 坤 (Earth) is compliant: comply with the time — when the time calls for retreat, retreat; comply with the ground — where the ground allows retreat, retreat; comply with the opening — when there is no opening to exploit, one naturally ought to coil back (dao juan 倒卷); comply with the momentum — when the momentum does not allow advancing, one should again retreat.

This is the yielding (Kun is the way of yielding, rou dao 柔道) overcoming the hard — taking retreat as advance is the way of Kun. Kun is the inverse (cuo 錯) of Qian 乾 (Heaven); Qian is hard. Kun is utterly yielding, yet in movement it is hard. This boxing outwardly seems yielding, but in reality is utterly hard. When the first line changes, it becomes Zhen 震 (Thunder); Zhen is the foot, movement. The foot moves — the image of retreating. It is the inverse of Xun 巽 (Wind); Xun means advancing and retreating. The boxing's advance and retreat have no fixed rule: advance when one can advance, retreat when one can retreat — judge what is fitting. [Xun] is "many white eyes": the eyes govern seeing; look left and right so that there is no failure. It is the reverse (zong 綜) of Gen 艮 (Mountain); Gen is the hand, the hand can stop things — use the hand to ward off the enemy so that he cannot injure oneself. It completes Fu 復 (Return): the two hands send and transport to and fro, ending and beginning again. When the second line changes it becomes Kan 坎 (Water), "full in the middle": the middle line is like a person's body — from Baihui 百會 to Changqiang 長強 the central qi (zhong qi 中氣) penetrates through; the four lines above and below are like the four limbs, left and right. It is the inverse of Li 離 (Fire); Li is the eye, the eyes can cast their light in all directions. Li means brightness. The hands and feet left and right move like sun and moon alternating in brightness. Li is empty in the middle: for one who retreats, his mind always thinks one step backward, not daring to be complacent, in hope of certain victory. The two lines above and below are like the left and right halves of the body, moved with real substance. It completes Shi 師 (The Army): shi means the multitude. With the mind as master, the five sense organs and the hundred bones all obey its command. When the third line changes it becomes Gen 艮: Gen is the gatekeeper (hun si 閽寺), the finger, the gate-tower. The hands, left and right, look after front, back, left and right — like a gatekeeper using his fingers to stop things, firmly guarding the gate-tower. It is the inverse of Dui 兌 (Lake); Dui is metal — smelt this body a hundred times into an iron man. As Dui occupies the West, it belongs to the metals Geng 庚 and Xin 辛. It is the reverse of Zhen 震; Zhen is the dragon — the boxing's transformations are like the dragon's, unfathomable. It completes Qian 謙 (Modesty): when I meet the enemy, I can guard myself with modest yielding; good fortune predominates and blame is rare. As for the changes of the fourth, fifth, and sixth lines, their meaning is the same. Since Kun's changing lines are thus, it is taken again. And that this posture is empty in front and solid behind naturally goes without saying.

Second Dao Juan Hong 倒卷紅 (Reverse Coiling Red) — Seven-Character Folk Verses

The First

Beneath the sky-facing stirrup (chao tian deng 朝天鐙) comes the Reverse Coiling Red; the left hand first returns, swift as the wind. When the left hand finishes turning, the right hand turns — retreating, truly a great hero.

The Second

The two hands turn like a screw-thread, one up and one down, perfectly even. It all depends on Taiji's true quickening (zhen xiao xi 真消息): four ounces can deflect eight thousand catties (this means that four ounces of strength can turn aside eight thousand jin 八千斤).

The Fiftieth Form: White Goose Shows Its Wings (Bai E Liang Chi 白鵝亮翅)

It echoes and corresponds to the two previous White Goose Shows Its Wings. This posture responds to the two earlier White Goose Shows Its Wings as a conclusion.

Guiding the Beginner (Yin Meng 引蒙)

This posture is purely the jing of leading in (yin jin 引進). In the Reverse Coiling Red 倒卷紅, the left hand arrives below; the right hand goes from right to left, the two hands about one chi apart. The right hand leads the left hand, first turning a small circle from the left, then following the momentum obliquely upward from the left to the right. The right foot likewise first turns a small circle, then steps out from left to right; the left foot follows the right foot to the right, the two feet five or six cun apart, the toes of the left foot touching the ground. The right hand and right foot turn together — only then does it become one qi (yi qi 一氣).

Trang sách 250 · scan p283

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated)

Inner Jing (Nei Jing 內精)

The method of movement along the route has already been diagrammed before. The right hand uses the compliant-turning method (shun zhuan 順轉); the right foot does likewise. The left hand uses the reverse-turning method (dao zhuan 倒轉) — all of these are coiling-silk jing (chansijin 缠丝精). The right hand and right foot move together with the left hand; only the left foot must wait until the right foot has landed, then the left foot follows it also toward the right, its toes touching the ground.

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang 取象)

In the previous posture, Reverse Coiling Red 倒卷紅, the body was amid peril. This posture resolves difficulties and untangles disputes, coming out beyond the peril. Therefore it is taken from Jie 解 (Deliverance/Difficulty). But to deliver from difficulty, leading in (yin jin 引進) cannot be dispensed with.

Seven-Character Folk Verses

The First

The third White Goose, its feathers full and rich; turning left toward the right, this technique is most refined. Within this lies boundless meaning reserved and contained — again it leads a person in and drops him into emptiness.

The Second

Each posture is harder than the last; this posture whirls like a rolling pellet. The marvelous mechanism originally issues from the heart — how could the enemy discern where the dragon coils (long pan 龍蟠)?

The Third

The secret of leading in can never be fully told: one yin, one yang — look within the hands. To suppress, first raise — this is the true principle; striking a person does not depend on whipping the horse first (i.e., on moving first).

The Fifty-First Form: The Third Brush Knee and Twist Step (Lou Xi Ao Bu 搂膝拗步 — lou in the level tone)

This posture responds to the two previous Brush Knee and Twist Step postures.

The left and right hands divide downward, level, from in front of the chest, both using reverse coiling-silk jing (dao chansijin 倒缠丝精). The right hand circles around the right knee, turns behind and back to in front of the chest, about a chi or more from the chest. The middle finger faces the tip of the nose (bi zhun 鼻准) in mutual regard; the eyes watch the middle finger. The left hand brushes the left knee from the left side: the hand sweeps from behind to the front around one full circle, then turns back behind, the hand regarding and settling with the spine, the five fingers gathered together. The left foot takes a large step out from right to left; after it lands, the rear foot is like a hook — above and below join together.

Inner Jing (Nei Jing 內精)

For the explanation, see the Sixth Form.

The head is upright, the eyes gaze level; shoulder joins with shoulder, elbow with elbow, hand with hand, thigh-root with thigh-root, knee with knee, foot with foot. Calm the heart and settle the qi,

陳氏太極拳圖說　卷三 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume III)

The Fifty-First Form: The Third Brush Knee and Twist Step (Lou Xi Ao Bu 搂膝拗步) (continued)

It is said "join" (he 合): above and below join together as one, and the qi returns to the dantian 丹田. All the joining methods use the reverse-coiling method; only the left foot's opening step uses the compliant-turning method. This posture is purely joining jing (he jing 合精).

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang 取象)

This posture takes Qian 乾, Kun 坤, Kan 坎, and Li 離. In terms of directions, the explanation is given in the Sixth Form. Speaking of Qian, Kun, Kan, and Li, the four corner trigrams — Dui 兌, Zhen 震, Xun 巽, and Gen 艮 — are contained within them. Here the trigrams are taken for their virtue (de 德), not merely for their positions or their line-structures.

Seven-Character Folk Verses

The First

Brush Knee and Twist Step reaches the third; it responds to those lying hidden before (referring to the two previous Brush Knee and Twist Steps), the zest at its height. Prepared on all four sides and eight directions — only when the work is complete does one realize it was no empty talk.

The Second

When the primal vital energy of Great Harmony (tai he yuan qi 太和元氣) reaches stillness, without stillness one does not see the marvel of movement. Six trigrams, four yin (on all four sides above and below) — who could explain it? Only the one who has attained it knows it himself.

The Fifty-Second Form: Dodge and Thread Through the Back (Shan Tong Bei 閃通背)

It responds to the previous Shan Tong Bei.

Section Explanation (Jie Jie 節解)

Guiding the Beginner (Yin Meng 引蒙)

When the previous posture, Brush Knee and Twist Step, is finished, the right foot takes a large step forward; the right hand goes from right to left, first circling a small circle, turning back to the front crown, stopping with the hand on the upper slanting edge. Bend the waist greatly, splitting down through the crotch 裆 until reaching the inner ankles, left and right; then from below sweep upward, the hand arriving at Xinhui 囟會 (GV22, fontanelle point). The left foot takes a large step forward, the left hand following the left foot from behind to the front, the hand level with the shoulder, the arm spread open. Then the body turns around reversed; the right foot follows the body in the reverse turn, landing behind the left foot. The right hand likewise follows the body's reverse turn, from above downward, landing behind the right foot, the hand level with the waist, the arm spread open — this is the great turning-body method (da zhuan shen fa 大轉身法). It depends entirely on

陳氏太極拳圖說　卷三 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume III)

the liveliness of the footwork, with both shoulders loosened open and hands and feet following one another above and below — only then is it attained.

Inner Jing (Nei Jing 內精)

The diagrams and explanations are all given in detail before.

Seven-Character Folk Verses

Then use the right hand again to face the enemy in front, while an enemy behind again grabs my waist. Who would know my waist suddenly bends down, the tailbone (tun gu 臀骨) thrusting upward (in the rising tone) — the enemy can hardly escape. This is speed jing (su jing 速精); if slow, it will be too late. It looks like a crude posture, but in fact its subtlety is beyond compare.

The Fifty-Third Form: The Sixth Performing-Hand Fist (Di Liu Yan Shou Quan 第六演手捶)

It is the direct counterpart of the Third Performing-Hand Fist. It also ties together the five previous performing-hand fists, and it initiates the Fist at the Crotch (zhi dang chui 指襠捶) that follows. This single fist, in the manner of literary composition, connects what precedes with what follows.

Section Explanation (Jie Jie 節解)

(Diagram: a figure standing in a stance showing the Sixth Performing-Hand Fist posture, left bow stance, the left hand extended forward with fingers spread, the right hand holding a fist in front of the chest with the elbow tip pointing up, the head turned to look back behind the body.)

Diagram annotations:

- the left fingers spread open, to correspond with the right fist

- the right hand grips the fist; the eyes watch the fist

- the jing at the crown is held up (ding jing 頂精領住)

- the waist jing settles down

- the crotch jing (dang jing 襠精) settles down well

- the left knee is bent and held

- the ground — the left foot treads level, using force

- the right hand joins in; the elbow tip points upward

- the ears listen behind, guarding against the enemy's secret scheme

- the right heel treads the ground — it must be with force

Trang sách 254 · scan p287

陳氏太極拳圖說　卷三 (Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated, Volume III)

Guiding the Beginner (Yin Meng 引蒙)

In the Third Performing-Hand Fist, the right hand strikes the enemy in front and the right foot likewise follows, landing in front — hence it forms a back-view diagram. Because the enemy was somewhat far, the right foot was especially advanced to reach the enemy. In this performing-hand fist, however, the right hand strikes forward using the joining fist (he chui 合捶); but the enemy is very close to my body, so the right foot need not advance a step to assist where the right hand falls short. The right foot does not move and remains behind — hence it forms a front-view diagram.

Inner Jing (Nei Jing 內精)

In Shan Tong Bei 閃通背 the body collided and turned back around; the right foot is behind and the right hand also behind. Use coiling-silk jing (chansijin 缠丝精), turning one full circle from behind, then joining forward into the fist — only then does the strike have force. Moreover, the jing of the whole body, above and below, must all join together, with the spirit gathered entirely in the fist — only then can it defeat the enemy. The diagrams of inner jing all appear before.

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang 取象)

Cui 萃 (Gathering), Xiao Guo 小過 (Small Excess), and Da Zhuang 大壯 (Great Strength) were already discussed for the Third Performing-Hand Fist; here Zhen 震 (Thunder) is taken again. For the fist can frighten those near and startle those far — "thunder startles for a hundred li"; the performing-hand fist is like this.

Seven-Character Folk Verses

A clap of thunder bursts from the dust; the myriad creatures are startled together — thunder for a hundred li. The right hand strikes from below toward the front, just like the rolling rumble (hui hui 虺虺) in the sky.
