Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3

Posture 54: Lan Ca Yi (攬擦衣)

This Lan Ca Yi (攬擦衣) echoes the previous Lan Ca Yi, and brings to a conclusion the first three Lan Ca Yi.

Jie Jie (節解, Section Explanation):

- Waist jing (腰精) sinks down

- Right knee bends and holds

- Right foot treads flat

- If the body is not stable, it must be propped round

- From beginning to end the crotch jing (襠精) sinks down; if the crotch jing does not sink down, it sinks below

- The left foot presses firmly against the ground

Yin Meng (引蒙, Guiding Instructions):

The right hand withdraws to in front of the right ribs; the right foot advances from behind to the right of the left foot, level with it. Then the right hand and right foot move together. The right hand, from in front of the left ribs, first circles a small circle from below upward, then slowly unfolds the arm from left to right; the hand opens, the five fingers bound together, the hand level with the shoulder. The right foot follows the right hand, also first circling a small circle,

Diagram labels:

- From beginning to end the fist must never lose its top jing (頂精); once the top jing is lost, the four limbs have nothing to attach to and lack spirit, therefore it must be led up, to serve as the guiding principle for the whole body.

- The eyes look at the right middle finger; the right hand opens with the fingers bound.

- The chest closes forward holding the jing; the chest slightly bent, naturally holding it.

- The left hand braces at the waist

- The left shoulder relaxed

- Waist jing sinks down

- Right knee bends and holds

- Right foot treads flat

- If the body is not stable, it must be propped round

- From beginning to end the crotch jing sinks down

- The left foot presses firmly against the ground

[Diagram: practitioner in bow stance, weight on the bent right leg, left leg extended straight; left hand braced on the waist, right arm extended forward at shoulder height, eyes on the right middle finger.]
Trang sách 256 · scan p289

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Then slowly bend from the left, the posture like a new moon, stepping out to the right. The left foot stays in place without moving; the left hand circles a round from inside to outside, then turns back to below the left armpit, bracing the waist.

Nei Jing (內精, Internal Energy):

The moving hand uses forward silk-reeling energy (順纏精, shun chan), reeling out to the fingertips. Reeling from inside to outside is called forward silk-reeling (順纏). The right leg likewise uses forward silk-reeling energy, reeling out to the toes. The left hand uses reverse silk-reeling energy (逆纏精): reeling from outside inward is called reverse silk-reeling (逆纏) or inverted reeling (倒纏). The diagrams are seen earlier, at the first Lan Ca Yi (攬擦衣) and Posture 22.

Qu Xiang (取象, Taking the Image):

Posture 2 took Tai (泰), Posture 21 took Xiao Xu (小畜), Posture 43 took Meng (蒙) — each has its own meaning, already stated before. This posture: the left arm bent is like "the hidden dragon does not act" (潛龍勿用); the right arm extended is like "the dragon appears in the field" (見龍在田); therefore it also takes from Qian (乾). The way of Qian transforms without fixed direction, possessing the virtue of yang firmness (陽剛). The left and right arms likewise transform without fixed direction, hence they are compared to the dragon.

Qi Yan Li Yu (七言俚語, Seven-Character Folk Verse):

Alone the right hand extends like the appearing dragon; the left hand coils back to seal the left side. Of itself the Great Harmony's primal qi (太和元氣) governs; one yin and one yang guard the chest.

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3

Posture 55: The Seventh Single Whip (第七单鞭, Di Qi Dan Bian)

Jie Jie (节解, Section Explanation):

It corresponds from afar with the previous six Single Whips.

Diagram labels:

- The left arm and fingers extended open, the five fingers bound together

- The shoulder relaxed

- The eyes fixed on the left middle finger

- The top jing (頂精) held up

- The ears listening behind

- The right arm must not be raised up like a rack; follow its natural course

- The left arm held with the back; the right five fingers bound together

- The chest must be contained (含蓄); the qi (氣) descends to the dantian (丹田); no horizontal qi left above

- The left knee bends and holds

- The waist jing sinks down

- The crotch jing sinks down and props round

- The left foot treads flat in an eight-character (八字) shape

- The left foot hooks forward

- The heel uses pressing jing (蹬精)

- The right leg must not go soft

Yin Meng (引蒙, Guiding Instructions):

The left hand draws out from the waist and closes with the right hand. The right hand first turns a small circle, using the forward-turning method (順轉法) to slowly extend the arm leftward, five fingers bound, eyes on the middle finger; the right hand turns a small circle from the front and closes with the left hand. The right hand uses inverted-turning jing (倒轉精). The left foot first withdraws beside the right foot, first turning a small circle; then steps out a large step leftward like the character eight (八字), swinging the right foot back to press the ground, treading flatly.

[Diagram: practitioner in single-whip stance, legs spread, left arm extended with fingers spread, right arm at the side, labels pointing to each body part.]
Trang sách 258 · scan p291

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Nei Jing (內精, Internal Energy):

The left and right hands closing are both closed with inverted silk-reeling jing (倒纏精). The method of moving the left and right hands: the left hand uses forward silk-reeling jing (順纏精); the jing goes up from below the ribs to the armpit, and from the armpit reels from outside inward, stopping at the finger pads. The right hand's jing travels up behind the ribs to the shoulder, and from the shoulder reels diagonally from inside outward, reaching the fingernails — this is inverted silk-reeling jing. Such is the method of moving these two hands. As for the feet, the right foot does not move from its place; only twist the heel so the toes hook leftward; the left foot withdraws and then opens again. When stepping out, this too is forward silk-reeling jing. Starting from the finger pads of the left and right, reel from inside outward up to the hip bone, the intention closing inward. The left hand leads the left foot, the right hand leads the right foot, moving together. In exposition it must be set out point by point distinctly; for the diagrams see in detail Posture 3, Single Whip (單鞭).

Qu Xiang (取象, Taking the Image):

Posture 3 took Kan–Li–Pi–Tai (坎離否泰), Posture 27 took Wu Wang (無妄), Posture 36 combined Kan–Li with Qian–Kun (乾坤), Posture 41 took Zhen (震), Posture 44 took the changed hexagrams of Kan–Li. This posture is soft outside and firm within, so it takes from Qian and Kun. Qian and Kun are the father and mother of the six children (六子, the other trigrams), and therefore encompass them all.

Qi Yan Li Yu (七言俚語, Seven-Character Folk Verse):

On the seventh day comes the return (the seventh Single Whip) — its turning is marvelous; empty above, solid below, the image of Kan and Li. Who would know that hard and soft are both fully present? The six children have ever taken the Two Modes (兩儀) as their ancestor.

Trang sách 259 · scan p292

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3

Posture 56: Left-Right Cloud Hands (左右雲手)①

It corresponds with the two previous Cloud Hands (雲手). This one occupies the lower position, hence it is called the Lower Cloud Hands. The one in front is the Front Cloud Hands; the one behind, the Rear Cloud Hands. "Cloud Hands" means the hands revolving back and forth, like clouds spiraling and gushing — an image-form (象形). It is also said: "moving the hands with the hands revolving" — the phrase also works as "moving hands."

Diagram: Yunshou Qi Shi Tu (雲手起勢圖, Starting Posture of Cloud Hands)

Diagram labels:

- Shoulder relaxes down (肩松下)

- Elbow relaxes down (肘松下)

- Chest closed and held (胸合住)

- Elbow slightly bent (肘微彎)

- Five fingers bound, hand goes backward (五指束住，手向後去)

- Crotch open and round (襠開圓)

- Lifting intention (提意)

- Rear waist sinks down, right knee slightly bent (後腰向下，右膝微屈)

- Right foot turns directly in toward the left foot without touching the ground, following the right hand back a step, about one chi (右足直向左足收轉，不落地，隨住右手回步，大約一尺)

- Waist jing sinks down (腰精下去)

- Hand falls, with an intention to draw inward and a tendency to rise (手落下，有欲往裏收兼有上泛之勢)

- Left foot treads solid; the rear heel and toes follow the left hand as if exerting force (左足踏實，後踵足趾隨左手似有用力)

Boxing depends entirely on the opening posture (起勢); once the opening gains the moment (得勢), everything below gains the moment. This posture succeeds Single Whip (單鞭): if an enemy comes from the right, one must first lead him with the right hand. To lead with the right hand, one must first drop the right shoulder; to drop the right shoulder, one must first lead upward with the left hand. When the left hand leads up, the left shoulder relaxes down, the chest closes forward, and the crotch jing (襠精) sinks down. The left foot is solid, the right foot empty. Body method and hand method move all together. After this, moving the right hand first, one naturally gains the opportunity and the moment, because the incoming channel is genuine. Even without an enemy, practicing the movement empty-handed, one still feels the sequence gaining the moment, the mechanisms lively. Therefore I say each posture depends entirely on its opening — at the joint where the bones connect (接骨頭筍處), how the previous posture comes down and how this posture rises up must be studied with care. And it all lies in the closing-down: one must consider how to move to complete satisfaction, without the least deficiency. When spirit and qi (神氣) are full,

① The original edition reads "Lower Cloud Hands" (下雲手); according to the table of contents it is here changed to "Left-Right Cloud Hands" (左右雲手).

[Diagram: figure standing with feet shoulder-width apart, knees slightly bent, arms extended outward, labels annotating alignment and movement directions.]
Trang sách 260 · scan p293

Main text

This posture seems as if it could stop, yet the mechanism of the next movement has already stirred: one wishes to stop but cannot stop. For the very mechanism of "wishing to stop, about to stop" has already summoned up the next posture. Therefore I say: at this moment the state is "seemingly stopped yet not stopped" (not stopped, because the spirit is still not full), "not stopped yet stopped" (what stops is only a single line — the next posture rises immediately). At this juncture one must examine it in fine detail. Moreover, the right arm has already extended itself; without bending it certainly cannot extend again. So the left hand leads upward, the right shoulder naturally releases downward, and the right hand naturally uses the jin of drawing-in to retract the arm. Thus what does not bend cannot extend; what does not extend cannot bend — this is natural principle, known to all. What is difficult lies wholly in using the silk-reeling jin (chan si jin, 缠丝劲) to draw it in and make it advance. Although the left hand leads upward and the right hand draws back in, everything must unite at the chest: the waist jin sinks down, the crotch jin (dang jin, 裆劲) rounds and props up, the left foot plants solidly, the right foot lifts empty — then the upper body feels ever more lively. Of the sixty-four postures, every single one is like this. I raise one corner only as an example for the rest; the student should reflect on it himself.

Diagram notes (Yun Shou, Cloud Hands — right hand moving)

The right hand turns one circle, clouding upward, then leads upward; the left hand follows the right hand moving rightward, also arriving at the chest. Yet the left hand, having gathered down from above, has already turned half a circle by here; the right hand likewise does not pause, moving at once upward and forward.

Here the right hand has withdrawn to the front of the body at the chest; from above downward and then rising, it uses silk-reeling jin to turn one large circle, arriving here without stopping.

The eyes follow the right hand's movement: wherever the right hand goes, the eyes go too. The same on the left side, using the middle fingertip as the target, the finger-pad exerting force.

The ears listen to the left; the crown jin (ding jin, 顶劲) holds well.

The right foot follows along; the right hand, gathered to the left, does not slowly bend at the foot's side, stops, then slowly bends outward as the posture opens the step; though the foot touches the ground it does not rest.

Diagram

Yun Shou you shou yun xing tu (云手右手运行图), "Diagram of the right hand's movement in Cloud Hands": the figure stands in a left bow stance (left leg bent forward and planted, right leg empty behind), the right arm circling along a dotted arc, the left hand gathered at the chest, the eyes on the right hand.

(Printed page number: 260)

Trang sách 261 · scan p294

Main text (internal jin, nei jin 内劲)

The dantian qi (dan tian qi, 丹田气) divides into five parts. In truth it is one qi threading through above and below, and cannot collapse — this is one and the same. The heart-qi leads; the dantian rises: six parts reach the heart, then divide again into two streams — three parts rise to the left shoulder, three parts to the right shoulder, all threading through the shoulder bone out to the fingers of both hands. What is within the bone is called central qi (zhong qi, 中气); what manifests in flesh and skin is called silk-reeling jin (chan si jin, 缠丝劲). The remaining four parts divide into two streams: two parts course down the left thigh, two parts the right thigh, all threading through the bone out to the toes of both feet. The heel lands first. The forefoot must be lively; the toes point when they should point and must use force; they move when they should move — toes and calf must both use force. Left and right Cloud Hands are both moved by the method of forward turning. First lead the left hand upward, then lower the right hand; next the right hand descends on the right, gathered back to the chest. The left hand turns half a circle from above to behind; when the right hand rises from the chest and moves rightward, the left hand descends and gathers back to the chest; when the left hand rises from the chest and moves leftward, the right hand descends from the right and gathers back to the chest. Neither hand ever pauses. This one goes, that one comes; that...

Diagram notes (Yun Shou, Cloud Hands — left hand moving)

The left hand, from the chest, uses silk-reeling jin to move upward and leftward, arriving here without stopping; the right wrist faces forward.

As the left hand moves, both eyes watch the left hand; the hand must be lively.

The shoulders relax down; the left hand turns its circle, and the shoulders follow the turning circle.

The crown jin (ding jin, 顶劲) holds well.

The eyes should stay fixed on the left hand.

The ears should listen behind.

When the left hand reaches the top, the right hand from below gradually gathers to the chest, the five fingers bundled; without pausing it moves at once rightward, rising from the chest to move leftward — the two alternately revolving without cease.

The left foot steps out leftward, very large, about a chi and a half; the left foot follows the left hand's movement, as the right hand's footwork was.

The right foot steps out rightward, small, about one chi. This step gradually moves leftward, the right foot naturally yielding.

Diagram

Yun Shou zuo shou yun xing tu (云手左手运行图), "Diagram of the left hand's movement in Cloud Hands": the figure in a left bow stance, the left hand raised to shoulder height in motion, the right hand gathered at the waist, dotted lines marking the circular paths of both hands, with labels for crown jin, eyes, ears and shoulders.

(Printed page number: 261)

Trang sách 262 · scan p295

Main text

...goes, this one comes; left and right revolve in linked succession, like the courses of sun and moon: when the sun goes the moon comes, when the moon goes the sun comes. Hence one hand governs only half the body. The left hand moves leftward, and the left foot follows the left hand, stepping leftward. The left foot's step is rather large — it purely uses the method of advancing sideways — therefore it cannot be too large. As for the right hand's movement, its turning circle is of the same size; only the footwork differs slightly. When the right hand moves rightward, the right foot also steps from left to right, but the step it opens is slightly smaller. The body moves sideways toward the left while the right foot advances; if the step were not small it could not go left, advancing gradually — so the right step must yield to the left step, yet it cannot be too small. Cloud Hands has no fixed number of repetitions; one stops according to the ground at hand. Generally it goes no more than three or four steps to the left. As to when it is about to stop: at the start the left hand leads upward on the left, the left arm half-extended, half-bent; at the very end of Cloud Hands the left hand still comes down on the left side, half-extended, half-bent, while the right hand comes to rest before the chest. Such are the specifications of left and right hands. As for footwork: when the left foot has stepped out leftward, the right foot should be drawn back inward; yet when "drawing back" it does not actually draw back — it plants right where it stepped, the left and right feet about no more than two chi apart.

Method of movement (yun xing fa, 运行法)

Left and right movement is all "one smooth advance" (yi shun qian qu, 一顺前去). For instance with the left hand and left foot: they rise from the chest, from within. The hand extends leftward, the left foot steps out rising from within; then the right hand descends and gathers to the chest. The right foot follows the right hand in gathering to the left foot's side, no more than four or five cun apart. When the right hand rises from the chest, extends rightward and moves, the left hand at once descends from the left and gathers to the chest. Both hands are always going toward the right side. The right foot, from within, slowly bends and steps out rightward; then the left foot draws from the left toward the right foot, drawing in only an inch or two — indeed it need not reach the right foot's side. This is the method of gradually stealing leftward; otherwise, if the left foot always drew fully to the right foot's side, it would remain in its original place and could not open leftward. Thus the moment one foot lifts, it is storing momentum for the next step and leaving room for it — the method of mutual yielding. Every posture is like this; remember: whether left or right, the hand and foot on that side move in one smooth line, differing only in the visible traces of rising, descending, and gathering from outside to within.

Diagram (zuo you yun xing shou tu, 左右运行手图)

Two intersecting circles, their intersection marked "chest" (xiong jian, 胸间). The left circle is the right hand's path: up, right, outside, down, run inward; the right circle is the left hand's path: up, left, outside, down, descending. Text above the diagram: the hand rises level with the upward movement; the right hand runs one down-stroke; the movement goes up from the right, by the right hand; together with the left hand descending inward; moving together as one smooth turn. Caption below: left and right revolve in linked succession without cease.

(Printed page number: 262)

Trang sách 263 · scan p296

Diagram: Movement of Left and Right Hands and Footwork (左右手运行足步图)

(The figure shows two arcs tracing the paths of the left and right hands, marked 起 qi "begin" and 落 luo "finish"; below are drawn the footwork and notes on step size and circling direction.)

Figure notes (right to left):

- 落处 luo chu — the finishing place

- The left foot steps large, gradually moving left — this is "seizing the moment" (趁 chen)

- As the right foot lifts it first traces a small circle

- As the right foot lifts it likewise first traces a small circle

- The right foot steps small, also gradually edging leftward

- 起 qi (begin) / 落 luo (finish) / 起 qi

Taking the Image (取象)

Left and right Cloud Hands (云手 yun shou): the heart is utterly empty and bright, and the two hands wheel and dance like sun and moon, so the image is taken from Li (离, the trigram Fire). The Image says: "Li means clinging. The sun and moon cling to heaven; doubled brightness clings to what is right; the second and fifth lines both attain correctness." The form runs on central qi (中气 zhong qi), and people are thereby won over in their hearts. This is the transforming of all under heaven. Li receives the central qi of Qian, so the central qi of the form is all the central qi of Qian. The Image says: "Brightness doubled — thus Li." The left hand is like the sun, the right hand like the moon; one extends and one bends, like sun and moon alternating in shining. The great man is Heaven's lord. With inherited brightness (that is, the rotation of the two hands) he illuminates the four quarters (guarding against the enemy above, below, and on all four sides). Because he holds the center, he goes out and gains.

When the middle lines first change we get Gen (艮, Mountain): Gen is a mountain; the central qi pours through and one stands firm as a mountain peak. Gen is the hand, and means stopping — with the hand one stops the opponent, and strikes. Its reverse is Dui (兑, Joy): in meeting the enemy I rejoice, purely drawing him in to advance with harmonious, rounded qi. Its inverse is Zhen (震, Arousing) — rousing, the idea of the spirits being stirred; Zhen is the foot, meaning movement left and right without a gap. On the second change we get Qian (乾), whose reverse is Kun (坤): one gains the upright qi of Qian and Kun. On the third change we get Zhen, the trigram of the east: all things issue from Zhen, one gains the qi of life and movement. Its reverse is Xun (巽): all things are ordered by Xun — the sense organs and limbs all move together, all obeying in their movement. Its inverse is Gen: Gen is the gate-tower, the gatekeeper, the hand — my guarding is so strict there is no opening to enter; even at dusk the gatekeeper bars passage, and with the hand the enemy is stopped from advancing and attacking. Li's reverse is Kan (坎, Water): a man can meet things with an empty heart, careful and prudent, not daring to rely on himself. Though enemies come from right, left, above and below, he views them as "no level ground without a slope." With this "yellow center piercing principle," suppleness aided by uprightness, he treads danger as if level ground, and no slope is not leveled. Li is fire; when locking and transforming move freely, nothing undertaken is inauspicious.

(Further on the image) — Same body. Dun (遁) means hiding: the spirit should be stored within, its edges never shown abroad. On the upper part of the ding tripod the two ears stand at the sides, with rings in them that, once set moving, revolve without end — like the movement of the two hands. Below, the three legs stand together, immovable: like the two feet in the form — one foot planted and unmoving is the tripod leg standing firm; the other foot moves like a tripod leg that seems to bend yet does not bend, utterly stable. For one foot steadies the other — how could there be a mishap? Though it seems unsteady, in truth nothing unexpected occurs. One takes the sure footing beneath; there is no need to insist that the tripod's three legs and a man's two legs share one body.

(printed page 263)

Trang sách 264 · scan p297

(Continuing from the previous page) ... the form of it.

Song (讼, Contention): two men contest a case — one says a word, the other a phrase, each claiming he is right. The alternating movement of the left and right hands simply means each acts according to its own part. Jia Ren (家人, The Family): the five sense organs and the hundred bones take turns moving, like one household, old and young, men and women, each fulfilling its proper duty. Wu Wang (无妄, No Error): the heart in the form is nothing but sincerity — with a true heart one does true deeds, never deceiving oneself; true principle runs all through it. Ge (革, Change): when the left hand should take command, change to the left hand; when the right should, change to the right — no error, no delay. Da Xu (大畜, Great Accumulation): to hold and nourish — taiji's yin and yang embrace a vast range. Kui (暌, Opposition): separation; in the movement of the two feet, though the spirit has no gap, there is inevitably some separateness in the visible traces.

Zhong Fu (中孚, Inner Truth) speaks of the disposition of the form — everything is sincere. Da Zhuang (大壮, Great Power): four yang lines advancing together, irresistible. When the form's central qi (zhong qi) goes somewhere, who can stop it? Xu (需, Waiting): from waiting in the mud to waiting with wine and food — it means going from danger to safety, sorrow first and joy after. The hexagram: "Xu has truth; brilliance, success; perseverance brings good fortune; it furthers to cross the great water." The Image says: "Xu means waiting: danger lies ahead. The strong and vigorous do not sink — their principle cannot come to impasse." It means gaining the central qi of Qian: nothing undertaken is unfitting. First line: waiting at the outskirts — favorable to persevere. Second line: waiting on the sand, with breadth at the center (衍 yan means breadth; dwelling at the center with breadth) — hence good fortune in the end. Third line: waiting in the mud, drawing in the enemy — careful respect and one is not defeated. Fourth line: waiting in blood, getting out of the pit. Fifth line: waiting with wine and food (an image of pleasant circumstances) — perseverance brings good fortune, through centrality and correctness. Top six: entering the pit; three uninvited guests arrive — respect them, and in the end good fortune. Moving the hands leftward is advance without retreat; with central qi moving through it, entering danger and emerging from it both end well.

Seven-Character Vernacular Verses (七言俚语)

First

Sun and moon shine bright, dawn upon dawn; left and right hands wheel, their forms entwined. Moving left, left and right (hands and feet all move left); left hand rising (from inside outward), right hand sinking (from outside inward and back) — do not disorder the sequence. Moving right, the right foot points squarely right; the left foot (the foot is named, but the hand is included in it) stands left while its intent joins rightward (though the left foot is on the left, its intent also faces right). Left and right each take command as they please; when the moment of command comes, they hand over in turn (whatever should go left all goes left, whatever should go right all goes right). Taiji's yin and yang — true creation's craft: embroidered mandarin ducks I embroider for you to see.

Second

One return, one advance, the hands move again; the rotation differs not a hair from before. Only look carefully from below at the peng (棚/掤) — the left foot differs slightly (edging north-west five or six cun), which opens what follows.

(printed page 264)

Trang sách 265 · scan p298

Posture 57: Gao Tan Ma (高探马, "Rising and Patting the Horse")

The upper figure is a front view, the new style; the lower figure is a back view, the old style.

Sectional Notes (节解)

Front-figure labels:

- Crown lifted by jing (顶精 ting jing) — hold it up

- Eyes fixed on the right hand

- Right arm as if bent yet not bent, as if extended yet not extended, palm facing up

- Elbow tip pointing down

- Left arm bent, wrist turned down

- Right knee bent and held

- Right foot planted flat

- Left foot touching with the toes

Back-figure labels:

- Crown lifted by jing — hold it up

- Facing the back of the hand (small note at the right humerus)

- Right humerus in front

- Left knee bent and held

- Crotch jing (裆精 dang jing) sinking down

- Right foot empty, lightly touching

- Left foot solidly planted

(printed page 265)

Trang sách 266 · scan p299

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation)

Yin Meng (引蒙, Leading the Beginner)

New Style (新式 xīn shì): The right foot steps forward to beside the left foot, without touching the ground, and is immediately drawn back and placed on the ground. The left foot is also drawn back and placed beside the right foot, its toes touching the ground. As the right foot is drawn back, the left and right hands also follow it, circling from above downward in a large arc toward the rear, then turning forward, the two palms closing together, about a chi (尺, ~33 cm) apart.

Old Style (老式 lǎo shì): The left and right hands likewise circle round from behind in a large arc. The body turns smoothly around, the right foot unmoving; the left foot is drawn back and placed behind, the right arm (肱 gōng) extends, the left arm bends. Although the two hands are about a chi and a half apart, the palms nevertheless face the face and are closed with jing (精, essence/internal energy).

Nei Jing (内精, Internal Energy)

In the Gao Tan Ma (高探马, High Patting Horse), both new and old styles, the right hand is reverse-winding jing (倒转精, dào zhuǎn jīng). It rises from below the back up to the back, from the back inward, then from below obliquely to the outside, coiling to the fingernail — this is yang jing (阳精). The left hand is forward-winding jing (顺转精, shùn zhuǎn jīng): it rises from below the armpit up to the armpit, then rises from the armpit, from inside to outside, coiling obliquely to the finger pad — this is yin jing (阴精). One yin, one yang, and the jing can be closed and held. In the new style the body posture does not turn, so the left foot is in front; yet the jing in the chest also turns smoothly following the hands and feet — this is called the internal and external as one qi (气) circulating. In the old style the body turns smoothly through half a circle, so the left foot is behind, and the body's turning arc is larger than in the new style; but it lacks the harmony of the chest jing of the new style. The new style is the back-bent elbow jing (背折肘精), its path short; the old method is the turning-silk-reeling method (转身缕法, the 缕法 being the method of leading in, 引进), its path longer. For the diagram, see the twenty-fourth posture (图见二十四势).

Qu Xiang (取象, Symbolic Imagery)

The former Gao Tan Ma took the hexagrams Shi He (噬嗑, Biting Through) and Bi (贲, Grace); this posture takes the hexagram Sui (随, Following). This means that internal and external, upper and lower must all follow the jing, and must not go against it.

Seven-Character Folk Verse (七言俚语)

Upper and lower, hands and feet each follow each other;

From rear to front, turning — do not linger.

It only depends on whether the body posture turns or not;

In striking and grappling, each has its own novelty.

(Printed page number: 266)

Trang sách 267 · scan p300

*Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3 (卷三)

Posture 58: Shi Zi Jiao (十字脚, Crossed Feet)

Jie Jie (节解, Section Explanation)

This posture corresponds to the earlier Left and Right Ca Jiao (擦脚, Rubbing Feet). It is called Crossed Feet because the hands strike to form a cross (十字) while striking with the foot.

Yin Meng (引蒙, Leading the Beginner)

When Gao Tan Ma (高探马, High Patting Horse) is finished, first step the left foot forward, slanting left, opening one diagonal step; the left hand blocks across the abdomen and is placed at the right ribs, the right hand bends, its arm laid across the top of the left arm. Then the right foot swings horizontally from left to right. The left hand moves from right to left, like a balance-beam, striking horizontally at the toes of the right foot.

Nei Jing (内精, Internal Energy)

The right hand first uses forward-winding silk-reeling jing (顺转缠丝精, shùn zhuǎn chán sī jīng), coiling from the armpit to the finger pad, settling at the left ribs with the back of the hand facing up. The left hand uses reverse-winding silk-reeling jing (倒转缠丝精), coiling to the finger pad, from below going left, rising and going right, pressing on top of the right arm. The left foot swings horizontally from left toward the right, while the left hand moves horizontally from right to left, striking the toes of the right foot. The left hand goes from right to left, striking the enemy on his left side; the right foot goes from left to right, swinging the foot horizontally to strike the enemy on his right side. If the left hand and right foot cannot strike in a favorable position, then lean in — either inward (里靠) or outward (外靠) — with the right foot first landing on the ground. The shoulder (right shoulder or left shoulder, used according to which gives you the favorable position, 得势) closes forward once (the faster, the better), striking the enemy's chest with the shoulder — this is the marvelous use of Crossed Feet. When another controls my two hands, I strike him with the lean; when I control another's two hands, leaning in from inside or outside to strike him feels even more effective and satisfying.

Diagram Labels (图注)

- 左肱屈住在上 — the left arm bends, positioned on top

- 右足面平，膝微屈 — the right sole is level; the knee slightly bent

- 眼神注于左手 — the gaze focuses on the left hand

- 身往前合，顶精领住 — the body closes forward; the crown jing (顶精) leads and holds up

- 右肱在左肱下 — the right arm is beneath the left arm

- 腰精下去，右足抬起，与大腿根平 — the waist jing sinks down; the right foot lifts level with the thigh root

- 左足平实踏地 — the left foot presses flat and solid on the ground

Diagram: a figure performing the Crossed Feet posture, arms crossed before the chest, left arm above, right arm below, right foot raised, left foot flat on the ground. (Printed page number: 267)

Trang sách 268 · scan p301

Diagram of the Internal Circulation of the Meridians in the Left and Right Hands (左右手内经运行图)

Whenever the coiling essence (chansijing 缠丝精) of left and right extends the arm outward, it coils from the shoulder and armpit all the way to the fingertips. When drawing inward and gathering, bringing the essence in, it coils from the fingernails and finger pads back to the shoulder and to the armpit.

[Diagram: a figure with both arms coiling, surrounded by six groups of labels arranged in a circle]

Labels in the diagram:

- Left hand follows the coil (shunchan 顺缠) and rises — one circle

- Right hand reverses the coil (nichan 逆缠) from the chest forward — one circle

- Right hand reverses the coil and descends — one circle

- Left hand reverses the coil and comes out — one circle

- Right hand follows the coil and comes in — one circle

- Left hand follows the coil from the chest forward — one circle

The essence of the whole body, when sent outward, is all issued from the dantian (丹田); when gathered inward, it is all gathered into the dantian. Yet all of it is governed by the mind (xin 心), and everywhere one sees the bearing of the great harmonious primal energy (taihe yuanqi 太和元气).

Taking the Image (取象)

I first strike the opponent with my right hand; he seizes my right hand and presses it against my body — this right hand has already lost its position: one difficulty. I then strike with my left hand, but he also seizes my left hand and presses it on top of my arm. The left hand has also lost its position: a second difficulty. It is not that I deliberately arrange my two hands into a cross; rather, I strike with my two hands one after another, and as the opponent controls me and puts me in straits, they form a cross shape — difficulty upon difficulty. Hence the image is taken from the hexagram Jian 蹇 (Difficulty/Obstruction). Jian means difficulty. The Yijing says: "Jian. Favorable in the southwest." Therefore the left foot steps out toward the southwest (because the southwest land is level and easy). First line: "Going meets obstruction; coming brings praise." Third line: "Going meets obstruction; coming turns back." In both, one waits for rescue with a sincere heart and guards oneself with a calm mind. At the fifth line (Nine in the fifth): "Great obstruction; friends come." Some use the foot to swing, some lean with the left, some lean with the right — countless methods; if one fails here, one succeeds there. Hence the Image says: "Great obstruction; friends come." Left and right shoulders, left and right hands and feet are all parts of the same body. Having this shared body, what is there to fear in obstruction? Top line: "Going meets obstruction; coming brings great abundance." What fortune could be greater!

Seven-Character Folk Verse (七言俚语)

Two sides cross hands to test who is stronger; above, below and all four sides can be defended. Only the "tie-across" (shuan heng 拴横 — when the opponent seizes my hand and holds it across against his chest, and I cannot move) traps my hand: like an army besieged at Gaixin, the momentum is hard to expand. Who knows that Taiji moves without fixed direction — countless methods are stored within the chest. "Mountains end, waters vanish, I doubt there is a road" — one sinking shoulder lean shatters a bronze wall. Until body meets body in the lean,

(final line continues on the next page)

[Transcription note: the original writes 脂肚, likely intended as 指肚 (finger pad); the 行 in 压在行胳膊之上 is suspected to be a scribal error. Both transcribed as printed.]

Trang sách 269 · scan p302

Five-Character Verse

Even a precious pearl can hardly give off light. The primal energy (yuanqi 元气) naturally stores the subtle secret; at the hungriest moment the hawk strikes the rabbit — watch it soar. The hawk chases, wheeling six times with the momentum; from where could the rabbit not flee in panic? Cao Cao burned his ships at Red Cliffs, Han Gaozu escaped Xingyang; extraordinary plans and extraordinary stratagems have no fixed form — all wisdom lies in using it on the spot. On the spot, one moment, victory by intelligence; preparedness averts peril — this lies in everyday practice. Everyday gongfu truly without lapse — a single point of numinous mind smells the wondrous fragrance.

(note: the verse opens mid-poem on this page; transcribed as printed)

Posture 59: Zhidang Chui 指裆捶 (Punch to the Crotch)

Sectional Explanation (节解)

[Diagram: two figures — the left in a low forward-leaning stance with the left hand reaching forward and down; the right more upright with hands at the waist]

Labels in the figures:

- The eyes fix on the enemy's crotch

- Top essence (dingjing 顶精) holds firm

- The chest closes forward, essence held

- Right hand grasps (□ unclear)

- Left foot in front, stamped down flat with force

- Right foot behind, also pressing down with force

- Crotch essence round and alive

This posture corresponds with Erqi (二起, Double Kick), Golden Rooster Standing on One Leg (金鸡独立) and Chaotian Deng (朝天蹬, Heel Kick to the Sky): the Double Kick strikes below the jaw, this one points to below the crotch — correspondence of above and below. The two Golden Rooster postures use the knee against the crotch, this uses the fist to point at the crotch — correspondence of difference and similarity. The closing is tight and rigorous, forming an elegant composition.

Introduction (引蒙)

Cross Feet (十字脚): the left foot steps forward, opening to the left. After the right foot finishes its swing, the right heel turns smoothly through more than half a circle. The face turns from the previous posture's rear; the right foot lands on the ground, the left foot takes a large step forward to the left. The left hand sweeps past the left knee and comes down behind the body, fingers pinched together, wrist facing up. Earlier, just as the left foot lands, the left hand goes from in front of the face top to bottom, sweeps behind the left ribs and rises again, turns over, comes forward top to bottom, and strikes the enemy's crotch with the fist. The crotch is a vital spot; striking it can secure victory.

[Transcription note: between the figure labels and the prose some short lines of the 节解 could not be fully captured; one character after 右手搦 in the labels is unclear, marked □.]

Trang sách 270 · scan p303

*Printed page number: 270 — Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)*

Main text

The body is like a balanced scale; once it turns with a single deflection, the heel of the left foot acts like the hub of a millstone, and turning comes easily. The left foot's rotation is "smooth-turning" force (順轉勁, shunzhuan jin); however, the left knee must be bent slightly — two or three parts — otherwise, when the right foot applies its sweeping force (擺勁, bai jin), the left foot cannot hold the stance, and the body above would twist and topple. The left foot steps forward; the left hand sweeps across past the left knee and, going backward, uses reverse coiling silk-reeling force (倒轉纏絲勁, daozhuan chansijin), coiling all the way to the fingers.

When the right foot lands, the right hand immediately uses reverse-turning force (倒轉勁, daozhuan jin) to coil diagonally to the armpit. When the right hand turns from behind and comes forward, the force beneath the armpit coils diagonally from behind the armpit to the fist head (捶頭, chuitou); the spirit of the whole body gathers in the fist. Use combining force (合勁, he jin), back of the hand facing upward, holding the force together, striking the opponent's groin. This is used when the opponent is close to one's body; at a distance it will not reach. The spirit of the whole body is all combining force.

The right hand uses the reverse-coiling method, the same as in "Brush Knee and Twist Step" (摟膝拗步, louxi aobu). But in Brush Knee Twist Step the right hand folds over from behind, arrives in front of the face, and the hand lands in front of the chest; here the right hand folds from behind to the front, diagonally toward the opponent's groin, with the combining fist striking the opponent. Though the force used is the same, the final destination is slightly different. In Brush Knee Twist Step the hand lands above, the five fingers stretched and gathered; here the hand holds a fist that lands below, hence the difference. As for the left hand, its movement uses reverse coiling, no different from Brush Knee Twist Step, so no drawing is needed.

This is a diagram facing west; west is the front. It is still within the bounds of the "sweeping foot" (擺腳, bai jiao) section.

Taking the Image (取象)

In this posture the right hand grips the fist, like "a great fruit not eaten" — hence it takes the image of Bo (剥, Splitting Apart). The previous posture was amid danger.

This posture emerges from beyond the danger; the difficulty is already resolved — hence it further takes the image of Xie (解, Deliverance). The Image says: "Deliverance — the danger stirs; by moving one escapes the danger." Deliverance is a matter of hard and soft finding the mean, whereby the difficulty resolves itself. Meeting danger in times of ease, and now returning again to ease — hence it further takes the image of Fu (復, Return). Walking the middle path, nothing fails to return. The Yi says: "On the seventh day comes return." Is this not the meaning of "when Pi (否, Standstill) reaches its extreme, Tai (泰, Peace) arrives"?

Seven-Character Folk Verses (七言俚語)

The First

Surrounded by foes, it is hard to break from the crowd;

Left arm and right foot sweep away the three armies.

Turning around, strike straight for the vital ground,

Subduing the demons, scattering them in confusion.

The Second

The body has many places of pain,

But the most painful dwells always in the groin.

To enter the tiger's den and seize the tiger's cub… *(sentence continues on the next page)*

Diagrams

- Circular diagram (center): illustrates the continuous flow of movement and energy (silk-reeling force, chansijin). Annotations around the rim:

- (upper arc): 右足踏地右手即从后转过来面向东右手即从下搂膝 — "Right foot steps down, right hand comes from behind, turn to face East, right hand brushes below past the knee"

- (inside circle): 缠丝精捶头合住精 — "Silk-reeling force, the fist head holds the force together"

- (lower arc): 左足向前开步 — "Left foot steps forward"

- (lower left): 左手从左膝搂过 — "Left hand sweeps across past the left knee"

- (lower left, lower): 倒转缠丝精缠到指 — "Reverse coiling silk-reeling force coils to the fingers"

- Stick figure (right of the circle): a figure leaning forward, right hand extended downward/forward in a striking or pressing technique.

- (vertical, above figure): 此处是身已倒转 — "Here the body has already turned"

- (vertical, right of figure): 過來面向東右手即從下摟膝

- (diagonal, near hand): 纏絲勁捶頭合住勁

Trang sách 271 · scan p304

*Printed page number: 271 — Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, Volume 3 (陳氏太極拳圖說 卷三)*

Main text

……a master can teach even the unteachable into heroes.

Explanation of the Boundaries Between the Two Postures (两势各界解)

Although below "Fist to the Groin" (指裆捶, zhidang chui) the name is "Green Dragon Emerges from the Water" (青龍出水, qinglong chushui), in truth this is a movement running through the seam between "Fist to the Groin" and the next posture, "Single Whip" (單鞭, danbian); it must not be taken as only one posture. "Fist to the Groin" is the first half of "Green Dragon Emerges from the Water," and "Green Dragon Emerges from the Water" is the second half of "Fist to the Groin" — together they form one posture. The reason "Green Dragon Emerges from the Water" is drawn separately is that its internal force arises from the farthest source: from the Pucan point (僕參, on the heel) it travels against the current upward, over the back to the Fufen point (附分), and on to the right fingers — hence it is drawn separately.

The Fifty-Ninth Posture: Green Dragon Emerges from the Water ① (the road after Fist to the Groin)

It closely corresponds with "Fair Lady Works the Shuttles" (玉女穿梭, yunü chuansuo). Its right hand's smooth turning (順轉, shunzhuan) matches the left hand's reverse turning; its level leaping method is likewise the same. But "Fair Lady Works the Shuttles" makes a great turn of the body, while this one does not turn; it corresponds at a distance with the two gatherings of the seventh and ninth postures.

The force of the left and right hands is entirely the same. But there, the hands gather from afar to the front of the chest; here, one leaps from near to far — one gathering, one releasing, responding across the distance.

Explanation by Sections (节解)

The left arm is bent and held; the left hand falls to the right ribs; crown force (頂勁, ding jin) leads the foot; the chest closes forward; the right shoulder sinks down.

Guidance for Beginners (引蒙)

Below "Fist to the Groin," "Green Dragon Emerges from the Water" follows immediately. In the seam between the two postures, first sink the right shoulder; the right half of the body sinks with it; once the foot has gone down, rise again and leap forward. Before the leap, the right fist is like the tassel of a rope whip: to strike forward, first gather backward. Then……

……the right hand goes forward. It lands, therefore the knee bends; the spirit of the whole body……

Diagram Labels

Left of the figure:

- 左胳膊屈住，左手落右（脅） — "Left arm bent; left hand falls to the right ribs"

- 右肩松下 — "Right shoulder sinks down"

- 頂勁領足，胸向前合 — "Crown force leads the foot; the chest closes forward"

Right of the figure:

- 眼神注于右手 — "The gaze fixes on the right hand"

- 右胳膊微屈一二分，不可太直，亦不可太彎 — "The right arm bends slightly, one or two parts; not too straight, nor too bent"

- 右手將所攛之捶展開，手束住指 — "The right hand opens out the fist it flung; the hand gathers the fingers in"

Below the figure:

- 左足隨右足向前飛縱 — "The left foot flies forward following the right foot"

- 裆中會陰、長強，勁隨頂勁上提 — "The Huiyin and Changqiang points in the groin: force rises with the crown force"

- 縱如靈貓撲鼠，純是精神，又虛又靈 — "Leap like a clever cat pouncing on a mouse: pure spirit, both empty and nimble"

- 此膝是右足足心向前縱，足始落地，故屈膝，全身精神皆右手前去 — "This knee: the right foot leaps forward with the sole, the foot just landing, hence the bent knee; the whole body's spirit goes forward with the right hand"

Footnote

① The original edition numbered this as the sixtieth posture; based on the preceding "Explanation of the Boundaries Between the Two Postures" — "Fist to the Groin is the first half of Green Dragon Emerges from the Water; Green Dragon Emerges from the Water is the second half of Fist to the Groin; together they form one posture" — the original "sixtieth posture" has been corrected to the fifty-ninth.

Trang sách 272 · scan p305

(The leap-forward / strike instruction)

Rising from behind, it circles forward in one great arc and strikes out; the body likewise follows with a forward leap (纵 zòng). The secret of the leap: the hand in front leads forward, while at the rear the Yinbai, Dadun, Lidui, Qiaoyin and Xiaxi points of the right foot all use jing (精 refined force). The jing travels from the sole through the Yongquan point, up over the heel, ascending against the usual course past Weizhong, Yinmen, Chengfu and Huantiao, slanting in along the side of the body (扶边), rising past Hunmen and Pohu to Fufen, then slanting upward again, by Quyuan past Xiaohai, slanting into Zhigou and Yangchi, twisting along the way; the hand opens out, the five fingers gathered, and the right hand leads the body leaping forward, the left foot pushing hard down against the ground. All follows the right hand forward; the left hand, following the body, lands in front of the right breast when the front foot touches down, and stops.

Nei jing (internal essence)

The whole right half of the body uses right-turning jing (right turn is the compliant/顺 turn, turning from inside outward). The right hand uses coiling essence (缠丝精 chansijing), rising from the armpit, from inside to outside, coiling aslant to the finger pads. The right foot likewise uses coiling essence, coiling compliantly to the thigh root, rising to meet the side of the body and ascending together, reaching Fufen and branching to the armpit, coiling aslant to the finger pads. The left hand and left foot must use reverse-turning essence before they can follow the right hand and right foot in their circle. The root of the forward leap lies wholly in the heart (心 xin). When the heart's essence is raised, the essence at the crown (顶) holds up top, the dantian essence in the middle is sent upward, inclining to the right half of the body. Below, of the two feet the right uses the springing method, its sole pressing hard backward. Before the leap all is storing essence — gathering essence and concentrating spirit, binding the qi (气 qi) together. At the moment of leaping it is purely a forward pounce, essence going straight ahead. The right hand turns and advances together, like a sparrowhawk pouncing on a quail, an eagle seizing a cunning [hare]... [end of page partially illegible]

Diagram

One figure: the forward-leap posture — right foot pushing off behind, body pouncing forward, right hand reaching out, left hand gathered before the right breast.

Trang sách 273 · scan p306

Posture 60: Dan Bian (单鞭, Single Whip)

This is the seventh Single Whip; it ties together the preceding six Single Whips, like "return comes after seven days" (七日来复, Hexagram Fu) — the composition is rigorous.

Seven-character folk verses (七言俚语)

First:

The dragon in the water nourishes its true self; like an inchworm it first bends in order to stretch. A sudden clap of thunder in the sky — the leaping in the pool is doubly spirited.

Second:

Turning the fist, hanging and advancing, enter without delay; like a dragon leaving the water, parting from the spring pool. Leaping through the air it flies up to heaven, with five-colored auspicious clouds following behind (the five-colored clouds are a metaphor for the whole body).

Section explanation (节解) — diagram labels

Upper row (right to left):

- Left five fingers bound together (左五指束住)

- Left elbow sinks down

- Left shoulder relaxed down; the eyes

- fix on the tip of the left middle finger

- Essence at the crown holds up (顶精领住)

- Right shoulder relaxed down; the ear

- listens behind

- Right elbow sinks down

- Right hand's five fingers pinched together

Lower row (right to left):

- Chest closes forward

- Knees bent

- Left foot, like the character "eight," angled out and flat on the ground

- Crotch open and rounded

- Right leg open in a circle

- Right foot slightly bent, two or three [tenths]

- pushing backward

- The essence of both sides of the waist sinks down

Diagram

Single Whip final posture: facing forward, left hand extended with fingers bound together, right hand pinched and extended to the right rear; left foot angled out flat, right foot slightly bent pushing back; knees bent, crotch rounded, chest closing forward, crown essence held up, waist essence sinking.

Trang sách 274 · scan p307

Yin meng (引蒙, Guiding the Beginners)

The two forearms with the left and right hands, and the two thighs with the left and right feet, first close from the outside inward. Then the left hand extends from beside the right ribs toward the left, the fingers bound together. The left foot likewise steps out from right to left, moving along its path — this is the "slow curve" posture (慢弯势). The right hand turns from behind through a small circle forward, the fingers pinched together, and joins with the left hand. When the two hands join, the whole upper body joins; the right foot hooks toward the left, and the two feet close with the two knees. Then the essence of the crotch (裆) naturally opens round. The remaining methods are as before.

Nei jing (internal essence)

The left and right hands first join; their essence is all coiling essence (缠丝精 chansijing), coiling aslant from the shoulders inward to the fingernails. Then the left hand first circles a small circle from below upward, then slowly, in a slow curve, moves left. Extend the forearm, open the fingers, bind the fingers — do not let them scatter. Using coiling essence from inside outward, coiling aslant to the finger pads — this is the compliant (顺) turning circle. The right hand turns from behind to the front, also through a small circle, using coiling essence in reverse, coiling aslant from the shoulder inward to the fingernails. When the two feet join, all is reverse coiling: from the toes, from outside inward, ascending against the course, coiling aslant to the thigh root. After joining, the left foot follows the left hand, turns a small compliant circle, then steps out left in a slow curve. Its essence goes from the thigh root, inside to outside, descending and coiling aslant to the toes, placed in the shape of the character "eight"; Dadun and Pucan must press firmly on the ground. The right foot hooks in front; upper and lower body alike gather the essence from outside inward, and only then does it not scatter.

Qu xiang (取象, Taking the Image)

Above is empty, like Li (离, Fire); therefore take Li. Below is full, like Kan (坎, Water); therefore take Kan. Kan and Li are the middle son and middle daughter of Qian and Kun (乾坤); water and fire intersect, and return still to Qian and Kun. Qian and Kun are of the ten thousand things... [continues on next page]

(No diagram on this page.)

Trang sách 275 · scan p308

(Conclusion of Qu xiang from previous page) ... The surfaces of the thighs and forearms seem wound with silk. Slanting coiling, compliant and reverse, each has its fixed rule — it well repays close, deep study. When genuine practice has accumulated long, one day it all opens suddenly into taiji quan. Every part of the human body is taiji; in every movement and every stillness there is wholeness. If you wish to fathom the source images of that wholeness — the bright moon of the third and fifth [days] just full, shining over the ten thousand things of the world; nothing escapes before the eyes and ears. To seize or release is all up to me; every turn is the Way — awaken at the fountainhead.

Posture 61: Pu Di Jin (铺地锦, Brocade Spreading over the Ground), also called Pu Di Ji (铺地鸡)

Section explanation of Pu Di Ji, first half of Shang Bu Qi Xing (七星捶, Seven-Star Fist)

Diagram labels

- Right elbow bent like the Dipper (右肘屈住如斗)

- The ear listens behind

- Crown essence holds up; the right hand grips the fist (搦捶)

- The eyes fix on the left hand and left foot

- The calf rests on the ground

- Right leg bent, knee facing up

- Lifting upward, waist bent forward; when the body rises the knee goes up, heel exerting force

- Right foot flat on the ground, awaiting the body

- The hip bone (髀骨) sits down; the Huiyin point below, lifted upward

- Left foot's Pucan rests on the ground; as the body is about to rise the toes close forward and Pucan exerts force — only then can one rise

Diagram

Pu Di Ji (low posture): one leg crouched bent, one leg straight along the ground; right hand gripping fist, elbow bent like the Dipper above, left hand below; waist bent forward, in a low crouch about to rise.

Explanation

The first half of Shang Bu Qi Xing (Stepping Up to the Seven Stars) is called Pu Di Ji; the second half is called Qi Xing Chui (Seven-Star Fist), the completed posture being like Jin Gang Dao Dui (金刚捣碓, Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Mortar). Why "Seven-Star Fist"? Because the left and right hands and feet form the shape of the Big Dipper, it is named Seven-Star Fist. It is not named Jin Gang Dao Dui because there the left hand moves from below upward, whereas here the left hand is bent and on top, shaped like the Northern Dipper. Hence it is not Jin Gang Dao Dui but Shang Bu Qi Xing Chui.

Trang sách 276 · scan p309

Yin meng (Guiding the Beginners)

The right fist and forearm are bent and held; the body sits down to the ground; the left hand and left arm open out. The left leg extends, calf resting on the ground, heel resting on the ground. The right knee is bent, held at the right of the body. The five toes grip the ground; the big toe and rear heel both exert force.

Nei jing (internal essence)

When the body is about to rise, the right hand uses compliant-turning essence (顺转精), coiling aslant from the hand to the armpit, from the armpit up to the shoulder, down the back to the right waist, from the waist to the left thigh, using reverse-coiling essence to the toes of the left foot — the reverse of Qing Long Chu Shui (青龙出水, Azure Dragon Leaves the Water), where essence runs from foot to hand; here it runs from the right hand to the left foot. The left hand uses forward-charging essence.

Diagram labels

Left diagram (top to bottom):

- Sitting down from here, the left leg cannot extend and the right knee cannot bend

- A straight line above and below — the body must not sit down to the ground from here

- At such a time the thigh sits back to the ground

- Only then can the left thigh extend and the right knee bend

- Body down

Right diagram (top to bottom):

- Right knee leads upward, heel exerting force

- Diagram of the legs, lower left and right

- Left knee faces up

- For the stepping-up foot, the right foot draws level

- Left foot

- with the heel exerting force together — only then can the body rise

- Lift the right knee; heel and toes of the left foot

- right foot closes inward

- calf rests on the ground

- initial sitting

Caption: The left diagram is of rising after sitting; its use of essence is as described. The right diagram is of the thigh first landing, before the body rises; the right one comes first, the left one after.

Echoes with other postures

This posture echoes Die Cha (跌岔, Falling and Splitting). In Die Cha one drops straight through the air, the right foot stamping the ground with a sound like metal or stone, to stamp the enemy's foot; the left foot kicks the enemy's shin bone (臁骨) and can break his daring; the right hand opens the arm, grips the ground and rises, the left hand charges forward to push the enemy's chest. Here instead the thigh sits back, sitting on the enemy's knee; the right fist is bent with the intent of striking forward. The left leg extends; if victory fails, both hands press to the ground to the right and use the sweeping whip (扫堂鞭) to sweep the shins of the crowd of enemies, and the difficulty dissolves of itself. So it echoes its kin. It also echoes Jin Ji Du Li (金鸡独立, Golden Rooster Stands on One Leg): there the left leg stands vertical, here it lies horizontal; there the right knee knees the enemy, here likewise the right knee bends and holds; there the left hand hangs down and the right forearm rises, here the right hand is bent and the left hand charges upward. Hence they echo above and below. It also echoes Er Qi (二起, Double Kick): there the body flies into mid-air, here the body drops to the ground — so again they echo above and below.

Pu Di Ji: the chicken is hot-tempered, its skin burning; it wants to lie on damp earth to cool its skin.

Trang sách 277 · scan p310

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo, Volume 3 (陳氏太極拳圖說 卷三)

Text

[It crouches] on the ground, one wing spread out, one leg stretched open. In the human body the left hand and right arm extend in the same way — hence the name [of this posture, "Chicken Paving the Ground" 铺地鸡/铺地锦, pùdì jī].

Correspondence with the trigram (取象 qǔxiàng)

Xun 巽 corresponds to the chicken. The chicken is naturally combative; when it fights it spreads its wings — the left and right hands resemble this. A hen brooding eggs loves to settle its body sitting on the ground; the form is similar, hence the image is taken from Xun. In the human body Xun corresponds to the thigh (股 gǔ). Xun means "entering" (入 rù). The hips and thighs sit on the ground, the left thigh spread open on the ground, the whole body sunk down upon the earth, like falling into a pit. "In the bed beneath" of the Kan–Xun lines refers to the ground. The chicken paves the ground, the body lying on the earth — just as Xun lies beneath the wood/forest. Of the first line (初爻 chūyáo): "It furthers one to see the great man" — here, "useful to the armed man" (利用武人). The left hand extends, the right hand bends — the image of the warrior. Hence this image is taken.

Seven-character folk verses (七言俚语 qīyán líyǔ)

First

未被人推身落地，如何下体坐尘埃，

下惊上取君须记，颔下得珠逞奇才。（此说到七星捶）

Not yet pushed by another, the body falls to the ground —

how can the lower body sit in the dust?

"Startled below, take from above" — you must remember this;

seizing the pearl beneath the chin displays extraordinary skill. (This speaks of the Seven-Star Punch, 七星捶 qīxīng chuí.)

Second

曩时跌岔甚无情（以足蹬臁），此又落尘令人惊。

人知扫腿防不住，岂料七星耀玉衡。

In former times the Falling Split (跌岔 diēchà) was ruthless (the foot kicks the shin),

and now this falling into the dust startles people again.

Others know to guard against the sweeping leg, yet cannot,

Who would expect the Seven Stars to shine upon the Jade Balance (玉衡 Yùhéng)?

[No diagram on this page; the text discusses the image (取象) of the posture "Chicken Paving the Ground" from the trigram Xun 巽.]
Trang sách 278 · scan p311

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo, Volume 3

Posture 61: Shang Bu Qi Xing Chui 上步七星捶 ("Step Forward, Seven Stars"), ending with Jingang Dao Dui 金刚捣碓 ("Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounds the Pestle")

Yinmeng 引蒙 (elementary instruction)

The left hand thrusts forward, circles upward one full turn, and settles in front of the chest. The fingers bend slightly, the wrist faces upward. The right hand travels from the rear forward and upward as well, also turning one large circle in the normal direction, clenching the fist and settling into the middle of the left wrist. The left foot closes slightly inward; the jing 精 at the crown of the head lifts once. In the lower body, the right knee and the right heel area (仆参 púcān region of the ankle) press together with the left heel with unified force; lifting upward, the body rises and the right foot steps — advancing from rear to front, also turning one circle upward and settling down, level with the left foot.

Neijing 内精 (internal essence)

When the body rises, use the jing inside the body, the same as in the previous three Jingang Dao Dui: in every case the qi 气 must return to the dantian 丹田, the heart calm and the qi harmonious, attaining the original image of Taiji 太极.

Quxiang 取象 (correspondence)

The Seven-Star Fist (七星捶 qīxīng chuí) takes the same image as the previous three Jingang Dao Dui. But the former took "the One trunk scattering into the ten thousand branches," while this one takes "the ten thousand branches returning to the One trunk." As the Zhongyong 中庸 says at its beginning: Heaven's decree (天命) scatters through the middle and ends in the ten thousand affairs. It says Heaven on high has no sound and no smell — the meaning is the same; only thus can the whole pattern be gathered to a close.

Diagram labels

- Thigh seemingly straight yet not straight; chest closes forward; right knee bends one or two [inches]

- Right shoulder releases down

- Right elbow sinks down

- Waist jing sinks down

- Left thigh seemingly straight yet not straight

- Left and right feet tread flat and even

- Crotch jing separates — otherwise it is not [correct]

- Crown jing holds up, level

[Diagram: line drawing of a standing figure, left foot forward, right foot behind; left hand raised forward-upward with slightly bent fingers and wrist up, right fist settled into the left wrist. Head erect, torso upright, knees slightly bent.]
Trang sách 279 · scan p312

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tushuo, Volume 3

Seven-character folk verse, third

脚踢拳打下乘拳，妙手无处不浑然。

任他四围皆是敌，此身一动悉颠连。

我身无处非太极，无心成化如珠圆。

遭着何处何处击，我亦不知玄又玄。

总是此心归无极，炼到佛家一朵莲。

功夫到此仍不息，从心所欲莫非天。

Foot kicks, fist strike — that is the lower-grade boxing;

the wondrous hand is nowhere but in seamless wholeness.

Let enemies surround me on all four sides —

one movement of this body topples them all in a chain.

No place in my body is not Taiji 太极;

without deliberate mind it transforms like a round pearl.

Wherever I am met, there I strike,

and I myself know not how deep the mystery runs.

All comes of this heart returning to Wuji 无极,

refined until it is a single lotus of the Buddha.

When gongfu 功夫 reaches this point yet does not cease,

following the heart's desire, nothing is not of Heaven.

Posture 62: Xia Bu Kua Hu 下步跨虎 ("Step Back, Riding the Tiger")

It corresponds to Lou Xi Ao Bu 搂膝拗步 ("Brush Knee, Twisting Step"). In Brush Knee Twisting Step the right hand is in front and the left hand behind; here the right hand is above and the left below. There the stance is wide and crossed (拗); here the stance is withdrawn and bound (束) — the two answer each other by opposition.

Jiejie 节解 (section analysis) — Yinmeng 引蒙

The right hand and left hand divide horizontally from in front of the chest and descend. The right hand turns one circle backward from front to rear; turning forward, the arm lies across and settles above the fontanel (囟门 xìnmén). The left hand divides downward, also turning one circle backward, the elbow rounding and rolling behind the body. The right foot retreats one large step, the knee bends, the foot treads flat on the ground. The left foot also retreats one step, set crosswise a foot (尺 chǐ) apart. When the foot lands, the toes press the ground, the knee bends; the crown jing 精 lifts upward, the crotch jing sinks downward — above and below contend for the jing in mutual tension. In between, the chest closes forward, the hips and thighs kick up toward the rear. The waist jing sinks down, the lower abdomen closes forward, and the face lifts to look at the middle finger of the right hand.

Diagram labels

- Right elbow bends and suspends above the head

- Right hand fingers gathered; the eyes' spirit fixed on the right hand

- Crown jing lifts up, fully raised

- Left hand behind, gathered (撮); fingers and wrist face upward, elbow bent rounding, like mounting the tiger

- Toes press the ground, hips and thighs kick up

- Waist jing sinks, knee bends, toes

- Crotch rounds open

- Crotch sinks, foot down

- Chest closes forward, right knee bent, right foot treads flat

[Diagram: Kua Hu "Riding the Tiger" posture — right elbow bent with hand suspended above the crown, left hand gathered behind with wrist up and elbow rounding; right foot flat with knee bent, left toes touching the ground, hips kicked up rearward, face tilted up watching the right middle finger.]
Trang sách 280 · scan p313

Nei Jing Left-and-Right Hand-and-Foot Diagram (內經左右手足圖, Nèijīng zuǒyòu shǒuzú tú)

Paragraph 1:

In this posture, the method of lowering the body: the smaller the better. Yet if the crotch (dang, 裆) does not open wide, the body will not sink. On the right arm it is as if a thousand-jin heavy weight presses on the crown; the left and right arms are square outside and round within; upper and lower, the spirit gathers together, all using embracing-closing jin (baohe jing). Above is empty, below is substantial; yet the substantial part must be worked with emptiness — only emptiness is agile; being agile, when things come one accords with them smoothly, and there is naturally no obstruction.

Paragraph 2:

In this posture there are ten faults easily seen:

1. The left arm must not be straight; if straight it cannot protect the head and jaw.

2. The left hand is behind; if it cannot close and hold the jin, the echo-response cannot watch over one another.

3. If the left and right feet are too close, the crotch does not open.

4. If the feet are known to be spread wide, yet the figure-eight (ren-character) crotch does not change, the body is made unable to sink.

5. If one forcibly stamps the foot downward without the crown jin leading, forcing the crotch open — forcing makes stiff, stiff makes dead-fixed; dead-fixed means no liveliness; no liveliness means no agility; no agility makes turning dull.

6. The crown jin may be known to lead upward, but if the left and right thighs have not used coiling silk jin (chansi jin, 纏絲精) to prop open and close, close and prop open, then although it is called "open," the crotch has opened only a crack of a seam — it cannot make the crotch like the mouth of a dipper, stable as Mount Tai.

7. The whole body's spirit lies entirely in the eyes; where the gaze fixes is where the spirit gathers. The right hand rises in peng (shang peng), the left hand closes behind; the two arms propped round — only then does it count as one posture. If one moves the hands muddleheadedly without using the mind, once the mind is unused, the spirit has nowhere to tend and cannot consolidate, lost in scattering.

8. (continuing) If the waist jin cannot sink down, the qi cannot return to the dantian (qì guī dāntián). If the qi does not return to the dantian, then zhongji and huiyin become frivolous. Because there is no horizontal qi filling the chest to repletion, then behind the back...

*(Text continues on the next page.)*

Labels within the figure: 胸 (chest), 背 (back); 左手胸平分 (left hand level with chest), 右手胸平分 (right hand level with chest).

Caption between diagrams: 背在後書法不得不如此 — the back is behind; the writing method cannot but be like this.
