Trang sách 281 · scan p314

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說, Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan), Volume 3 — page 281.

Daodao (陶道), Shenzhu (身柱) and Lingtai (靈台) [points on the back]; the transverse qi on left and right is likewise all filled and sufficient, yet front and back are both sluggish and obstructed. For if one does not close forward, the fault is leaning back; if one closes forward, then the crotch essence (dang jing, 襠精) becomes light and floating, the soles of the feet unsteady, and the upper body is no longer lively and responsive — this is the eighth fault. If the crown essence (ding jing, 頂精) is led too far, the head is suspended too high; if it cannot be raised, the body collapses — this comes from not settling the waist essence (yao jing, 腰精) and crotch essence, so the body is not under its own control — the ninth fault. Once crotch and waist essence are both well settled below, if the thighs and hips still cannot rise, not only can the front crotch not close, but even the upper body cannot be locked together. If upper and lower cannot lock the essence together, the soles have no strength, and anything outside can topple me — such is the tenth fault. With these ten faults, how could the whole body, above and below and on every side, be correctly formed everywhere, and lively everywhere? Not only can it not be correct and lively; moreover all sorts of strange ailments arise in profusion. At that point, even a good physician cannot remedy it — through the depth of accumulated faults one enters a wrong path. How lamentable!

Question: in performing this posture (shi, 勢), what constitutes a correct form? The two hands in front part evenly from the chest, one going right and one going left. For the right hand going right: use the essence of parting (pi jing, 披精) from above downward to separate. The right hand uses the reverse coiling method (dao chan fa, 倒纏法) to coil to the Jianyu point (肩髃). This is the hand going from above downward behind the right ribs — this is the first half-circle. Then from below and behind, move upward, bend the arm (gu, 肱), and let the hand fall level with the head five or six inches from the fontanelle (xin men, 囟門). Spread the hand and bind the fingers; when bound, the heart-mind gathers in. The little finger's outer side faces up, the wrist faces outward, the back of the hand faces inward; using the reverse-coiling essence coil back again from the Jianyu, coiling obliquely to the side of the five fingers — this is the second half-circle of the right hand; combining them makes one great circle. The arm on top, its shape like a moth's eyebrow (emei, 蛾眉) — this is the form of the right hand.

The left hand parts down from the chest, using coiling-silk essence (chansi jing, 纏絲精) in reverse coiling to the shoulder. Then the left hand turns from behind outside the left ribs, forward and again backward, falling behind the left ribs; its essence again coils from the shoulder in reverse to the fingers. The five fingers are held together in a cluster, the arm bent and propped round; the left hand and right hand close together, their essences echoing each other — this is the form of the left hand.

The two eyes fix their spirit on the fingernails of the right hand; where the eyes fix, there too is the heart-mind, giving the spirit a place to return — this is the form of the gaze (yan shi shi, 眼視式).

Raise the crown essence. Leading the crown essence does not mean stiffly tensing the two great tendons behind the head at the nape; it means lifting the central qi (zhong qi, 中氣) upward. As if intending, as if not intending, neither light nor heavy, seemingly present seemingly absent; one point of sudden alertness in the heart, the essence flowing to the back of the crown. One must not lift too far, nor fall short. Too far and the body hangs suspended above; too little and the qi stays in the chest, hard to send down — this is the form of the crown essence (ding jing shi, 頂精式). The crown must be lively; when lively, turning left and right becomes easy — this is the crown form.

The ears listen left and right and behind, fearing unexpected assault. If someone comes from behind, there must first be a sound; one can hear the sound — a sound is naturally different from silence. Therefore with the heart calm and the qi still, the ears are naturally sharp — this is the form of the two ears (er shi, 耳式).

The two shoulders must constantly relax and sink; on seeing them rise, immediately relax them down. Yet when they must unavoidably rise, let them rise; once the rising is finished, relax at once. If not relaxed, the whole arm cannot change nimbly; therefore rise when rising is fitting, relax when relaxing is fitting. When each posture finishes, the chest closes forward and the two shoulders echo each other — this is the form of the two shoulders (jian shi, 肩式).

The two elbows should sink down; if not sunk, the shoulders rise, and they are unfit for use. This posture alone is otherwise: here the right elbow is bent on top, propping the forearm upward, horizontally pushing up. If elbow and arm do not push up, they cannot hold up what is above. The left elbow, with the back bent, props against the right… (text continues on the next page)

Trang sách 282 · scan p315

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說) — page 282.

…[elbows] echoing each other — this is the form of the left and right elbows (zhou shi, 肘式). The strength of the right hand's five fingers all pours into the little finger's side (xiao zhi jia, 小指胛), holding up and propping upward. When strength is led from this place, then the elbow and the upper and lower arm both exert strength. The left hand is behind, fingers clustered, wrist turned upward, so that one's finger cannot be seized by another and bent backward. And when the fingers are clustered, it also shows the heart-mind gathering in. The two hands, one up one down, one front one back, echoing as one breath — this is the form of the two hands (shou shi, 手式).

From the waist up: behind, the Pohu (魄戶) and Gaohuang (膏肓) points close toward the front of the ribs. On the chest and flanks: the first row — Yuanye (淵腋) and Dabao (大包) belong to the Triple Burner (san jiao, 三焦). The second row — Zhejin (輒筋) and Riyue (日月) also belong to Lesser Yang Triple Burner. The third row — Yunmen (雲門), Zhongfu (中府), Shidou (食竇), Xiongxiang (胸鄉) belong to lung and spleen. The fourth row — Jianyin/Houyin row (頷陰), Qimen (期門), Tianchi (天池) belong to liver and gallbladder. The fifth row — Yangming large intestine's Quepen (缺盆), Qihu (氣戶), Liangmen (梁門), Guanmen (關門) belong to intestine and stomach. The sixth row — Lesser Yin's Shufu (腧府), Shencang (神藏), Youmen (幽門), Tonggu (通谷/通骨) belong to heart and kidney. The middle row — Huagai (華蓋), Zigong (紫宮), Yutang (玉堂), Danzhong (膻中), Zhongting (中庭), Jiuwei (鳩尾). The flanks on both sides from Yuanye and Dabao down to Youmen and Tonggu all close toward Yutang and Danzhong; the flanks left and right all echo each other — this is the form of the two flanks above the waist.

From the waist down: left and right Qichong (氣衝) and Weidao (維道) all close toward Qihai (氣海), Guanyuan (關元), Zhongji (中極) — this is the form of the two soft flanks below (ruan xie xia shi, 軟脅下式). The flesh of the two thighs and buttocks rises upward; if it does not rise, the front crotch cannot close. Below the soft ribs is the waist; if the waist essence (yao jing, 腰精) does not sink down, knees and feet have no strength. Inside the thigh at Huantiao (環跳), the bone closes inward; if it does not close, the two thighs lose themselves in scattering — these are the forms of three places: the waist, the buttocks, and the bone inside Huantiao.

The transverse qi (heng qi, 橫氣) in the chest descends and returns to the dantian (丹田, that is, Qihai 氣海); the qi of the dantian gathers at the perineum (yin, 陰); the transverse qi accumulates there and the hard qi transforms into soft qi. When the heart-mind does not move, this qi is always still; when the heart's qi issues forth, this qi rises to assist the heart's qi. If this qi is well used, it becomes the central qi (zhong qi, 中氣); if ill used, it becomes transverse qi. There are not two kinds of qi: the soft yet powerful is the central qi; what is merely hard is transverse qi. As for its function: impartial, not leaning, without excess or deficiency — that is the *function* of the central qi, not its *substance*. The substance of the central qi is the correct qi of yin and yang within my heart — what Mencius called the qi matched with the Way and righteousness, the flood-like qi (haoran zhi qi, 浩然之氣).

Here, the qi of the dantian below the chest: thus with not a single thing in the heart, utterly empty and responsive (xu ling, 虛靈); the moment anything is grasped at, it is neither empty nor responsive. Only with stillness sustain it, nourish its sincerity, until in motion and stillness all is fitting and transformation is unfathomable — this is the form of the heart-mind (xin zhi shi, 心之式).

As for the crotch: the qi of the upper body gathered above the testicles (luan, 卵) sinks straight down — this is what the vulgar call the "thousand-jin drop" (qian jin zhui, 千斤墜), utterly substantial! When not used, the substantial transforms back into empty — this is called "moving the substantial into the empty"; if not empty, above and below are both unresponsive. At both sides of the perineum the roots of the two great thighs prop apart; if they prop apart one inch here, the two knees open a foot by themselves. This posture should open two inches, yet what opens must be empty, must not trespass into the substantial: once it touches the substantial, turning becomes unresponsive. Yet where they open, the roots of the two legs are all closing essence (he jing, 合精); the thighs rise far, the lower abdomen closes forward, and the crotch opens of itself. One who is good at opening the crotch: even a single thread's width of opening may be called open, because it is empty and round, both sides closing together while the middle opens wide. One who is bad at opening the crotch: the crotch like the character 人, narrow above and wide below, neither empty nor round — though it looks open, it cannot be called open! This is the form of the crotch's middle (dang zhong shi, 襠中式). The two thighs close in front and open behind, close outside and open inside, paired against each other, echoing… (continues on next page)

Trang sách 283 · scan p316

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Volume 3 — page 283.

…[the two thighs] responding to each other — this is the form of the thighs (da tui shi, 大腿式). Both kneecaps close inward; both knees are bent, with a space of a bit over a foot held open between them — this is the form of the knees (xi zhi shi, 膝之式). The outer shins (wai lian, 外臁) of both lower legs close their essence toward the inner shins, paired facing each other — this is the form of the two lower legs (liang xiao tui shi, 兩小腿式).

As for the two feet: the right foot treads flat, like earth settled on the ground; the left foot is planted on its point, like an awl driven into the earth. The right foot is flat and substantial; the left foot is firm yet empty. What is empty conceals the momentum of the lowered posture; the toes and the outer edge of the foot both exert strength closing inward, and the heels both press heavily on the ground — this is the form of the two feet (liang zu zhi shi, 兩足之式).

As for the lower body: both feet use coiling-silk essence (chansi jing, 纏絲精) in reverse coiling, moving against the flow upward, from the toes past the Yongquan point (湧泉) to the outer edge of the foot, coiling from outside inward; the coil reaches the roots of the two thighs and enters the dantian (丹田) — this is the use-of-essence form of the lower body (xia ti yong jing shi, 下體用精式).

So minute a posture: its arising is compared to that [hexagram], and its established rules are these. From head to foot each part has its fixed form. If one can truly strive to remove the faults, transform oneself into the midst of the compass and square, then go beyond the compass and square — following the compass and square yet not confined by them — then one has attained it.

Taking the Image (Qu Xiang, 取象)

The right arm dwells above, like the top line of Li (離, Fire ☲). Within, the emptiness and brightness of the heart is like the empty middle of Li. Below, the essence of the dantian is full and the soles of the feet exert strength, like the bottom line of Li; therefore the image is taken from Li.

Seven-Character Vernacular Verses (Qi Yan Li Yu, 七言俚語)

*The First*

Two hands part evenly, hips and tailbone floating; the small body crouches, curled like a gibbon (yuan nao, 猿猱). The right hand holds up the mountain's ridge pressing down; the left arm strides below, the tiger's body firm. The crotch root opens wide, the posture of jade tablets (gui bi, 圭璧); the eyes gaze up at the high fingernails. One foot substantial, one empty, the feet differ; with one turn of the body, all the youngsters submit.

*The Second*

Mount Tai (比喻 a strong enemy) presses on the eggs (比喻 the hand), holding the upper stream (meaning: above the head). Qian (乾) miscast becomes Kun (坤), bearing the globe of the earth (Li is the middle line of Qian; changed from Qian, which is the father of Li — hence speaking of Li must go back to Qian). Another change of the middle line of the Qian hexagram: the doubled Li's fire blazes on the emerald peak.

Trang sách 284 · scan p317

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說) — page 284.

Posture 63: Front Half-Turn of the Body, Sweeping the Foot (前半勢轉身擺腳)

This posture echoes the previous Sweeping the Foot, but its transition between what precedes and what follows differs in momentum; the middle is the same.

Diagram labels

Right palm faces back, arm in a slow-bending posture (右掌朝後，胳膊慢彎勢)

Right elbow props outward (右肘向外撐住)

Ears listen behind the body (耳聽身後)

Crown essence held in lead (頂精領住)

Eyes look toward the chest (眼視胸前)

Left hand falls in front of the right breast (左手落右乳前)

Left knee slightly bent (左膝微屈)

Foot treads flat on the ground (足平踏地)

Right leg raised on the body's left, foot level with the thigh root (右腿抬起在身左，足與腿根平)

Guiding the Uninitiated (Yin Meng, 引蒙)

The previous posture's step is Straddling the Tiger (kua hu, 跨虎): the right hand is held up over the head, back of the hand facing up. The right arm seems motionless yet moves — moving without moving — turning backward with the body. The left hand is behind, also gradually rising, also turning with the body. The left foot steps out toward the northwest; the right foot turns back with the body, opening one large backward step, landing to the northwest of the left foot. The left and right arms also spread out toward the northwest; the hand spreads open and binds the five fingers together; the two hands are level with the breasts, and the right leg lifts toward the southeast, foot level with the thigh root. Then the right foot moves horizontally through the air from south to north, while the left and right hands sweep horizontally from north to south. When the sweeping of the right foot is finished, the right foot lands in its original place; the left and right hands pour down from the south, not stopping until the northwest; from behind to the front they turn one great circle, falling in front of the chest.

Trang sách 285 · scan p318

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說) — page 285.

The left hand is in front, the right hand behind, both fists grasped together (nuo, 搦); close the chest, close the crotch; the feet left and right do not move, the knees are bent.

Internal Essence (Nei Jing, 內精)

The left hand turns around from behind; its essence rises from the Riyue point (日月) up to the shoulder, using the forward coiling method (shun chan fa, 順纏法) in front, coiling obliquely to the hand. The right hand uses reverse-turning coiling-silk essence (dao zhuan chanshi jing, 倒轉纏絲精), coiling from the shoulder and back, outside to inside, until it reaches the fist (chuitou, 捶頭). The left leg uses forward-coiling essence, coiling from the toes up to the leg root, returning to the dantian (丹田) and descending into the crotch. The right leg uses reverse-coiling essence, rising from the toes, coiling to the leg root, returning to the crotch.

Seven-Character Vernacular Verse (七言俚語)

The right hand props upward, the body turns back; first release the right arm and yield to the hero. Then strike both hands to the left; the left foot sweeps across and seizes the very work of creation (duo hua gong, 奪化工).

Posture 64: Cannon Overhead (Dang Tou Pao, 當頭炮)

This completed posture takes this [movement] as its head, joined with the Sweeping Foot (bai jiao, 擺腳) as one posture. It is called Cannon Overhead because first one strikes at the opponent before the face with the fist.

Analysis of the Sections (Jie Jie, 節解) — diagram labels

Waist essence sinks down; without the waist essence sinking, the soles have no strength, and the crotch cannot close (腰精下去……)

The two elbows face outward; the two fists face each other, one front one behind, essence closed together (兩肘向外……)

The two shoulders relax down; do not raise them like a shelf (兩肩鬆下，勿上架)

The crown essence is well led; the crown essence connects below to Changqiang (長強) — the key to the body (頂精領好……)

The eye's spirit fixes on the left hand and left fist (眼神注於左手左拳)

The chest must close forward, empty and hollow, containing the ten thousand images, utterly empty (胸要向前合住……)

The whole body, joint by joint, all closes essence facing one another; above and below close essence as one breath (全體節節……)

The right foot hooks inward, the inner heel pushes back, the toes close inward (右足鉤住……)

The right knee is slightly bent; bent, the crotch opens (右膝微屈……)

The crotch must open wide, must be empty, must be round, must be closed together (襠要開大……)

The big toe closes inward, exerting strength gripping the ground (足大拇向裏合……)

Bent and held, the left knee passes beyond the toes (屈住，左膝皆過足趾)

Trang sách 286 · scan p319

(Printed page: 286)

Yin Meng (Guiding the Novice)

The left and right hands go from above downward, from the front to the right and then behind; then from behind the right, turning forward again, they sweep one large circle, and the stroking fist (掳捶 lu chui) comes to rest before the chest. The left hand (when the hand is named, the forearm is included) uses coiling energy in the forward direction (顺转精 shun zhuan jin); the right hand uses reverse coiling energy (倒转精 dao zhuan jin); the left leg uses forward-coiling energy, the right leg reverse-coiling energy — this was already explained under Shuai Jiao (摆脚). The left and right elbows face outward; the left and right fists point upward along the arm. The whole upper body applies embracing-closing energy (抱合精 bao he jin) — it is the energy within the chest. The movement runs from the left, from above downward, then from below upward, turning one circle from the right around to the left, the chest closing forward. The crotch energy opens round and closes firmly (裆精 dang jin); the toes of both feet grip, holding the energy; the energy at the crown is held up (顶精 ding jin); the shoulders, knees, and ankles all turn outward-to-inward, and the combined force gathers in the fist. The eyes look at the space between the left and right hands. This posture is also called Hu Xin Quan, "Protect the Heart Fist" (护心捶), closely echoing the first posture Jin Gang Dao Dui (金刚捣碓, "Buddha's Warrior Attendant Pounding the Mortar"): both take protection of the heart as their chief aim. If the heart-mind does not waver, then above, below and on all four sides one is attentive without omission.

Nei Jin (Internal Energy)

After turning the body, the left and right hands circle from behind forward. The left fist uses back-folding energy (背折精 bei zhe jin) — the fist strikes upward; using the back-folded elbow, the right fist holds its energy closed and comes forward to support the left hand. In this posture the left hand reverses its rotation from above downward, and the whole body turns following the turning of the left hand. For when the body secretly steps out one pace with the left foot and turns around, the right arm naturally gains momentum downward, unloading the heavy pressure from above. Once that pressure is eight or nine tenths unloaded, the left hand immediately uses forward-coiling back-folding energy to strike the opponent's left ribs, and the attack can hardly be resolved. Now the left hand, in usefulness, never matches the right hand in strength; but here the left hand is near the opponent and gains the position to strike first, so the spirit of the whole body must follow the momentum to aid the left hand. Outwardly the two hands face each other and join, yet in truth they both join from right to left; the unloading downward from the left is opening; the turning of the energy, joining from left to right, is closing. One rise and one join — and the art of the fist is complete. For the opening and closing of my body are the closing and opening of heaven and earth, and the closing and opening of heaven and earth are the opening and closing of my body. The human body is a small heaven-and-earth: one and two, two and one — uniting them is Taiji. Taiji is that in which yin and yang are already present but not yet formed; yin and yang are the names given once Taiji has divided. Movement engenders yang — this is opening; stillness engenders yin — this is closing. Therefore I say that one opening and one closing exhaust the art of the fist. When the left foot is in front and the right foot behind, the right foot advances level with the left, and the hands circle from below upward and settle before the chest — that is Jin Gang Dao Dui. Ending yet beginning again, beginning yet ending again, ending and beginning cycle without end. Hence in practice, perform each circle according to your own strength: one repetition will do, ten will do — the number does not matter. If you have strength, keep moving; when strength is spent, stop; do not force movement beyond the rules. Simply follow what is natural, and you will attain it.

Trang sách 287 · scan p320

(Printed page: 287; Volume 3)

Right-margin vertical title: 陳氏太極拳圖說 卷三 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo, Volume 3)

Zuo You Yun Xing Tu (Diagram of Movement Left and Right)

Left diagram (a curved line showing circular movement, five labels arranged counter-clockwise around it):

- 从北转东 cong bei zhuan dong — from the north turn to the east

- 往南去 wang nan qu — go toward the south

- 往北去 wang bei qu — go toward the north

- 往西去 wang xi qu — go toward the west

- 合左手 he zuo shou — joins with the left hand

Right diagram (a curved line showing hand movement, three labels):

- 左手自此卸下 zuo shou zi ci xie xia — the left hand lets the load down from here

- 左肘向左 zuo zhou xiang zuo — the left elbow toward the left

- 右手由右肋前推 you shou you you lei qian tui — the right hand pushes forward from before the right ribs

Main Text

This is the place of turning in the movement (转关 zhuan guan): once the bend is turned, the hand goes forward — that is the place of striking the opponent. If one does not turn a full circle, the strike has no power.

This is the method of moving along the path; the coiling silk energy (缠丝精 chansijin) belongs precisely within the movement of the two forearms.

Qu Xiang (Taking the Image)

The two hands part — this resembles the top line of the hexagram Kan (坎, Water); the body lying level in the middle resembles the middle line of Kan; the two feet apart resemble the bottom line of Kan — hence the image of Kan is taken. "Kan is full in the middle" means the solidity of yang resides within. Soft outside, firm within — that is the image of Kan. Kun, with the yielding of its middle line, exchanges with the middle line of Qian; the yielding changes into the firm — this is the proof that Kun crosses by its central qi, the image of the middle son. Joined with the previous posture: Li below, Kan above, forms Ji Ji (既济, After Completion); reversed, it forms Wei Ji (未济, Before Completion). The first posture, Jin Gang Dao Dui (金刚捣碓), is Taiji generating the two forms. Confucius said: "After heaven and earth exist, the myriad things arise; after the myriad things, there are male and female; after male and female, husband and wife; after husband and wife, father and sons — and from father and sons onward, generation is endless." The last two postures are middle son and middle daughter, blood and qi at their prime, principle full and qi abundant, with the promise of endless generation — hence Li and Kan are taken. Li below and Kan above is called Ji Ji: things are not exhausted; therefore it ends receiving Wei Ji.

Qi Yan Li Yu (Seven-Character Folk Verse)

阖辟刚柔顺自然，一扬一抑理循环。当头一炮人难御，动静形消太极拳（言皆归于太极，合太极）。

Closing and opening, firm and yielding, follow what is natural; one raising, one lowering, principle cycles round. A cannon straight at the head is hard to withstand; movement and stillness, form dissolved — all is Taijiquan (meaning: all returns to Taiji, merging into Taiji).

Wu Yan Li Yu (Five-Character Folk Verse)

太极理循环，相传不计年。此中有精义，动静皆无愆。收来名为引，放出箭离弦（此二句，上句言引进落空，下句言乘机击打）。虎豹深山踞，蛟龙飞潭渊（上句言静，下句言动）。开合原无定（活泼泼地），屈伸势相连（却有一定）。太极分阴阳，神龙变无方。天地为父母，摩荡柔与

Taiji's principle cycles; handed down without counting years. Within this there is refined meaning: in movement and stillness, no fault. Drawing in is called "leading" (引 yin); releasing is an arrow leaving the string (these two lines: the first speaks of leading in to emptiness — 引进落空; the second of striking by seizing the moment). Tiger and leopard crouch in deep mountains; flood-dragon flies in pool and abyss (first line speaks of stillness, second of movement). Opening and closing are by nature unfixed (lively and alive); bending and extending, the postures connect (yet there is a constancy). Taiji divides into yin and yang; the spirit-dragon changes without fixed method. Heaven and earth are father and mother; the grinding, tossing of the soft and the…

(The final line 摩荡柔与 breaks off at the foot of this page and continues on the next.)

Trang sách 288 · scan p321

(Printed page: 288)

Left-margin vertical title: 陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo)

(Note: the opening character 剛 gang — "firm" — completes the last line of the Five-Character Folk Verse carried over from page 320, 摩荡柔与…刚.)

Wu Yan Li Yu (Five-Character Folk Verse, continued)

剛。生生原不已，奇正不尋常，乾坤如囊籥，太極一大囊。盈虛消息故，皆在此中藏。至終復自始，一氣運弛張。有形歸无迹，物我兩相忘（與道為一）。太極拳中路，功夫最為先。循序無躐等，人盡自合天。空談皆漲墨，實運是真詮。鸢飛上戾天，魚躍下入淵。上下皆真趣，主宰貴精研。若問其中意，道理妙而玄。往來如晝夜，日月耀光圓。會得真妙訣，此即太極拳。凡事都如此，不但在肘間。返真歸璞后，就是活神仙。隨在皆得我，太璞自神全（仍歸太極）。

English rendering:

…firm. Generation upon generation never ceases; the direct and the unexpected are nothing ordinary. Heaven and earth are like a bellows (囊籥 nang yue); Taiji is one great sac. The workings of fullness and emptiness, increase and decline, are all hidden herein. Reaching the end, it begins again — one qi drives the slackening and the drawing taut. What has form returns to the traceless; self and things are both forgotten (one with the Dao). Of the paths in Taijiquan, gongfu comes first of all. Proceed in order, skipping no steps, and every person accords with heaven. Empty talk is but ink run wide; actual practice is the true interpretation. The kite flies up and strikes the sky; the fish leaps down and enters the deep. Above and below hold true delight; the master of it all prizes careful study. If you ask the meaning within it: the principle is subtle and mysterious. Going and coming are like day and night; sun and moon shine with rounded light. Once you grasp the truly marvelous secret — this very thing is Taijiquan. All affairs are so — not only at the elbow. After returning to truth and the uncarved block, one is a living immortal. Wherever one is, one finds one's self; the great uncarved block is whole in spirit (returning again to Taiji).

Trang sách 289 · scan p322

Appendix

Chen Shi Jia Cheng 陳氏家乘 (Chen Family Genealogy)

Chen Zouting 陳奏庭, given name Wangting 王廷. In the Ming he was a government-school student (xiangsheng 庠生); under the Qing he entered the military school, and was expert in Taijiquan 太極拳. He once traveled to Shanxi to visit friends and saw two boys wrestling; two old men stood by watching, and he watched too. One old man said: "Would you like to wrestle, sir?" He said: "Yes." The old man had one of the boys grapple with him. The boy wrapped his arms around his waist, lifted him, and struck his qihai 氣海 point with the knee three times, then set him down. Suddenly both old and young vanished; it was growing dark, and he returned home melancholy.

He was close friends with Li Jiyu 李際遇, a military graduate of Dengfeng County. When Dengfeng rose in disorder because officials drove the people to it, with Jiyu at its head, he tried to stop him. As they went up the mountain, arrows rained down like rain, yet could not wound him. He met one adversary and chased him, circling the imperial fort three times without catching him. After Li Jiyu's cause failed, a servant surnamed Jiang 蔣 served him — the very man he had once chased. This man could outrun a rabbit over a hundred paces, and was also skilled in boxing. Living in turbulent times, his exploits in sweeping away the disorderly hordes were beyond counting, but all records have been lost, leaving only one song of long and short lines (changduanju 長短句), which reads:

"Alas for those years, wearing armor and grasping sharp weapons, sweeping away the disorderly hordes, several times amid peril. Receiving imperial grace bestowed — all in vain. Now, old and gasping what breath remains, I have only the scroll of the Huangting 黃庭 to keep me company. When bored I create quan 拳 (boxing); when busy I till the fields. In spare moments I teach some disciples and sons and grandsons — to become dragons or tigers, as it may. Pay the official grain early, repay private debts at once. Pride and flattery: never use them; patience and yielding come first. Everyone says I am a fool; everyone says I am mad. I keep my ears washed and never dust my cap (seek no office), laughing to scorn the ten-thousand-household nobles whose anxious toil cannot compare with mine. My heart is ever at ease. Fame and profit I never covet; I have penetrated the mechanism and recognized that Handan 邯鄲 (dream of glory). Delighting myself among fish and water, lingering amid mountains and rivers — thriving means nothing to me, perishing means nothing. If only the world were at peace and I could live serene and tranquil, neither scheming nor craving, why should I care how the world fawns or freezes? Success is no concern, failure is no concern. If I am not an immortal, then who is?"

Chen Jingbai 陳敬柏, courtesy name Changqing 長青, a man of the early Qianlong era, loved Taijiquan. A Shandong thief of eighteen, coveting a pane of glass from the governor's window, flew over roofs and walls and escaped beyond the city; the constables dared not arrest him. Then he, with the camp, was ordered to make the arrest. The thief stabbed a knife toward Jingbai; he gripped the blade with his teeth, threw the thief out the door, and the thief submitted. After the case was solved, the thief also joined the camp

Trang sách 290 · scan p323

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo 陳氏太極拳圖說 · Appendix · Chen Family Genealogy (continued)

[He proved] effective. At that time the famous masters of Shandong were no match for him in art, so he was nicknamed "Gai Shandong 蓋山東" (Cover of Shandong), a testimony to the height of his skill.

Chen Yuhui 陳毓蕙, courtesy name Chuting 楚汀. A juren 舉人 (provincial graduate) of the renzi 壬子 year of Qianlong; magistrate of Huating 華亭, Fengxian 奉賢, Jinkui 金匱 and other counties in Jiangsu; Grain-Intendant Assistant Judge (tongpan 通判) of Changzhou Prefecture; Sub-prefect of Chuansha 廳; assistant examiner of the jiamao 丁卯 provincial examination.

Chen Bulai 陳步萊, courtesy name Pongsan 蓬三. A juren of the guiyou 癸酉 year; magistrate of Nanpi 南皮, Qinghe 清河, Julu 鉅鹿 and other counties in Zhili. Transferred as acting magistrate of Qiubei 丘北 County, Yunnan, later specially appointed magistrate of Mile 彌勒 County.

Chen Bushan 陳步蟾, courtesy name Lüqing 履青. A juren of the jiawu 甲午 year of Qianlong; magistrate of Mayang 麻陽 County, Hunan; assistant examiner of the wushen 戊申 provincial examination.

Chen Shan 陳善, courtesy name Jiamo 嘉謨. A government student; in the sixtieth year of Qianlong attended the Feast of a Thousand Elders (qiansouyan 千叟宴).

Chen Yuying 陳毓英, courtesy name Guanqian 冠千. A county-school student; in the sixtieth year of Qianlong, aged eighty-eight, attended the Feast of a Thousand Elders.

Chen Jixia 陳繼夏, courtesy name Bingnan 炳南, a man of the end of the Qianlong era, expert in Taijiquan 太極拳. Whenever he milled flour he began by pushing with both hands, then reduced by stages; when reduced to one finger he would still press with it, and even a single turn of the mill was never idle work; later his art surpassed his teacher's. He excelled at painting; the murals of the Guandi Temple in Zhaobao 趙堡鎮 were all his, lifelike and masterly. One day, while painting a Buddha image at Gusheng Temple 古聖寺 (west of Chengou 陳溝 village), someone snatched at him from behind; he threw the man down before him. Asked his name, it proved to be Rensan 荏三 of Henan, a famous figure in the art. He was filial to his mother, supporting her with simple fare (shushui chenghuan 菽水承歡), and the whole village was transformed by his example.

Chen Bingwang 陳秉旺, Bingren 秉壬, and Bingqi 秉奇 — all three were adept at Taijiquan; honing one another's skills, their art became refined to the point of spirit; people called them the "Three Heroes" (sanjie 三傑). Bingren was also expert in medicine. Bingwang's son Changxing 長興 fully inherited his father's learning, upright and dignified in bearing, nicknamed "Tablet Chen" (paiwei chen 牌位陳); his following of disciples was especially great — Yang Fukui 楊福魁 (Yang Luchan) being the most famous. Changxing's son Gengyun 耕耘, courtesy name Xiacun 霞村; Gengyun's sons Yannian 延年 and Yanxi 延禧 carried on the art. Gengyun once fought the Yue bandits (Cantonese Taiping rebels) under Zhongsheng 仲牲 and won military merit.

Chen Peng 陳鵬, courtesy name Wanli 萬里, a famous physician of the early Jiaqing era. He practiced Taijiquan to perfection; none could fathom its depths. Though poor, he kept himself upright; his qi 氣 was relaxed and free; his nature was tranquil, free of vulgar cares.

Chen Yaozhao 陳耀兆, courtesy name Youguang 有光, born in the Qianlong era, died in the Daoguang era at eighty. Charitable and generous, strict in household discipline, orderliness within and without; he taught his sons by righteous methods, and sons and grandsons all entered the government school. He was devoted to Taijiquan; the warriors of the day all received his teaching, yet in subtlety none surpassed him.

Chen Gongzhao 陳公兆, courtesy name Deji 德基. His learning was pure and correct; many famous scholars came from his school. Upright and square in his own conduct, in affairs he did not bend for private ends; charitable and generous. In the seventeenth year of Daoguang, a year of famine, he set up porridge kitchens and gave out food, saving countless lives. Every severe winter he bought clothing for the poor. For villagers hard-pressed at weddings and funerals he gave open-handedly without any air of benefaction. "Sowing grain and bequeathing good counsel" — his teaching was righteous. His sons Youheng 有恆,

Trang sách 291 · scan p324

Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo 陳氏太極拳圖說 · Appendix · Chen Family Genealogy (continued)

...Youben 有本 also entered the government school; of upright conduct, he was expert in Taijiquan 太極拳. His grandson Zhongsheng 仲牲 mastered its details and later repeatedly won merits in battle (he has a separate biography). He lived to eighty, and his neighbors, honoring his age and virtue alike, inscribed a tablet over his gate.

Chen Youheng 陳有恒, courtesy name Shaoji 紹基, and his younger brother Youben 有本, courtesy name Daosheng 道生, were both government students who practiced Taijiquan; Youben especially obtained the pearl of the art, and the skills of sons and nephews were all shaped by him. His bearing was modest and yielding, as if he were always falling short. The Taijiquan experts of the day nearly all came from his school. Elder and younger brother were friendly and respectful, constant from first to last, in perfect harmony. Youben's disciples Chen Qingping 陳清平, Chen Youlun 陳有綸, Chen Fengzhang 陳奉章, Chen Sande 陳三德 and Chen Tingdong 陳廷棟 all gained from him, and Chen Gengyun 陳耕耘 also studied under him. Qingping transmitted to He Zhaoyuan 和兆元, Zhang Kai 張開 and Zhang Gaoshan 張皋山 of Zhaobao 趙堡鎮; Youlun transmitted to Li Jingyan 李景延 and Zhang Dahong 張大洪. Jingyan also took Zhongsheng as master and once fought the Yue bandits (Taiping rebels). Tingdong was also skilled in saber technique.

Chen Zhongsheng 陳仲牲, courtesy name Yichi 宜箎, sobriquet Shichang 石厂. Clever from childhood, he ranged over the classics and histories; then, taking up the family Taijiquan, he abandoned letters for the martial and, obtaining its secrets, his art rose above others, with marvelous martial strength. In the third year of Xianfeng 咸豐 (1853), the Yue-Nian (Taiping) chieftains Lin Fengxiang 林鳳祥 and Li Kaifang 李開方 led several hundred thousand men into northern Henan, crossing the river by boat from Gong 鞏 on the eighteenth of the fifth month. He called for resistance, mustering his full brother, the military student Jisheng 季牲, clan brothers Hengshan 衡山 and Gengyun, eldest son Yao 堯, nephew Sen 森 and others, together with several hundred clan followers and over ten thousand village militia. On the twenty-first he met them in battle, leading his men into the enemy formation and killing several of the rebel "commanders." The bandits were defeated; pursuing, he slew several hundred more, enraging them, and on the twenty-second they plundered, burned and massacred, leaving every household on their path empty. Angered further, he resolved to fight on, repeatedly tearing down banners and slaying commanders. In melee of more than eighty exchanges he suddenly saw among the rebels men in yellow kerchiefs and yellow armor, beating drums, with the banner of Sima 司馬 raised beside them. Knowing this must be the chief, he flew forward and cut down the rebel leader as easily as taking something from a bag; the rest broke in terror. Yet foreseeing their vengeance, he first planned an ambush. On the twenty-third Jisheng lay in ambush north of the Hu 滹 River, Yao and Sen in the temple, Hengshan and Gengyun as reserves; he led his men luring the enemy across the Hu, when the ambushers burst out and attacked from three sides. The rebels could not hold, cast off armor, dragged weapons, trampled each other, and corpses lay over the fields — yet the enemy's main camp was still untouched. Another ambush was prepared: on the twenty-fourth Hengshan lay at Wujun village 伍郡村, Jisheng on the left of the ditch and Gengyun on the right, clan nephews Jingben 敬本 and others as wings, and the clan elder Junde 俊德 with Li Nanfang 李南方 and others as reinforcements. The rebels indeed marched out in force from Liulin 柳林. He met them first, his men vying not to fall behind; when blades met they mowed the enemy down like weeds. Suddenly he met a fierce foe — the rebel champion "Big-Head King" (Datou Wang 大頭王) Yang Fuqing 楊輔清, six chi tall, several spans around the waist, who once carried a bronze cannon under his arm and leapt over the parapet of the Wuchang walls, which thereupon collapsed — undefeated ever since. Seeing his earlier followers defeated, he came forward to fight. Seeing him imposing and huge, our hero would not underestimate him, but lured him into the ditch and wore him down with flanking ambushers. When the rebel's strength failed and he fled, he pursued, hooking his lance about his neck. The rebel was still crafty, hiding under the saddle; as he moved to strike at the horse's belly the rebel flew back onto the saddle; swiftly with one hand he thrust the lance and struck him squarely in the throat. The rebel toppled from his horse; he took his head; the rebel host fled in horror with scarce any hope of escape. Suddenly dust darkened the sky to the west and cannon shook the earth to the east; he swiftly ordered his forces split to meet them, and just as

Trang sách 292 · scan p325

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陈氏太极拳图说) — book page 292

Biographical continuation (no posture diagram on this page). ...Upon the relief arriving, Lord Li Wenqing led troops to assist; the bandits had already fled back into the willow groves. Being like a bird startled by the bow, they could not rest in their nest: they moved their rabble and besieged Qinhuai, but after fifty days it did not fall. Hearing that our lord was ordered to the rescue, they slipped away behind the Taihang Mountains. The commanders, hearing his name, vied to invite him with courtesy, but because his mother was old he could not bear to leave. Later when his mother fell ill he personally attended her with medicine, not undressing for months. When she died he mourned deeply, and the funeral followed the ancient rites exactly. From then he devoted himself to teaching students, and his pupils grew ever more numerous, his door always full of visitors.

In the sixth year of Xianfeng (1856), bandits harassed Bozhou. The imperially appointed militia commissioner ordered matters of bandit suppression; Grand Marshal Yuan of the Court of the Imperial Stud directed him to administer Henan's general military-supply bureau, and provincial officials Mo and Yu sent letters to former Wen county magistrate Zhang Li to accompany the camp. Our lord led village militia at double pace to Bo, and with his younger brother Jisheng won five victories in succession: first destroying the reinforcements of the Bailongwang temple, then striking the lair at Zhiheji, recovering Bozhou within days. The remnant fled to Chenzhou; pursuing them there, he won three battles in three engagements, beheading over a thousand and capturing carts of weaponry. In the seventh year bandits occupied Lu'an; in the sixth month, by letter from Grand Marshal Yuan and Governor Ying, he hastened to relieve Lu'an, besieging day and night and recovering the city in three days. For this great merit the Board of War, by imperial decree, granted him the sixth-rank cap and plume, to await appointment under the Hebei garrison. In the eleventh month bandits from Kaizhou and Qingfeng raided Anyang, Hua, Xun and other counties; Prefect Luo of Zhangde requested he relieve Zhangde — before he arrived the bandits fled east at the news: his repute preceded him. In the eighth year bandits rose on all sides; Zhang Lexing attacked Sishui. Our lord, on county orders, recruited militia and defended the river banks strictly; the bandits scouted for days but found no way to cross north, and Wen's territory remained safe.

In the ninth year Mengcheng and Fuyang fell; the imperially appointed commissioner's secretary, Lord Mao of the Shuntian prefecture, directed our lord to act with the grand wing-commanders Jia, Yi, Qiu and Luo, attacking the bandits from flank positions; they broke several stockades and recovered two cities. He was rewarded with fifth rank and the peacock plume, attached to the Hebei garrison as standby captain. In the eleventh year, the Long Spear Society bandit Li Zhanbiao led over a hundred thousand from Shandong raiding the three prefectures of Zhang, Wei and Huai; the commissioner Lord Lian ordered him to recruit militia for defense. He met the enemy at Muluan store in Wuzhi; the bandits reversed their banners and dared not move west.

In the sixth year of Tongzhi (1867), the bandit Zhang Zongyu led several hundred thousand from Jiang into Huai. Our lord, with his son Xin, nephew Sen, clansmen, disciples and several thousand militia, fought from morning until noon on the fourteenth day of the twelfth month. Sen killed several bandits one after another; grievously wounded, he still fought bravely to the death, but his horse stumbled and he fell to cannon fire. Our lord, grief-stricken and furious, urged the multitude to a furious battle: they killed two fierce generals, two banner commanders, and over two hundred crack soldiers. Fighting on till evening and rallying the remaining courage, they killed several hundred more. The bandits were finally routed and fled out of Huai territory. His lifetime's堆积 battle merits, on every tongue, were all rooted in his mastery of Taijiquan (太极拳). When he died, mourners came from many commanderies; all proposed renaming him "Yingyi" (英义, Heroic-Righteous), and I followed the multitude, saying: agreed. (Liu Yunan 刘毓楠)

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說) — book page 293

Appendix (附錄) — biographical sketches from the *Chen Family Genealogy* (《陳氏家乘》):

Chen Jisheng (陳季牲)①, courtesy name Jisui (伋隨), a military school graduate (武庠生), twin milk-brother of Zhongsheng (仲牲). He repeatedly won battle merits alongside his elder brother.

Chen Huamei (陳花梅), courtesy name Hezhai (鶴齋), studied under Changxing (長興) with very pure gongfu. His sons Wuchang (五常) and Wudian (五典) carried on his art; disciple Chen Xi (陳玺) fought the Yue bandits (粵匪) together with Zhongsheng.

Chen Hengshan (陳衡山), courtesy name Zhennan (鎮南), was expert in Taijiquan (太極拳). In the battle of Liulin (柳林), Hengshan was in the very front rank — a true warrior. Later he taught students.

Chen Zhongli (陳仲立), grand-nephew of Sande (三德), a military graduate. Highly practiced with bow and arrow; learned the quan (拳) from Sande, and was skilled with spear, saber, and eyebrow-level staff (齊眉棍).

Chen Tong (陳同), Chen Fuyuan (陳復元), Chen Fengju (陳丰聚), Ren Changchun (任長春)② — all disciples of Zhongsheng. In the third year of Xianfeng (咸豐, 1853) they fought with merit.

Chen Miao (陳淼), son of Zhongsheng's elder brother, courtesy name Huaisan (淮三), of righteous conduct. In the sixth year of Tongzhi (同治, 1867) when Zhang Zongyu (張總愚) invaded Qinhuai (覃怀) and raided Wen county, Miao led brave men against the bandits, shot several dead, was severely wounded, and though wounded still shouted in command of the multitude; his horse stumbled and he fell to cannon. His wife née Ran (冉氏) was honored for chastity and filiality (節孝).

Chen Yao (陳垚), courtesy name Kunsan (坤三), son of Zhongsheng, entered the military school at nineteen. Every year he practiced the quan (拳) ten thousand times, unremitting for twenty years. He fought bandits alongside his father and was never once defeated.

The above is excerpted from the *Chen Family Genealogy* (《陳氏家乘》).

Footnote ①: Based on extant historical materials, Chen Jisheng served before the 23rd year of Daoguang (道光) as magistrate of Julu county (鉅鹿縣知縣), later accumulating merits alongside his brother: promoted to sixth rank in the 7th year of Xianfeng (1857), granted fifth rank in the 8th year, and in the 9th year imperially titled Wu Jie Jiangjun (武節將軍, Martial General of Military Integrity).

Footnote ②: The original edition misprinted "Ren Changchun" as "Liu Changchun" (劉長春). Based on the 1915 *Zhongzhou Wenxian Jizhi* (《中州文獻輯志》) and the *Zhongzhou Xianzhe Zhuan: Biography of Chen Zhongsheng* (《中州先哲傳·陳仲牲傳》) and other sources: in his youth Ren Changchun worked as a hired laborer in the household of Chen Zhongsheng, the Qing "Wu Jie Jiangjun", while also learning Taijiquan, until Zhongsheng's death. Hence "Liu Changchun" has been corrected to Ren Changchun.

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Appendix (附錄) — book page 295

Biography of Lord Chen Yingyi (陈英义公传)

The master Chen of Yingyi (英义, "Heroic-Righteous" — his posthumous name) was named Zhongshen (仲甡), courtesy name Zhixun (志壎), also Yichi (宜篪), sobriquet Shichang (石厂). His ancestors lived in Hongdong (洪洞), Shanxi, and migrated to Wen (温) in the Hongwu era of the Ming (明洪武); for generations the family held hidden virtue, passing down farming and study. The master had three brothers; he and his younger brother Jishen (季甡) were born of the same milk, so alike in face that neighbors could not tell them apart. As a child he was remarkably intelligent; at three he accidentally fell into a well, and a white tiger carried him out — the water was over a zhang deep, yet his clothes were not wet. When somewhat older he grew weary of the study of exegesis (章句), abandoned letters for the martial arts, learning to be "a match for ten thousand men"; stratagems and skills, none did he not master. Yet he was always scholarly and courteous, never once fighting with anyone, settling disputes for his community — his reputation for righteousness was known to all. By nature hospitable, he admired the manner of Beihai (北海, Kong Rong); with friends he made no distinction between "you and me". He entered the military school together with Jishen, both hoping to advance, to glorify the family gate and serve the state. But fate was perverse (数奇), and he failed the examinations. So he withdrew to the woods, taught students, farmed with his own hands and cared for his parents, with no further thought of office.

In the fifth month of the third year of Xianfeng (咸丰, 1853), the Yue bandits (粤匪, Taiping) crossed the river, several hundred thousand strong, intending to flatten Heshuo (河朔). The whole prefecture trembled, none sure of life; Wen, right on the river, feared most. County magistrate Zhang personally visited his home and earnestly begged him to resist the enemy. Thinking of his native place (桑梓), righteousness would not permit refusal: he donned armor, grasped weapons, championed loyalty (勤王), led several hundred students straight into the enemy camp, charging left and right as through empty ground. He killed their fierce general nicknamed "Big Head King" (大头王), and dozens of their pseudo-sima and pseudo-commanders. The bandits fell back greatly, then secretly moved to besiege Huaicheng (怀城) — but their keen edge was blunted, and when the great commanders' troops arrived they fled at mere rumor. The commanders, hearing the master's name, all revered him, sending envoys daily, many calling in person. Mindful of his aged mother he firmly declined. Later, having no choice, he went to see the commanders, among them Li, governor of Henan (河南省巡抚), his examiner-patron (座师), who at their meeting said he regretted not having recognized the hero earlier, and pressed him to stay and break the enemy; only after repeated courteous refusals did he let him go home to care for his mother. When the affair was settled, memorial was made and he was granted the fifth rank with peacock plume (五品花翎). His heart at rest serving his mother, he was never moved by rank or fame — such was his detachment. Later his mother fell ill; he personally attended her with medicine, not undressing for over a year. At her death he mourned until flesh clung to bone; the funeral followed the ancient rites, and mourners came from several commanderies. From then he devoted himself to teaching; his pupils grew ever more numerous, outside the door—

He had three sons: Yao (垚) entered the military school (武庠); Yan (焱) and Xin (鑫) became annual tribute students (岁贡); all were brilliant and spirited with their father's manner, and people said the lord had worthy sons. The lord was born in the 14th year of Jiaqing (嘉庆, 1809), 27th day of the first month, at the yin hour (寅时, 3–5 a.m.), and died in the 10th year of Tongzhi (同治, 1871), 14th day of the tenth month, at the xu hour (戌时, 7–9 p.m.), aged sixty-three. On the day of his death the neighbors grieved; mourners filled the gate, and all proposed renaming him "Yingyi" (英义). I, unworthy, enjoyed twenty years of friendship with the master, and I too followed the multitude, saying: agreed.

*Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo* (陳氏太極拳圖說), Appendix (附錄) — book page 297

Biography of Chen Zhongshen (陳仲甡傳), from the Zhongzhou Xianzhe Zhuan (《中州先哲傳》)

Chen Zhongshen (陳仲甡), courtesy name Yichi (宜篪), was a native of Wen county (溫縣). In the early Qing there was one Chen Wangting (陳王廷), expert in the quan method (拳法), on good terms with Li Jiyu (李際遇) of Dengfeng. When Jiyu raised troops, Wangting went to stop him; arrows fell like rain yet could not wound him. For this reason many in the Chen clan through the generations studied his art, and Zhongshen's skill was counted the finest.

In the fifth month of the third year of Xianfeng (咸豐, 1853), the Yue bandits (粵寇) Lin Fengxiang (林鳳翔) and Li Kaifang (李開方) led several hundred thousand across the river at Gong (巩) and occupied the willow groves on the east bank at Wen, their power swelling greatly. Zhongshen called on the villagers to drive out the bandits: with his younger brother Jishen (季甡), Gengyun (耕耘), nephew Sen (森), eldest son Kun (壵), and several hundred of their students, over ten thousand village militia (鄉勇) assisted. On the twenty-first he met them in battle, plunging into their ranks and killing several enemy commanders; the bandits broke, and he pursued and cut down several hundred more. Next day the bandits burned and slaughtered wildly — everything they passed became wasteland — and sent fierce cavalry to press in. Zhongshen led his men hand-to-hand, each one matching a hundred; the bandits were swept away, the dead lying one upon another; he beheaded one chieftain, and the bandits again fled. Frustrated in successive battles, the bandits poured out of the willow groves in full force, about a hundred thousand.

Zhongshen ordered Jishen to ambush with his men on the left of the gully, Gengyun on the right, and himself faced the enemy. One fierce bandit, six chi tall, his waist several arm-spans around, fought to the death. Zhongshen marveled at his appearance and lured him into the gully; the ambush sprang, and Zhongshen struck at his neck with a spear. The bandit hid under his horse's belly, grappled Zhongshen down, then leapt back into the saddle; Zhongshen's spear pierced his throat and took his head. This was the bandits' fierce general Yang Fuqing (楊輔清), the "Big Head King" (大頭王) who, when Wuchang fell, had leapt onto the walls carrying a bronze cannon. The bandits scattered in four directions; by the time Li Tangjie (李堂阶) arrived with village troops, the bandits had fled back into the willow groves. Since the bandits had risen in western Yue (粵西) and swept through several provinces unopposed, resisting them with village militia began with Zhongshen; his name spread among the commanders.

In the sixth year, militia commissioner Yuan Jiasan (袁甲三) ordered him to attack Bozhou (亳州): five battles, five victories. Pursuing the bandits to Chenzhou (陳州): three battles, three wins, over a thousand slain or captured. Seventh year: took Lu'an (六安州) with others. Eighth year: Zhang Lexing (張樂行) attacked Sishui (汜水); Zhongshen defended the river. Ninth year: commissioner Mao Changxi (毛昶熙) ordered him to attack Mengcheng (蒙城) and Fuyang (阜陽). In the twelfth month of the sixth year of Tongzhi (同治, 1867), the Nian bandit Zhang Zongyu (張總愚) led several hundred thousand from Shanxi against Huaiqing (怀庆). With his son Xin (鑫), nephew Sen and several thousand students Zhongshen resisted, from dawn to mid-afternoon beheading two enemy generals, two banner-holding commanders, and several hundred bandits before the enemy was finally routed. Sen shot dead several—

Trang sách 298 · scan p331

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo (陈氏太极拳图说, Chen Family Taijiquan Illustrated Explanation) — book page 298

English translation

...the bandits: although struck by a spear he fought to the death; his horse suddenly stumbled, and he fell to cannon fire in the line of battle. Zhongsheng (仲牲) was then over sixty years old; he died not long after, and people near and far mourned him, posthumously styling him "Yingyi" — "Heroic and Resolute" (英毅). Zhongsheng served his parents with filial piety, taught his sons strictly, and kept faith with his friends; yet he was always gentle and scholarly in manner, and never quarrelled with anyone. Jisheng (季牲), courtesy name Fangsui (仿随), was a military licentiate of a county school (wu xiangsheng, 武庠生). Those who transmitted his teaching were Chen Tong (陈同), Chen Fuyuan (陈复元), Chen Fengju (陈丰聚), Li Jingyan (李景延), and Ren Changchun (任长春); yet none of them equalled Zhongsheng.

(In the fourth year of the Republic, yimao year of the sexagenary cycle [1915], Mr. Minxiu, collecting documents of Zhongzhou [Henan], obtained the Chen Family Genealogy (陈氏家乘) of Wen county, took the deeds of our late forebears, and placed them in the "Biographies of Men of Righteous Conduct" (义行传) of the Zhongzhou documents collection. Because I wrote a preface for Mr. Minxiu and there discussed the deeds of our late forebears, I have carried this account forward and set it before it here, so that readers may know that Taijiquan has rendered service to the world. Recorded respectfully by your son, Xin [Chen Xin]).

Trang sách 299 · scan p332

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo (陈氏太极拳图说) — book page 299 (Appendix, 附錄)

English translation

[Margin] Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo — Appendix (fulu, 附錄)

[Title] Epitaph of Mr. Chen of Wen County (Wenxian Chen Jun Mu Ming, 溫縣陳君墓銘) — by Zhang Jiamou (張嘉謀) of Nanyang

The Chen clan of Chenjiagou in Wen County has transmitted Taijiquan (太极拳) through the generations. During the Xianfeng reign, Lord Yingyi (英義公), Zhongsheng (仲牲), practised it with exceptional refinement and rendered service to his home region. Mr. Chen was Yingyi's youngest son; his taboo name was Xin (鑫), his courtesy name Pinsan (品三), and he was a stipend tributary student (lin gongsheng, 廩貢生). Carrying on his forebears' aspiration, he took the boxing classics (quan jing, 拳經) to heart, synthesized the many manuals, and rooted everything in the Yijing (易). Of the River Chart and Luo Writing (河圖洛書), the Former Heaven and Later Heaven arrangements, the hexagram images and line images — whatever he saw was nothing other than Taiji (太極); he reduced it all to the method of silk-reeling essence (chansi jing fa, 纏絲精法) and completed the Taijiquan Tu Shuo (太極拳圖說) in four juan. He also compiled the Chen Family Genealogy (陳氏家乘) in five juan. He may truly be called one who continued and transmitted well, bringing honour to the martyrs who went before him.

As Taijiquan spread over time, although all schools trace their ancestry to the Chen family, each took what it had gained and taught it on in turn. Some taught orally with no books; booksellers sought profit, plagiarizing and abridging, pandering to the mentality of those who fear difficulty, want quick results, and do not seek thorough understanding. Students suffered from having no way to see the whole. Mr. Chen was deeply troubled by this. In old age, without a son of his own, poor and ailing, he summoned his elder brother's son Chunyuan (椿元)① from Xiangnan and, on his return, entrusted the book to him, with the words: "If you can transmit it, transmit it; otherwise burn it — do not give it to reckless men!" It happened that Henan was compiling its provincial gazetteer; Director Han ordered Jiamou, together with Wang Ziyuan-bai (王子圜白) and the Reader-in-ordinary Du Youmei (杜編修友梅), to visit and see Mr. Chen's book. By then several years had passed since Mr. Chen's death and he was about to be buried. Through the mediation of Liu Yingxian (刘瀛仙) of Gong county, Chunyuan sent the book with a request for an epitaph. Jiamou admired that Chunyuan could read a chest of books and had inherited the family learning; he also grieved that our country, long accumulated in weakness, was gradually falling ill, and that readers of the essential Way of the ancient sages readily lose its truth. So he entrusted it to Wang Ziyuan-bai, and appended these words:

The Taiji circle (太極圈) embraces heaven and earth. Who has laboured to break it open? The Chen family's hereditary fist. Its source is probed in the Yijing, its subtleties studied and deep mysteries hooked out: the River Chart and the dragon-horse, wood and fire rising in the dark; the Luo Writing and the tortoise-and-serpent, metal and water hidden and firm; thunder, wind, mountain and lake, Kan, Li, Kun and Qian. At fifty Confucius studied the Changes — the mind-transmission of Mount Ni...

[Footnote ①] Chen Chunyuan (陈椿元) was the third son of Chen Xin's elder cousin Chen Sen (陈森). Chen Sen had three sons: eldest Meiyuan (梅元), second Huaiyuan (槐元), third Chunyuan (椿元); the Chen family genealogy and stele inscriptions record Chunyuan. Zhang Jiamou of Nanyang, in the "Epitaph of Mr. Chen," and Liu Huandong of Gong county, in the "Postface to the Wenxian Chen Pinsan Taijiquan Manual," mistakenly wrote "Chunyuan" (椿元) as "Chunyuan" (春元). Zhang Jiamou and Liu Huandong were the proofreaders of this book, and while proofreading they further misaltered the editor "Chunyuan" (椿元) to "Chunyuan" (春元). This edition uniformly corrects "春元" back to "椿元"; this explanation is hereby given.

[Printed page number] 299

[No diagrams on this page.]
Trang sách 300 · scan p333

Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo (陈氏太极拳图说) — book page 300

English translation

[Margin, book title] Chen Shi Taijiquan Tu Shuo (陳氏太極拳圖說)

Issuing from Zhen, completing at Gen — thus the four seasons move (出震成艮，四时行焉). A summary of the essentials: the subtle words on silk-reeling (chansi, 缠丝), rooted between waist and spine (腰吕 — the printed character reads lü 吕, probably for lü 膂, the spine). Up and down, left and right; forward, reverse, inverted, overturned; great circles and small circles — the square of the carpenter's square, the circle of the compass (矩方规圆). Waning and waxing, emptying and filling (消息盈虚); coming and going like thunder and whip; twisting and coiling, folding and unrolling; the sail turned with the wind or against it. Riding the whirlwind on ram's-horn gusts, roaming free and unfettered (逍遥 — after Zhuangzi's Peng bird); above ninety thousand li, in the sixth month planning the southward flight. The bones and joints sound in unison, harmonious as the cries of phoenix and luan; light as a drifting swan's feather, heavy as a settling Mount Ao. Water flows and flowers open; the gorge is cut but clouds join across it; with mind and without mind (有心无心) — all of itself so, naturally (自然而然). When dragon and tiger finish their battle, the True Man lies hidden in the deep (真人潜渊); floating and roaming within the compass-circle (规中), subtly attaining the ring/hinge (妙得其环). Therein both martial and civil (乃武乃文), therein the sage aspires to Heaven (乃圣希天); boxing — or immortality? The Way comes before the art (道在艺先).

[Printed page number] 300

Trang sách 301 · scan p334

*This scan page is not a form-posture page: it is the opening page of prose from the appendix — a postscript (跋 ba). No diagrams, no labels.*

Center title (horizontal):

跋 (Ba — Postscript)

Right margin (vertical):

陳氏太極拳圖說　附錄 (Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo — Appendix)

Main text:

The four fascicles of the Taiji Quan Illustrated Manual (太極拳圖譜) reproduced here were transmitted by Mr. Chen Shichang (陳石廠) of our Wen county; his worthy son, the xiucai Chen Pinsan (陳品三), following the postures, elaborated them fully with illustrations and explanations, intending to hand them down to later generations far and wide.

I observe that people in the world who possess boxing skill often indulge in the courage of animal vigor and do not keep to what is right; some, deeming themselves bold knights-errant, run rampant and bully their home villages — these are what the Grand Historian (Sima Qian) called the "Zhi the Robber living among the people." As for those who throw away their bodies to repay favors through vengeance, like the class of Zhuan Zhu and Nie Zheng, they are called men of lofty righteousness, yet in truth they are moved only by private obligation. Those who possess refined boxing skill and direct it toward loyalty and righteousness are few indeed; and those who root it in principle and the Way (li, dao) are fewer still. Now, in observing this Taiji quan fa (太極拳法, the method of Taiji boxing): it traces its source to the He River and Luo River charts (Hetu–Luoshu), draws upon the Neijing (內經, the Inner Classic of medicine), and mostly rests on the established sayings of the former Confucians; and where it presses most urgently on a man, it is again in governing oneself with reverence (jing 敬) and receiving things with humility (qian 謙). Is this not precisely what it is to root oneself in principle? As for its issuing forth in loyalty and righteousness, this shines all the more plainly before the eyes and ears of all. Yet I hold that the master's loyalty and righteousness were not merely the brief glow of "one hand and one foot" (a single act of personal prowess); their bearing on the larger course of events was truly not slight.

In the third year of Xianfeng (1853), the bandits of the south, followers of Hong [Xiuquan] and Yang [Xiuqing], having already taken Jiangning (Nanjing), dispatched their partisans Lin Fengxiang, Li Kaifang and others to invade the north. In the fifth month of that year, from Gongxian on the Luo River they seized civilian boats, crossed the Yellow River, and attacked Wen county, encamping among the willow groves of the river flats. One Yang Fengqing (楊奉清), known among the rebels as "Big-Head King" (大頭王), was the fiercest and most daring, able to scale a city wall carrying two bronze cannon. The rebels relied on his valor: wherever he went nothing solid withstood him, no assault failed. But when he reached Wen, Master Shichang destroyed him with the method of Taiji boxing in a ditch. At that time the fame of Chenjiagou's boxing valor was known throughout the realm, and the rebels, their spirit broken, left Wen and laid siege to Huaiqing. Yet having lost the man they relied upon, they besieged it for more than forty days and could not take it.

When the rebels crossed the river, their intent was to drive straight north and fall directly upon the capital. But barely had they reached Wen when the savage chieftain was destroyed, so that they were pinned beneath strong walls, the days dragging on; the capital made its preparations, relief armies gathered on all sides, and the rebels' original plan came to nothing. Therefore I say the master's loyalty and righteousness bore on the whole enterprise, and were no small matter. Had Yang not been destroyed, Huaiqing might not have held; and had Huaiqing not held, the rebels, bearing the unchecked momentum with which nothing solid withstood them, would have reached the capital itself — the whole situation would have been beyond imagining. Of old, when the two lords Zhang [Xun] and Xu [Yuan] died defending Suiyang, commentators said that with one corner [of the empire] they screened the Jiang–Huai region, so that the rebels could not drive the whole of their force straight at Chang'an. By extension...

*(continues on the next page)*

Trang sách 302 · scan p335

*Continuation of the appendix postscript (跋 ba) — prose page, not a form-posture page. No diagrams, no labels.*

Margin (vertical):

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo)

Main text (continuing from the previous page):

...it amounted to a great merit of the restoration of the Tang. If, then, the master destroyed Yang so as to protect Huaiqing and thereby preserve the capital, how could his merit be allowed to pass unrecorded? From this I came to understand the method of Taiji boxing (太極拳法): that it issues forth in loyalty and righteousness because it is rooted [in the Way] and comes to fulfillment in principle (li 理). Compared by this standard, those in the world who merely carry boxing skill on their backs — how could they be spoken of in the same breath!

Mr. Pinsan (陳品三, Chen Xin) and I were once fellow zhusheng (諸生, government students) together, and we were friends through letters; that is why, on the basis of this Taiji manual (太極拳譜), I have reasoned things out at such length. As for the master's other military merits, since they bore no relation to the larger course of events, I do not record them here.

Colophon (end of page):

大中華民國十一年壬戌孟冬　晚生李春熙敬跋

(First winter month of the renxu year, eleventh year of the Republic of China [1922]; respectfully postscribed by your junior, Li Chunxi 李春熙)

Printed page number: 302

Lời Tựa & Phụ Lục / Preface & Appendix

Trang sách 303 · scan p336

*A second postscript (跋 ba) in the appendix — prose page, not a form-posture page. No diagrams, no labels.*

Right margin (vertical):

陳氏太極拳圖說　附錄 (Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo — Appendix)

Center title:

跋 (Ba — Postscript)

Main text:

My friend Sun Zhonghe (孫仲和) related to me the story of how Mr. Chen Zhongsheng (陳仲牲) seized the "Big-Head King" (大頭王) with his bare hands — a display of heroic spirit and righteous bearing that left one awestruck. He also told how at the house of a certain elderly gentleman, several dozen big bandits arrived suddenly; the gentleman entertained them with fair words and urgently summoned Zhongsheng. By then the red candles were burning high and the banquet was arrayed in splendor. Zhongsheng entered and at once extinguished the candles; the bandits raised a great clamor and drew their blades, striking at one another in the dark. In moments all was silent — the bandits had all killed one another, while Zhongsheng remained entirely unharmed. For when the bandits were striking each other down, he kept himself at their hand's side the whole time, using the bandits to die in his place, while he himself stood in the middle of the banquet with a bowl held in his hand and one foot planted — one may call it utterly marvelous, and it made one's awe boundless.

Now I have seen this Taiji quan manual (太極拳譜): it is what Mr. Chen won through a lifetime's application of effort, and the art was used to be transmitted to his family. That is why, down to today, the Chen clan of Chenjiagou in Wen county — men and women alike — all practice this art and are known for divine valor. Because of the fear that it be kept secret and shown to no one, [Chen Xin] has now expounded it in full detail and had it printed to hand down to the world — this is truly "the great Way held in common" (大道為公). Readers of this book, if they can ponder it deeply and study it with diligence, will gain great benefit for their health and strength; from this comes self-strengthening, and with it a strong nation will not be hard to reach. As for the principle of taiji (太極之理), the author's own preface and the various prefaces by the worthies have discussed it in detail, so I do not repeat it here; I have recorded his lesser-known deeds so that later generations may have something to examine.

Colophon:

後學荊文甫謹跋 (Respectfully postscribed by your later student, Jing Wenfu 荊文甫)

Printed page number: 303

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo)

Printed page number: 304

Trang sách 305 · scan p338

*This page is the postface (後敘 hou xu) of the appendix — prose, not a form-posture page. No diagrams, no labels.*

Right margin (vertical):

陳氏太極拳圖說　附錄 (Chen Shi Taiji Quan Tu Shuo — Appendix)

Title (centered):

溫縣陳品三《太極拳譜》後敘 (Postface to the Taiji Quan Pu of Chen Pinsan of Wenxian)

Main text (paragraph 1):

In my youth I was friends with Master Guan of Wen county, Shaozhou (關紹周), and from him heard of the great deeds of the taiji grandmasters of Chenjiagou, Chen Zhongsheng (陳仲牲) and his brothers, in killing enemies and defending their homeland, and I secretly admired them. When I grew up, I traveled north and south for more than twenty years and saw a good many taiji boxing books, yet of Chenjiagou alone nothing was heard; I privately wondered whether their learning had perhaps been lost. This spring I met Chen Chunyuan (陳椿元) at Jiaozuo, and he showed me the Taiji Quan Pu (太極拳譜) written by his uncle, Mr. Pinsan (品三). Grounded in the profound purport of the Yi of Fuxi (羲易), following the acu-points and channels of physiology, it explains the marvelous function of every posture and points out the knack of entering the gate; it sets out the results of six hundred years of painstaking research by the famous philosophers of the Chen lineage generation after generation, and sets them down freely in writing concealing nothing — washing away once and for all the old habit of boxing masters who guard secrets and pass on nothing. I received it and read it with joy and astonishment: the learning of Chen-style taiji had indeed not died out, and moreover had achieved great new insights. Truly he is the Mencius or Xunzi of the Confucian school, the Ashvaghosha or Nagarjuna of the Buddhists!

Mr. Pinsan's name was Xin (鑫). He was the second son of Lord Zhongsheng and a suigongsheng (歲貢生, annual tribute student) of the Qing. Beyond his teaching duties he studied the boxing arts intensively and transmitted his father's learning in full. In his later years he exerted himself for more than ten years to complete this book. He wished to print and publish it in his lifetime, but his wish was not fulfilled. He had no son; on his deathbed he handed the complete manuscript to his nephew Chunyuan, saying: "This is my life's heart's blood. If you can print and circulate it, very good; otherwise, burn it." When I heard Chunyuan's words I was grieved, and reflecting that in this day of powerful invaders pressing upon us, this manual urgently ought to be printed, so as to train the physiques of the nation's people. In the seventh month, on business I traveled to Xu–Hai, Ping–Tian [Beiping–Tianjin] and Datong, and everywhere sought out able-minded fellow enthusiasts. The Henan guoshu masters Chen Junfeng (陳峻峰), Zhang Jiruo (張霽若) and Bai Yusheng (白雨生) all contributed generously. Returning to Kaifeng (Bian 汴) in the eighth month, the gentlemen Zhang Zhongfu (張中孚), Guan Baiyi (關百益), Wang Keting (王可亭) and Han Zibu (韓自步) likewise thirsted for the cause, zealously pooled funds, and after two meetings the book was given to the engravers (剞劂). Mr. Pinsan may now close his eyes beneath the ground: from today the world of national arts has opened another fascinating great way!

Main text (paragraph 2):

Yet I still harbor a quiet, secret worry: it is human nature that for a rare book kept in secret, one longs for it day and night, braving mountains and rivers, traveling tens and hundreds of li to seek it, copying and reading it morning and evening till the tongue wears out and the hands callus, and still not content. But once it circulates openly and can be had anywhere, one often cannot finish reading even one fascicle — why? Because familiarity breeds carelessness! My hope is that the people of this nation who read this manual will, just as...

*(continues on the next page)*

Trang sách 306 · scan p339

(Spine title: 陳氏太極拳圖說 — Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo, "Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan")

Preface (conclusion), by Liu Huandong (劉煥東):

异僧传道，黄石授书，特别宝重而熟玩之，不仅得之于心，更进而实有诸身。十年锻炼，一可挡千，孟贲遍地，四夷敛迹，恢复失土，发扬国权，则同人等努力刊行此书之微愿也。

Translation: "When an extraordinary monk transmitted the Way, and Huang Shi (the Old Man of the Yellow Stone) presented the book [of strategy], they were treasured and studied with special care — not only grasped in the mind, but further made real in the body. After ten years of training, one man could withstand a thousand. If men of Meng Ben's strength were everywhere, the surrounding peoples would withdraw and hide; lost territories would be recovered and national sovereignty exalted. This is the modest wish of us colleagues who have striven to publish this book."

中华民国二十一年双十节 — "Double Tenth Day (October 10), the twenty-first year of the Republic of China (1932)."

刘焕东谨叙 — "Respectfully prefaced by Liu Huandong."

(Printed page number: 306)

*Note: This is the final page of the preface (colophon-type page); the form-instruction diagrams end on the preceding pages.*

Trang sách 307 · scan p340

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo)

Appendix (附錄 / fulu):

Names (姓氏) connected with the newly published, revised and supplemented edition of the "Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan" (新刊订补《陈氏太极拳图说》姓氏):

- Original author (原著者 yuanzhuzhe): Chen Xin, courtesy name Pinshan (陈鑫品三)， of Wen County (温县).

- Editors (编辑者 bianjizhe): his nephews Chen Xueyuan (雪元) and Chen Chunyuan (椿元).

- Collaborating redactors (参订者 candingzhe): granddaughter Shuzhen (淑贞)； grandsons Jin'ao (金鳌) and Shaodong (绍栋).

- Collators/reviewers (校阅者 jiaoyuezhe): Chen Panling, courtesy name Junfeng (陈泮岭峻峰)， of Xihua; Liu Huandong, courtesy name Yingxian (刘焕东瀛仙)， of Gong County; Guan Baiyi (关百益， known by his courtesy name), of Kaifeng; Zhang Jiamou, courtesy name Zhongfu (张嘉谋中孚)， of Nanyang.

- Publication supporters (助刊者 zhukanzhe): Chen Panling of Xiping; Han Yunzhang, courtesy name Zibu (韩运章自步)， of Biyang; Zhang Jingming, courtesy name Jiruo (张镜铭霁若)， of Gong County; Bai Yusheng (白雨生) of Gong County; Guan Baiyi of Kaifeng; Zhang Jiamou of Nanyang; Wang Dishu (王帝枢) of Nanyang.

Editorial notes (注)：

"Xinkan dingbu" (新刊订补)： "xinkan" means first publication, "dingbu" means revision and supplementation of the original work — hence the title "newly published, revised and supplemented" (per the 1992 explanation of the National Copyright Administration regarding this book's title).

"Original author: Chen Xin Pinshan of Wen County" designates the author of the original work.

"Editors: nephews Xueyuan and Chunyuan" carries two meanings: the editors organized, revised and supplemented the incomplete manuscript into a complete work that could be published; "bosheng" (胞侄， nephews) indicates the kinship between the editors and the original author.

(Printed page number: 307)

Trang sách 308 · scan p341

陳氏太極拳圖說 (Chen Shi Taijiquan Tushuo)

Continuation of the editorial notes (注) on the appendix of names:

"Collaborating redactors (参订者 candingzhe): granddaughter Shuzhen, grandsons Jin'ao and Shaodong" — these are the workers who took part in organizing, revising and supplementing the book. The granddaughter Shuzhen was a granddaughter of Chen Sen (陈森)； the grandson Jin'ao was the eldest grandson of Chen Yao (陈垚)； the grandson Shaodong was the eldest grandson of Chen Sen. In this book, the "original author," "editors," and "collaborating redactors" are all listed in order of generational seniority and age.

"Collators/reviewers (校阅者 jiaoyuezhe)": among them, Chen Panling of Xihua was the director of the former Henan Provincial Guoshu (National Arts) Academy (河南省国术馆)； Guan Baiyi of Kaifeng was the director of the former Henan Provincial Museum (河南省博物馆)； Zhang Jiamou of Nanyang and Liu Huandong of Gong County were both highly respected public figures of the time.

"Publication supporters (助刊者 zhukanzhe)": Chen Panling, Han Zibu, Zhang Jiamou, Zhang Jingming, Guan Baiyi, Bai Yusheng, and Wang Bangshu were all well-known figures of the day, deeply concerned with the publication and distribution of the "Illustrated Explanation of Chen-Style Taijiquan" (《陈氏太极拳图说》)； for this they donated seven hundred silver dollars (大洋 dayang), without which the book could not have been published and distributed.

(Printed page number: 308)

*Note: No diagrams, figures, or verse text (歌诀) appear on this page. This concludes the back matter of the book.*
